All notable changes to Audiogravity (core, ui, license server, installers and this landing) are documented here. Format based on Keep a Changelog; the project follows Semantic Versioning.

[Unreleased]

Added

  • [site][ui][lic] The editions, the EULA, the third-party notices, the release notes and the changelog open on audiogravity.app. Each of these Markdown documents, at the root of the site's repository, is rendered by the manual's generator into a page of the site — docs/legal/ for the editions, the EULA and the third-party notices, docs/releases/ for the release notes and the changelog —, with its parts in the outline. The landing's footer, announce bar and version, the app's Proprietary License link and sign-in page, the license server's release-notes address and the manual's front page opened GitHub's view of the file; they now open these pages. The Manual workflow regenerates a page when its document changes.

Changed

  • [ops] A release keeps the footer's version and the documents' pages in step. changelog_rollover.py finds the footer's version by the changelog page's link as well as by the former GitHub one — anchored on that one alone, the version would have stopped moving without a word —, and release.sh prepare commits docs/legal and docs/releases with the regenerated manual.
  • [site] The EULA's definitions are a list. The five definitions ran together as one paragraph; the words are unchanged.
  • [site] The blog's Quad 33 and 303 post opens on the sound, and adds Haute Fidélité's review. Haute Fidélité (issue 275, June–July 2025) compared a restored pair of 1973 with the pair of 2024 on Rogers LS3/5a, and gave the new one its Meilleur achat: its side-by-side account, its hearing of the new pair and of its phono stage, the LS3/5a as a partner, the design team, the technical details and the French price join the post, attributed and translated. How the pairs sound now comes first, history after. The team replaces "engineers led by Peter Comeau", which no readable source supported: Quad's own history gives Jan Ertner as its electronics designer and Peter Comeau as head of acoustic design.

[1.0.1] - 2026-11-08

Added

  • [core][ui][manual] The library's Refresh says when it is over, and whether the music folder had changed. Once a Refresh of the local library has read the music folder and the list is back, a message says so: Library refreshed, or Library up to date when the music folder has not changed since it was last read. The turning icon was easy to miss on a phone, and nothing said the end. What the box compares is the player's own database stamp, before and after the reading: measured on both boxes, it moves when an album is added or removed or a file modified, and never when nothing changed. A refusal keeps its own message, a failed reading or reload shows in the list, and no message comes for a Refresh left for another source or from an artist's page that was left, nor for the streaming services, whose Refresh reloads in a second. The API's POST and GET /library/rescan gain changed. The manual says what each message means.
  • [core][ui][site][manual] A USB drive plugged in or pulled out is told, as a notification and in the app. A drive plugged into the box went unannounced, and nothing mounted the music library's drive until something read its folder: on the x86 test box, a minute and a half later, by a shell. The box now hears the drive arrive and says what became of it. The library's drive is mounted on the spot, and the message says it is ready — your music library is available, or the library is elsewhere and the drive can be chosen in Config → MPD → Guided —, or that it could not be mounted, with the mount's own reason. Another drive is said to be not mounted, with how to make it the library, or that it cannot be used, its format named, when the box does not mount it — only ext4 and exFAT are. A drive pulled out is told too — for the library's drive while MPD reads it, that the music is unavailable until it is back. The same words go to the devices subscribed to notifications and, as a toast, to every app open; the box listens only while one of them can be told, and at rest that costs nothing — no signal in 90 s, measured. A drive pulled out while in use leaves a mount of a device that is gone, which kept the drive plugged back in from mounting (measured on the x86 test box): it is taken away first, once nothing holds it, and the message says when something still does. The library is not read again when its drive comes back: it is read when it is chosen, or on a Refresh. The dashboard stream gains a usb_drive event; the manual's notifications, EDITIONS.md and the landing list it.
  • [site] The landing shows the VU meters, and the player on a phone held sideways. A screen joins the hero's carousel after the radio: the player with its two VU meters in place of the cover, with its caption and three labels — a needle per channel, the peak lamp, the Cover | VU meter switch. Under the VU meters' card, a full-width card, Held sideways, shows the player on a phone turned on its side, the same with its meters, and the showcase; three side by side, one under the other below 900 px. Each capture follows the page's theme, as the others do.
  • [site] The press release of Audiogravity 1.0, in English and in French. Each is a PDF of four pages — the release, a fact sheet, nine screenshots —, beside a ZIP of the nine screenshots at full size, all three in press/. A blog post presents them, Audiogravity 1.0: the press release, and the blog's front page now says it carries news of Audiogravity too. The landing's footer links the two PDFs on one line, Press release EN / FR.
  • [manual] What to do when the page does not open, and how to find the box's name again. Troubleshooting gains The page does not open — "refused to connect": what Chrome, Safari and Firefox say, told apart from took too long to respond; three commands typed on the box — ip route get 1.1.1.1 for its address, after src; pgrep -a avahi for its name; pgrep -af audiogravity-ui for how the interface is served, its port right after --port and --ssl for HTTPS —, the address to open in each case, sudo systemctl restart ag-ui-server for an interface that is stopped and sudo systemctl status ag-ui-server for why it stays so. Installation gains Find your box's name, for whoever no longer has the installer's last screen: the router's list of devices, the app's System tab and its support report, pgrep -a avahi on the box, and the names a box nobody renamed answers to, raspberrypi.local and dietpi.local; every page that writes <name>.local links to it.
  • [core][ui][manual] A track of the queue moves by its handle, and plays at a tap. Each row of the box's own queue has a handle at its left: held and slid up or down, the track follows the finger, the rows it passes slide aside, and the list scrolls by itself while the finger stays near the top or the bottom of what shows of it — above the mini-player on a phone. Dropped at the top of the list, the track plays next; the music goes on through a move, the track playing included (measured on a private MPD 0.24.5). The track takes its place at once, before the box answers, and a refused move puts it back with the box's reason. The box is told the track it lands beside, not a place, and finds that track when it moves the other: a queue changed meanwhile — a removal on its way, another device's edit — still gets it right where a place counted on the list shown sent it one further (measured). In random order the handles are greyed: the order decides nothing. A tap on a track's row — anywhere but on its handle, its arrow and its padlock, with the mouse too — plays it, the tracks above it skipped; a track a box without Pro skips leads to the licence panel, as its padlock does. While the music goes out through HQPlayer or a network speaker, which do not play the box's own queue, the tap is refused and the message names where the music goes. A guest account can play a track, as it can skip to the next one; it cannot move one. A Roon queue, changed from the Roon app, has neither. New: POST /library/queue/{queue_id}/move with { "before": id } or { "after": id }, and POST /library/queue/{queue_id}/play.
  • [site] Christmas — a cartoon scene in the snow, every 24 and 25 December. By the visitor's own clock — or any day with ?christmas in the address — the landing dresses up: at the foot of the phone a decorated tree whose bulbs blink, two elves, a snowman, a penguin and a polar bear stand in a bank of snow; snow falls around the phone, baubles swing on red ribbons, holly sits in two corners, stars twinkle on the dark page, and Santa's sleigh crosses the band above the first line now and then. Down the page, snow falls in the margins beside the text, a bauble hangs from each section's top and a figure stands at its foot on a mound of snow — on a phone, a small bauble above each section's label; on the footer's edge, a last scene and a gold bauble. The page opens dark unless the visitor chose a theme, and a fir tree heads the tab's title. Nothing covers a line of text or a control (checked at fifteen widths, in both themes); for a visitor who asks for less motion the snow and the sleigh go and the figures stand still. A part of the page out of sight is not drawn at all: with a CPU slowed four times, the first screen kept the browser no busier than on an ordinary day, the rest of the page at most ten points busier (Chromium). Nothing is fetched on another day.
  • [site] Christmas — the strip plays the Nutcracker, and an excerpt to hear. Those two days the strip that shows a track playing shows the Dance of the Sugar Plum Fairy by the Berliner Philharmoniker and Sir Simon Rattle, and its round button, Christmas red, plays a 30-second excerpt, fetched at the first press only; while it plays, the strip shows what is heard — AAC, 44.1 kHz and the excerpt's own time.
  • [site] Christmas — a hidden gift to find on the 24th, five lifetime licences to win. On 24 December 2026, Paris time, the licence server wakes five lifetime licences one at a time, each at a secret moment in its fifth of the day. The first visitor to open the page after one wakes is shown a gold gift — unlike the decoration's red and green ones — in the band above one of the sections, and has twenty minutes to open it; the page then shows the licence key, keeps it on the device and says how to activate it. The announce bar says how many licences are left, then that all were found; the rules are on hidden-gift.html. Halloween's orange ghost and the gift are one game in the page (assets/days.js), each with its own prize, words and rules.
  • [site] The hidden gift, announced before the day. Until 23 December the announce bar gives its place every six seconds to the news of the 24th — a gift hides on this page, five lifetime licences to win — which leads to the rules; it takes the place of the orange ghost's, over since 31 October. A phone reads a shorter sentence, on one line from 320px, and the bar keeps its height with either.
  • [lic] The landing's game runs on 24 December 2026, the hidden gift (GHOST_DAY's default). The keys of 31 October nobody found play again, under the same orange-ghost@audiogravity.app, and a key found then — activated or not — never does. A deployed .env needs no change: it names no game day, and one that did would hold the server to that day.
  • [ui] Christmas — the installed app opens in the snow, every 24 and 25 December. By the device's own clock, the screen the installed app shows while it opens dresses up as the landing does: snow falls on its black ground, Santa's sleigh crosses the sky once, a fir tree whose bulbs blink stands beside the icon with two gifts set down at its foot, the two waves turn red then gold, and the line reads Your audio chain, merrily controlled. The sign-in page's screen too; nothing else in the app changes. The screen stays no longer than on another day: with a CPU slowed four times it began to fade at the same moment, the extra work spent while the app loads, every animation run by the compositor (Chromium). For a viewer who asks for less motion the snow and the tree stand still and the sleigh does not fly. The drawings are the landing's, fetched from the box on those two days only. The sleigh flies in the middle of the sky, clear of the tree, a phone held sideways included. The script that decides the days, halloween-boot.js, is now holiday-boot.js.
  • [core][ui][manual] A media server's album or playlist plays whole, or from one of its tracks. Browsing a media server such as MinimServer, an album only opened, and a track tapped inside it played alone: the album stopped after it. An album or a playlist of the server now carries Play and + on its row, which play it or add it to the queue whole — a tap on the row still opens it —; inside one, a track tapped plays the album from that track on, the tracks before it left out, as on HIGHRESAUDIO and Qobuz, and its + adds that track alone. A folder only opens, as does a server's photo album. The box reads the album page by page, where one read stopped at 200 tracks and would have queued a longer album cut short; past 5,000 tracks it refuses, saying how many the album holds, rather than queue part of it. On MPD, HQPlayer and a network speaker alike; measured on a private MPD with MinimServer: an album of 13 tracks queued in order, 11 from its third track. POST /library/queue takes a media server's album or playlist, and start_index / start_item_id for it.
  • [ui] A media server's long lists go on as you scroll. Every list of a media server stopped at its first 200 entries: MinimServer's 474 albums showed 200, ending on IRM, and nothing after it could be reached. The next 200 now come as the reader nears the end; past 1,000 entries they wait for a tap on Show more at the list's foot, since every row stays in the page — MinimServer's 6,472 tracks scrolled to their end took the page from 14 MB to 42 MB (Chromium). An answer for a list already left is dropped, where it could write itself over the one opened since. The library's album grid shares the mechanism, and asks for its next page by itself when the one just listed leaves the end in sight.
  • [ui][manual] In Chrome or Edge on a computer, signing in to Tidal no longer needs a paste. On the box's https address, coming back to the app after copying Tidal's return address is enough: the card reads it from the clipboard — once the browser is allowed to — and completes the sign-in. It reads only while it waits for Tidal, and takes nothing but Tidal's own return address (https://tidal.com/android/login/auth?code=…): a password copied to sign in, or another site's address, is left alone and never sent. A code the box has already answered about is not sent again on its own, even after a reload. In Safari — so on an iPhone or iPad —, in Firefox, or with the permission refused, the paste remains.
  • [ui][manual] Reopen Tidal page starts a Tidal sign-in afresh. While the card waits for Tidal, the button asks the box for a new sign-in and opens Tidal's page again: after a closed tab, a refused address or a box restarted meanwhile, only reloading the page used to help. It waits while an address is being sent, so as not to replace the sign-in that address completes. When the browser blocks Tidal's page, the card says so and offers it as a link, which a tap opens.

Changed

  • [ui] The manual's copy button keeps its 28px on a phone. The touch rule made it 44px there, and every code block grew to hold it: it took a fifth of a phone's code line, and a one-line block stood 56px tall instead of 45 (measured in Chromium at 390px). It now keeps its 28px, as on a computer — above the 24px WCAG 2.2 sets as a minimum target —, as the dialogs' buttons keep their 29px.

  • [manual] Installing the app on a phone, step by step and device by device. First run's steps 7 and 8 are numbered steps for the iPhone or iPad, Android, the Mac and Windows, and a line for Firefox, each certificate ending on a check; their tables are gone — on a phone, the answer sat off screen. The certificate is fetched on the box's name, http://<name>.local:8081/ca.crt (served whatever name the box is reached by, measured), with its address as the way out. The words are the devices' own, read in Apple's help for iOS 27 and iPadOS 27 and in Chrome's: on an iPhone, Share behind ⋯; on an iPad, Share → View More; on both, the Open as Web App switch, without which the icon only reopens Safari; on Android, the app's own Install bar, or ⋮ → Install and create shortcut, and the certificate found by searching Settings for CA certificate. The quick start no longer sends an iPhone to https://<ip-of-your-box> before the certificate is trusted, which Safari does not open; Installation says to press Enter at the installer's two questions on a new install — HTTPS, port 443; the FAQ and the landing's FAQ follow. Troubleshooting adds a profile installed but not trusted, an icon that opens in Safari, admin / admin123 refused after installing again — the accounts are kept —, and an interface installed again in the other mode or on another port.

  • [site] The landing's players, Stats and Tuning show the app as it is today. Six of the carousel's screens are captured anew, in the light and dark themes: the HIGHRESAUDIO, Qobuz and Tidal players, the radio — another station —, Stats and Tuning. The players now carry the Cover | VU meter switch under the cover, which moved everything below it 4.7 % down, and Stats and Tuning changed: their labels are measured again, on the new captures, whose light and dark themes line up to the pixel. The Output → DAC row now sits under the phone's bottom edge, so the Qobuz screen loses its Switch output mid-track label and Tidal's Your DAC names the chain line, which ends on the DAC. HQPlayer's screen keeps its capture: its player offers no meter, which follows the box's own MPD only. The dark captures are named -dark, where they were -black.

  • [site] The landing's footer and top bar lead where their words say. Releases in the footer opened the GitHub page of the packages, which holds the install commands and nothing else: it is now Release notes, and opens them. Community / Issues share a line. The top bar's button, which said Releases and opened that same page, now says GitHub and opens the site's repository, whose README presents Audiogravity and whose Discussions, Issues and release notes are the footer's links.

  • [site] The social preview and the README's image show the landing as it is today. Taken anew from the page by gen-og-image.js: no Release candidate ribbon, the GitHub button and the Blog link in the bar.

  • [site][manual] EDITIONS.md and the manual's editions table list what each edition holds today. Starter gains AirPlay and casting — what an iPhone, iPad or Mac sends to the box, or an app casts to it, which the core leaves open to Starter —, and in EDITIONS.md the one-click updates from the Admin page and the eight push notifications the box sends, where it named four. Pro's Level meter is the VU meters, as the app names them, now in the manual's table too; Performance adds its network tests (ping, iPerf3). EDITIONS.md also gives the platforms (x86-64 and Raspberry Pi, aarch64) and the licence reset on request when moving to another machine, as the landing does.

  • [site] The README says what 1.0 does, where to read about it and where to ask. Its features add the VU meters and their showcase, the player on a phone held sideways, HQPlayer as the output — on the box, or on another computer with the box as its NAA — with the streaming services going through it, Qobuz's purchases and your own playlists, Tidal's playlists, HIGHRESAUDIO's Vault, sections, playlists and search, and the radio's lossless filter. Documentation lists the guides, the blog and the press release; Support sends questions, ideas and setups to GitHub Discussions, bugs to the issues, and gives the contact address; Contributing sends feature ideas to Discussions → Ideas.

  • [ops][site] UPnP Bridge on an ARM box is upmpdcli 1.10.1, the author's latest. Its author publishes no ARM package, so Audiogravity builds one: it was 1.9.17, and 1.9.18, 1.10.0 and 1.10.1 have followed since — according to the author's notes, mostly for the media server (its Tidal and Subsonic plugins, icons for plugins, caching), with a crash fixed in OpenHome. Built from the author's signed sources, it is offered as an update in the Audio Software tab of an ARM box where UPnP Bridge is installed, at once with CHECK UPDATES or after the box's daily check; the two libraries it needs are unchanged. An x86 box gets it from the author's own repository, as before. An ARM box still on 0.9.37 or older can no longer install UPnP Bridge: it looks for the 1.9.17 package, no longer published, and updating Audiogravity first lifts that.

  • [ui][manual] Pasting Tidal's return address completes the sign-in: the Finish login button is gone. The card recognises the address by the core's own rule and completes on the paste; an address without a code — the sign-in page's own, say — is answered at once, and nothing is sent. Enter still sends whatever the field holds. A refusal by the box points to Reopen Tidal page; a box out of reach asks to retry, the sign-in being still pending there.

Fixed

  • [core][ops][manual] A service's output names its sound card: a card that wakes under another number no longer takes the music elsewhere, nor makes the DAC disappear. The kernel numbers the sound cards in the order their drivers wake, and that order moves: on the x86 test box the USB DAC was card 0, 1 or 2, and the two built-in cards swapped numbers. MPD, AirPlay and HQPlayer Embedded were given hw:<card>,<device>, and played on another card, or on none, once the numbers moved; and the number the guided setup reserved for the DAC (/etc/modprobe.d/ag-audio.conf) made the kernel refuse the DAC altogether when a built-in card took it first. Every output Audiogravity writes or compares now names its card — hw:CARD=Abacus,DEV=0 —, which ALSA finds whatever its number: measured with MPD in PCM, DoP and native DSD, HQPlayer Embedded in DSD128 and AirPlay, and MPD still playing on the DAC after a reboot moved it from card 0 to card 1. The guided setup no longer writes the reservation. At the update, the installer names the cards in the files the guided setup wrote and in HQPlayer Embedded's, and takes the reservation out once nothing that may be the DAC's is numbered with it. A number only says which card had it the day the file was written: an address is named only once its card is confirmed — by the kind of output its MPD block was written for, by the card's own name, by the card the guided setup chose — and nothing contradicts it; one that is not keeps its number, the installer says so, and the next update tries again. The step that decides whether MPD turns DSD into PCM before its volume no longer reads such a number either: the x86 box's files, written when the DAC was card 0, would have had the DAC's DSD turned into PCM, card 0 being the built-in card today. Measured on copies of the three test boxes' files: the x86 box's left numbered and untouched, the two ARM boxes' named. The manual no longer says the DAC's number is pinned: First run says outputs are known by their card's name, and Troubleshooting what a flagged line of the support report's Audio stack means now.
  • [core][ui] The output switch is offered for the services whose output can change, and only for them. The UPnP renderer — which plays through MPD — and Roon Bridge — which audio-config.json declares as roon — were said not to be steerable, though a switch of either worked: neither got its output pills on the phone. The NAA was said to be: its output is chosen in the HQPlayer that streams to it, and written in no file of the box. A switch of the NAA's output now says so — The NAA's output is chosen in the HQPlayer that streams to it, not on this box — instead of writing nothing; the computer's diagram lists under Steer → only the services whose output can change; and the UPnP renderer's pill lights MPD's output, paused too. GET /steering/status says which services can be steered, and each service of the pipeline's nodes carries steerable.
  • [core] The signal path no longer shows Spotify Connect. The model topology Audiogravity copied into a box until 2026-07-10 declared a Spotify Connect service, and the installer copies the model only to a box that has no topology: a box set up before still showed it, though Audiogravity has never played Spotify. The installer takes it out — only that entry, as Audiogravity wrote it, and only when nothing else in the file names it —, the previous file kept beside it. Comments that still spoke of it as a source are corrected.
  • [core] The signal path's Controllers view lights a phone app only while it plays through the box. It lit JPlay whenever MPD played, JPlay being declared as commanding the box's UPnP renderer, which plays through MPD: seen on the x86 test box with the renderer stopped and the music started from Audiogravity. The app is now lit only when the renderer carries the track — running, and MPD playing what it was handed, never one of Audiogravity's own streaming addresses nor a file of the library. For a media server's track or a radio, which Audiogravity and an app may both play, the renderer is asked: it hands back an app's own description of the track, and marks the one it makes for a track another player queued (measured with upmpdcli 1.9.17, through UPnP AV and OpenHome alike, an app that sends no description included). One question per play, shared with the cover-art lookup, the diagram never waiting for it; the lookup now asks the box's own renderer even when HQPlayer Embedded's answers too, and no longer takes a cover from an answer about another track. One case stays out of reach: a track Audiogravity plays again at the very address an app played it at, which the renderer still describes as the app's.
  • [core][manual] Choosing another USB drive as the music library in Guided takes effect when it is applied. Guided rewrote the library's mount for the new drive and left the previous one mounted in the folder MPD reads, until that drive was pulled out or the box restarted (measured on the x86 test box, with two disk images on a throwaway mount). The previous drive is now unmounted first — MPD stopped for that moment when it is what holds the drive, and started again once the mount names the new one —, and again once the new mount is in place, should something have read the folder in between and mounted the previous drive anew. A drive still in use refuses the change, saying what to do: close what reads it — a terminal open in its folder, a renderer playing from it — then apply again. The library's own drive still mounted from before it was pulled out while in use is unmounted the same way. A full guided setup refused so leaves the output as it was: the one chosen is recorded once the library is in place. The manual's First run says it.
  • [core][ops] With the library's USB drive unplugged, opening its folder gives up after 5 seconds, not a minute and a half. Whatever opened the music library's folder while its drive was pulled out — MPD playing a track or reading a cover, a screen of the app, a terminal — waited systemd's 90 seconds for the drive before No such device (measured on the x86 and ARM test boxes). The library's drive is now waited for 5 seconds. A drive is seen about 3 seconds after it is plugged in: plugged in and opened at once, it is still waited for; a hard disk slower to spin up when the box starts can miss that time, and the next opening of the folder waits for it again. Guided sets the wait when it chooses the drive and takes it off the drive the library leaves; a box whose library drive was chosen before gets it at the core's next start, and uninstalling removes it.
  • [ui][manual] A track that cannot be reached or read no longer reads as an unavailable output. When MPD could not open a track — an internet radio off the air, a box offline, Audiogravity restarting while a streaming track was due, a damaged file —, the notification and the fullscreen player said Output unavailable, though the output was fine. They now say Track could not be reached — told by the words of the library MPD fetches with, the same on the x86 and the ARM box — or Track could not be read; an output that fails keeps its message. The manual's troubleshooting explains both.
  • [core][ui][manual] A track a streaming service cannot provide no longer reads as a failed play, nor puts the core's key on screen. On a HIGHRESAUDIO playlist whose first track the service gave no address for — answering, for a subscribed account, with no address at all, every time —, the app said Playback failed while MPD went on to play the next track, and its message quoted the box's own stream address with the core's API key. The addresses MPD is given carry no key any more — those paths are public, the key bought nothing —, and a key in what MPD says is masked wherever it is shown: a play's error, the player's output, the support report. Measured on a private MPD 0.24.5: playing or paused, MPD refuses the first track and plays the next; stopped, it accepts and moves on; with nothing it can play, it stops. The play's verdict is now read from where MPD went, woken by MPD's own changes of track (idle player, through one watch the library rescan's wait now shares): the play succeeds and says HIGHRESAUDIO could not provide “Jolene”: the next track plays. — POST /library/queue answers refused, refused_titles and a new refused_by; a play of which nothing could be had answers 503 in words, HIGHRESAUDIO could not provide any of the 16 tracks.; in random order only the refused track is named. From a stopped MPD, such a play said nothing at all: the error MPD stopped on now stays on the local output for two minutes, and the app shows its sentence where it said Output unavailable. Tidal's 404 for a track it does not make available is read the same way. The journal no longer blames the subscription (no address given, subscription active). The manual's troubleshooting says what the message means, and to check the service in Library → Sources when every track fails.
  • [ops] The interface's installer gives the app's install steps as they are today. Its last screen told an iPhone owner Share > Add to Home Screen — Share now sits behind ⋯, and without Open as Web App the icon only reopens Safari —, an Android owner to accept an Install app prompt Chrome no longer calls so, and left Android's certificate to menus that vary by brand. It now gives the manual's steps, shortened: remove an older icon, fetch the certificate on the box's name with its address as the way out, install it, then turn on the line named exactly as the iPhone lists it — the authority's name is read from the certificate, which keeps the hostname of the box's first install —; Android's steps; the iPad's; and a link to the manual's step 7 for every other device. A test holds the words the installer and the manual share. In the next release of the interface.
  • [site] EDITIONS.md no longer promises what the Systemd tab cannot give. It said the tab's priorities eliminate scheduling jitter for bit-perfect, glitch-free playback: priorities keep the processor for the audio services when the box is busy, and bit-perfect — the data reaching the DAC unchanged — does not depend on them. The VU meters' showcase is no longer said to be for a tablet only: it opens on a phone too.
  • [ui] A track removed from the queue leaves it at once, and the list no longer flashes. Removing a track — swiped, by its arrow, or with Clear — read the whole queue again behind Loading…: the list blanked, every cover was drawn again, and a reader who had scrolled down was sent back to the top of it (measured in Chromium at 390 px). The track now leaves under the finger before the box answers, the rows around it stay where they are, and the list is read again behind them; a refused removal puts the track back, with the box's reason. A track whose removal is on its way stays out of every reading, however close two removals or a Clear come. Refresh greys while its reading is out, the list no longer flashing to say so. Loading… now stands in only while there is nothing to show — on the queue, and on Outputs and Sources, which blanked the same way after a switch of output or a connection, Sources rebuilding every card below over about a second; the output asked for says SWITCHING… until the list is back.
  • [ui] The button beside the track playing in the queue plays and pauses, and the queue keeps up with the music. It showed a pause sign whatever the player did, and opened the full-screen player. It now plays and pauses the player that holds the queue — the box's own, stopped or paused, even while a phone plays over AirPlay, or the Roon zone shown —, shows what that player does, changes at the tap, and stays greyed until the player's state is known; a play refused for want of Pro says why. The full-screen player now opens from the track's cover, on that same player. It takes the finger over 44 px, drawn at 36. While the queue is on screen it follows the player: it is read again a moment after the track changes, and when the app comes back on screen, where it went on showing a track long over until Refresh. A queue changed from another device still needs Refresh.
  • [core][ui][manual] The queue's Up next lists what comes next, not what has played. It listed every track but the one playing, the tracks before it in the queue included — played or skipped, and not to play again: the queue's first track headed the list while a track far below it played. It now lists the tracks after the one playing; in random order, every other track, since any can come next; with repeat on, the tracks after it, then those from the top of the queue up to it, the order they will play in — measured on a private MPD 0.24.5: repeating, it plays the first track after the last; not repeating, it stops. The played tracks stay in the queue, and Clear still removes them. Turning random order or repeat on or off in the player updates the list, as a change of track does. GET /library/queue gains shuffle and repeat.
  • [site] The landing and the README no longer say Audiogravity tunes interrupts. Their five-layer stack listed IRQs in the kernel layer, which nothing in the app does: the layer now reads RT scheduling · CPU pinning · ALSA. On a box tuned that way today, interrupts are confined by hand, on the kernel's command line; doing it from the app is in the backlog.
  • [ui][manual] The mini-player's progress bar no longer moves the track. It took the finger on a band 14 px high along the very bottom of a phone's screen — the 4 px bar and an invisible margin —, where the phone's own gestures start: a touch there, or a swipe of the mini-player that began there, moved the track without being meant to (measured in Chromium at 375 px; on a computer, the band also covered the top of the page's footer). The bar now shows the position only, and moving in a track is the full-screen player's. A swipe that starts on it — down to fold the mini-player, up to open the full-screen player, sideways to change source — is a swipe. The manual says where to move in a track.
  • [ui] The album-details bubble no longer flickers when the cover is tapped. The bubble was drawn inside the mini-player's cover, which shrinks and fades under the finger like every button on a touch screen: the bubble shrank and faded with it — see-through for a moment, its top jumping 23 px — as it opened or closed (measured in Chromium at 375 px). It now hangs beside the cover, which alone takes the press. The cover is a real button, which the keyboard reaches too.
  • [core] The album tracklist names each track once, disc by disc. The bubble's tracklist of a streaming album is read from the box's queue: an album queued three times listed each of its 19 tracks three times. An album kept in the library in two editions, a FLAC and a DSD, could list its tracks twice, and an album on two discs read 1-1, 2-1, 1-2, 2-2 (measured on the x86 test box's library). Each track now comes once — the same disc, number and title, case and accents aside; a track with no disc number is on the first —, in disc order, then track order. A track number that names none, a vinyl side's A1, no longer empties the list. GET /audio_pipeline/album-tracks answers so; the library reads a track's number the same way.
  • [ui] A missing cover shows a music note, everywhere. A track or an album without artwork showed a disc, close to the drawing of an output, and a playlist a list, where the player and the listening history show a music note; a media server's album without artwork showed a folder. The music note now stands for every missing cover — queue, search, albums, playlists, a media server's lists —; a folder keeps its folder. On a media server's lists, a track's Play and + touched and read as one button: they are now spaced as on the library's other rows.
  • [ui] The full-screen player's Queue button looks like the Queue tab. It drew the Browse tab's three lines: the two buttons that open the queue had two drawings, one of them Browse's.
  • [core][ui][manual] An update leaves each service as it was: running on the new version, stopped, or disabled. Each package's own scripts did something else to its service (read in them, and measured on the x86 test box): MPD's stopped MPD and started nothing — after an update from Audio Software, the box had no player until Restart required was pressed —; HQPlayer NAA's left the previous version running; Shairport Sync's turned the service back on at boot and started it, even one stopped or disabled, as upmpdcli's and HQPlayer Embedded's start one that was only stopped. The box now reads how the service stands before the operation and puts it back once it is over: one that ran runs again on what was installed — started, or restarted when it still ran the previous version —, and one stopped or disabled stays so. What is asked of it from the Services page or by a profile while the package downloads wins. An update that changed no version touches nothing; one that could not put the service back says so. The update's confirmation says This restarts … if it is running, and REPAIR — an install over a package still there — now says it too.
  • [core][ui][manual] Restart required restarts the right service, and shows only when the service could not be restarted. After Shairport Sync was updated, the badge answered Service 'airplay.service' is not managed: the card asked to restart the service's key in audio-config.json, airplay, which names no service — nor do the keys of HQPlayer NAA and Roon Bridge. It now restarts the service itself, shairport-sync.service. The badge was also lit after every install and update of a package with a service, though some packages restart their service as they update it: Shairport Sync's had, seconds before the badge asked again (measured on the x86 test box), and a press only cut the music. It now shows only while a service still runs the version from before because it could not be restarted, and the box asks systemd again each time it describes the package: the badge goes out once the service has restarted, whatever restarted it, and every device shows the same, where it lived in the browser that ran the update. Pressed on a second device, it restarts nothing the first already did. The API's packages gain service_unit and restart_needed, and lose restarts_after_install; the manual describes the badge.
  • [core] The services audio-config.json declares are read in one place, and each one's unit found one way. The Services page, the audio configuration and the profiles each read them and found a service's unit by a rule of their own: the audio configuration answered roon.service for Roon Bridge, whose unit is roonbridge.service. A rewrite of the file within the same few milliseconds was not read — its time alone could not tell it from the previous version —, and an entry naming its unit as something other than a name made every action on the services fail. The file is read once now, its versions told apart by more than their time, and an entry that names no unit is left out.
  • [ops][site] The ARM packages of UPnP Bridge come with their source code, and the third-party notices give their real licenses. Audiogravity builds upmpdcli and its two libraries for ARM and serves the packages, which makes it their distributor: upmpdcli and libupnpp are under the LGPL, version 2.1 or later, with parts under the GPL, and their source has to be offered with the packages — it was not. The archives they are built from, exactly as their author publishes them, and the script that builds them are now served beside the packages, in packages/upmpdcli-arm64/source/, and build-upmpdcli-arm64.sh --publish replaces them with each rebuild. THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md listed upmpdcli as GPL-2.0, said that every package came from its vendor, and named neither library; it now says which part is under which license, and reproduces the BSD notice of libnpupnp.
  • [site] The third-party notices name HQPlayer Embedded among the software the box can install. Their table listed six of the seven packages the Audio Software tab installs; HQPlayer Embedded, proprietary and fetched from Signalyst like the NAA, was missing.
  • [ui] Tidal's sign-in page could send the app's tab elsewhere. Opened without cutting its opener, the page kept a handle on the app's tab: a page from another origin, opened the same way, redirected it (measured in Chromium). The handle is now cut as the page opens — not with the noopener feature, under which a blocked page could no longer be told from an opened one.
  • [ui] Edit station opens its form in Favorites, and in sight. Every card of My Live Radio and Favorites offers Edit station, but the form was drawn by My Live Radio alone: in Favorites the tap showed nothing. And above a long list it opened out of sight — 1,348px above the screen for the 30th station (measured in Chromium) —, which read as nothing too. The form now opens above the list it was asked from, and the page goes back to its top to show it; it closes when the tab changes or its station leaves the list, and a save that ends after the reader moved on no longer closes the next form.

[1.0.0] - 2026-11-06

Added

  • [site] A blog post on the Rega Planar 3. How Rega's turntable of 1976 became the deck others at its price are measured against, and how it sounds by Stereophile's reviews of 1984, 1985, 1996, 2008 and 2017 and What Hi-Fi?'s of 2021 and 2024: its rhythm, its tone, its bass, what it gives up against far dearer decks, and the cartridge, on which Michael Fremer and John Atkinson disagree. Its five generations, from the Planar 3 to the P3, P3-24, RP3 and the Planar 3 of 2016, and the RS Edition of 2024; its rivals, the Linn LP12 first; a green P3 of about 2005 with an Elys 2, playing through a NAIT 2 into a pair of Rogers LS3/5A; setting it up from Rega's own guide. Rega's photograph heads it.
  • [site] Hi-Fi World's "NAITology" of October 2007, kept on the site as a source of the NAIT 2 post: the scan of its three pages, linked from the post. robots.txt asks search engines not to read it — a PDF cannot carry the noindex tag the archived web page does, and GitHub Pages sends no header —, and two tests keep every archived scan out of the crawl and every archived page readable, its noindex included.

Changed

  • [site] The blog's NAIT 2, LS3/5A and Quad 33/303 posts start from how they sound, and why they became references. Each opens on its standing, then gives most of its room to its sound as the specialist press heard it across the decades — where reviewers agree, where they disagree, and in what conditions they listened —, then its rivals and successors and what to pair it with; its history and figures come after, shorter. Read in full for them: Stereophile's reviews by J. Gordon Holt (1977), Robert Harley (1989), Sam Tellig (1990), John Atkinson (1989, 2007), Art Dudley (2016, 2019, 2020) and Herb Reichert (2021), The Absolute Sound's by Paul Seydor (2016), Robert Harley (2018) and Chris Kelly (2025), Hi-Fi World's of 2007 and Hi-Fi News' of 2025. Gone: the sections on streamers and on Audiogravity where the equipment has nothing to do with them, a comparison of a cartridge's output with a DAC's, and estimates the posts had made themselves, such as a listening level worked out from the BBC's figure. The titles follow: The Naim NAIT 2: the small amplifier that became a reference, The BBC LS3/5A: the little monitor that became a legend, The Quad 33 and 303: Peter Walker's transistor classic, and its return. The Quad post shows an original 33 and 303, Quad's photograph, and, at its head and on its card, a 33 of 2024.
  • [site][manual] The landing and the manual no longer call it a release candidate. The landing's corner ribbon and the « (Release candidate) » of its footer are gone, and so is the FAQ's « What does “Release candidate” mean? », in the manual and on the landing. The announce bar, the light / dark toggle and the GitHub button take back the room they kept for the ribbon; the announce bar still holds one line, cut at its own edge below 1100px. The release notes open on what Audiogravity 1.0 does, area by area, before what changed since 1.0.0-rc.4.

Fixed

  • [core][manual] Stats counts AirPlay and the radio in the right sound quality. Two causes. A station started straight after another track could count as Unknown for its whole listen: the box read it a split second before the player had decoded it, so without a format, and the format that came 0.5 to 2.3 s later (measured on a private MPD 0.24.5) went out only when something else changed or while a screen was open; France Inter, which names no song, kept none — both of its listens counted Unknown on the lab box had started straight after another track. A source's format now goes out when it arrives, its bitrate left aside: that moves at every read, and would send a state every second while a screen is open. A file's codec read from its header once it plays (an .m4a) now comes in a further state too, as the API said it would. And AirPlay counted as Hi-Res: the player showed ALAC written in and the 32 bits shairport-sync opens the sound card at, whatever it receives, where the boxes run classic AirPlay — DietPi's shairport-sync 5.6.2 without NQPTP, Debian's 4.3.7 built without AirPlay 2 —, which only sends ALAC at 16 bit / 44.1 kHz (during a session, shairport-sync received ALAC/44100/S16_LE/2 and opened the card at S32_LE). AirPlay is now read from what shairport-sync says it receives — its SourceFormat, else a classic service's only format — and never from the card. On the x86 box, the old core and the new side by side with no screen open: the same switch to France Inter counted Unknown against lossy (AAC, 220 kbps, 48 kHz), the same AirPlay track Unknown against CD quality. Listens already recorded keep their category. The manual says what Unknown means under Sound quality.
  • [core] A change seen as a screen opens now reaches Stats and the screens already open. The state a screen receives when it opens became the box's record of what had been announced, without being announced: a change it saw first — AirPlay stopped without a word, its service switched off during a session — reached neither the listening history nor the other screens until something else changed, and an AirPlay listen stayed open on the lab box. That state is now only read for the screen that asked; the box's watch, which a screen opening wakes, announces the change as it does any other.

[1.0.0-rc.4] - 2026-11-02

Added

  • [core][ui][site] A level meter: two needles that follow what the box's player plays, in step with the DAC. In the full-screen player, a Cover | VU meter switch below the cover puts two dials in its place, left above right — the choice kept on each device —, and a Showcase mode button opens them over the whole screen, side by side in landscape, with what plays below: the screen stays on and the portrait lock is lifted while it is open. On a phone held sideways the showcase fits the screen — it overflowed 2 to 49 px, measured in Chromium on phone sizes —: the dials take the height left and Exit moves to the end of the track's line. The dials are drawn in one ink, the theme's text colour, in light and in dark alike: the scale's red zone is a heavier band of it, its figures bold, and the peak lamp a ring that fills. The needles read the RMS level of each 40 ms of the sound, 0 VU at −10 dBFS, and swing as a VU meter's do, 99 % of the way in 300 ms; a lamp lights above +3 VU. The levels come from a copy of MPD's stream: Audiogravity gives MPD a fifo output, audiogravity-meter, off, after the sound cards — the guided setup writes it, and the installer adds it to an MPD configuration Audiogravity wrote whose socket is in /run/mpd, then restarts MPD; a configuration without Audiogravity's mark is left alone. The copy is on only while a screen shows the meter and MPD plays on the DAC: MPD reports a failed output only when all of them fail, so a copy left on would have kept it playing, silent and without an error, to a DAC that could not be opened — measured: with the copy on MPD said it played, with it off it paused on Failed to open ALSA device, which Audiogravity shows. A DAC another player holds — Roon, AirPlay, any program — does not count: the process holding the card, which the kernel names, must be MPD's. With the copy on for such a DAC, Play had MPD say it played, silent, where it says Device or resource busy — measured with the DAC held by another program, before and after. On, it cost MPD 0.4 % of its core at 44.1 kHz and 4.2 % at 384 kHz on the lab's x86 box, and the DAC's buffer stayed as full as without it. The copy runs ahead of the sound — 3.4 ms a minute at 44.1 kHz, 11 in DSD128, 42 at 384 kHz, measured on the lab's DAC —, so each window waits until the card has played it, read from the card's position in the kernel: the needles move with what is heard, through a seek, a pause or a new track. Only a stride of the samples is read: over a whole track, its levels were within 0.12 dB of every sample's on nine windows in ten, the median 0.00 dB. DSD is read from its density of ones. On a box where MPD turns DSD into PCM before its volume (audio_output_format "*:32:*"), the copy is read in the format MPD's outputs receive, which MPD's status does not name — it names the file's. The copy is left out of every list of MPD's outputs: the output switch, DoP and the DSD readings see the sound cards only. A copy left on — a core stopped while a screen showed the meter, MPD restarting when the last one closed — is turned off as soon as MPD answers. Paused, the meter does nothing until MPD plays again. With the copy on, MPD wrote Decoder is too slow; playing silence to avoid xrun at each start of a 384 kHz or DSD256 track (8 out of 8, none out of 2 without it), at the very start only; whether it can be heard was not checked. For what the box's own MPD plays — its music, the radio, the streaming services —: for AirPlay, Roon, HQPlayer or a network speaker the switch is not offered, an open showcase says why in the dials' place, and nothing is turned on. Pro: on a box without it, the switch shows a padlock that opens the licence panel. New: GET /player/levels (server-sent events, Pro), PlayerState.level_meter. The manual's Listening chapter describes it under The level meter, with the player and the showcase on a phone held sideways, and the landing lists it among the Pro features.
  • [site][manual] Search engines and link previews know what each guide, post and chapter is. A page pasted into a forum or a message shows a preview: a post's photograph, wide; the site's logo for a guide, a chapter or a list — not the landing's own preview, whose price and Install now would make a guide read as an advertisement. Each page describes itself to search engines in schema.org terms: an article — a post with the day it was published —, the latest day its Checked on line names, and its path from home, Audiogravity › Guides › …, which a result can show in place of the address; the lists of guides, posts and chapters describe themselves as collections. A chapter's snippet in search results takes the Contents' words for it — Listening — sources, transport, the Now Playing screen — where it gave its name alone. The first image of every page loads at once and the others as they near the screen: a post's photograph, the largest thing on screen when it opens and which search engines time, was fetched after the layout.
  • [site] Finding your way in the guides, the blog and the manual. The top bar links the guides and the blog to each other, the section being read underlined; the manual's names the manual alone, and a guide that has something to say about Audiogravity links to the chapter that says it, in its text. Below 360 px wide, the bar keeps the logo without the name. A page's parts are listed under its own entry in the sidebar, the part being read marked as the page scrolls and kept in view — a chapter runs to 33 parts, a post to 13 screens on a phone —; on a phone they are in an On this page menu under the title. Each page ends on the page before it and the page after it in its list — on the blog, the newer and the older post —, and on a link back to the list. The guides are filed under four themes, in their list and in the sidebar, each title on a line of its own with what it answers under it; the "Guide N" under their titles is gone. The blog's front page shows its posts as cards, each with its photograph — which grows a little under the pointer, and stays still for whoever asked for less motion —, its date written out and its reading time. Each guide's short answer sits in a box at the top, and the line saying what a guide or a post was checked or measured against, and when, moves under its title, after a check mark.
  • [site][manual] A community for owners, on GitHub Discussions. The landing's footer links it as Community, beside Issues; the roadmap's invitation to say which direction matters most now leads to the community's Ideas, instead of the issues; the manual's Getting help sends questions, ideas and setups there, and keeps bug reports for the issues. A pinned welcome says where each kind of post goes.
  • [site] A blog post comparing HIGHRESAUDIO, Qobuz and Tidal. Who runs each — a Berlin company led by its founder; Xandrie, a mostly French family-owned group, since 2015; TIDAL Music AS of Oslo, part of the American group Block since 2021 —, the size each claims for its catalogue, the countries it serves, what it streams and sells — FLAC up to 352.8 kHz in HIGHRESAUDIO's catalogue, read through its API, and DSD and DXD to buy; FLAC up to 24-bit / 192 kHz at Qobuz, which never streams DSD, an album bought in DSD streaming as PCM in the lab; the same on Tidal with Dolby Atmos, MQA dropped in July 2024, and music sold only by US artists who upload their own —, its prices on 2026-11-02, how you listen — Qobuz Connect, TIDAL Connect, one stream at a time per account —, what artists get — Qobuz alone publishes an average, $0.01873 per stream —, what each does about music made by AI, and which suits what. A short last section says what Audiogravity does with each: the quality it asks for, purchases, favourites, playlists. Everything is dated and sourced; Tidal's own pricing page could not be read, so its prices are its help centre's, in US dollars. Its photograph, by Vu Nguyen, is from Unsplash.

Changed

  • [ui] The full-screen player shows everything at once on a phone held sideways. It kept its upright layout there, one column to scroll through, 380 to 430 px of it out of sight on the phones measured. From 560 px wide it now takes the tablet's two columns: the cover — or the level meter — on the left, as large as the height leaves; the title, the format, the controls, the progress and the output on the right. The page dots move up under the header's title, the album joins the artist's line, the artist taking at most 60 % of it, and a long title keeps to two lines. Next shares a line with the output where the screen is wide enough; narrower — an iPhone SE or mini, a small Android phone — it moves under the cover, on one line. The page runs under the notch of a phone held sideways: the header and the columns keep clear of it. Measured in Chromium on phones on their side from 560 × 320 to 932 × 430 — the cover or the meter, with Pro or without, Next or not, short or very long titles —: nothing overflows. Under 560 px wide, a phone sharing its screen with another app, the single column stays, and scrolls.
  • [site] The blog post on the Quad 33 and 303 shows the inside of the new 33, and takes the new pair's size and power from Quad's manuals. Quad's picture of the 2024 33 with its cover off, its toroidal transformer in view, and links to Quad's user manuals for the 2024 33 and 303. By those manuals, the new 33 is within 2 mm of the original in every dimension and the new 303 is 1.7 cm taller — the post said, after Hi-Fi News, 15 mm deeper and 11 mm taller —, and the 303 gives 49.5 W into 8 ohms, not 50, and 170 W bridged into 4 ohms.
  • [site] The blog post on the Naim NAIT 2 shows the NAIT 50. What Hi-Fi?'s photograph of it at the High End show in Munich, in May 2023, at the head of its section, credited to What Hi-Fi? and linked to its news of the launch.

Fixed

  • [ui] The progress bar starts the next track at 0:00, and follows a track that goes back. After Next, the full-screen player's bar carried on from where the previous track had stopped and stayed ahead of the music until the player was closed and reopened — with the sources that say 0 as a track starts: Roon, AirPlay, a network speaker. Neither bar, the full player's nor the mini-player's, ever moved back on its own either: a track restarted, moved back from another device or app, or followed by another of the same name left both up to 2:36 ahead (measured on MPD, 2026-11-02), and a track played again with Previous resumed where it had been left. Both now follow the source back as soon as its position drops — within half a second on MPD; a title blank for a moment no longer counts as a new track, and on a source that gives no position (AirPlay through shairport-sync 4.3.7, Debian's) a new length under the same title does.
  • [core][manual] AirPlay no longer offers a seek it cannot make. The bar of an AirPlay track could be dragged, and the box refused every move: shairport-sync declares it cannot seek (CanSeek false, in 4.3.7 and 5.6.2); where it gives no position, the bar then fell back to 0:00. An MPRIS player's can_seek now follows what it declares — false for AirPlay, unchanged for a player that says nothing. The manual's AirPlay section says to move within a track from the iPhone, iPad or Mac sending it.
  • [core] Each AirPlay track shows its own cover. On a change of track, shairport-sync names the new album about 0.3 s before its picture; the previous picture, read in between, was kept as the new album's cover for a day and shown in place of the right one — 3 tracks out of 5 on 2026-11-02, and the same album again when replayed with Previous. A picture AirPlay gives is now read as it is, never stood in for by its album's; one already gone falls back on MusicBrainz and iTunes for its album, as before, without hiding the file when it comes back. A cover a browser already received wrong stays in its cache for up to a day.
  • [site] dev.sh serves a page asked for without its .html, as GitHub Pages does. The generated manual, guides and blog link to each other that way, and every such link answered 404 on the local server: the sidebar and the links between pages could not be tried before publishing. /page is served from page.html when only the latter exists, its query string kept.
  • [manual][site] The streaming services' section no longer says that Tidal sells nothing, nor that HIGHRESAUDIO streams up to 352.8 kHz. Tidal sells music since 2026, from US artists who upload their own; the manual now says only that Tidal, unlike Qobuz and HIGHRESAUDIO, has no Purchases shelf. HIGHRESAUDIO's catalogue lists FLAC up to 352.8 kHz, but a stream at that rate was never tried: the manual and the site's README now say what Audiogravity does — it plays each album's FLAC at its own resolution, as HIGHRESAUDIO sends it.

[1.0.0-rc.3] - 2026-10-31

Added

  • [site] A blog, beside the manual and the guides. Dated articles, the newest first, under a Blog tab after Guides in the landing's bar, on the manual's pages: each says when it was published and how long it takes to read, and the sitemap lists them. The first two are on Naim. The Naim NAIT 2: its inputs and power from Naim's manual and Stereophile's bench, what three reviewers heard, buying and servicing one, and every NAIT from 1983 to the NAIT 50 of 2023 on a timeline. How old is your Naim?: the year of each serial number from 1982 to 2022, from the table Naim's website gave until early 2024 — it now asks owners to write to its service department —, with its gaps and overlaps explained, and Naim's beginnings with its two founders. A NAIT 2 servicing guide whose site has closed, hi-fi AF's, is kept on the site as it was, credited to its author, with an address to ask for its removal; its 39 photos are shown in WebP at most 1640 px wide — 5.8 MB to read it all, where its original photos weighed 21.7 MB —, and the five it offered full size keep their original behind their link.
  • [site] Three more blog posts. The Heed Abacus: Heed's DAC of 2017, its inputs and formats from Heed's manual and from Linux, its streaming card and the Abacus S, and why its DSD stops when its USB volume drops below 0 dB — measured in the lab: DoP falls silent, native DSD turns to noise. The BBC LS3/5A: the BBC's monitor for outside-broadcast vans from its report of 1976, the 11-ohm revision of 1987 from Stereophile's measurements, and what a Rogers pair asks of a NAIT 2 and a streamer. The Quad 33 and 303: Quad's first transistor pre- and power amplifier, of 1967, from Quad's own manuals and circuit diagram — a DAC goes on the 33's Tape Replay input set to its highest level, not on Radio, which takes 2 V at most, a DAC's level at full scale —, Hi-Fi News' measurements of the originals, and what the versions of 2024 changed; the DIN guide points to it. Each photograph is credited to its source. The NAIT 2 post links to the LS3/5A one, and its tables now fit the column.
  • [site] A new guide: A streamer into an amplifier with DIN inputs — why a 5-pin DIN cable that plays on one amplifier may give one channel on another, and why a socket may carry power; how far a DAC's 2.1 V outruns a line input rated at 75 mV; where to set the volume — on the amplifier, on a PCM512x DAC's −6 dB analogue step, or in the player —; and how to check both channels with speaker-test. Like the others, it says what it was checked with and when, and lists its sources.
  • [ui][manual] The installed app's icon counts the notifications that arrived while it was out of sight. On an iPhone or iPad (iOS 16.4 or later), the icon of the app added to the Home Screen shows how many of the box's notifications arrived since the app was last opened on that device; opening the app or coming back to it clears it, the sign-in page included. A notification that arrives while the app is on screen is not counted; each device keeps its own count, in its service worker's IndexedDB; turning Badges off in the device's notification settings hides it. On an iPhone, a worker woken by a push sometimes failed its first opening of that store (UnknownError: Unable to open database file on disk), and three notifications arriving together showed 2: each change of the count is now tried twice, one change at a time — measured on an iPhone, three notifications at once on a worker asleep, the first opening failing, the second succeeding: 3. Chrome and Edge offer the same to an app installed on a computer (the API answers in Chromium 145); the number on a computer's icon was not tried. The manual says what the number means.

Changed

  • [manual] The certificate steps on an iPhone or iPad say where each switch is. The trust switch sits at the very bottom of About, iOS asks to confirm it with Continue, and a profile no longer offered at the top of Settings is found under General → VPN & Device Management — or downloaded again.
  • [core] Covers come from MPD in large pieces, at a fraction of the processor. MPD sends a picture in pieces of 8 KiB unless told otherwise, and opens the file again for every piece — for a cover inside an M4A, ffmpeg reading the file each time: on the lab's x86 box a cover of 844 KB took 104 requests and 510 ms, 476 of them MPD's own processor time. The core now asks MPD for pieces of 1 MiB (binarylimit): one request, 12 ms, 7 ms of processor; a cover of 44 KB went from 34 to 5.5 ms. Each piece is acknowledged at once: in pieces that large, a cover MPD sends in several pieces otherwise waited about 40 ms for Linux's delayed acknowledgement — a cover.jpg of 330 KB, sent in two pieces, took 43 ms, 1 ms with it (MPD 0.24.5). An MPD that refuses the command — older than 0.22.4, or with its max_output_buffer_size set under 1 MiB by hand, where its default is 8 MiB — keeps its 8 KiB pieces, and the picture comes whole as before. MPD 0.24.4 on ARM accepts it.
  • [ui] The Systemd editor's preset hint reads like the form's other hints, and a profile name takes the accent on hover. The hint under the presets is in italics, as the form's other help texts are. A profile name, which dimmed to grey on hover, takes the theme's accent — the colour its rule named from the start, under a name no theme declares.

Fixed

  • [site] The landing's bar keeps every label on one line, and its Releases button clear of the ribbon. Between 951 and 970 px wide and between 1161 and 1174 px, Signal path and Audio priority broke onto a second line; from 1161 to 1300 px — a 1280 px laptop among them — a corner of the Releases button sat under the Release candidate ribbon (measured in Chromium at every width from 320 to 1500 px). The button now goes below 1240 px, its link staying in the footer, and stands further in up to 1300 px; the links take a row of their own below 1030 px, where they did below 950. Measured again at every width: no label on two lines, the button 2.5 px clear of the ribbon at the worst, the theme button 4.3 px.
  • [core][ui][manual] The library's Refresh reads the music folder again, so music copied into it appears. Both Refresh controls of the local library — at the right end of the Library's bar and beside the Albums title — only rebuilt the list from what the player had already indexed: an album copied onto the NAS never showed, whatever was pressed. A Refresh now has MPD read the music folder first, then reloads the list: the icon turns and the title's link reads Refreshing… meanwhile, the albums already listed staying on screen — 9 to 26 s on the lab's 6,474 tracks on a NAS, music playing in DSD64 without a dropout. A Refresh always ends with a scan that began after it was pressed; presses that come together share one, and no more than one waits behind a scan already running. When the music folder reads as empty or does not answer while the library holds music — a NAS that is off, a share not mounted —, no scan is asked for: it would empty the library and give every album a new date in Recent (measured); the library is left as it is and a message says so. Moving to another source during a Refresh frees it at once. The API gains POST and GET /library/rescan; refresh=true on /library/albums keeps rebuilding the list from the player's database only. The manual says what to do when music is added, and what the message means.
  • [core][ui][manual][site] MPD's sound is no longer ranked below the rest of MPD. MPD makes its own sound threads real time, at priority 40; the Systemd tuning page's Audio Optimized preset put the whole service at SCHED_FIFO 80, so everything else MPD does — reading cover art, scanning the library — ran ahead of them. Measured on the lab's x86 box, MPD set above its sound by hand (RR 45) and its output buffer shortened by hand to 50 ms: while MPD read 40 album covers, five at a time as the core asks for them, during DSD64 playback, the sound threads went 47 ms without the processor and the DAC dropped out 16 times in 10.5 s; with them above the rest, none. The preset's FIFO 80 ranks them the same way, higher, and MPD's default buffer, 500 ms, which Audiogravity leaves as it is, would ride out such waits: neither was measured. On MPD the preset now leaves the policy and the priority at Default. A real-time priority of 40 or more on MPD is warned about by the core when it is saved, and by the editor as soon as it is typed, or on opening an MPD already set that way. The RT process monitor read MPD's main thread — NON-RT on an MPD as installed, whose sound is real time, which sent readers to the very setting that caused the dropouts —: it reads MPD's sound threads now, the weakest of them, and shows OUTRANKED when another part of MPD runs ahead of its sound. It also took a policy carrying reset on fork, as MPD sets it, for none. The manual, the real-time guide and the landing no longer give MPD priority 80. If Audio Optimized was applied to MPD: in Systemd tuning, open MPD, click Audio Optimized again, then Save Changes.
  • [ui] Systemd tuning's editor opens on the service's own settings. A value typed and then cancelled stayed in the form at the next opening — of the same service, or of another with the same setting — and Save Changes would then write it. Each opening now starts from the service's settings.
  • [ui] Four styles that named a token no theme declares take effect. The devices listed under Face ID / Touch ID in the configuration panel, with their remove button, the passkey list of a user's card — its key icon and No passkeys registered yet. — and the hint under the Systemd editor's presets took the colour of the text around them: they read in the themes' muted grey. The sound cards of the System page had square corners: they take the theme's small rounding, like the network panels beside them. The network test type chosen kept its label's colour: it is written in the accent.
  • [ui] The install banner no longer covers the mini-player. On a computer, the Add Audiogravity to your home screen banner sat 64 px from the bottom whatever the player bar's height, and hid the bar's title and controls (measured in Chromium at 1440 × 900); on a phone it bit 3 px into the bar. It read a height the player never published. It now sits above the bar — or its pull tab once folded — and the footer.

[1.0.0-rc.2] - 2026-10-27

Added

  • [site] dev.sh takes start, stop, restart and status. The landing's local server only ran in the foreground, whatever it was asked: ./dev.sh restart started a second server on the port the first one held, and stopped on Python's "Address already in use". start serves in the background, its output in dev.log; stop finds the server by its port, however it was started, and ends this directory's only; ./dev.sh alone serves in the foreground as before, and says so when a server already runs. Called by a relative path from another directory, it started and served nothing; it serves the site now.
  • [site] Halloween — ghosts, and the strip's Danse macabre to hear. On 31 October, ghosts in the page's ink rise through the margins beside each section's text, below its spider, and from behind the pumpkins on the footer's edge and at the foot of the phone — none on a phone, whose margins are too narrow, nor for a visitor whose system asks for less motion. A round button in the strip that shows a track playing plays a 30-second excerpt of the recording, fetched at the first press only; while it plays, the strip shows what is heard — AAC, 44.1 kHz and the excerpt's own time — and the box's again at the pause. A screen reader reaches and names the button, and putting it in moves nothing on the page.
  • [site] Halloween — an orange ghost to catch, ten lifetime licences to win. On 31 October 2026, Paris time, the licence server wakes ten lifetime licences one at a time, each at a secret moment in its tenth of the day. The first visitor to open the page after one wakes is shown an orange ghost in the band above one of the sections, and has twenty minutes to catch it; the page then shows the licence key, keeps it on the device and says how to activate it. The announce bar, in turn with the release's sentence, says how many licences are left, then that all were caught; the rules are on orange-ghost.html. With the licence server out of reach, the page stays as on any day; the page decides nothing, and can neither make a ghost appear nor know a key before it is given.
  • [site] The orange ghost, announced before the day. Until 30 October the announce bar at the top of the landing gives its place every six seconds to the news of the 31st — an orange ghost, ten lifetime licences to win — which leads to the rules; then the release's sentence comes back, its words untouched. A phone reads the news in a shorter sentence. The bar holds still while the pointer is over it or the keyboard is in it. On the 31st the game's own news takes that turn; from 1 November the release's sentence stays alone.
  • [site] Two new guides: “Undervoltage detected!” on a Raspberry Pi — what the kernel's message means, how to tell whether a Pi's supply has fallen short (vcgencmd get_throttled, the kernel's log), what it does — measured on a Pi 4 over six days: 68 drops a day, the processor at 600 MHz while Linux still showed 1.8 GHz, playback keeping up — and which supply and cable each model needs; Lossless internet radio — which stations stream FLAC and how to check what a station sends with ffprobe: of 333 streams read, 88 sent FLAC, 82 of them listed as Ogg by the Radio Browser directory, whose bitrates are what the stations announce; with a list of FLAC stations, how MPD plays HLS, and what its format readout says of a lossy stream. Like the others, each says what it was checked with and when, and lists its sources, with a short Audiogravity section at the end.
  • [lic] The orange ghost's two routes, POST /ls/portal/ghost-draw and POST /ls/portal/ghost-claim, answered to the landing's origin only. They hand out the pending lifetime keys the admin created under orange-ghost@audiogravity.app and never create one; one chance per address per hour, one key per address, and a ticket that gave its key gives it again, so an answer lost on its way costs the winner nothing. Visitors' addresses are held in memory, for the day only. GHOST_DAY (2026-10-31) and GHOST_ORIGINS need no change to a deployed .env.
  • [ops] nginx — the orange ghost's routes are public, with their own request budget: 30 a minute per address and a burst of 10, turned away before they reach the licence server's single worker.
  • [ui] Halloween in the app, every 31 October. From midnight to midnight by the device's own clock — or any day with ?halloween in the address — the app dresses up: two spiders let themselves down on their threads in the screen's side gutters, under the bar and the tabs, and climb back; now and then a bat flits across the empty band of the title bar, right of the name (computer only); a ghost floats above the message of an empty page — no history, an empty queue, no result, no albums, an empty folder, no stations, nothing recorded in Stats — with Nothing here but ghosts under it, kept from screen readers; the fullscreen player's cover glows orange, like a lantern. The installed app's opening wears it too: a spider comes down above the icon as the wave, a pumpkin's orange that day, goes out, and climbs back as the screen fades; a bat crosses behind the name; the line reads Your audio chain, frightfully controlled. The sign-in page hangs the same spiders, behind its card, and a bat comes out from behind the name. Nothing covers a control or takes a touch, nothing makes a sound, and nothing moves on a device that asks for less motion, nor with the app's Animations setting off — the opening aside, as always; an app left open across midnight puts the dress on and takes it off by itself. Its cost is the browser's alone, the box only serving the files: in a browser without a graphics chip, a page left alone went from 11 % to 24 % of one processor core that day (Chromium, measured on 27 October 2026).
  • [ui] The radio's Halloween stations. On 31 October, a 🎃 Halloween button above the radio's search lists the stations the catalogue tags halloween — 13 on 27 October 2026, all MP3 or AAC — and clears the query, the country and Lossless only, any of which would leave few or none; the genre list offers Halloween that day, and keeps it while it is still chosen after midnight.

Changed

  • [site] The undervoltage guide names the supply of the Pi it was measured on: a third-party USB-C supply rated 5 V and 3 A, where Raspberry Pi asks for 5.1 V, with nothing plugged into its USB ports.
  • [ui][manual] In Minimal, the Stats view's sound quality and listening hours take the latency histogram's colours. The latency test's histogram draws its bands in grey, ink, amber and red; the Stats view drew every chart in ink and greys. In the Minimal theme, the sound-quality bar and its legend now show DSD in the histogram's grey, Hi-Res in ink, CD in amber and MP3/AAC in red, and the week's hours run from grey, the quietest, to red, the busiest — each drawn the histogram's way, the colour slightly see-through inside and full at the edge. Slate and Gravity are unchanged (compared pixel for pixel in Chromium, light and dark). The manual describes the hours by their legend, Less to More, where it said the darker the square, the more played, and its Stats figure is retaken.

Fixed

  • [site] The grid of the black panel beside Install in one gesture shows. Its lines were white at 4%: a lightness gap of 2.7 from their black ground, where the hero's grid shows 9.1 — next to nothing on screen. They now stand as far from their ground as the hero's do, in the light theme as in the dark one (9.3 and 5.9 against 9.1 and 5.7, measured in Chromium).
  • [ui][manual] The app's messages can be closed, and stay until they are read. A message — the toast that says how an action went — has an × now. An error or a warning stays until it is closed, where it left after four to eight seconds, whether or not its words had been read; a success or an information still leaves after a few seconds, but not while the pointer, a finger or the keyboard's focus rests on it. Three show at most, the one shown or said again longest ago giving way, and a message already on screen is not shown a second time. An action that takes a while — starting playback, saving the pipeline's topology, applying a service's systemd settings, checking or updating the audio software — no longer stacks its result under its "in progress" message: that message turns into the result, and two plays started together each keep their own. A screen reader is told each message once, with its type, two that come together included. The messages are a card on the page's ground, their type in a coloured left edge and icon; in Minimal the information is in black, where it was blue. The manual describes them.
  • [ui] The messages no longer cover the app's controls.
