In the app installed on an iPhone, a message lay over the top bar, and its menu, Library and settings buttons did not answer while it showed (measured in Chromium with an emulated notch); on a computer three messages hid the Library and Manual tabs, every tab in a narrow window, and on a phone the offline banner's RETRY. They now start below the top bar, the tab bar and the offline banner. On a phone they are centred, where they sat 6px from the left edge and 18px from the right, and their × answers a finger over 44px, still drawn at 28.
  • [ui] A favourite that cannot be saved says so, and why. The message was a blue box headed "ERROR", with nothing else: its call passed the words where the message's type goes. It reads Favorite not added — or not removed — with the reason. Every call that shows a message is now checked for that order.
  • [ui] The messages' words say what happened. About seventy are reworded: a title that gives the outcome — Topology saved, Settings not applied, Passkey not added — and a sentence that says what follows from it, where many repeated their title ("Configuration Saved — Configuration updated successfully") or showed an error as the core wrote it. A cancelled passkey and an empty log to copy are information, no longer warnings. The Update All confirmation says 1 package where it said 1 packages.
  • [core][ui][manual][site] The radio search keeps the stations that really stream lossless, and its box says so: Lossless only. Its Hi-Res only kept a station listed as FLAC or WAV, or announced at 256 kbps or more: an MP3 at 320 kbps passed, while the FLAC stations the Radio Browser catalogue lists as OGG — the wrapper they are sent in, which also carries Vorbis and Opus — passed only on the bitrate they announce. Ticked, Lossless only keeps the stations listed as FLAC or WAV, and reads the first 4 KB of each station listed as OGG, UNKNOWN or with no codec, to keep those that send FLAC; on the lab's box a search for "flac" took under ten seconds the first time, and was answered at once the next. A station read that way shows FLAC and its resolution — 24bit / 48kHz — in the search, My Live Radio and Favorites, and while it plays the player and the signal path show FLAC and its bit depth where they showed OGG, as lossy. The box reads four streams at a time at most, follows a station's redirections checking each address, never reads an address on the local network for a station taken from the catalogue — a station added by hand may be on it —, and tries a station that failed again a day later only. The API's lossless_only replaces hi_res_only, kept as a deprecated alias; a station gains verified_codec, verified_bits and verified_rate_hz. The manual, the editions page and the landing's radio card say so.
  • [ui][core][manual] A Raspberry Pi short of power shows its processor Slowed down, not at a speed it is not running at. While a Pi's supply falls short, its processor runs slower than the speed Linux gives — 600 MHz on a Pi 4 that Linux still showed at 1.8 GHz, measured on the lab's Pi —, and the Performance tab showed Linux's figure. While the THROTTLED badge shows on a Pi, the core's frequency and Current Freq above the cards now read Slowed down, and the support report says slowed down (undervoltage) in place of a figure — its audio_live gains cpu_slowed. An Intel processor's figure is the speed it measured, and stays, even while it is slowed for heat.
  • [ui][manual] The network test's verdict reads in every theme. EXCELLENT, GOOD, FAIR or CRITICAL was a solid block of colour with the page's ground as its words: under the 4.5:1 floor in 11 of the 24 combinations of theme, mode and grade, down to 1.94:1, and in Minimal GOOD was a black block that looked like a button. It is drawn as the app's other state badges are — a tint, and the word in its colour —, at 4.5:1 or more everywhere. The manual's figures of the network and latency tests are retaken.
  • [ui][manual] The performance tests' charts follow the theme. A UDP network test's jitter was a smoothed line in a blue no theme sets — it read a colour no theme declares —, which drew values between the seconds it never measured: it is one bar per second now, in the theme's network colour, as on the System tab. In Minimal, the latency histogram draws its bars under 50 µs in grey and up to 200 µs in black, and keeps colour for the delays from 200 µs. Both charts are drawn again when the theme or the light/dark mode changes: they kept the colours of the mode they were drawn in. The manual gives the histogram's bands and says the jitter is drawn second by second.
  • [core][ui][manual] The iperf3 network test takes a server's port. The field asks for an address and its port, and iperf3 read the whole as a host name — "unable to connect to server … Name or service not known", measured with iperf3 3.18 —: with a port, the test stopped at once. The port is passed to iperf3 on its own, 5201 when none is given; an IPv6 address takes brackets, [fe80::1]:5201; and an entry that names no server — empty, or with a port that is not a number or outside 1–65535 — is refused before the test starts (422), and no longer kept among the field's suggestions. The UPnP media server search, which split an address the same way, writes an IPv6 host in brackets in the addresses it probes.
  • [ui] The Pipeline writes a link's latency and buffer fill in colours that read. On a link, in its bubble and in the legend, the latency took the latency histogram's bar colours: 2.02 to 3.76:1 on its pill in every light theme. It takes its band's text colour now — in Minimal grey under 50 µs and ink up to 200 µs, as the latency test's histogram, then orange and red —, and the buffer fill beside it, ink at rest in Minimal, likewise: 5.07:1 or more in every theme and mode. The legend gives the bands in their colours instead of naming them — "Green <50µs · Blue …" was false in Minimal —, and the thresholds — the latency's, written in five places, legends included, and the buffer's, in three — are kept in one.
  • [site] The social preview and the README's image show the landing as it is today. The card networks and messengers show for audiogravity.app, and the image heading the site's README, still showed the public beta: its ribbon, an older capture of the app, the previous carousel controls — the README's even the opening line the landing dropped. Both are taken anew from the page by gen-og-image.js: the release candidate's ribbon, the HQPlayer screen the carousel opens on, with its annotations.
  • [site] Halloween — the spider beside the phone hangs from its thread in Safari. Safari drew the spider and not its thread, a pseudo-element sized with container units; each thread is now drawn in its spider's own box, its length in pixels.
  • [lic] A licence key no box holds is no longer written in full in the server's log. Created by the admin or by a PayPal payment, deactivated or restored, such a key is a bearer secret — the first box to send it keeps it; those lines now write only its first group (AG-XXXX…), and so does uvicorn's access log, which wrote it in the address of the admin's routes on an order.
  • [ops] nginx — the licence server's access log redacts licence keys, in the path as in the query (AG-XXXX-[key_redacted]), as it already dropped credentials.
  • [site] The README says what the app does today. Its opening line is the landing's; the editions table puts the box's own music, the radio and the player in Starter, where it put them in Pro; Tidal reads Hi-Res up to 24-bit / 192 kHz, no longer a "HiFi" plan Tidal has dropped; the interface's installer flags and --version are listed, and an omitted --vapid-email gives Audiogravity's own push address, not a placeholder with a warning; installing the app starts with trusting the box's certificate, and no longer says Chrome on iOS cannot; contributions follow the interface's own policy — no pull requests yet, issues on this site —; and the link to the core's repository, private, which a visitor could not open, is gone.
  • [ui] The README describes the repository as it is: the test count no longer reads 188 — the badge gives it —, npm test runs the Storybook tests in a browser as well, the signal-path diagram is the one component in a shadow root, and ESLint, npm run lint and ./dev.sh start --https are listed.
  • [ops] releases repository — the README's download check works. Its command fetched audiogravity-ui-*.tar.gz, a name curl does not expand, and got a 404; it now reads the file name from SHA256SUMS, which covers the three tarballs — not every file, as it said. It also gives the system required and the address the installer prints.
  • [manual] The login figure no longer shows the BETA badge. The app writes its stage in its corner — BETA up to 0.9.80, 1.0 RC now, nothing once 1.0 is out —, and the figure of the login screen still showed BETA. The badge is erased from it, the rest of the figure left as it was.
  • [ui][manual] Days and months are written in English whatever the phone's language, and dates read the same everywhere. On a phone set to French the Stats view wrote its week as lun. to dim., its bars as 28 sept. and a square as lun. 10:00, beside its English words; a configuration card's date and the performance tests' histories did the same, and the service and profile cards and the history panel wrote American English (Oct 27, 05:45 PM). One function writes them now, in British English — Mon 12 Oct, 27 Oct, 17:45, September as Sept —; a time keeps the phone's clock (17:45, 05:45 PM) and a date in digits its conventions. A moment from another year carries its year (27 Oct 2025, 17:45), a missing one reads -- where it read Invalid Date, and a configuration file saved at 23:50 reads yesterday at 00:10, where it read today. The manual's Stats and configuration figures are retaken.
  • [ui] The fullscreen player stays in place when a station's inset cover is tapped. Tapping the small cover in the corner of a station's artwork — the station's logo or the track's cover, to swap the two — threw the whole player off the screen and slid it back up, on an iPhone as in Chromium on a touch screen; a new track's cover did the same after a touch. Each new background tint rewrote the player's whole style and dropped what the touch had set, so its opening slide-in played again. The tint is now set on its own, and the slide-in is taken off once it has played: with a mouse, Close now slides the player down, where it stayed for a third of a second, then vanished. A drag the system interrupts — a call, Control Center — now puts the player back in place; it stayed where the finger had left it.

[1.0.0-rc.1] - 2026-10-25

Added

  • [site] Halloween on the landing, every 31 October. From midnight to midnight by the visitor's own clock, the page is dressed from top to bottom, never over a line of text. Bats fly across the hero and across the band above each section's label; on a screen wide enough to leave margins beside the text, more bats swoop through them, and in them spiders come down from each section's top on their threads, then climb back — on a phone, a spider hangs in each band instead. Webs hang in two corners of the hero. Jack-o'-lanterns stand at the foot of the phone, with a mist in the dark theme, and on the footer's top edge, under which a last spider hangs; their carved faces are lit from inside by a candle that flickers, each pumpkin at its own pace. On a screen too short to show the foot of the phone, its pumpkins rest on the bottom of the screen until it comes into view. That day the strip that shows a track playing plays Saint-Saëns' Danse macabre, and the tab's title starts with a pumpkin. The page opens dark unless the visitor chose a theme with the button, and nothing is stored. On any other day nothing of it is fetched; ?halloween in the address shows it on any day, to check it beforehand. Nothing moves in a part of the page scrolled out of sight, and for a visitor whose system asks for less motion only the pumpkins, their candles still, and the webs remain.
  • [site] Each guide says how long it takes to read, and the guides are numbered. Under a guide's title, a line gives its number and its reading time — Guide 10 · 5 min read —, and the list of guides ends each entry with its time: the words a reader sees, at 238 words a minute, the mean silent reading speed for non-fiction in English (Brysbaert, 2019), rounded up — 3 to 8 minutes today. The sidebar, the phone's menu and the list number the guides by their place in the list; a guide's address carries no number, so a guide added in the middle renumbers the list without breaking a link.

Changed

  • [site] The hero's carousel shows the app as it is today, in the page's theme. Seventeen screens captured anew, each in the app's light and dark themes; the page shows the capture of the theme it is in — the system's, or the one chosen with the button — and switches with it. Four screens are new: listening statistics, the menu, a service's network activity, and the audio software with its updates. The screen of Qobuz's new releases with a station playing is gone, for want of a new capture. Every annotation beside the phone is measured again on the new captures, and the captures weigh 1.9 MB where the originals weighed 32.9. The passkey login's capture, further down the page, is new too and follows the theme the same way.
  • [site] The hero carousel's controls are a pill of dots, and the current one fills over its screen's time. A dot per screen in a pill, beside a round play button: the dot of the screen shown is drawn long and fills from left to right over the 3.5 seconds before the next screen, a pause holds it where it is, and pressing a dot shows its screen. The arrows are gone. The dots are buttons now, reached with the keyboard and named for a screen reader, and the play button says what a press will do. The panel's grid, painted over the dots and the buttons until now, lies under them, and so does the phone's shadow, which greyed the controls in the light theme.
  • [site] The guides' sidebar gives their titles room. At 240px a guide's title — a whole question — broke onto up to four lines, at the browser's tightest line spacing, and the list read as one block. The column now takes what its titles need, up to 320px, and the lines of a title are spaced a little, less than two titles: three lines at most, the guide's text keeping its width (Chromium, 1440 × 900). The manual's chapters keep their 240px column; their numbers now sit on their title's line, where they sat a little high.
  • [site] The guides no longer carry the trademark notice: it speaks for the app, which the manual documents and a guide does not. The manual's chapters keep it.
  • [ui] The corner badge names the release stage: "1.0 RC". The purple badge in the bottom right corner of the app read BETA; it now reads the stage from the version itself — "1.0 RC" for this release candidate, BETA for a 0.x release, none for a final release or a development build —, where a build setting kept by hand on one machine decided it.
  • [site] The landing says "Release candidate". The corner ribbon, the announce bar, the footer and the README name the stage; on a phone the ribbon reads "1.0 RC" and keeps the old ribbon's place, the long words covering the end of the links and leaving the theme toggle no room beside the logo at 320px. On a wider screen the announce bar's sentence, the theme toggle and the GitHub button keep their distance from the longer ribbon; the GitHub button leaves the bar from 1160px instead of 1130px, where it found no room beside it.
  • [manual] The FAQ answers what "Release candidate" means, in place of "Public beta": the version proposed as 1.0, complete, taking only corrections until then. The landing's FAQ, generated from it, follows.

Fixed

  • [lic] A box on a release candidate is offered its final release. The update offer and the automatic publication compared the numbers alone, so 1.0.0-rc.1 and 1.0.0 were one version: the final release would never have been offered to a box on its candidate, nor published by itself. They now order versions as SemVer does — a candidate before its release, rc.2 before rc.10 —, and a development build keeps its offers as before.
  • [ops] self-update — the health check tells a release candidate from its final release. After an update it checks that the core answers with the version asked for; on the numbers alone, a release candidate still answering after an update to its final release passed for a success, and the update was not rolled back. The check that judges an update is the one the installed version shipped, so it leaves with the release candidate.
  • [ops] release — verify promotes a final release over its release candidate. It refused to move the latest release backwards with sort -V, which ranks 1.0.0-rc.1 after 1.0.0; it now orders versions as the licence server does, and stops when it cannot compare them.
  • [ui] Update banner — a failed attempt at a release candidate is no longer shown against the offer of its final release.
  • [lic] The admin's device table sorts versions in the order updates are offered: a release candidate before its release, rc.2 before rc.10. Reading every digit of the version put a release candidate after its own release.
  • [ops] release — the README version badges follow a release candidate. sync-version.mjs skipped every version with a dash, which left the ui, lic and core badges on the release before; update-test-report.sh wrote the landing's with a single dash, which a shields.io address reads as a field separator. Both write a pre-release with the dash doubled (1.0.0--rc.1), and "beta" after a 0.x release only.
  • [site] The pipeline drawing's moving dots no longer keep the whole page busy. They kept the page redrawing sixty times a second wherever the reader was, the drawing on screen or not — on a modest phone, more than half of what the page asked of it; they now move only while the drawing is on screen, and stay still for a visitor whose system asks for less motion, which they used to ignore.

[0.9.80] - 2026-10-24

Added

  • [core] Network renderer — the player shows the track's format. FORMAT, SAMPLE and BITRATE stayed empty for whatever a network renderer played, and CODEC for anything but a DSD file: a renderer reports none of them. They are now filled as Audiogravity knows them — the header of the bytes the box relays to the renderer (Qobuz, HIGHRESAUDIO), Tidal's stream description, the file's own header for a library or media-server file (FLAC, WAV, AIFF, DSF, MP4), a station's catalogue bitrate —, read once per track in the background, at no request of its own for a streaming service. A value nothing tells shows a dash.

Changed

  • [manual] Listening → casting to a network speaker: the hi-fi readout shows what Audiogravity knows of the track sent, where it said the readout was absent; a cell nothing fills shows a dash.
  • [ui] API.md documents format.codec: none when nothing tells it, read a moment after an .m4a/.mp4 starts (a further state event), and filled for a network renderer, with its format, sample_rate and bitrate.

Fixed

  • [core] HIGHRESAUDIO tracks show their codec. The CODEC cell stayed empty: MPD plays the box's relay, whose address names no format. The relay now records the codec of the file the service serves.
  • [core] Qobuz shows the codec it serves, read in the address Qobuz resolves for the track: the relay's address named none, and every track read FLAC — an MP3 included, on a box set to MP3.
  • [core] An .m4a or .mp4 file shows ALAC or AAC, as its header says. Both were shown as ALAC: an AAC — an iTunes Store purchase — passed for lossless. Read once per track, from the box's disk or the media server.
  • [core] Roon — the CODEC cell reads "—" when Roon names no codec, no longer "Roon".
  • [core] A file whose codec is not known yet is no longer shown as "PCM" — an MP4 the moment its header is read, or an extension Audiogravity does not know.
  • [core] DSD played on a network renderer no longer brings the box's DAC to full volume. The DSD protection took the one card playing on the box — AirPlay's, say — and set it to 100 % while the DSD went out to the speaker.
  • [core] A library file cast to a network renderer is recognised by its extension, which a signature follows in its address: a DSD file there was not seen as DSD.

[0.9.79] - 2026-10-23

Added

  • [core+ui] Push notifications — three new alerts. A new release of Audiogravity is announced once per version, from the daily check of the licence server, and its notification opens the Admin tab, where the update banner installs it; only an answer of that server withdraws it — a server that does not answer, at a restart before the network is up, withdraws nothing. The system disk almost full — the Disk Usage tile's figure, read every ten minutes while a device is subscribed — is announced at 90 % and again at 95 %, and once more only after it went back under 85 %. A Raspberry Pi whose power supply lets the voltage drop below the safe level is announced at most once a day: the core listens to the Pi's own under-voltage flag, through the lookup the Performance tab's THROTTLED badge already uses, rather than reading it on a timer. Measured on the lab's Pi 4, whose supply drops 65 times a day: every drop and every return seen within the second of the kernel's message, one notification for all of them, 0.003 s of CPU over 100 s of waiting. A box no device subscribed from runs none of the three checks, and a machine without the flag never has the third.
  • [core+ui] Push notifications — turning Notifications on sends a test notification, and says why when they cannot be turned on. The box sends that device, and that device only, a notification: the proof that the whole way works. When it does not go — notifications blocked for the site, an app not opened over HTTPS or, on an iPhone or iPad, not installed on the Home Screen, a box without its notification keys, a subscription the box refuses, a push service that refuses it or cannot be reached —, a message names the reason, the subscription is undone on both sides and the switch goes back off. The switch used to stay on after a failure, with nothing said. An app newer than its core, which has no test yet, turns notifications on as before and says no test could be sent. Measured in Chromium against the dev core: subscription, test accepted by Google's push service, Notifications on; without keys, The box cannot send notifications and the switch back off. POST /push/test sends it, with the API key.
  • [ops] Passkeys work on the box's name, set up by the installer. Passkeys (Face ID / Touch ID) were left off on a box installed without --public-url, and every page said they needed a real domain. They need a name, never an address — measured on an iPhone: on https://musics.local, with the box's own certificate trusted and no domain bought, a passkey registered and signed in, twice. The interface installer now gives the core that origin — the name it prints and puts in its QR code, with its port — when it runs on the same box in HTTPS, the certificate carries the name and the core has none; a box whose name another device already holds is left alone, its name can change. The origin it set is marked as its own and follows the box's port or name at the next install; one given with --public-url, or by hand, is never touched.
  • [manual] Administration → Push notifications lists the seven alerts, says Profile Activated goes to the other devices, and explains the test notification and the messages shown when notifications cannot be turned on.

Changed

  • [manual] A real domain is optional: notifications and passkeys work on the box's name. Installation, First run, Administration, Troubleshooting, the FAQ, the glossary and the quick start said push notifications and passkeys needed a real HTTPS domain and could not work over a bare IP. On an iPhone or iPad they work on the box's name, its own certificate trusted — measured for both, the notifications arriving with the app closed. Getting HTTPS — for passkeys and push becomes A real domain — optional: what a domain adds is the app, passkeys included, from another network than the box's, and no certificate to trust on each device. Also put right: add the app from the box's name only from the box's network — from another Wi-Fi or VLAN the name does not open, so add it from the address and reserve that address in the router; iOS 16.4 or later for notifications; turn them on from the installed app; the FAQ no longer says only Safari installs the app; --vapid-email and --public-url say what they do. A new Troubleshooting entry, Notifications never reach an iPhone, iPad or Mac, says to update the box.
  • [site] Landing — the install section no longer says passkeys and push need a public HTTPS domain: it says to trust the box's certificate once on each phone, offers plain HTTP for a box behind a reverse proxy rather than as a way round the certificate — plain HTTP gives no app, no notifications and no passkeys —, and presents a domain of one's own as optional. The FAQ is synced from the manual again, the Starter Edition answer included.
  • [site] Landing — the push notification card and the app section list every alert: disk almost full, a Raspberry Pi's power supply too weak and a new version of Audiogravity join service down, critical temperature and audio software updates; the app section names Profile Activated too, which goes to the other devices.
  • [ops] sync-landing-faq.py writes the landing's links in --warn-text, the colour the landing's rework gave them for contrast: run, it put back --warn in every FAQ link.
  • [core] POST /push/subscribe refuses an endpoint over 2048 characters (422), the bound the test notification and a profile activation share; push services hand out a few hundred.

Fixed

  • [core] Push notifications — deactivating a profile no longer sends Profile Activated. A deactivation runs through the activation with a profile that starts nothing, which reads as active once done: every subscribed device was told the profile had just been activated — reproduced with the real profiles code over stand-in services, one activation and one deactivation giving two notifications.
  • [core+ui] Push notifications — Profile Activated is no longer sent to the device that made the change, only to the other subscribed devices: the app names its own push endpoint with the activation (push_endpoint of POST /profiles/{profile_id}/activate).
  • [core] Push notifications — an update announced is no longer announced again after the box restarts. What had gone out was kept in memory, and the core restarts at every update of Audiogravity and every reboot: an audio software update left uninstalled was announced anew each time. What reached at least one device is now kept on the box, beside its subscriptions, and is forgotten only once the update is installed or withdrawn — never on a pass that could not read the installed versions. A notification no device received is not recorded, and is tried again.
  • [core+ops] Push notifications reach iPhones, iPads and Macs. Every box the installer set up signed its notifications with mailto:admin@audiogravity.local, a contact Apple's push service refuses: measured against Apple with an invented device, a .local or localhost contact gets 403 BadJwtToken where a real address is accepted — so an Apple device never received a single notification. --vapid-email could not help: it wrote into .env, while the core signs with the copy in vapid_keys.json, which the installer always filled with the placeholder. The installer now writes the contact given, else push@audiogravity.app, where the core reads it; an update replaces the old placeholder — keys kept, so subscribed devices stay subscribed — and keeps a contact set by hand; the core itself replaces a contact that cannot work, and says so in its journal. Measured on a copy of an ARM box's own keys: refused before, accepted after. A key file without a contact no longer leaves push off.
  • [core] Push notifications reach Android and Apple devices of the same box. The library that sends them writes the push service's address into the signature it is handed, and the core handed it the same one every time: after a notification to Google, every one to Apple carried Google's address, and Apple refused it (403, measured) — a box with both kinds of device reached only one. Each push now gets a signature of its own.
  • [ops] Installer — --public-url is applied on an update too. It was read on a first install only: a box given a domain later kept its first passkey origin.
  • [core] Manual install guide (docs/INSTALL.md) writes the push contact into vapid_keys.json, and says passkeys need a name, not a real domain.
  • [core] Push notifications — a box without notification keys says so. GET /push/vapid-public-key answered 500 there instead of its 503 — the dev core of the lab's x86 box —, and the app could only report that the box did not answer.

[0.9.78] - 2026-10-22

Added

  • [site] Four new guides: What your USB DAC accepts on Linux, and how to read it — where Linux lists the formats, rates and DSD a USB DAC takes, why the list can stop at 96 kHz, and which settings copied from forums the kernel now ignores; Software or hardware volume on a Linux streamer — what a player's, a DAC's and an amplifier's volume each do to the music, MPD's settings, its slider in decibels (50 % is −18.5 dB), and DSD; AirPlay to a Raspberry Pi: what quality actually arrives — CD quality in classic AirPlay, lossy AAC in AirPlay 2 from an iPhone, iPad or Mac, and a Debian 13 receiver that plays classic AirPlay only; Your streamer on the network — the bandwidth music takes, Wi-Fi or cable for a streamer and an NAA, and how AirPlay, UPnP and NAA devices find each other across VLANs. Like the others, each gives the steps on Raspberry Pi OS and Debian and the messages as they show, says what it was checked with and when, and lists its sources, with a short Audiogravity section at the end.

Changed

  • [site] Four guides rewritten for every reader, Audiogravity user or not: Your NAS music on a Raspberry Pi streamer, DSD on a Linux streamer, Qobuz, Tidal and HIGHRESAUDIO on a Raspberry Pi and HQPlayer NAA on a Raspberry Pi. Each now gives the steps on Raspberry Pi OS and Debian — the commands, how to check, the messages as they show —, says what it was checked with and when, and lists its sources; Audiogravity keeps a short section at the end, a tenth of the text or so where it was up to half. A guide that explains how to do something no longer walks through the other products; the streaming guide, whose subject is the choice, compares the ways to play the three services on a Pi — upmpdcli, Volumio, moOde, Lyrion, HQPlayer, Roon — with what each costs and allows. The NAS guide's commands and ten error messages were measured against a Samba and an NFS server, and so was a trap it now steers around: an MPD update run while the share is not mounted empties its library, without a message, where a share mounted when its folder is first opened is refused and the library kept. The NAA guide gives Signalyst's rule — an NAA 6 needs HQPlayer 5.16 or later — and how DietPi installs the newest NAA whatever the HQPlayer. Statements found wrong on the way are put right: Linux recognises native DSD from a list of DACs and of makers, not by a generic detection; HIGHRESAUDIO names Volumio's own players, not Volumio.
  • [site] Five more guides rewritten the same way: How to check bit-perfect playback, HQPlayer Embedded on a Raspberry Pi 5, “Device or resource busy”, Roon on a Raspberry Pi and Real-time priority and reserved CPU cores. The HQPlayer Embedded guide installs Signalyst's package step by step and says what to set by hand after it — the web page's password, the output, the log, the half-hour trial; the Roon guide installs Roon Bridge with Roon's own script, or on DietPi; the real-time guide shows how to read a player's priority, pin it to a core and reserve that core on a Pi; the busy guide gives MPD's message as measured. Audiogravity's sections come down to about a tenth of each text, where they took from a quarter to almost two fifths. Put right on the way: a 2023 remark by HQPlayer's author on the Pi 5, made the day the board was announced, was read as a measured limit — his 2025 findings on its USB output replace it; of the ten arm64 versions Signalyst lists for Debian 13, only three can be downloaded, and version 5 cannot install there, with apt's message given.
  • [manual] Troubleshooting → Manual NAS mount mounts the share when its folder is first opened, with nofail, the account written in a file that never passes through the shell's history, and a check that says why a mount failed. The share was mounted at start-up: with the NAS off then, its folder stayed empty, and a library scan could empty MPD's index. It no longer says that kernels disable SMB1 by default: Debian 13's still accepts vers=1.0.

Fixed

  • [core+ui] Audio Software — a vendor's site that does not answer no longer turns software into Not Supported. On 21 October the site of upmpdcli's author answered HTTP 503 to every client during an emergency maintenance at its host, for over a day. The boxes updated meanwhile recorded UPnP Bridge as published for no machine at all, and its card read Not Supported while it was installed and playing; on ARM, AG also stopped offering its own build of it, which that site does not serve. A check that gets no answer now keeps the vendor's last answer for the box — same address, same Debian release, same machine — for up to five days, and asks again every day; on ARM, AG's own build of UPnP Bridge is used whenever its author offers none for ARM, whether his site says so or says nothing, and his site is still asked every day, for the day it does. The card no longer calls a check that could not be made Not Supported, and a list of machines left empty reads Unknown. Measured on an x86 and an ARM box of the lab, the author's site still down: the x86 one keeps UPnP Bridge available for amd64 and i386 from an answer of 6 October, and the ARM one goes back to AG's own build from the configuration it has installed. A box with no answer to keep — the lab's x86 one, updated during the outage — reads as unchecked until the site is back, no longer as unsupported. In GET /packages/, unknown now means the source could not be reached and gave no answer for this box in the last five days.
  • [core+ui] Support report — a package that cannot be installed is given its real reason. It read not supported on this architecture whatever stood in the way: the lab's x86 box had UPnP Bridge (its author's site down) and Roon Server (Roon Bridge installed) in that case, both supported there. It now gives the reason the software card shows — cannot be checked: repository answered HTTP 503, unavailable: Roon Bridge is installed — the two cannot run together, not available here: … —, and keeps not supported on this architecture for the one case it describes. The report's packages entries give availability and availability_reason, as GET /packages/ does.
  • [site] On a phone, the manual's and the guides' list of pages is a menu. Below 860px it was a strip scrolled sideways: fine for the manual's one-word chapters, unusable for the guides, whose titles are whole questions — on a 375px phone not one title showed whole, and the guide being read sat far off to the right. An All guides or All chapters line now opens the list, every title on its own line and the page being read marked; closed, the page starts on the first screen. On a wider screen the column stays as it was. The Guides and User manual labels in the top bar now lead to their contents, which no guide page linked back to.
  • [site] On a phone, Pricing and Install in the landing's bar bring their section to the top of the screen at the first touch. The page stopped short, the section showing near the bottom of the screen, until a second touch: the app's sign-in screenshot further up took its room only once it loaded, during the scroll. It now has its size from the start; nothing else on the page moves.

[0.9.77] - 2026-10-21

Added

  • [core+ui] Library → Stats shows covers. Most played and Recently played show each row's cover — an album's, a track's, a station's logo — where they showed a music note, which stays when none is known. An artist shows the cover of the album of theirs listened to most over the whole history, the same whatever the period. A cover is kept as its address, never as an image, and read when Stats shows it: of the addresses an album's or a track's listens gave, the surest — the picture where it is kept, before a search by name, before a file AirPlay deletes once the track is over —, the latest among them; a track with none of its own takes its album's. What was recorded before keeps the note until the same track or album is heard again. Measured on ten years of synthetic listens: the history file grows from 33.0 MB to 39.7, and 12 months takes 104 ms to read instead of 101 on x86, 256 instead of 248 on ARM. GET /listening/summary gives top_artists[], top_albums[], top_tracks[] and recent[] a cover_token, for GET /audio_pipeline/cover.
  • [manual] Library & streaming → Stats says each row shows its cover, an artist the cover of the album of theirs played most, a station its logo.

Fixed

  • [core] Library → Stats no longer counts a radio station under a word that names no genre. A station's tags in the radio directory name its codec, its country or its owner as readily as its style: France Inter's are aac, general, public radio, radio france, and its listens were counted under a genre called Aac. A station — and anything played live — now counts only under a genre of AG's common list, from its tags as from what its stream announces, and under Unknown otherwise; a file still keeps its own word when it names a genre no service uses. The listens already recorded are put right at the box's next start, and so is what an older version records should the box go back to it: on the lab's box, Aac (1 h 41) and Eclectic Programming (29 min) moved to Unknown, the time listened unchanged.
  • [manual] Library & streaming → Stats says a radio station counts as Unknown when its description names no genre of the list.

[0.9.76] - 2026-10-20

Fixed

  • [core+ui] Library → Stats counts what is playing. A listen was recorded once it ended, and Stats counted only what was recorded: a station left on all evening showed nowhere until it stopped, and Stats read Nothing recorded yet while it played. What plays now counts as it stands when Stats is read — once it has played 30 seconds — in every figure and in the count of listens, and comes first in Recently played, marked IN PROGRESS where the others show when they began, with its time so far; a track on pause keeps its time, without the mark. Nothing more is written for it, and it costs nothing while the music plays. While Stats is on screen it is read again when what plays changes, once a track just started counts, and when the app comes back to the foreground — from the player stream the mini player already holds open. The history's reads and writes now run one at a time, in the order asked, so a track ending as Stats opens is counted once, never left out or counted twice. A history file the core can no longer write (a card gone read-only) shows what it holds instead of an error. GET /listening/summary gives recent[] its in_progress; listens of GET /listening/settings counts the listens in progress.
  • [core] A device that no longer takes notifications is forgotten at its first refusal. One that had unsubscribed — the app removed from a home screen, its notifications turned off — was sent every notification again: the core never read the status of the push service's refusal. It is now forgotten as soon as the service answers that the subscription is gone — Apple's 410 Unregistered, or an expired subscription's 404 —, and kept on any other refusal. On the lab's box, 7 of its 10 subscriptions had been refused 133 times over 19 notifications since 10 October, none forgotten; they go at the next notification.
  • [manual] Library & streaming says what is playing counts in Stats, marked IN PROGRESS, and Troubleshooting (A listen is missing from Stats) no longer says a listen shows only once it ends.
  • [core] HQPlayer — every internet radio station sent to HQPlayer goes through the box's own relay, on a port of its own (8010). Fetched by HQPlayer itself, Radio France's MP3 stations stopped after 30 to 40 seconds; served by the core's web server, the same stations played one evening and failed five times out of five the next morning (HQPlayer Desktop 5.28.1). The relay answers in HTTP/1.0 — the end of the body is the end of the connection —, the shape a separate test program held in. A station in MP3 or FLAC is passed on as it is sent; the others — AAC, or a format the catalogue does not name — are converted to FLAC as they arrive, as AAC stations already were. A station whose server answers the old SHOUTcast way (ICY 200 OK, which the core's HTTP client refuses) goes through ffmpeg, its frames copied, unconverted. A connection HQPlayer has stopped reading for a minute is closed, and what it held with it. Measured: France Inter and FIP, in MP3 and converted from AAC, held all their plays and resumes after a pause — 13 and 13 on HQPlayer Desktop, 6 and 6 on HQPlayer Embedded 6.2.6. A station already in HQPlayer's list when the box is updated is to be sent again.
  • [core] HQPlayer — Play on a paused station picks up the live broadcast. A bare Play resumed it where it was paused, then stopped once HQPlayer's buffer ran dry; AG now selects the station's entry again, then plays — the sound back a few seconds after the press —, and reports a refused selection instead of resuming the past.
  • [core] HQPlayer — Previous and Next are greyed where they lead nowhere: Previous on the first entry, Next on the last, both on a station played alone. HQPlayer answered either with an error and stopped the music. can_next / can_prev of the player state follow the entry HQPlayer plays.
  • [core] HQPlayer — a station or an album started after HQPlayer sat paused on a station for a while now plays. The Play that followed the new list was ignored after a pause of two minutes; the first entry is now selected before it.
  • [core] HQPlayer — what AG sends to HQPlayer says whether it is a live broadcast (live of the player state, true for a station), as for a network renderer; what HQPlayer plays from its own remote stays unknown (null).
  • [ops] installer — the core's settings carry HQPLAYER_RELAY_PORT=8010, added to an existing box's at its update, never over a port changed by hand; another program on that port makes the relay take a free one and say which in the journal. The interface's installer refuses that port when the core runs on the same box.
  • [manual] Installation → the interface installer's --port refuses 8010 too, where the core serves HQPlayer its radio stations.
  • [core] HQPlayer — the player's signal path names the HQPlayer that plays, and reaches the DAC. With the chain description a new box arrives with, an HQPlayer on another computer read hqplayer — the internal name — with no DAC, and the box's own HQPlayer Embedded had no DAC either; with a description of one's own, the step took whatever name it gave another machine's HQPlayer (on the lab's box, HQPlayer Embedded while the Mac's HQPlayer Desktop played). The step is now named as HQPlayer calls itself — HQPlayer Desktop, HQPlayer Embedded, or HQPlayer until it has said — asked once when it answers a status read, never while the music plays, nor again of one that went silent. Another computer's HQPlayer goes through the box's NAA to the DAC (HQPlayer Desktop → HQPlayer NAA → DAC), the box's own straight to it; one whose sound the box does not play is shown alone, with no DAC. A service of the box that the description leaves out — HQPlayer Embedded or the NAA, on the one delivered — is placed on the box, when the description has a single streamer, and reaches the DAC as MPD does.
  • [manual] Outputs & engines → Use as output names the HQPlayer in the signal path: Library → HQPlayer Desktop → HQPlayer NAA → your DAC.

[0.9.75] - 2026-10-19

Added

  • [ops] installer — the interface's installer takes --http, --https and --port. They choose how the interface is served and win over an existing install, so re-running the installer with one of them is how a box changes mode or port; --http or --https alone means that mode's own port (8080, 443), and nothing is asked. Without them a re-run, or a self-update, keeps the mode and port in place. The all-in-one installer passes them on, and both hosted installers check the port before downloading anything. An installer from before them refuses them instead of ignoring them.
  • [ops] installer — the interface's installer ends on the box's name, and its QR code carries it. http(s)://<name>.local comes first, the address below it should the name not open: without a fixed address, the name is the only way back to the box, and an app added to a home screen keeps for ever the address it was added from. The name is the one avahi announces, once settled — a box renamed because another device holds its name (audiogravity-2.local) shows the one it answers to, with a warning in yellow to give the box a hostname of its own and run the installer again, since which device keeps the plain name can change at a restart; in HTTPS it is offered only when the certificate carries it. The address is repeated under the QR code. The addresses to open stand out in green, and what blocks when skipped — trusting the certificate, its step 3, the limits of plain HTTP — in yellow.

Fixed

  • [site][manual] The install instructions open the streamer by its name, and offer plain HTTP. The landing's install block and Installation → all-in-one sent the reader to https://<ip-of-your-streamer>; they now point at the address the installer prints, https://<name>.local, and the landing gives the --http install for those with no certificate to set up on their phone.
  • [ops] installer — the core installer announces admin / admin123 only when it has just created that account. Run over an existing install it keeps the accounts, yet it ended on "Default login: admin / admin123", so an owner who had changed the password was refused without a word. It now says the accounts were kept, and gives the two commands that bring admin / admin123 back; the password itself cannot be shown, the box keeps only its hash.
  • [ops] installer — the interface's certificate names the .local name the box answers to. It was built from the hostname, so a box renamed by avahi because another device holds its name got a certificate for that other device's name, and https://<name>-2.local was refused. It now carries both, compared exactly; the interface's start never waits for avahi, only the installer does, a few seconds, and each question to avahi is bounded to one second.
  • [ops] installer — in plain HTTP, the summary no longer says the home-screen icon receives notifications. A browser grants notifications and the offline start to HTTPS only; the summary now says to open the interface through the reverse proxy's https:// address for those.
  • [manual] Installation, First run and Troubleshooting say which .local name to use: the one the installer prints first. A box that takes -2 because another device already answers to its name is to be given a hostname of its own — Troubleshooting says how.
  • [ops] installer — the interface's installer asks again when an answer is not one it can use. "2" typed for the mode and landing on the port question gave an interface on port 2: a port is now 80, 443, or 1024 to 65535 — not the core's port on the same box, nor in HTTPS the one that hands out the certificate (8081) — and the mode 1 or 2.
  • [ops] installer — the core installer shows the API key once. A new install printed it in two boxes in a row.
  • [manual] Installation → Getting HTTPS switches the interface to plain HTTP with --http. It said to re-run the ui installer and pick plain HTTP, but an installed interface was never asked again. The flags have their own table.
  • [ops] uninstaller — after an uninstall without --purge, it names what it kept and the command that removes it. It ended on "Run with --purge to remove them", but it lives in the directory it has just removed: there was nothing left to run.
  • [manual] Installation → Uninstalling runs --purge in place of the plain uninstall, not after it. It gives the command that removes what a plain uninstall kept — now naming the processor setting of the Performance page among them — and says that the interface keeps the box's certificate authority, and why.

[0.9.74] - 2026-10-19

Added

  • [core+ui] Library → Stats: what the box played. A sixth tab in Library shows the time listened over the last 7 or 30 days, 12 months or the whole history — one bar per day or month, tap one to read it —, where it came from and went to, its sound quality (DSD, Hi-Res, CD quality, lossy), its genres, the most played artists, albums and tracks, and the latest listens. A listen counts from 30 seconds actually played, pauses left out; a station is one listen for as long as you stay on it, its genre taken from its tags. The box records on every edition, from what its player already announces — the player reads nothing more for it, with or without a screen open (measured: 8 reads in two minutes of playback, none following a screen); the figures come with Pro. Roon and HQPlayer are not counted. A listen whose source vanishes from the player for a moment, or that plays across a restart of the core, stays one listen. The history is one file on the box, readable by the core's account only, never sent anywhere; ten years of listens open in 75 ms, 12 months in 280 ms at most, on the ARM box (200,000 synthetic listens, measured on a 1.8 GHz board, the week's hours included). New routes: GET /listening/summary (Pro), GET/PUT /listening/settings and DELETE /listening/history (every edition, refused to a guest account).

  • [core+ui] Library → Stats shows when the box plays. When you listen maps the week: a row per day, Monday to Sunday, a square per hour, the darker the more the box played at that hour, on average per week over the period chosen; tap a square to read its time. A listen counts in the hours it actually played — one started at 20:50 for half an hour fills 20:00 and 21:00, a pause fills none — on the box's clock, including the nights the clocks change and in the countries half an hour off UTC. A track replayed after a pause is dated by its own start. It costs nothing while the music plays; opening the view takes 14 ms more on the x86 box and 35 ms more on the ARM box, at most, with ten years of listens. GET /listening/summary gains week_hours.

  • [core] Streaming tracks have a genre, from one list shared by every source. Qobuz, Tidal and HIGHRESAUDIO each file music under genres of their own — the same style as Soul/Funk/R&B, R&B/Soul or R&B — and their tracks reached Library → Stats as Unknown. The box now counts and shows them under one list of 23 genres, at the level of the genre, never of the sub-genre: Pop, Rock, Pop/Rock, Alternative, Metal, Electronic, Dance, Hip-Hop, Soul & R&B, Jazz, Blues, Country, Folk, Classical, Soundtrack, World, Latin, Reggae, French, Easy Listening, Children, Spoken Word, Religious. Qobuz tracks and HIGHRESAUDIO album tracks carry their album's genre from the moment they are queued; a Tidal track, or a track of an HIGHRESAUDIO playlist, has it asked of its service once it starts playing — one request per track, in the background, answered in 0.17 s for Tidal and 0.30 s for HIGHRESAUDIO (medians over 40 tracks and 15 albums). The track playing shows its genre in its details, HIGHRESAUDIO's album genre rather than the one its file names. A file's or a station's genre tag is read against the same list, each genre it lists in order: Pop & Rock and Pop/Rock are one genre, and a name no source uses is kept as written. Listens recorded before keep their genre. LibraryTrack gains genre; sources[].genre of the player state carries the common genre for a streaming track.

  • [ui] Recording can be switched off and the history cleared on every edition. At the bottom of Library → Stats, Keep a history of what plays and Clear history… (asked first) work on a Starter box too, where the figures stay behind the padlock.

  • [ops] installer — the listening history is enabled on an existing box at its next update.

  • [manual] Library & streaming describes Stats: the periods, the charts, what counts as a listen, switching the recording off and clearing it. The editions table, Troubleshooting (A listen is missing from Stats) and the backup list (listening.db) follow.

  • [manual] Library & streaming says the genres in Stats follow one list whatever the service, with an example, and that files and radio stations are read against it.

  • [manual] A figure of Stats, and the tab menu with its Stats tab. Library & streaming shows the Stats view over 30 days — the time listened, a bar per day, the week's hours — with an example history, set in the capturing browser only. First run's two figures of the tab menu, Pro and Starter, and its list of Library's views now include Stats.

  • [site] The landing has a Pro card for the listening history, beside the self-update card, now half width.

  • [site] Search engines are told where the site is and what it holds. The site had no sitemap, the landing named no address of its own while www.audiogravity.app served the same page, its search snippet ran past what a result shows, and Bing returned nothing for the domain (measured 2026-10-17). The landing now names https://audiogravity.app/ as its address, and www.audiogravity.app redirects there; its snippet says what Audiogravity is in 145 characters; structured data gives the site's name and what the product is, with no price. A sitemap.xml lists the landing and every page of the manual, and robots.txt points to it. The sitemap is written with the manual's pages, from the same Contents list, so a new chapter reaches it in the same run, at the address its page gives.

  • [ops] release — the manual's regeneration commits the sitemap with it, so the landing is not left modified for release.sh ui to refuse.

  • [site] Guides, with their own tab in the landing's bar. Practical answers to questions asked on audiophile forums, each checked against the app and dated, the other products' facts sourced: HQPlayer Embedded on a Raspberry Pi 5, Qobuz, Tidal and HIGHRESAUDIO on a Raspberry Pi, HQPlayer NAA on a Raspberry Pi, How to check bit-perfect playback on a Linux streamer and Audiogravity, Volumio or moOde: which to choose?. They are written in Markdown, listed in docs/guides/README.md, and rendered by the manual's generator on the manual's page shell, each with its own address for search engines and its entry in the sitemap. With one more link, two labels of the bar broke onto a second line from 1101 to 1118 px and from 851 to 934 px wide: the bar's two thresholds move to 1130 and 950 px, and no label breaks from 320 to 1400 px (Chromium).

  • [ops] release — the manual's regeneration commits the guides with it.

  • [site] Six more guides: A DAC HAT on a Raspberry Pi: why it is not detected, and the line that fixes it, Your NAS music on a Raspberry Pi streamer: mount the share, or use a UPnP server, Roon on a Raspberry Pi: Roon Bridge, and Roon through HQPlayer, DSD on a Linux streamer: native, DoP, or converted to PCM, Real-time priority and reserved CPU cores for audio on Linux and “Device or resource busy”: two players, one DAC. What they say of Audiogravity is checked in the app and measured on the lab's boxes where it could be — the DAC held and released, DSD as the DAC receives it, wake-up delays on an isolated core and on a shared one —, and what they say of other products cites its sources, dated. The guides' list now follows the order of building a streamer. How to check bit-perfect playback on a Linux streamer no longer says that the guided setup turns DoP off: until this version, it did not.

  • [core+ui] A DoP switch in MPD's guided setup. Config → MPD → Guided has a DSD field: Send DSD as DoP (DSD over PCM), off by default, for a DAC that decodes DoP but that Linux cannot send DSD to as it is. It is set on the output MPD plays on — in that output's block of MPD's file alone, its old dsd_usb alias taken out with it — and MPD takes it without a restart, from the next time it opens the DAC — restarted instead when the switch moves MPD's DSD conversion before its volume (below). The switch shows what MPD applies, or what its file says while a Roon or HQPlayer profile has stopped it; it waits while another output is chosen and not yet applied. Reset to default keeps it on the same output, and leaves it off for another. An output with no DoP setting (not a sound card) is refused before anything is written.

  • [core+ui] The player says how a DSD track reaches the DAC. The format shown is the track's: a DSD128 file read DSD128 even when the DAC received PCM. A couple of seconds into a DSD track played by the box's MPD, the mini player and the full-screen format strip add a DoP tag, or Converted to PCM, when it does not reach the DAC as DSD — read once per track from the sound card and MPD's DoP setting, again when the track starts over.

  • [manual] Outputs & engines explains the three ways DSD reaches the DAC — as DSD, as DoP, converted to PCM — and the DoP switch: when to turn it on, when to leave it off, a HAT, DSD256 too fast for DoP. Listening, First run, Administration and the glossary (DoP) follow.

  • [core+ui] Full DAC volume during DSD, a switch per DAC. Config → MPD → Guided has a second switch in its DSD field, under DoP's: Full DAC volume during DSD, on by default. On, the DAC MPD plays on has its own volume brought to full while it receives DSD — as DSD or as DoP, from any player of the box, HQPlayer included — and given back when the DSD ends. Off, for a DAC that lowers DSD properly, its volume is left as set. The setting belongs to the DAC, not to MPD: it is kept in audio-topology.json, found again by the DAC's USB id, else by its name, and it applies at once, a DSD track playing included. Like DoP's, the switch waits while another output is chosen and not yet applied; Reset to default leaves it as it is. New route: POST /audio-stack/dsd-full-volume; GET /audio-stack/status gives the mpd entry its dsd_full_volume.

  • [manual] Outputs & engines says what happens to your DAC's volume during DSD, and how to turn it off, with a figure of MPD's DSD field. First run's figure of the Guided view is retaken with that field; First run, Administration, Introduction, Listening and the glossary (DSD) follow.

  • [core+ui] HQPlayer's volume on its card. In Library → Sources, the HQPlayer card shows HQPlayer's volume in dB, with a slider that sets it — for the box's own HQPlayer as for one on the network, whether it plays through the box or not; it sat in the DSP menu, closed by default. The slider covers the range set in HQPlayer, from its Volume min to its Volume max, and the card follows a change made in HQPlayer or by Roon every 5 seconds while it is on screen, asking nothing while it is out of sight. A volume fixed in HQPlayer's settings shows its level and no slider; a volume that cannot be read shows a dash, where it read 0.0 dB. GET /hqplayer/status gives volume_min_db, volume_max_db and volume_fixed.

  • [manual] Outputs & engines describes HQPlayer's volume on its card, and its two figures of the card are retaken.

  • [core+ui] The volume works on DSD on a box whose DAC does not take DSD. A HAT on a Raspberry Pi, a computer's built-in outputs or a USB DAC Linux sends no DSD to receive DSD converted to PCM, and MPD converted it in the last filter of its output, after its volume: the volume did nothing to DSD, and the player showed a padlock. Where none of MPD's outputs takes DSD as it is and DoP is off, the guided setup now writes audio_output_format "*:32:*" in MPD's file: DSD becomes PCM at the decoder, before the volume, and the player keeps its slider. Measured on a HiFiBerry DAC+ Pro on a Raspberry Pi 4 (MPD 0.24.4): DSD64, DSD128 and DSD256 reach the card as 32-bit PCM at 352.8 kHz, MPD taking 3.9, 37.4 and 68.7 % of one core, against 3.0, 36.6 and 65.1 % with the conversion in its output; PCM goes out in 32-bit words, its values unchanged at 100 %. Whether a card takes DSD is read without opening it — a USB DAC's formats as Linux lists them; a computer's built-in sound, and a Raspberry Pi's HAT on its I2S, jack and HDMI, take none —, and a card that is not known, or not there, changes nothing. The line follows the outputs: written or taken out when the guided setup writes MPD's file, when the DoP switch moves — MPD then restarts, since it reads that line only when it starts — and when a box with one output is given another card. Config → MPD → Guided says so under DSD. GET /audio-stack/status gives the mpd entry its volume_on_dsd; the player state and its sources[] entries carry volume_on_dsd for a DSD track.

  • [ops] installer — boxes already set up get it at their update, and a box given a DAC that takes DSD since loses it. Only in a configuration the guided setup wrote, once the DoP alias is taken out; the previous file is kept among the configuration editor's saved copies, and MPD is restarted. A USB DAC switched off during the update leaves the file as it is.

  • [manual] Listening, Outputs & engines and the glossary (DSD) say the volume works on DSD on a box whose DAC does not take DSD, and that turning on DoP there restarts MPD.

Changed

  • [ui] Stats says when recording is off. The switch sat at the bottom of the view, under the rankings: with it off, Stats simply stopped growing and nothing said why. A line at the top now says Recording is off: nothing new is recorded, with a button that takes you to the switch. Its words are in the text colour on the warning tint — the warning's own text colour read 4.35:1 there in Slate light; 12:1 to 19:1 now, measured in the six palettes.
  • [ui] In the tab menu, a Library tab sent to a new line starts it at the left. With six tabs, the sixth took the menu's whole width alone under the five others. The page's own Library bar is unchanged: it scrolls.
  • [ui] A switch names what it turns on to screen readers, and a filter bar says which option is chosen. The switch's checkbox had no name of its own; the filter buttons said their choice by colour only (aria-pressed).
  • [core] During DSD, the player's volume is left alone, and the DAC's own volume is what goes to full. Until now a DSD track set MPD's volume to 100 — and that of every other source of the box — then the DAC's, and put them back when it ended. Measured with a DSD128 file on MPD 0.24.5: MPD's volume changes nothing to DSD, sent as DSD, as DoP or converted to PCM, so it now stays where it was; a DAC's own volume does — a USB DAC lowered by 10 dB played DoP as silence and native DSD as noise. The DAC goes to full only while it receives DSD: when the DSD reaches it converted to PCM, its volume is given back as soon as the player shows Converted to PCM, and left alone the next time the same player sends it DSD at that rate — or from the first second, when MPD's configuration converts DSD before its volume. Lowered while DSD plays, paused included, it goes back to full at once: while it holds the DAC, the box listens to the sound card's events rather than reading its volume every second — which it still does when it cannot listen — and it stops listening when the DSD ends.
  • [core] The DAC set to full is the one the DSD plays on. It was the first sound card playing, whatever held it, and the box's first card when none was playing yet. With several DACs playing, it is now the one held by the player that sends the DSD; none is touched when that cannot be told.
  • [ui] The DSD padlock tells screen readers that the volume has no effect on DSD, and to set the level on the amplifier — it said the volume was locked at 100 %.
  • [site] The editions page says DSD protection, and the landing's comparison DSD volume protection — they read DSD bit-perfect and Per-source bit-perfect lock (DSD volume protection); the other products' cells are unchanged.
  • [site] The DSD and bit-perfect guides say what the volume does to DSD, with the measurements above, and how to turn off the DAC's full volume.
  • [site] The DSD and bit-perfect guides say when MPD converts DSD before its volume, with the HiFiBerry's measurements, and that MPD then works in 32-bit words: S32_LE on a card that takes them, the values unchanged at 100 %.
  • [core] The player's volume covers the range set in HQPlayer. While HQPlayer plays through the box, the player's slider ran from −60 to 0 dB whatever HQPlayer's settings: on an HQPlayer whose Volume max is −3 dB, the top of the slider did nothing and −6 dB read 90. It now runs over HQPlayer's own range, read with its status on the same connection, and over −60 to 0 dB for an HQPlayer that does not say one; it is not shown while HQPlayer's volume is fixed.
  • [core] PUT /hqplayer/volume keeps the volume within HQPlayer's range, and answers the volume it sent. It refused anything outside −60 to 0 dB, which a range set in HQPlayer may pass; it now brings a value past either end back to it, and to −60..0 dB while HQPlayer has not said its range.
  • [ui] The HQPlayer card shows the output mode and rate beside the name as soon as it opens. They appeared once the DSP menu had been opened.
  • [core] shairport-sync's settings are read with libconf instead of pylibconfig2. The values read are the same — compared on the guided setup's file, on two boxes' files and on shairport-sync's own sample with every setting switched on — and they now keep the file's order. A 64-bit value written with its L, such as airplay_device_id, no longer makes the file unreadable. A setting named twice is still refused, as shairport-sync refuses it, and so is a line that includes another file, which is never opened. The third-party notices list libconf.

Fixed

  • [core] Saving AirPlay's settings in Structured mode writes a file shairport-sync can read. A value holding a quote, a backslash or a line break, a true/false setting and a list were written in forms that libconfig — the library shairport-sync reads its file with — refuses; they are now written in its syntax.
  • [ui] Config → Guided preselects the right output of a card that has several. The jack and HDMI of one card share its name: the output chosen for a service was matched without its device number, so the other one could be shown as chosen. The first-time setup matched the same way.
  • [manual] Listening no longer says the format readout shows exactly what reaches the DAC: it shows the track's format, and for DSD how it reaches the DAC. The glossary no longer says the DSD lock shows for native DSD only, nor does the padlock's description for screen readers.
  • [core] DSD goes out as DSD when the DAC accepts it, and as PCM otherwise — no longer as DoP. The guided setup wrote dsd_usb "yes" beside dop "no", meant as native DSD; MPD still reads dsd_usb as the old name of dop, so DSD left wrapped in PCM (DoP). Measured with a DSD128 file on MPD 0.24.5: the DAC received PCM at 352.8 kHz with that line, and native DSD at 176.4 kHz without it. A DAC that cannot decode DoP received PCM full of markers, with the volume held at 100. The setup no longer writes the line.
  • [ops] installer — boxes already set up lose that line at their update. Only in a configuration the guided setup wrote, and only where the line sits beside dop "no": an output that asks for DoP itself, and a file written by hand, are left as they are. The previous file is kept among the configuration editor's saved copies, where it can be restored, and MPD is restarted so that it reads the corrected one.
  • [core] Closing a subscription the event bus had dropped as too slow no longer raises.
  • [site] The landing says what Starter and Pro each bring. The configuration card counted Roon among what Starter edits — Roon's settings come with Pro, as HQPlayer's do. The library card said the music on the box was only browsable on Starter: Starter browses, searches and queues it, and casts it to a renderer. The FAQ now says that listening to a streaming service, and connecting one, come with Pro, and the introduction to the features names what Pro adds: the streaming services, the media servers, Roon and HQPlayer.
  • [manual] The manual says what comes with Pro wherever it sends you there. Roon's settings come with Pro, alongside HQPlayer Embedded's and the NAA's (Introduction, First run, Administration, and the editions page). Troubleshooting marks the tools it suggests from the Systemd and Performance tabs as Pro, and Administration says the 30-day trial is the time to tune the box — the Systemd overrides, the disk and network counting among them, stay in force once it ends. The FAQ says connecting a streaming service comes with Pro, Listening describes the padlock a streaming track wears in the queue on Starter, and Outputs & engines marks the Pipeline tab as Pro where it first describes it.
  • [site] The landing's comparison table matches each vendor's own pages, checked on 17 October 2026. The streaming row no longer says "official APIs": Audiogravity is a partner of none of the three services. Roon offers Tidal and Qobuz, not HIGHRESAUDIO; HQPlayer streams Qobuz and HIGHRESAUDIO, and JPLAY all three; moOde offers Qobuz only, through upmpdcli, and has not browsed UPnP media servers since 2022; Volumio's Premium needs an account; HQPlayer runs on Windows, macOS and Linux and costs €319 for version 6, not €265; JPLAY runs on Apple-silicon Macs too; Volumio's monthly price is added.
  • [site] The guides describe the box as Audiogravity installs it. Settings made by hand on the test box — a shorter audio buffer, processor cores reserved at boot, its own music share — no longer read as if they were the reader's: the buffer is the one the configuration Audiogravity writes leaves to MPD, and a measurement says where it was taken and what it does not represent. The HAT guide now says to edit the two audio lines Raspberry Pi OS already has, rather than add two new ones beside them.
  • [site] A guide's title carrying the brand reads in one piece in the sidebar. Audiogravity, Volumio or moOde: which to choose? was split into three pieces set side by side like columns; every title of the manual's and the guides' sidebars is now one block.
  • [site] The landing no longer promises CPU cores reserved for the player. Audiogravity pins each audio service to the cores you choose for it; it does not keep the other programs off them. The card and its Dedicated cores line now say so: "on the cores you choose for it".
  • [manual] The manual no longer says the guided setup's configuration does no volume math. First run said the configurations send audio to the DAC "untouched — no resampling, no volume math in the digital domain", and the glossary that bit-perfect means "no software volume": the volume Audiogravity sets is MPD's software volume, which recalculates every sample below 100. Both now say MPD's configuration is bit-perfect with the volume at 100 — a 16-bit file padded to 24 or 32 bits, its values unchanged — except on a DAC that accepts 16 bits only, and that AirPlay is not bit-perfect: it adds or drops a sample now and then to stay in time with the sender. Quick start, Introduction and Outputs & engines say bit-perfect with the volume at 100.
  • [site] The landing's configuration card and the README say the same: bit-perfect for MPD at full volume. The README's Bit-perfect lock with DSD volume protection now reads DSD protection — your DAC's own volume at full while it receives DSD, from any player.
  • [site] The bit-perfect guide says what MPD's volume does towards a DAC that accepts 16 bits only. MPD widens 16-bit audio to 24 bits for its volume, then brings it back to 16 bits with dither for such a DAC: measured on MPD 0.24.5 (2026-10-18), half the samples of a 3-second test came out changed, by up to 3 steps, at 100 % too.
  • [ui] The HQPlayer card no longer keeps what it read from the HQPlayer it showed before. When the box's own HQPlayer started or stopped, the card showed the other instance with the previous one's filter, shaper and mode lists, and a filter is sent as a position in the list: one picked there could reach the new HQPlayer as another filter (the Mac's Desktop 5 and the x86 box's Embedded 6.1 name different filters at 54 of the 70 positions they share).
  • [core] A filter, shaper, mode or volume changed on the HQPlayer card shows at once: the status read back right after could be the one kept from before the change, for up to 2 seconds.
  • [site] The third-party notices list what the core's binary carries besides its Python packages. The Python interpreter, compiled with the core, whose licence asks for its text to go with it; OpenSSL, libffi, Expat, Zstandard, libuuid, bzip2 and XZ's liblzma, copied from the machine the binary is built on; the runtime of Nuitka, the compiler, under AGPL-3.0 with the exception that lets a program compiled with it be distributed under its author's terms; and SQLite, which the binary carries from this version for the listening history. Each comes with its copyright and licence, read from the official sources of the versions the binary carries.

[0.9.73] - 2026-10-16

Added

  • [core+ui] On a Starter box, the queue shows which tracks will not play. The streaming services' tracks left in the queue — Qobuz, Tidal, HIGHRESAUDIO, queued during the trial — wear the Pro tint and a padlock in place of their length, in Library → Queue (the track playing included) and on the full-screen player's Next, a network speaker's too; a tap on the padlock opens the licence panel, and the track can still be removed. The core marks each queued track from its address — a station saved with a streaming relay's address is marked as well: pro on /library/queue, on the player state's queue_next and on a renderer's status (queue_next_pro).
  • [core+ui] Play says why when only streaming tracks are left in the queue. On a Starter box, Play on such a queue did nothing and said nothing: the box's player skipped every track and stopped, a network speaker replayed a refused one. It is now refused with Only streaming tracks are left in the queue…, shown by the full-screen player; the box's queue is judged whole, a speaker's from the track it is on. A queue holding anything else plays as before, the streaming tracks skipped. Measured on MPD 0.24.5, and on a UPnP renderer (upmpdcli) driven by the box.

Changed

  • [core+ui] The free Starter edition plays the music on the box and the internet radio. Starter opened the box's administration only, and playing anything needed Pro. It now has Library's local library — browse, search, queue — and Radio, the choice of output — the box's DAC or a network speaker —, the mini-player's buttons and the full-screen player with its sleep timer. Pro adds the streaming services (Qobuz, Tidal, HIGHRESAUDIO), the UPnP media servers, Roon and HQPlayer — as the output, its settings, HQPlayer Embedded's and the NAA's output and file — and keeps the Pipeline, systemd tuning and Performance. On a Starter box what Pro adds stays in view behind a padlock — a tab, a Library source and its chip in Search, a Config tile, the outputs of a Roon or HQPlayer source — and a tap takes you to the licence panel, with the activation window open.
  • [core] The box refuses what Pro opens to a box without it, whoever asks. The padlocks were in the interface alone: a request that skipped it got through, and Library, the players and HQPlayer refused nothing. The core now answers 403 to the Pro routes and to a Pro source, whether the request names it with or without its src_ prefix; to a player command that reaches Roon or HQPlayer, judged on the device it reaches; to the address of a streaming service's track handed over as a file, a radio station or a speaker's track; to HQPlayer's and Roon's output, and to HQPlayer Embedded's and the NAA's file. The relays of the streaming services refuse too: a track queued while the box had Pro no longer plays once it has not — on the box's player, on a network speaker, or sent by another app — and the box's player skips it.
  • [core] A Starter box never sends anything to HQPlayer. HQPlayer chosen as the output stays chosen for when the box has Pro again; meanwhile the music plays on the box's own player. While the box's own HQPlayer Embedded runs and holds the DAC, a play from Library, from Radio or from the player's Play button is refused with what to do: stop HQPlayer Embedded from Services, or activate a license.
  • [ui] What Pro locks wears one tint, from the theme's brand colour. The locked tabs wore a rosy tint of the error colour, all but invisible on a near-black bar (1.07:1 in Minimal and Gravity dark) — and red marks an error. The locked tabs, the Pro sources' cards and chips, the outputs of a Pro source and Config's HQPlayer tiles now take the theme's brand colour at 8 % in light, 16 % in dark: 1.10 to 1.47:1 against their ground, measured in Chromium in the three themes. In Search, a chip's padlock no longer touches its name.
  • [core+ui] A play refused for want of Pro is said where it was asked. The full-screen player's Play button kept such a refusal silent, as it does a device that declines, and the Radio screen showed one neutral sentence for every failure. Both now show the box's own words — HQPlayer Embedded holding the DAC, a station that plays a streaming service's relay, a queue left with streaming tracks only — which the core answers as every refusal for want of Pro, with 403 and a mention of the licence, so the padlocks follow. A device that declines stays as it was: the button simply does not move. Only the Play button shows them: a volume drag sends one command per movement.
  • [manual] Three figures show what a Starter box sees. The tab menu with its badge and the padlocks of Systemd, Performance and Pipeline (First run); the Library's sources, the Pro ones behind their padlocks (Introduction, under the editions); the licence panel once the trial has ended (Administration, between the trial and an activated licence). Taken on 2026-10-15 from the live interface, at the same iPhone 13 mini size as the others.

Fixed

  • [core+ui] A box reporting tampered no longer shows the Pro tabs open. The interface kept its own list of the statuses that open Pro, and it disagreed with the core's: a tampered box showed every Pro tab open while the core refused them all. The core now publishes the verdict — pro on /license/status, on each source, on the player state and on each Config service — and the interface reads it.
  • [core] A licence with no end date is no longer checked against the clock. The check meant for a trial and a licence with an end date ran for a perpetual one too, and its verdict is kept an hour: a box starting far off the right time — a Raspberry Pi has no battery-backed clock — could lock its paying owner out of Pro.
