A service's details no longer start systemctl for the control group of a stopped service, nor to ask whether a missing service starts at boot, and a disabled service read through that fallback reads disabled, no longer unknown.

  • [core][ops] The network view no longer starts programs, and asks the WiFi without sudo wherever it can. While a screen follows the audio chain, the box's network interfaces were read again every minute: ip was started for each of them, and sudo iw for the WiFi, whose three lines a minute went to the system journal and the core's log screen. The addresses are now read from the kernel, a WiFi that is not connected is not asked at all, and iw is first asked without sudo: on both Raspberry Pi test boxes it answers in full that way, for 2 ms of CPU where sudo took 24 (measured). Only a WiFi chip that refuses — the x86 test box's — gets the sudo call, and that call no longer writes to the journal. Screens and requests arriving together share one reading, and the support report reads the WiFi the same way.

  • [site][manual][ops] The requirements say what Audiogravity runs on, and the core installer turns down the rest. The landing, the README, EDITIONS and the manual promised Debian / Ubuntu — any recent kernel. The core is built against Debian 13's C library and needs glibc 2.38 or later — Debian 12 has 2.36, Ubuntu 22.04 2.35 — so the install went to its end there, and the core did not start (measured on the 0.9.63 binaries, x86_64 and aarch64). And installing audio software from the app looks the vendors' builds up by Debian release, which an Ubuntu box is not. They now say Debian 13 (Trixie) or later, DietPi and Raspberry Pi OS included in their Trixie release. The core installer checks both before it changes anything, and says why it stops; a box already installed on another distribution is warned, and keeps its updates.

  • [core] The Services tab's figures no longer start a program, nor make systemd load the stopped services. While a screen is open, the figures of the services Audiogravity follows — CPU, memory, disk, network — were read every 10 seconds, every 2 while one of them works hard, by starting systemctl show for the eight of them, and systemd loaded every stopped one to answer: 46.7 ms of CPU each time on the x86 test box, 72.8 ms on the ARM test box (measured). They are now asked over D-Bus — the states in one question that loads nothing, then the figures of the running services only: 9.6 and 23.4 ms (measured). The tab shows what it showed — same states, same figures, same dashes, compared service by service on both boxes. When D-Bus does not answer, systemctl takes over, as before.

  • [core] AirPlay and UPnP are no longer asked to describe themselves before every question. Each question to AirPlay or to a player that speaks MPRIS — what it plays, every second while the music plays with the audio chain on screen, or a command such as play, pause or volume — started by asking it for the description of all its interfaces, and reading it: 4.3 ms of CPU per question to a running AirPlay on the ARM test box. The description is now kept, and asked again only when the player stops answering: 1.45 ms (measured). And a player of the box that systemd says is stopped — AirPlay, MPD — is no longer asked what it plays: a stopped AirPlay answered every question with an error, 0.65 ms each on both test boxes (measured).

  • [core] The topology editor refuses what the box would leave out. Its check let anything through in a device's services, connection, network interfaces and input ports, and in the outputs Audiogravity pins for each service: a number among a service's controls was saved, then stopped the drawing of the whole chain. The editor and the box now read the file through one model: a value of another type anywhere in it is an error that blocks the save, as it already was in a device's outputs. Numbers and yes/no are read as before — "1" is the number 1, "true" a yes.

Fixed

  • [ui] The latency test's result said milliseconds for microseconds. Its four figures — minimum, average and maximum delay, standard deviation — were labelled (µs) in capitals, and the capital of µ is the Greek Μ, which reads as an M: MAX LATENCY (MS) over a value in microseconds made a 2 ms spike read as 2 seconds. The unit now follows the value, as it does for the percentiles below, and a test fails on a stat box that puts µ in its label.

  • [core] What runs in the terminal ends with it, a command ignoring the hang-up included. Closing the terminal sent SIGHUP to its shell and to everything it ran, and stopped there as soon as the shell had left: a command started with nohup, or under trap '' HUP, kept running after the terminal was gone. Whatever is left after the grace period is now killed, whether the shell has gone or not, and a close cut short — the core stopping with a terminal open — kills what is left at once instead of leaving it. Still out of reach: a command that leaves the terminal's process group (setsid, a daemon, tmux, or sudo running its command in a session of its own).

  • [core][ops] The secrets kept on the box are readable by Audiogravity's account alone. The Tidal, Qobuz and HIGHRESAUDIO accounts, the Roon token, the licence and the push notifications' private key were written readable by every account on the box (measured on the x86 box and the ARM box), and so was the accounts file the installer creates. The core now writes them for its own account only, each through a temporary file of its own: the one writer of secrets used a fixed temporary name, and a leftover readable by all passed its mode on to the secret. The installer creates the push key and the accounts file that way, and at every update narrows the copies earlier versions left, whether their module is enabled or not — each change made as the service account, so that a file swapped for a link cannot lead it to a system file. And the core narrows them again each time it starts: the manual's backup is restored with tar, which puts each file back with the mode it was archived with, and an archive made before this version brought the accounts file back readable by every account until the next update.

  • [core] The Roon token and the licence are replaced whole. Both were written straight over the previous file, so a write that failed half-way left a truncated one — a licence then read as unreadable, a token as none. They now replace the previous file in one step, as the other settings do, and the log no longer says token saved when it was not.

  • [core] Backups of a configuration no longer pile up. Every save of the audio topology left one more dated copy beside it, and nothing removed them — nineteen on a test box. The ten most recent are kept, the one just taken always among them, even on a box whose clock runs behind until it reaches the network.

  • [core] Each HQPlayer keeps its own DSP settings. The filter, noise shaper and mode kept for one HQPlayer were sent to the next one the HQPlayer card chose, as positions in HQPlayer's lists — which differ from one HQPlayer to another: 54 of the 70 filter numbers an HQPlayer Desktop 5 and an HQPlayer Embedded 6.1 share name different filters (measured), so the new one played a filter nobody had picked, with no word. Each HQPlayer now keeps its own: one met for the first time starts from the settings it has, one chosen again gets its own back, and a setting that answers after the card has changed HQPlayer is kept for the one that took it. Reset to HQPlayer defaults concerns the chosen one only; the last ten HQPlayers are remembered. API: DELETE /hqplayer/dsp forgets the chosen HQPlayer's settings only.

  • [core] Service-down and critical-temperature notifications reach a device with the app closed. Both were raised by the watch that feeds an open screen, which runs only while the app is open somewhere: closed everywhere — the moment a notification is for — a service that failed or a CPU that overheated went unannounced. A watch of their own now checks the services every 30 s and the temperature every minute, whatever the screens do, and only while a device is subscribed to notifications: a box nobody subscribed from reads nothing. A service counts as down as the Services tab shows it, one that systemd keeps restarting after a crash included, even when a reading caught it between two restarts; a service stopped on purpose does not count, nor services stopped by a shutdown or a reboot of the box. A service that stays down is announced once, one that keeps failing once per quarter of an hour at most, and a failure that happened while nobody could be told is not announced as new. The critical-temperature alert could not go out in the first quarter of an hour after the box started. The services' states are read without starting any program, in one call to systemd that makes it load none of them: 0.6 ms for 8 services on the x86 box (measured).

  • [ui] An unknown figure of the top bar is a plain dash. Before the first reading, and for good on a machine without a temperature sensor, the temperature showed --°C on the red, pulsing background of a critical one.

  • [ui] The tabs, the logs and the Audio Software badges follow the Animations switch. Switched back on, the tabs' bell, the logs' LIVE badge and Audio Software's update badges stayed still until the page was reloaded: the tabs and the logs waited for a notice nothing had sent since 0.9.4, and the Audio Software page read the setting only when it redrew.

  • [core] The DSD volume protection keeps running once the app is closed. It is started with the box so that a DSD track gets its volume set to 100 even with no screen open, and it stopped with the last screen that closed the player — MPD's own listener, which catches a DSD file the moment it starts, included. From the first time the app had been opened and closed, nothing set the volume of a DSD track to 100 any more. It now runs for good. Tried on the x86 test box: after a screen had opened and closed, a DSD128 file started from MPD's queue took the volume from 24 to 100 within a second, and the radio played after it got its 24 back.

  • [core] The sleep timer checks what plays when it fires. It trusted the last state the player had published, which could be hours old once the app was closed: a toggle on a stale playing would start music paused since, instead of pausing it. It now reads the player's state at that moment, and falls back on the last one only when that read fails.

  • [core] A service that crashes and is restarted at once is announced. The service-down notification read the services every 30 seconds, and systemd restarts a crashed audio service within 0.1 to 5 seconds (restart settings read on the x86 and the ARM test box): both readings found it running, and the crash went unannounced. Audiogravity now listens to systemd, which tells each change as it happens: on a service crashing every four seconds, the notification went out at the moment of the first crash, on both boxes (measured), and once per quarter of an hour after that, as before. Listening costs nothing while nothing changes, and happens only while a device is subscribed to notifications; the 30-second check no longer asks systemd anything.

  • [core] A WiFi network's name shows as it is written. iw prints every character beyond plain ASCII as a code, so a network named Café showed as Caf\xc3\xa9 in the network view and the support report — and the report also cut every name at its first space. Both show the whole name now.

  • [core] A topology edited by hand no longer loses the whole audio chain. One value of a type Audiogravity does not expect — a device or a service written as a bare name, a number among a service's controls, a text where a connection is expected, a value left empty (null) such as a service's label — stopped the drawing of the whole chain, and the player lost its signal path, with nothing on screen to say why. The file is now checked against the topology editor's own model when it is read: each such value is left out, the core's log names it once, and the rest is drawn. The output switching and the guided setup read the file the same way.

  • [core] Choosing an output no longer wipes a topology that cannot be read. When audio-topology.json had been broken by a hand edit — a comma left out, in a terminal —, choosing an output in the guided setup or in Guided mode wrote it over with the outputs alone: the chain described in it was gone, kept only in a dated copy beside it that no screen brings back. The file is now left as it is: the service changes output all the same, the choice is not recorded in the topology, and the core's log says why. A file holding no JSON object — a list, say — made the save fail after the output had changed, so the screen called a change that had happened a failure; it is left as it is too.

  • [core] A topology saved from the editor reaches the output switching at once. The output switching read the topology when the core started, and only then: an output renamed or added in the editor stayed unknown to it until the next restart. The drawing of the audio chain caught up with a save from the editor, but not with a file edited by hand. The core now holds one copy of the file for all its readers, read again as soon as the file changes, whoever changed it; a file caught half written leaves the last good copy in use. Asking the file whether it changed takes 2.6 µs on the x86 test box (measured): about 34 µs a second while music plays with the app open.

Removed

  • [ui] The radio card and the player's cover no longer answer the keyboard. Audiogravity is used by touch, mostly on a phone, and neither can become a button: the card holds three buttons of its own, and the swipe that removes a station does not start on a button; the cover holds the album details. The keyboard code that stood in for a button is gone. Nothing changes by touch or with a mouse, and a screen reader still announces them as buttons.

[0.9.63] - 2026-09-28

Changed

  • [core][ui][manual] Restore Backup and Remove Override apply the settings they put back. They rewrote a service's settings and stopped there: the service went on running on the settings being undone — those someone reaching for these buttons most wants gone — until its next start. They now apply them as a save does: a running service is restarted on them and watched; if it does not start, it gets back the settings it was running on and is restarted on those, and the message says so; a service already restarting in a loop keeps them and is stopped; a stopped service stays stopped. Remove Override now says when systemd refuses to reload, where it answered success. Like a save, both are refused on Audiogravity's own service while software is being installed. The confirmation says a running service is restarted, and the message shown afterwards is the core's. API: both routes answer { success, message, service, service_restarted }, 400 with the reason when the service did not start, and 409 on the core's own unit during an install.
  • [ui][site] The pipeline's green badge reads FULL RES. It read BIT-PERFECT, but what it checks is that the connection can carry the format sent to the card, not that nothing upstream altered the audio. The graph's bubble and legend, the Editions window, EDITIONS.md and the manual say FULL RES too.
  • [ui] The INFO buttons are gone. Thirteen tabs opened a window of their own explanations, most unchanged since the first version: of about 115 sentences, some 55 were at least partly wrong and 9 plainly false (checked against the code on 2026-09-28), and nine windows repeated the manual — errors included. The manual, corrected, is the one explanation.
  • [ui] The interface shows no tooltips. They are removed, every one: a button with an icon alone is named for screen readers instead, and what needs reading is on the screen — the reason the role and the enabled state of an account cannot change is written under them, in the account window. A test fails when a title comes back. Our components that had a title (windows, panels, tiles, rows) read it from a heading attribute: from title, the browser showed it as a tooltip over the whole component. A window is still named by its title for screen readers.

Fixed

  • [core][ui][ops] Sessions end when the account behind them changes. A token was checked for its signature and expiry alone: a new password, a new role, a disabled or deleted account left every open session working for up to 12 hours. The account is now read back at every request that carries a token, whatever the route — a stat of the users file, and 0.1 to 0.2 ms (measured on the x86 box and the ARM build box) — and the token refused as soon as the account no longer stands behind it: most routes asked for the API key alone, so an ended session went on working from its tab, as no one in particular; an account deleted and created again under the same name no longer takes back the old account's tokens, nor a token the role it was issued under — the built-in admin included, when the manual's recovery has the installer create it again. An open terminal checks its session every 15 s and closes once it has ended; whenever it closes, what runs in it now stops too — an interactive shell ignored the signal it was sent, and a command running in it went on after the terminal was gone. An admin who changes their own password stays signed in on the device they changed it from; the other sessions end. In the interface, a refused session sends to the sign-in page at its next request — for the page's requests, its uploads and its passkey registration alike — but only when the token refused is still the one the tab keeps: a tab whose token was replaced meanwhile, by itself or by another tab, carries on with the new one, its role as it now is, and its live updates reconnect with it; a tab never takes up, overwrites or erases the session of another account signed in the same browser. The terminal says its session ended, rather than asking to sign in. Tokens issued before the update keep working until something changes on their account. API: PATCH /auth/users/{username} returns access_token when the change ends the caller's own sessions; an ended session answers 401 with WWW-Authenticate: Bearer.
  • [core][ui] Nobody can disable their own account or change their own role, and the built-in admin keeps its role. The core refused only to disable a protected account: an admin could disable or demote themselves by the account window's own save, and demote the built-in admin. Both are refused now, with the reason; the account window greys the fields out and says why, and the status badge of your own card is no longer a button. API: PATCH /auth/users/{username} answers 400 for them.
  • [core] A guest controls what is playing, and nothing else. Many actions were open to a guest account: HQPlayer's filter, shaper, mode, connection and output, signing in to and out of Qobuz, Tidal and HIGHRESAUDIO, the radio library and its favourites, playing a station, switching the MPD output, restoring a service's systemd settings, the licence's upload, check and activation, starting the metrics and log streams. They are closed to guests; what is playing stays theirs to control — play and pause, skip, seek, volume, repeat, shuffle and the sleep timer, on the box, a network speaker (which it could not, before) or HQPlayer. A test now reads every route of every module and fails on a write route that says nothing about who may call it.
  • [core] An idle terminal no longer sends twenty empty messages a second. The terminal forwarded each empty read of its shell's output, one every 50 ms (measured on 2026-09-28): it now sends only what the shell writes.
  • [ui] Restart Core and Reboot OS reconnect by themselves again. Waiting for the core to come back, the page asked /health at the interface's address — a page the interface does not serve, answered 404 on an installed box — so it waited to the end of its countdown. It now asks the core.
  • [ui] A service's detail window stays live. It showed the service as it was when opened, while its metrics moved on around it. It now follows the service's current state.
  • [core][ui] A failed install, update or removal shows as such on its card. The core recorded the failure and then published the package's state read from the system, so the card never showed ERROR, and its REPAIR button — offered to a required package that failed while installed — could not appear. A failure now stays shown until the next operation, or until the version on the box changes (the package was put right from a terminal); the card keeps saying a service is not configured, or has no web password, when the previous version is still installed, and the INSTALLED filter keeps it. API: status can now be error in /packages/* and in the package_state event.
  • [core][ui] The THROTTLED badge of the Performance tab appears. Each CPU card compared the throttle counters with its own previous reading, and the tab rebuilds its cards on every reload: there never was a previous reading. The core now compares them at each tick of its monitoring, forgetting the last tick of an earlier run, and hands the answer out with the metrics. A Raspberry Pi exposes no counter; its under-voltage alarm marks every core instead (tried on the ARM build box under load on 2026-09-28: alarm raised with the kernel's Undervoltage detected!), and a quiet alarm says nothing, since the Pi also slows down for heat without reporting it. API: the sysinfo event carries cpu_throttled, null when nothing was measured.
  • [core][ui] The latency test runs where it is told, and its figures are measured. The CPU affinity was sent as cpu_affinity while the core reads affinity: it was dropped, and the test never ran on the cores asked for. The percentiles are now read from the histogram rather than estimated, and the progress counts every thread. The form stays within what the core accepts (1–16 threads, 1–99 priority, at least 1 000 loops) and names a setting out of range before anything is sent. The test's title read LATENCE TEST.
  • [ui] Apply All applies the governor last chosen. It applied the one CPU 0 had at the last reload of the tab.
  • [ui] Three colours were missing. The NOT INSTALLED badge of Audio Software, the state badges of the Config tiles and the added and removed lines of the raw diff had no colour: their CSS rules named classes the interface did not write.
  • [ui] The licence panel's portal links accept only web addresses. The License portal and Download .lic links took the address the licence server gave as it came, a javascript: one included.
  • [ui][site] The Editions window says what each edition holds. It classed the configuration editor in Pro, where the code and EDITIONS.md put it in Starter, and it now matches EDITIONS.md, itself corrected: push notifications are sent for four events, not for "playback events"; choosing the output belongs to Pro; the configuration check is a basic syntax check; the DSD volume protection, which every edition runs, is listed in Starter.
  • [ui] Buttons that are not buttons answer the keyboard. A clickable badge, the icons of the Audio Software and Services titles, the API icon of the Settings panel, the radio card, the player's cover and source dots and the pull tab could only be clicked: they are now reached with Tab and pressed with Enter or Space — on the element itself, so a key pressed on a radio card's own buttons still does what that button does. A test fails on an element announced as a button that does not answer the keyboard.
  • [manual] The manual describes the app as it is. Every sentence was checked against the code on 2026-09-28. Among the corrections: what each role can do, and that changing an account's password, role or state signs it out everywhere; that a session lasts 12 hours, the installed app included; the Config editor, whose guided mode and CONFIGURED badges are for admins; Audio Software; a rewritten Performance chapter, with the latency and network tests; Systemd, whose Validate button does not exist; the licence; the Pipeline graph, its colours and commands; the profiles, whose switch is not undone when a service fails, and starts nothing when an essential one cannot be stopped; and a Roon queue, which is changed from the Roon app. The sentence saying every tab has an INFO badge is gone with the badges, and the seven figures that showed one are retaken without it: the Audio Software, Config, Services and Audio Pipeline tabs, the network test and both licence panels — the Config tiles with their state in colour.

Removed

  • [ui][manual] The cockpit's LOW POWER badge and Device Battery tile are gone. LOW POWER turned the app's animations off, refreshed its live figures half as often and slowed two of its checks, on the device showing the app — the box never depended on it. It came on by itself from the battery level in Chromium browsers only, and after a switch by hand it took three taps to turn off. The Animations setting already turns animations off. The battery tile existed for it, and the app no longer reads the battery at all. The cockpit's timer table loses its Effective column, which only differed from the interval under LOW POWER.

[0.9.62] - 2026-09-27

Added

  • [core][ops][manual] A command applies a change made by hand to the audio services and profiles. Between two versions, support may still have to change a box's profiles — to try one, or to fix one at a customer's. Over SSH, copy /etc/audiogravity/audio-config.json, edit the copy, then run sudo /opt/audiogravity/ag-apply-audio-config <copy>: the core checks it with the very rules it loads the file with, and a file it would not load is refused, entry by entry with the reason, with nothing changed; a file it would load takes the file's place in one step, the core is restarted, and the command waits until it runs the profiles. --check checks without applying. A copy, as visudo works: parts of the core re-read the file as soon as it changes, before any check; should the core not start on the copy, the previous file is put back. On a box, the command only reads and replaces /etc/audiogravity/audio-config.json, whatever the core's .env names. The change lasts until the installer runs again — the next update, or a reinstall — which puts the shipped file back and keeps the replaced one as audio-config.json.previous. The manual's troubleshooting chapter has a new entry, Changing the profiles between two versions. The command runs as root and reads files the audio service's account may write: it refuses a symbolic link, and never goes through a proxy — its requests carry the API key.
  • [site][ui] The manual's commands are coloured, and a button copies each one. In the manual — on audiogravity.app and in the app's Manual window — the command, its options, quoted text, variables and comments each take a colour, as JSON keys and values do, and every code block carries a button that copies it, turning to a check mark once done. The copy leaves without its final newline, so the last line waits for Enter. A block holding values to replace — an address, a password, the example NAS share — has no button, and the manual says so; the NAS mount is now one step per block, and one variant per block. The site and the app colour a block identically, byte for byte, which a test checks on the whole manual.
  • [site] Who I am shows the system it names. A photo of the author's listening system, its Naim logo lit green, stands beside the note, taking a third of its width, the words centred against it in the other two; on a phone it heads the note. The file carries no metadata, so no location.

Changed

  • [core] One set of rules reads audio-config.json, and it refuses what used to slip through. The core loaded the file with one model and checked it with another, stricter one, read by another parser: the standard one reads NaN, Infinity and a lone surrogate, which the loader refuses (measured on 2026-09-26), so a file could be approved, then dropped at the core's start with an empty Profiles page. Both now share one model and one parser. A key the model does not know is refused — stpo for stop used to leave a profile stopping nothing — except the keys of earlier versions, accepted and read by nothing (appconfigfile, depends_on, topology_file, added_by_upgrade); so is a service a profile both starts and stops, which left the profile partial for good. A missing configuration file of an installed service is now a warning: it says something about the box, not the file. API: POST /config_validation/validate reads its body as the core reads the file — what it cannot read answers valid: false with a json_invalid error — and reports the warnings above.

  • [core][ops] The audio services and profiles are delivered with each version. audio-config.json was copied once, at the first install, and afterwards only received what an update knew how to add: a box installed before a change to the shipped file never got it — the ARM build box kept an MPD profile that needed the UPnP bridge, disabled at boot, so its MPD tile could not be active after a restart. The installer now replaces the file with the one its version ships, at every install and update, as it does for the software registry. The file it replaces is kept beside it as audio-config.json.previous, with its date; a file already identical is left alone, and a self-update that rolls back puts both back as they were. A change made by hand therefore lasts until the installer runs again — the next update, or a reinstall. The copies the retired import and export left on the box are removed (/etc/audiogravity/audio-config.json.backup.*, /opt/audiogravity/core/audio-config-export-*.json). API: GET /profiles/configuration no longer returns added_by_upgrade. The manual's backup and restore no longer counts these files as yours, and leaves Audiogravity's own files out of a restore.

  • [core][manual] The profiles are named after what they run, and a profile is critical when it starts a critical service. Each name joins the software it starts — Roon Bridge + HQPlayer NAA, MPD + HQPlayer Embedded — and each description says what the profile is for: Roon plays directly to this box, Local library through HQPlayer, or MPD directly — one at a time; HQPlayer-Signalyst becomes HQPlayer NAA. A profile's criticality — the orange left edge of its tile, and the Critical Profile confirmation, This will stop other audio services — is derived from the services it starts, the NAA and HQPlayer Embedded being critical, instead of being written in the file, where it had drifted: Roon Bridge + MPD + HQPlayer NAA, which starts the NAA, now has it, and Stop All, which starts nothing, no longer does — it keeps an ordinary confirmation. A critical written for a profile in audio-config.json is ignored. The file opens with a line saying Audiogravity manages it, and how to change it: on a copy, applied by the support command.

  • [site] The landing's text comes in six sizes, and nothing on it is drawn under 11px. The stylesheet set 38 different font sizes, five of them under 9px, and 1,457 characters of the page were drawn at 9px — the tier badges, the chips, the section labels (measured on 2026-09-26, at 1440px). Text now takes one of six steps — 11, 13, 15, 17, 20 and 28px — and the big titles and figures four display sizes of their own, the fluid ones following the width of the screen; only the brand's ty, a command named in a sentence and the currency sign are sized from the word beside them. Labels and badges move from monospace to Inter; monospace is kept for what is read character by character, the install command and the configuration file. The open-source line under the pricing lead, an inline 0.9em at 70 % opacity, read at 3.1:1 on its grey (4.2:1 in the dark theme); it now takes a step of the scale and the lead's colour, at 6.1:1 (7.5:1). A test holds the scale, the fonts and the use of monospace: a new Landing workflow runs it on each push that touches the landing, and the Manual workflow now runs its own tests only, so a stylesheet regression no longer holds the manual back.

  • [site] The site serves its own fonts, and the online manual is drawn in Inter. Every visit fetched Inter and JetBrains Mono from Google Fonts, handing the visitor's IP address to Google. They now come from audiogravity.app itself — the same files, byte for byte, as the interface's, plus Inter's italic for the landing's titles — and the two faces of the first screen are preloaded. The manual's pages named Inter without ever loading it, so they were drawn in the system font; they now show Inter, like the landing. The licence texts travel with the fonts, and the third-party notices no longer say they come from a CDN.

  • [site] The pricing shows two editions, with the trial above them. Three tiers drew the 30-day trial as a third card at €0, beside the Starter Edition's €0, and left the Pro card last in the row, though the trial is a period every install goes through, not an edition to choose. The section now reads Two editions. Lifetime license.: a band over the Starter and Pro cards says every install starts with thirty days of Pro, and Editions / Plans opens on the same idea.

  • [site] The hero's kicker reads Now playing. The audiophile streamer · Now playing, where it said Live.

  • [site] The landing's carousel opens on HQPlayer. The first screen every visitor sees now shows UPnP feeding HQPlayer Embedded in DSD128, and the DAC receiving it, where it showed HIGHRESAUDIO; HIGHRESAUDIO, Qobuz, Tidal and the radio follow, each with its caption and annotations.

  • [manual] Fourteen figures show the interface as it is now. Most were taken on 2026-09-16, and the interface had moved since. Retaken on 2026-09-27, at the same iPhone 13 mini size: the Audio Software tab, where MPD has no Uninstall; the fullscreen player, the queue, the cast to a network speaker and the Qobuz grid, with the add-to-playlist button; the playback bar without repeat and shuffle, in the queue and the Pipeline; the stream-origin badge, no longer a pill; both HQPlayer cards; the HQPlayer Embedded install window, with its 30-minute notice; the Services charts, drawn from what was measured. The first-run panel and the network test are whole again, where the playback bar covered their end — the packet loss the text points to among them. Every playing figure is real playback, the busy output included: another player held the DAC, where that figure used to be staged. Five alternative texts name what their picture now shows.

  • [ui][ops][site] The app carries its manual, and reads it from the box. The Manual window fetched every chapter and figure from audiogravity.app: a box without internet access had no manual, and every box showed the website's latest edition rather than the one matching its version. Each build now copies the manual of the site repository into the app (ui/scripts/sync-manual.js) and the box serves it: the window asks audiogravity.app for nothing, and the page no longer accepts images from it. A chapter read once opens from the device's cache until the next version, offline included. A build stops when the manual is missing, and release.sh ui refuses a landing repository that is dirty or behind origin/main, so the copy is the release's. A fix to the manual published on the website reaches the app with the next version: the contents page, the FAQ and the landing's FAQ say so, where they promised a manual "always up to date" in the app.

  • [ui][site] The trademark notice appears once wherever the manual is read. It closed each of the thirteen Markdown files, and read as part of every chapter. It now sits in the footer of each chapter page on the website, under each chapter in the app, and in the contents page GitHub shows first. The app and the site's generator read its words from that page; the generator refuses a chapter that repeats it, and an app build refuses a manual whose contents page lost it.

  • [ui] The manual's figures are retaken from the live interface, beside the published ones. tools/manual-shots/ replaces tools/shoot-manual.cjs, which drew the figures from Storybook stories at 390×844 and double density and wrote them straight over the manual's images: none of the thirty published figures came from it any more, and running it would have replaced twenty of them. The new tool takes each figure from the dev instance in the manual's own format, an iPhone 13 mini at 375×812 and density 3, and writes nothing into the manual: a review page sets each new figure beside the published one, with the share of pixels that changed. What a box cannot be put into without consequence, a trial licence or an update on offer, is staged in the capturing browser only, and the licence panel's Device ID and Order ID are blurred. The figures taken from what plays now wait for --playback. A test fails when a figure of the manual has no recipe; the five recipes lost with the session that wrote them are written again.

Fixed

  • [ui][site] The manual's paragraphs and rules have their spacing back. In the app's Manual window and on the website, two paragraphs read as one and a rule sat on the text above it, since the manual was first published: both pages reset every margin, and the manual's styles gave paragraphs and rules none back. They now have their spacing, and the contents page's version line keeps its own.
  • [core] A mistake saved in audio-config.json no longer takes the audio services away from the Services tab. The list of units the tab may drive is read from that file, and re-read as soon as it changes: a file saved with a mistake left only Audiogravity's own units in it, so starting or stopping an audio service — and switching profiles — was refused until the file was corrected. It now keeps the last good read, as the services' monitoring already did.
  • [ops] An install cut short no longer risks leaving the software registry half-written. The installer copied packages-registry.json straight over the one in place, so a full disk or a power cut during the copy could leave a truncated file for the core to read. It is now written beside it, then renamed over it in one step — as audio-config.json is.
  • [site] The comparison table and Five layers drew every word at 16px. Both asked for sizes named in the interface's vocabulary, which this stylesheet never declared, so every word fell back to 16px, ever since the two blocks were added. On a phone, the table's header broke AUDIOGRAVITY over two lines. They now take the sizes they were written for, and the header holds on one line: 38px tall on a 390px phone, where it was 67.
  • [ui] Syntax colours are readable in every theme. The colours of the configuration editor, the event viewer of the System tab and now the manual's code blocks fell as low as 2.2:1 against their ground — numbers and options at 2.6:1 in Gravity's light theme — under the 4.5:1 the interface holds its text to; every theme and mode had some. Sixteen values are raised, and the check that guards the text colours now guards these too.
  • [ui] The HQPlayer card names a network HQPlayer Desktop, and its Forget button stays inside the card on a phone. The card of an HQPlayer on the network read HQPlayer 5.28.1, while the card of the one running on the box read HQPlayer Embedded 6.2.3 and the manual promised Desktop or Embedded: it now reads HQPlayer Desktop 5.28.1. While HQPlayer Embedded runs, the card offered Forget 10.0.4.200:4321 beside DSP and Web interface, and on a phone the button ran out of the card, which cut it off — from 320 to 430 px wide, 420 px of buttons for a card of 270 to 380 (measured on 2026-09-27), since Web interface arrived in 0.9.59. The button now reads Forget, the address staying in the sentence above it, and the buttons go to a second line when the card is too narrow. Web interface is renamed Web UI: with it the three buttons hold one row from 375 px wide, and the manual names it WEB UI.
  • [ui] Copying no longer says Copied when nothing was copied. On a box served over plain HTTP, the fallback that copies text reported a refusal by returning a value nobody read: the device ID in the licence panel, the logs and the support report showed their success message with the clipboard untouched. They now say the copy failed.
  • [manual] The fix for the memory dash on a Raspberry Pi works as written. Memory shows a dash for every service told the reader to remove cgroup_disable=memory from cmdline.txt — where it is not: the Pi adds it ahead of the file's contents — and offered cgroup_memory=1, which the kernel does not know. The entry now gives the procedure tried on a Pi on 2026-09-26: one command adds cgroup_enable=memory, which takes over, the file is checked, and only then does the box restart, in a block of its own. It also says how to undo it.
  • [site] The third-party notices gave ffmpeg the wrong licence. They listed it under the LGPL. The build Debian and Raspberry Pi OS ship — the one installed on the box — enables FFmpeg's optional GPL parts, and FFmpeg's licence then becomes the GPL 2.0 or later: the notice now says so, carries the GPL text, and names both of its uses, Tidal streaming and the conversion to FLAC for HQPlayer.

Removed

  • [core][ui][manual] Settings no longer exports or imports the audio configuration. audio-config.json — the audio services and the profiles built on them, behind the Services and Profiles tabs — becomes Audiogravity's own: it defines the profiles it covers. An import would overwrite that file, and the export only existed to be imported again, so Export Configuration and Import Configuration are gone from the Settings panel. API: GET /profiles/configuration/export-file and POST /profiles/configuration/import-file are removed; GET /profiles/configuration stays. The manual's Settings figure is retaken without them.

[0.9.61] - 2026-09-25

Added

  • [core][ui] Your Qobuz playlists are filled, opened, played from any track and edited from the box, as your HIGHRESAUDIO ones are. The Add to playlist button now shows for the Qobuz track playing in the full-screen player, on Qobuz album covers and on the album rows of a search. Every Qobuz playlist has an open button and a page of its own, and a tap on one of its tracks plays the playlist from there. On a playlist the account owns, a track comes out with one tap, the pencil renames the playlist and edits its description, the bin deletes it; New playlist, the first tile of Mine, creates one. Only the account's own playlists are written — the box checks their owner again before every write — and one the account merely follows stays read-only, like the selections Qobuz publishes. A playlist is created private: Qobuz makes it public unless told otherwise (measured on 2026-09-24), which shows it on the account's public profile. Nothing goes in twice: Qobuz keeps a track added twice, and its own guard refuses a whole album as soon as one of its tracks is already there, so the box reads the playlist first and sends only what it lacks. Removing a track takes out every copy of it. Qobuz lists a new playlist about half a second after creating it (measured on 2026-09-24); the box shows the list as it will be meanwhile, the new playlist at its head, where Qobuz puts it. Played from a track on HQPlayer Embedded and on MPD on the x86 box: the queue starts on the track tapped, with the rest of the playlist behind it. API: /library/playlists* and /library/playlist-tracks take src_qobuz; in /library/qobuz-playlists?type=mine, a playlist the account owns comes back as mine:<id>; start_index and start_item_id of POST /library/queue work for Qobuz albums and playlists.

Fixed

  • [core][ui] Connecting the Qobuz account works on a slow line, and says why when it cannot. To sign in, the box downloads Qobuz's web player — 9.2 MB, which Qobuz serves uncompressed even to a browser asking for compression (measured on 2026-09-25) — to read the app credentials in it. It gave up after 15 s, which failed any line under about 5 Mbit/s; it now waits as long as the download keeps moving — up to 2 minutes, giving up after 30 s without a byte. The sign-in window now opens on the click and is pointed at Qobuz once the address arrives: opened after the box's answer, it was blocked by the browser beyond a few seconds — Chromium blocked it, and the new-tab fallback with it, 6 s after the click (measured on 2026-09-25). A failure is shown on screen with its reason — Qobuz too slow, the connection cut — where it went to the browser's console only, and the reason itself was empty for a timeout. An answer without an address no longer leaves the button on Waiting for login….
  • [core] Signing in to Qobuz takes a quarter of the memory, and no longer holds the core. The web player was decoded into text to be searched — 36.5 MB of text for its 62,116 non-ASCII characters — and the core grew by 46 to 50 MB during a sign-in. It is now searched as the bytes it arrives in, read once: 11 MB (measured on the x86 box). The search also held the core's event loop — playback streams included — for 160 to 240 ms, one of its patterns being tried at every position of the file; about 30 ms now. An answer over 64 MB is refused as it grows.
  • [core] A Qobuz playlist longer than 500 tracks is read whole. Qobuz hands out a playlist's tracks 500 at a time, whatever the limit asked for (measured on 2026-09-24), and the box read a single page: the playlist, and a play of it, stopped at the 500th track without a word. The tracks are now read page by page, to the end.
  • [core] A Qobuz playlist deleted elsewhere is said to be gone, not Qobuz to be failing. Playing it answered Qobuz API error (404) as a service failure (503); playing or opening it now answers that the playlist no longer exists (400).
  • [core][ops] Restarting Audiogravity, updating it or restarting the box no longer cuts an install short. A stop of the core killed an install still running — apt and dpkg with it, since they run inside the core's service — and left the package manager interrupted: nothing more could be installed until sudo dpkg --configure -a had been typed on the box. Measured on 2026-09-24 on a service set up like the core's: stopped 2 s into a package's install script, it was gone in 38 ms and dpkg was left interrupted. While software is being installed, Restart Core, Reboot OS and the update of Audiogravity are now refused with a message naming what is being installed, and so are a stop, restart or reload of the core's own service from Services. A restart of the core counts what the core itself installs; a reboot or an update counts any installation on the box — from the Terminal tab, over SSH, or the network-share and USB-library set-up. What decides is dpkg's own lock, so a download, a simulated install or a vendor script stuck elsewhere never blocks a restart. A stop that comes anyway — systemctl, a shutdown — lets the installation finish, for up to four minutes (HQPlayer Embedded takes 51 s on a Raspberry Pi 4, measured on 2026-09-22), then goes on; an operation that has not yet reached the package manager — a download, a request waiting for another — gives up instead of starting, and says so. The service's stop limit is 20 s instead of 10, and the core asks systemd for more time only while an installation is finishing: measured on 2026-09-24, an install whose script ran 40 s went through a stop with a 20 s limit and ended installed; without that request, the same stop was killed at 20 s. A stop with nothing to finish no longer waits on an open page either: 5 s, where the service used to be killed when its limit ran out.
  • [core][ops] An install no longer waits on a screen nobody can see. Audiogravity installs packages with apt's no-questions settings (DEBIAN_FRONTEND=noninteractive and its companions), but sudo removed them on the way, so apt never received them (measured on 2026-09-24). Where the core had a terminal type in its environment — a core started from a terminal, as in development — a package that asks a question at install time drew its screen on a terminal nobody was looking at and waited for a key forever: HQPlayer Embedded froze on its licence screen, and the core's next restart killed it and left the package manager interrupted. A box's own service has no terminal type, and its installs went through (measured on the same box, with the same package). apt now keeps those settings — a sudoers env_keep rule for apt-get, installed with the core — and commands run as root never receive the terminal type. The vendor's licence is still shown by Audiogravity before the install, as it has been since 0.9.59.
  • [core][ops][manual] An interrupted package manager is named before anything is downloaded, with the command that repairs it. When an earlier operation had been cut short, an install downloaded its package — 121 MB for HQPlayer Embedded — and then failed with Installation failed, apt's reason visible only in the log window, and worded for someone at a terminal. The core now looks first: an install, update or removal is refused before anything happens, with the repair to run — sudo dpkg --configure -a — which the Terminal tab (System, admin) may now run as root: that exact command, no other use of dpkg. If another installation is running at that moment instead, the message says which and asks to try again once it has finished, rather than calling it an interruption: dpkg keeps the same journal while it works as after it was cut short, and only its lock tells the two apart. An operation is also refused while Audiogravity is stopping or updating itself. The manual's troubleshooting chapter has a new entry, Audio Software: "the system's package manager was interrupted".
  • [core] The version offered for HQPlayer Embedded and the NAA is the vendor's own, and it is asked again after each install. The Audio Software page could offer an older version than the one just installed: Older version offered, HQPlayer Embedded 6.1.0-4 installed and 6.0.2-3 offered (seen on 2026-09-24). For a package downloaded from its vendor, the list asked apt as well as the vendor, and apt answers such a package with the version already installed — an answer the list could keep until the next check. The list now asks these packages' vendor only, as the package's details already did, and the version on offer is asked again after every install, update or removal, in the background, instead of at the next daily check.
  • [core][ui] A service restarting in a loop no longer holds every profile on PENDING, and Stop all stops it. On 2026-09-24, HQPlayer Embedded saved from the Systemd tab with real-time scheduling at priority 0 failed at every start — the kernel refuses that pair — and systemd restarted it every 2 s for two hours. Between two attempts such a unit reads activating: every profile naming it — all but AirPlay — read as switching, and its tile stayed on PENDING for good, since the page only lets go of a switch on a final state. A unit that systemd keeps restarting after a failure now reads as failed — on the services, the profiles and the metrics alike — while one restarting after a clean exit keeps systemd's own state. A profile switch now stops a service that is starting, reloading or failing, where it stopped only a running one: even Stop all left the loop running. A profile tile gives up waiting after 75 s and shows the profile as it stands, and a switch whose end reaches the page before the answer to the click is settled at once. The three restart sub-states systemd 254 added are known, where they read unknown.
  • [core][ui] Settings a service cannot start with are refused before they are saved. systemd-analyze verify, which a save relied on, passes every one of them (measured on 2026-09-24): the service then failed at start while systemctl answered success. The Systemd tab now refuses, before anything is written and with the reason: a real-time policy (fifo, rr) with priority 0, and a priority above 0 without one (the kernel's status 214); a CPU affinity naming a CPU the box does not have (215) — a CPU isolated at boot is the box's own, and pinning a player there stays allowed; a memory limit (MemoryMax, MemoryHigh) under 16 MB; an open-file limit under 1024, the default every service gets (203). A limit of 1 KB is not a slow service but a frozen box: measured on 2026-09-25 on the x86 box (systemd 257), MemoryMax=1K hung systemd itself for two minutes, every service with it — systemd starts a service through a copy of itself born inside the service's limit, which the kernel can then neither feed nor stop. Every service is started that same way, so one saved with such a limit would do it at each of its starts, at boot too. Validate gives the same answers. The kernel's refusals were measured on the x86 and the ARM box alike.
  • [core] A save that stops a service from starting is undone, and a stopped service stays stopped. A running service is restarted on its new settings and watched for 3 s; if it does not stay up — or systemd refuses to start it at all, as with a task limit of 1, where HQPlayer Embedded could not even be launched (measured on 2026-09-25) — the previous settings are put back, byte for byte, and it is restarted; the answer says so, and whether it runs again. It used to answer Properties updated successfully over a dead service. A service that is not running — stopped by a profile, or dead after a failure — is no longer started by a save, which overrode the profile that had stopped it: the settings apply at its next start. One already restarting in a loop keeps the new settings, since the old ones failed too. A service that does not start in the end is stopped, so that systemd stops restarting it. systemd is given the time it takes to stop and start a service — HQPlayer Embedded may take 15 s to stop — instead of the 5 s after which a slow stop read as a failed start. Checked live on HQPlayer Embedded: it was back on its previous settings, running, 4.5 s after the save.
  • [core] Restore Backup works on a box, and brings back what the last change replaced. Backing up and restoring a service's settings used sudo cp and sudo mv inside the systemd folder, which the account Audiogravity runs under on a box is not allowed (checked on 2026-09-24): no backup was ever made on a box, and Restore Backup always failed there — only a development box, whose account may do anything, ever saw them work. Both now go through /tmp, like the settings themselves. The backup is written once the new settings stay: a save refused or undone no longer replaces it.
  • [core][ui] FAILED shows in red, and only on the profile in use. A profile one of whose services had failed read IDLE, like a stopped one, with a small badge as the only sign, and a failed service read FAILED in grey; both read FAILED in red, and the dot of a failed service in a profile's details, which had no colour at all, is red. A profile reads FAILED only when it is the one in effect — a service it starts has failed, or one it stops keeps being restarted, and everything else is as it describes: any profile naming a failed service used to, even one never activated, even one that only stops it — killing mpd on the x86 box turned ten tiles of eleven red, for one profile in use. For the others a dead service counts as not running, and shows in their failed count only. Activate on the red tile starts again what failed.
  • [core] A service no longer reads enabled as its state once a minute. The reader of a service's details reused one variable for its running state and for whether it starts at boot: each time its 60 s cache ran out, a service read enabled or disabled where its state belongs — HQPlayer Embedded did on 2026-09-24.
  • [ui][lic][site][manual] After reinstalling the operating system or moving to another machine, you are told to write to support@audiogravity.app, not sent back to the licence portal. A reinstalled system gives the box a new Device ID, and a licence belongs to the Device ID it was activated on: the portal hands a .lic back only to that one (/ls/portal/download), a kept .lic is refused, and a key already activated answers No license found for this key on this device. The manual, the licence panel's About Licensing and the emails delivering a key or a licence file said otherwise. They now keep the key and the portal for a reinstall of Audiogravity on the same system, where they work, and send a new system or a new machine to support@audiogravity.app, with the Order ID and the new Device ID. The manual's troubleshooting chapter has a new entry, Licence not recognised after a reinstall or a new machine, with the three messages the customer meets. The manual's backup restore leaves the licence file out: brought back onto a reinstalled system it was refused, and kept even the 30-day trial from starting (read in the code, not measured on a box). The emails give one address, support@ — the purchase email also gave contact@ — and no longer speak of a support team; the licence portal's support link and the site's FAQ point there too. The licence panel takes the address from the licence server's Contact email setting, set to support@ on 2026-09-25, and leaves the line out when there is none.

[0.9.60] - 2026-09-23

Added

  • [core][ui][manual] The track playing now, or a whole album, goes into your HIGHRESAUDIO playlists from the box. An Add to playlist button sits beside the title in the full-screen player, and next to the star on HIGHRESAUDIO album covers and album rows in search. It opens the account's own playlists with New playlist at the top: pick one, or name a new one and the item goes into it. The box reads the playlist before writing and sends only what it lacks — HIGHRESAUDIO keeps a track added twice, and a later removal then takes out every copy at once (measured on 2026-09-23) — and the message says what happened: added, already there, or an album added in part. An album goes in as the list of its tracks, never through HIGHRESAUDIO's album route, which inserts the album's last track twice (two albums out of two). A read of the playlist that fails stops the add rather than passing for an empty playlist, and a playlist that no longer exists is refused: HIGHRESAUDIO itself accepts a write to a playlist number that does not exist. A playlist just created is listed at once, although HIGHRESAUDIO's own list shows it only seconds later. Purchases (the Vault) and playlists are not offered the button; Qobuz and Tidal are not written yet. New routes GET|POST /library/playlists and POST /library/playlists/add; neither write is retried by the interface, so a lost answer cannot make a second playlist.
  • [core][ui][manual] A HIGHRESAUDIO playlist opens on a page of its own, and the account's own playlists are edited there. An open button sits at the bottom left of every HIGHRESAUDIO playlist cover and at the end of its row — the cover itself still plays it. The page lists the tracks with their durations and counts them itself (HIGHRESAUDIO's own count lags behind the content: 0 for a playlist of 3, measured on 2026-09-23); Play and Queue take the whole playlist, and a tap on a track plays it from there, the tracks before it left out, on every output. On the account's own playlists a track is removed in one tap — every copy of it, as HIGHRESAUDIO does — the pencil renames the playlist and sets its description, the bin deletes it after a confirmation; on HIGHRESAUDIO's selections each track carries Add to playlist instead. New playlist, the first tile of Playlists › Mine, creates an empty one. A playlist just created, renamed or deleted is listed as it now is at once, everywhere, while HIGHRESAUDIO's list catches up. The account's playlists are read strictly for the page and the queue as they were for the writes: one deleted — whose tracks HIGHRESAUDIO keeps readable — or unknown is refused, and a failed read is an error, never an empty playlist. The dialogs of the page are drawn above the whole app, not under its top bar and player. New routes GET /library/playlist-tracks (open to guests, like the rest of the browse), POST /library/playlists/remove, PUT /library/playlists (a description left out is kept, an empty one clears it) and DELETE /library/playlists; POST /library/queue takes start_index and start_item_id — the track is looked for again when it plays, and a track that left is refused — for HIGHRESAUDIO albums and playlists, and refuses them anywhere else. Playlist ids must be numbers before anything reaches HIGHRESAUDIO. None of the writes is retried by the interface.
  • [core] The player names the track it plays, not only the source it comes from. content_item_id, beside content_source_id in the player state, is the track's id on its streaming service — read off the address the box handed MPD, HQPlayer or a network renderer. Measured on MPD: it reports the box's own address, not the service's after the redirect, so the id is there. null for a local file, a station, a media server, or a playback started outside Audiogravity.

Fixed

  • [ui] On a phone, the last lines of a list no longer stay under the mini-player; in a low window, the tabs can be reached again. Two layout heights were declared as a bare 0 for phones and low windows, and calc(44px + 0) is invalid: every declaration adding them was dropped without a word. Measured on 2026-09-23 in Chromium: a 1000×450 window with the tabs as a bar put the bar at the top of the screen, under the top bar, none of its eleven tabs reachable; the library of a phone kept 0px for the mini-player. They are 0px now, guarded by a test that refuses a bare 0 for any layout height, width or offset. Measured the same day, and fixed with them: the card grids of the other tabs kept only 12px at the bottom in portrait; the library reserved nothing at all, top or bottom, with the phone on its side; and in a low window the content of a tab started under a tab bar. Content that runs to the bottom of the screen now keeps --bottom-clearance free — the mini-player's height while it shows, and once it is folded the 44px touch area of its pull tab (--pull-tab-height, which the pull tab now reads too), which used to cover the lower half of the last row. A normal computer window is unchanged.

[0.9.59] - 2026-09-23

Added

  • [core][ui] HQPlayer plays what it cannot decode, because Audiogravity converts it on the way out. HQPlayer reads a short list of formats, and an Apple-encoded library is not on it: it answers Play result="Error" on an ALAC file without so much as opening it, whether the file is served over HTTP or handed to it by its path (measured on HQPlayer Embedded 6.0.4). A library ripped with iTunes or Apple Music is almost entirely M4A — the media server on the lab network answers with 185 M4A files out of 200 — so choosing HQPlayer as the output meant losing nearly all of it, and the manual could only advise converting the lot by hand. Audiogravity now converts each track to FLAC as it hands it over, losslessly, leaving your files untouched: a CD-rate track takes about two seconds and the result is the same size as the original. The conversion is a complete file, not a stream, and that is what lets you move inside the track — asked for 180 seconds in, HQPlayer is there. Three paths are covered: a file in your own library, a track a media server publishes (MinimServer, Plex — the same album was accepted from the box and refused from the NAS until now), and an internet radio station, which is transcoded as it arrives for 0.8 % of a processor core. AAC is what many of the best-sounding stations broadcast in, with no equivalent elsewhere, and they were refused outright. DSD is never converted: turning it into PCM behind your back is not something a music player should decide, so those formats stay refused with the reason, as does an address Audiogravity only forwards. An album whose tracks must be converted starts on its first one and fills in behind it, because HQPlayer inspects every entry before accepting it and that inspection is what triggers the conversion — queued in one go, a sixteen-track album would have been half a minute of silence. The conversions are kept in a bounded space on disk, sized from what the box actually has free, emptied at every start, and remade on demand.
  • [core][ui] HQPlayer Embedded installs from the Audio Software page, on a line this machine can run. Signalyst publishes it for Debian 13 on Intel and on 64-bit ARM, and Audiogravity now offers it beside the adapter it has always installed. The vendor keeps two major lines side by side and only you know which one your licence covers, so the box does not pick for you: it lists the newest build of each line — and checks each against what this machine can actually install. On Debian 13 that leaves one: the 5 line depends on libgmpris, a library neither Debian nor Signalyst publishes, so it is named with the reason instead of being offered as a choice that would end in a half-installed package. The line you install is then held: updates stay on it, and moving to another major stays something you ask for. Signalyst's licence provides for a time-limited trial. A machine the vendor publishes nothing for is told so by name — Debian 12 gets no build of this package. Checking a line costs a few seconds of low-priority processor time the first time it is asked, and nothing for the next ten minutes.
  • [core][ui] The vendor's licence is shown before the install, and the install waits until you accept it. Signalyst ships its end-user licence agreement as a screen its own installer displays. Audiogravity installs without that screen, so the agreement was being accepted by someone who had never seen it — for the adapter too, on every box, not just for the new package. Both licences are now read from the package itself, each time, so the text is always the vendor's current one, and shown before anything is downloaded. The acceptance is held by Audiogravity itself, not only by the screen: an install asked for without it is refused. If the licence cannot be read — the vendor out of reach, a proxy in the way — you are told so, and still asked to accept, rather than shown nothing and let through.
  • [core][ui][manual] The 30-minute trial of HQPlayer Embedded is announced before it can surprise anyone. Signalyst lets it run without a licence key, but only 30 minutes at a time — and the clock runs from the moment HQPlayer Embedded starts, not from the first note: measured twice on a box, once playing nothing at all, the control port refused at 32 min 11 s of service uptime after accepting a command at 31 min 51 s. So a box whose player has been up half an hour has no music left in it, which the vendor's own wording ("plays for 30 minutes at a time") does not suggest. What happens then looks like a breakdown: HQPlayer keeps running and keeps the DAC, its web page still answers, and only its commands are refused — it writes nothing about the expiry in any log, so Audiogravity, which can read that state nowhere, showed HQPlayer unavailable and the music stopped for no stated reason. The limit is now named where it can still be acted on: in the install window before anything is downloaded, on the package's card once it is installed, and in the manual, which says to restart it from Services — and to start the music again, since the restart leaves HQPlayer with an empty queue. Declared by the package itself (trial_notice), like the password its web interface needs, so no screen holds a rule about a particular package; and worded as a condition, with no badge, because the box never learns whether a licence was entered. Audiogravity does not restart it by itself: that would work around a limit the vendor set, and cut the sound without warning.
  • [core][ui][manual] HQPlayer Embedded's settings page is one click away, from the HQPlayer card. Its filters, its modulators, its DSD rate and its licence key are set on a web page of its own, which the manual sends you to and which you had to reach by typing the box's address followed by :8088. The HQPlayer card in Library → Sources now offers Web interface beside its DSP controls, opening in a new tab. Only for the HQPlayer running on this box — an HQPlayer on your network serves no such page — and also when it does not answer: that port keeps answering while its control port refuses, which is exactly what an expired vendor trial looks like, and that page is where the licence key is entered. The address is composed by the interface from the host you reached the box on — a name, a LAN address, a tunnel all work — and the port comes from the package's own declaration, where it already stood for that page's password. It is served over plain HTTP, so your browser will say so about HQPlayer's page: HQPlayer serves no HTTPS of its own.
  • [core][ui] HQPlayer Embedded gets a password for its web interface when it is installed, and keeps it out of the logs. Its Debian package installs its web interface with no credentials at all: the settings pages refuse everybody, and every sign-in attempt writes a line to a log kept in memory — nine million lines in two minutes. The install dialog now asks for a password, prefilled with a random one shown in clear so you can note it down; Audiogravity sets it with the vendor's own tool right after the install, then restarts HQPlayer once, when it has finished starting, so it reads it. Credentials already on the box — a reinstall keeps them — are never overwritten, and are not asked for again. If the password could not be set, the card of the installed package offers Set web password: no need to uninstall and reinstall. The password is never logged, returned or shown in a message; the system journal does not keep it either — sudo records the command it runs twice, in its own line and in the lines of the session it opens, and both are turned off for this one command (measured: a marker passed the same way appeared twice before, nowhere after).
  • [core] HQPlayer Embedded's log lives in 10 MB of memory, with a hard limit. The package writes its log to /tmp — memory, with no limit, never emptied — and a loop in its UPnP server wrote 600 MB a minute there on a test box. The log now goes to a memory space of its own, where the kernel refuses to write past 10 MB: measured with that space full, HQPlayer keeps playing, restarts normally and uses no more processor time. Nothing is written to disk, which spares the SD card of an ARM box, and the log is emptied when HQPlayer starts once it is past half the space. Only the log's location changes in HQPlayer's configuration, written as its own account and replaced in one step, so a full disk can never leave it cut short.
  • [core][ui] HQPlayer Embedded can be started, stopped and switched to like the rest of the stack. It is declared on every box — a new one from its template, an existing one when it updates — with two profiles of its own: HQPlayer Embedded, and MPD HQPlayer Embedded, which keeps MPD running so that your own library plays through it. Every profile that starts or stops the HQPlayer adapter stops HQPlayer Embedded too, since the two cannot share the DAC. Declared once: if you take it out of your configuration, an update or an import does not put it back, and a box where it was already declared by hand, under any name, is left as it is.
  • [core][ui] Music plays through HQPlayer Embedded while it runs on the box. When a profile starts it, what you start from the Library goes to HQPlayer Embedded, and from it straight to your DAC — no NAA in between, so nothing asks for one. The HQPlayer card shows This box with its name, and Use as output on and locked for as long as it runs. An HQPlayer you had chosen on your network is kept, with its own Use as output and its saved DSP settings, which nothing on the card can reset meanwhile; the card goes back to it when HQPlayer Embedded stops. The rule follows the service itself, not the name of a profile. The player shows what HQPlayer Embedded plays, the signal path starts from it, and the output switcher moves its output. The network search no longer lists the box itself, and the box's own address is refused as the card's choice: its HQPlayer is used on its own while it runs. A format HQPlayer cannot decode is converted to FLAC on the way out (see above); what stays refused is refused with advice that works — a profile that stops HQPlayer Embedded, since its switch is locked.
  • [core][ui] HQPlayer Embedded's output is chosen in Audiogravity, like any player's. It has none after installation. Its tile in Config now has a Guided view that offers the box's outputs, and choosing one changes that setting alone: the rest of the file is HQPlayer's own, set in its web interface — its DSD rate included — so the tile has no Reset to default and no form, and the Expert view edits the whole file as text, comments kept. The file stays HQPlayer's, written as its own account and replaced in one step. Saving it restarts HQPlayer only if it is running: it takes the DAC as soon as it starts, so a save never starts it — and the answer no longer says restarted when nothing was.
  • [manual] HQPlayer Embedded has its own section, and the Audio Software page is described as it now behaves. The Outputs & engines chapter walks through installing it — the version your licence covers, Signalyst's licence, the password to note down — choosing its output, setting its DSD rate on its web page before the first play, switching to it with its two profiles, playing music through it, and what to do when a step did not go through; it says that HQPlayer Embedded needs a licence from Signalyst. Troubleshooting says how to set a new password when it is lost, and what to do when the DAC hisses instead of playing. An Apple-encoded (ALAC) library needs nothing done to it: the box converts each track as it hands it over. The Audio Software part of Administration now describes the install window, an uninstall that keeps a package's settings, and Install Incomplete. The install window is pictured as a phone shows it.

Changed

  • [core][ui] The box's HQPlayer adapter is now called naa, and stops borrowing HQPlayer's own name. Audiogravity used the one word hqplayer for two different machines: the Signalyst Network Audio Adaptor, the daemon running on the box, and the HQPlayer instance it receives sound from, which runs somewhere else. The collision had reached the interface it offers — /packages/hqplayer meant the adapter, /hqplayer/status meant the player — and ran through the service identifiers, the configuration file it edits and the output switching. The adapter is naa from now on, everywhere it is named by identifier; the player keeps hqplayer and every one of its routes. Nothing changes on screen: each screen shows the label, HQPlayer NAA, which has not moved. A box that already holds an audio-config.json has the name changed in it when it updates — profiles included, so no chain loses a step — and keeps the file's owner and permissions. The configuration editor's saved copies of the adapter's file move with it, so none of them disappears from the editor, and a failed update that rolls back restores the previous file and still finds them. A configuration exported before this release is brought over when it is imported back. For API clients: the package id and the service id hqplayer become naa, in /packages/*, /audio_app_config/*, /audio-stack/* and the service-metrics events; /hqplayer/* is untouched.
  • [core][ui] Removing a package keeps its settings; erasing them is a separate choice. Debian distinguishes removing a package from erasing what it leaves behind, and Audiogravity only ever did the first — so a box that tried HQPlayer once kept its system user, its privileged grant and its device rules for ever. Uninstalling now offers to delete all of it, as a distinct and explicit action, because the same sweep also takes the DSP settings with it: every filter and modulator choice, which are the hours nobody wants to lose to a misclick.
  • [core][ui] An install whose next step could not be done says "Incomplete", not "Failed". The package is on the box, and the card says so; what is left to do — its password, its log space, its restart, or other software apt had to remove on the way — is what you read, as a warning. It used to read Install Failed about a package that was installed, and an unexpected error in one of those steps — a full temporary folder, a command that cannot start — was reported as the install's own failure and skipped the steps after it. Update all now says it too, where it used to announce All Updates Complete.
  • [core] HQPlayer Embedded is turned on after any install, a reinstall included. Its removal script disables the service, so a reinstall left it off, now and at every boot, where a first install turned it on.
  • [core] A stop of HQPlayer Embedded ends within 15 seconds. Stopped within about a second of its start, HQPlayer ignores the request and systemd waited 90 seconds before ending it — after a quick second restart, two profile switches in a row, or while it was being removed. A normal stop takes under half a second.
  • [core][ui] The restart badge only shows when a restart is owed. After installing or updating HQPlayer Embedded, the card asked for a restart Audiogravity had just done itself, and a click cut the music for nothing. It now shows only when that restart failed.
  • [core] An install that removes other software says so. apt takes out, without asking, a package that conflicts with what it installs, and the screen said Successfully installed. Audiogravity now compares what the system holds before and after, and names what went.
  • [core] Audiogravity no longer holds the right to install any package file with dpkg. Nothing has used it since packages from a vendor install through apt; a box loses it at its next update.

Fixed

  • [core] A successful install no longer shows errors, and is only called successful once the package is really there. A package downloaded from its vendor was installed with dpkg, which never installs what a package depends on: whenever something was missing it stopped, the log showed dpkg failed and dependency problems in red, and apt repaired afterwards — so a normal install of HQPlayer Embedded read as a string of errors before its success. It is now installed through apt, which brings its dependencies along in the same step, as the upmpdcli bundle always did; switching the adapter back to an older line still works. The repair step is gone with it, and so is its worst case: apt mends a package whose dependency cannot be met by removing it, and that used to be announced as "installed successfully" for a package absent from the box. The verdict now comes from dpkg itself, after the fact.
  • [core] A box set to another language than English is offered updates again. apt writes its answers in the language of the system, and Audiogravity looked for an English word in them: on a box set to French or German, no update was ever offered for the software installed from the system's repositories. apt is now always asked in English, and an answer that cannot be read is "could not check", never "nothing available" — which the new check of what a vendor's package needs relies on too. That check also looks first at what is already installed, which takes a few milliseconds instead of seconds.
  • [core] System files written by Audiogravity are handed to the administrator, and the ones left behind are repaired. A file Audiogravity writes into the system — a mount unit, a service setting — is prepared under its own account, then handed to root.
