That last step was never checked, and the permission it needs for mount units never existed: every USB library and network share mount written so far stayed owned by Audiogravity's own account, although the system runs it with full rights. Every step is now checked, and the update hands those files back.
  • [core] A command that overruns its time limit is stopped, even when it runs with administrator rights. A deadline ended only the sudo in front of the command and then waited for the command itself — the refresh of the package lists could keep running, holding apt's lock, and a support report probe could wait far past its budget. The command is now asked to stop through sudo, which passes it on, and the wait is bounded.
  • [core][ui] Restarting a service no longer reports a failure while the restart goes on. The Services tab's restart went through a command given 5 seconds, and a service that took longer to stop was reported as failed although it restarted. It now goes through the system's own interface, as start and stop always did. The restart badge of the Audio Software page also says so when a restart really fails, where it announced success whatever the answer.
  • [core] Importing a configuration no longer stops on a service that is not installed. A service declared before its package is installed is an ordinary state — its profiles read as unavailable — yet the import refused it, so a box without every optional package could not restore its own export. It is now a warning, and the import goes on after you confirm.
  • [core] A network share whose name has an accent is recognised as mounted. Its mount point was read with the wrong decoding, so /mnt/Musique été was never found. The support report also reads a mount point with a space correctly.
  • [ui] Log lines show >= as >=. The install log and the system log viewer escaped each line twice, so a comparison in what a tool printed — libomp5-19 (>= 1:19.1.6) — appeared as (&gt;= 1:19.1.6). A line containing markup is still shown as text.
  • [core][ui] A refused install says why, on screen. A package that cannot go on this machine — a dependency nothing provides, not enough room — is now turned down before anything is downloaded or changed, and the reason is what you read, instead of Failed to install.
  • [core] A package is no longer downloaded into memory. The download went to /tmp, which on these boxes is a RAM disk — so installing HQPlayer Embedded would have held 144 MB of memory on a machine whose whole job is to play audio without a glitch. It now goes to disk, chosen when the install runs rather than written down, since which folder is in RAM differs between machines. A TMPDIR set by the operator still wins, and a download left behind by an install that never finished is cleared the next time.
  • [core] An install that cannot fit is refused before it starts, not halfway through. Only the download was checked for room, and not even from the right point of view; nothing looked at what the package unpacks to — 257 MB for HQPlayer Embedded against 144 MB downloaded. The unpack is now measured with the space dpkg really has as root (5 % more on ext4 than a regular user sees), counts the download when both land on the same disk, and is checked before a byte is fetched. A package that declares no size is let through rather than refused on a guess.
  • [ui] Notes in dialogs keep the space, colour and line height their style gives them. A rule shared by every dialog reset its paragraphs with more weight than any style of their own, so none of those applied — measured at 0 px between a note and the next block in the Audio Software dialogs. It now only sets a default for paragraphs that have no style of their own: in the install window the version a box cannot install sits with the version choice, the web password and uninstall windows get their spacing back, the No activity this session line of the service and profile details takes the lighter grey it was given, and the support report's introduction its spacing.
  • [core][ui] The support report describes the HQPlayer you actually use. Its HQPlayer section read an address from the settings file, which only seeds the card: on a box whose card had chosen an HQPlayer, it said not configured. It now reports the instance chosen in the card, whether the box's own HQPlayer is the one played through, whether library playback goes to HQPlayer, and which HQPlayer answers, by product and version. An HQPlayer that does not answer is asked once, not twice.
  • [core] HQPlayer as the output no longer refuses every play after a start-up where its adapter started at the same moment. Audiogravity read the adapter's state, then began listening for its changes: a start completing in between went unseen, and every play was refused as NAA not running until the adapter next restarted. It now listens first.
  • [core] A UPnP renderer is recognised as this box on any loopback address, and a renderer address with no host never is.
  • [ui] A box no longer publishes the interface's own source code. The build asks for hidden source maps, which only keeps the reference comment out of the bundle: the .map files were still written, and copied into the package — so every box served the whole original source at an address anyone could guess. They are now left out of the package, and stay on the build machine for debugging. The comment that presented the option as a protection said the opposite of what it did; it is corrected.
  • [manual] The certificate entry carries the box's name, not its address. In First run, trusting the local authority on an iPhone or an iPad: the entry reads Audiogravity Local CA followed by the box's name in brackets. The manual announced its address, which is not what the certificate carries.
  • [core] Adding tracks to what HQPlayer is playing no longer blanks the album on screen. Audiogravity follows the album HQPlayer plays by counting the entries it pushed, and lets that list go when the count stops matching — which is how a playlist changed from HQPlayer's own remote stops wearing your album's titles. But HQPlayer only acknowledges a longer playlist on a timer of its own: measured on HQPlayer Embedded, about five seconds after the tracks were added, whether one was added or ten. So every append blanked the title, the artist and the cover about three seconds later, and for good. That wait is now recognised for what it is — and only it: the exact count from before the append, and only for as long as an acknowledgement can still be owed. A playlist that really did change is still let go at once, a stopped HQPlayer cannot hold the exception open, and a list too long to follow takes it with it.
  • [core] A command HQPlayer carried out is no longer reported as failed. Every command opens its own connection, and HQPlayer answers the first one on a connection only after a delay of its own — measured across the network on HQPlayer Desktop, with that instance stopped: 2.37 s for a status, and four pauses at 2.60, 4.55, 3.43 and 2.59 s. Audiogravity waited three seconds, so next, previous, pause and a jump within the track went through and were announced as failures. A command now waits eight seconds. What Audiogravity reads over and over keeps the shorter wait: the playback state, and the volume, which a slider sends at every step of a drag.
  • [core] An HQPlayer named rather than numbered is told where to fetch from. Audiogravity hands HQPlayer the address of the box on the route toward HQPlayer — the only right one when the box has two networks, sits behind a VPN, or is on an audio LAN with no gateway. That route can only be worked out from an address and not from a name, since resolving one there could hold up everything else the box is doing, so an HQPlayer configured as musicscore.local fell back without a word on the default route — wrong on exactly the networks this exists for. The address is now read from the connection Audiogravity already holds open to it, where the system has done the resolving and the routing itself. An HQPlayer reached over IPv6, and a name that resolves to the box's own loopback, still get the box's ordinary address: that is where its files are served.
  • [ui] The tracks HQPlayer could not open are named on screen. HQPlayer accepts an address and then drops it without an error of its own — a file it cannot open, a server it cannot reach — and Audiogravity has been reporting which ones since the previous release, with nothing showing them: the album played short, and nothing was said. A notice now names them, without calling the playback a failure, since what was kept is playing.
  • [ui] Starting an album says it is on its way. The tracks leave as one exchange, and HQPlayer answers the first command of a connection only after that same delay of its own: measured on the box's own HQPlayer, 0.47 s for a single track but 2.39 s for sixteen, and longer over the network. Nothing on screen said anything during that wait, so the album was clicked a second time. A notice now appears when a start takes more than a second and a half — never for a single track, which is the common click.
  • [ui] The filter you pick after changing HQPlayer's mode is the one that applies. HQPlayer offers different filters and shapers in PCM and in DSD, and what it takes is a position in that list: the card kept the previous mode's lists, so a pick after a mode change silently applied another filter. The lists are now re-read when the mode changes.

[0.9.58] - 2026-09-20

Changed

  • [ui] The Compact Mode switch is gone; the interface keeps the density it has always shown. The switch was on from the very first launch and the only thing turning it off did was make everything larger — a second layout nobody asked for, carried through 31 rules and every screen of the interface. Those rules are now simply what the interface is: the spacing steps read 4/6/12/18/24 px and the type steps 12/13/14 px where three names were being substituted underneath. Nothing moves on screen, except in two places. The settings drawer gives each switch a full-width row — light/dark mode, notifications, animations, portrait lock and Face ID / Touch ID were paired two to a line, each label squeezed into half the width beside its switch. And a service tile that starts, or a profile that becomes active, no longer shifts its contents by a pixel as its border thickens: the second border pixel is taken out of the padding, as the processor tile beside it already did. The sign-in page is deliberately left untouched: it is the one screen the setting never reached, so it had been drawing the larger layout alone, and following the interface would have shrunk it by itself (its card 558 → 460 px on a computer, 536 → 445 px on a phone). It now states the steps it used to inherit, and is pixel-identical — checked over every element it holds, at both sizes. Verified the same way across ten tabs on a computer and a phone: no other difference.
  • [manual] The Administration chapter stops offering a switch that no longer exists. Compact Mode was listed among the settings and the drawer's picture showed it; the picture has been retaken and the list now describes what the drawer holds.
  • [ui] Repeat and shuffle leave the playback bar for the fullscreen player. They sat on a second line under the track title, in a bar whose job is to show what is playing and offer the transport — the same two controls are in the fullscreen player, where the rest of the playback settings already live. The bar keeps the height it has always had: the space they leave is not taken back, so nothing above the footer moves. As a side effect of pinning that height, the interface now reserves the right amount of room for the bar — the figure it went by said 61 px for a bar that measured 83.
  • [manual] The Listening chapter says how to open the fullscreen player, and stops saying something that never worked. It read "Tap it … to open the fullscreen player"; tapping the bar does not open it, and tapping the cover art opens the album's details and tracklist instead. The three ways in are now listed — the tab at the top-right corner on a computer, a swipe up from the track title on a phone, and by itself when you start something playing from the Library — which matters more now that repeat and shuffle are only in there. The bar's picture has been retaken on the same track it always showed.

Added

  • [core][ui] Audiogravity now reads which HQPlayer it is talking to, and says when the box cannot work with it. Connecting an instance reads its identity once — the product, so a Desktop is told from an Embedded, and the engine version, now shown beside the name on the card and against each result of a network scan. From it comes the one thing that decides whether the chain carries sound: HQPlayer and the box's network audio adapter have to be on the same major line. When they are not, the card says so and names both versions, and Use as output is refused rather than routing playback into silence — the same treatment an adapter that is not running already got. An unknown pairing is not a broken one and blocks nothing. The refusal also applies when a track starts, not only when the setting is switched on — the setting is remembered, and the pairing can break under it. Nothing is polled: the identity is read when the instance answers and forgotten when it goes away, so an upgrade on the other machine is picked up by itself.
  • [core][ui] The adapter now follows the HQPlayer it serves, which is what repairs a box that drifted. Holding it to the line already installed protected a healthy machine but left a broken one broken. It now takes its line from the HQPlayer in front of it when that one can be reached, and falls back to the installed line otherwise. A box running adapter 6 with HQPlayer 5 is therefore offered the 5 line — an older version than the one installed, on purpose. The Audio Software page no longer calls that an update: it says an older version is offered, and asks to switch rather than to update. Which way a version goes is now decided by the core, because ordering Debian versions is not a comparison of two strings — and the update notification follows the same rule, so a repair no longer arrives on your phone announced as an update.

Fixed

  • [core] The HQPlayer NAA update no longer offers a version that cannot work with your HQPlayer. Signalyst publishes the 5 and the 6 line of networkaudiod in the same directory, and the update took the last link on the page — 6.1.4-71 on a machine running 5.1.8-68, although NAA 6 does not work with HQPlayer 5. The choice is now made by comparing versions instead of reading the listing in order, and the NAA is held to the major line the machine already runs: that machine is told it is up to date, and one on 5.1.5-67 is finally offered the 5.1.8-68 that was hidden underneath a version it could not use. A machine with nothing installed still gets the newest published. Checked against the vendor's own listing on amd64 and arm64. Holding a package to its line is declared for that package rather than applied to every downloaded .deb, and where the line cannot be established the update is refused instead of falling back to the newest — a Debian epoch or a rebuild suffix in the installed version used to make the constraint vanish silently. The package name is also read from the registry rather than matched as the literal networkaudiod.
  • [core] A package is no longer reported as missing while it is running, nor as present while its installation is broken. Whether a package is installed was decided by looking for the word "installed" anywhere in what dpkg reports. half-installed — an installation interrupted midway — contains it and passed; triggers-pending, triggers-awaited and half-configured, which describe a package that works, do not contain it and failed. The answer now comes from the status word itself, in the one place all three readers share.
  • [core] Finishing an install no longer sends Audiogravity out to the vendor's website, twice. The notification pushed after every install, update or removal asks for the package's details without its available version — and that request was being honoured everywhere except where it mattered, so each operation ended with two lookups over the network, plus an apt-cache query for packages apt cannot answer for at all. That query costs seconds of processor time on DietPi, whose apt cache is disabled. The last known version is still shown, from memory.

[0.9.57] - 2026-09-19

Changed

  • [ui] The default theme's corners are softened, to one 2 px radius everywhere. Minimal drew every corner square, which read as hard. Cards, panels, buttons, filters, badges, the top bar's figures, the player's artwork, the pipeline's controls and the notifications now share a single 2 px radius — graded values were tried and dropped, because 6 px on the large panel of each tab changed the theme's character more than it softened it. Elements whose corners were written into their own style, and so ignored every theme, now take them from the theme; slate and gravity keep exactly the corners they had, except the offline banner's Retry button (2 px in slate) and the reference box of the help windows (8 px in gravity), which now follow each theme's own scale.
  • [ui] The Services and System charts show what was measured, and nothing else. On Services, each box draws a thin line over a light area with a dot on the latest value; on System, each tile draws one bar per measurement, the newest in full, with the highest value of the window and the time it covers written above — measured from when the readings arrived, since the box sends them every 2, 10 or 30 seconds depending on activity. Both start empty and fill from the right, leave a gap where a reading is missing or the stream was paused (the app hidden, the box offline), and start their scale at zero. The second curve of a box (outgoing, writes) is grey instead of the warning orange it fell back to.
  • [ui] In the mini player, the stream-origin badge is the twin of the output badge beside it. QOBUZ next to USB: same height, type, greys and border, drawn by one shared rule so they cannot drift apart. The HIGHRESAUDIO logo is left out of that badge — wider than tall, it stretched the badge to 26 px beside an 18 px one.
  • [ui] Status dots no longer glow. The green dot (up, online, active) and the orange one (a service starting or stopping) are flat discs, in every theme; they still blink.
  • [core] With HQPlayer as the output, playing or queuing now says what HQPlayer actually kept. HQPlayer accepts an address and may then drop it without an error of its own — a file it cannot open, an address it cannot reach — so the answer was "OK" whatever happened, and the only trace was a track missing from its playlist. Audiogravity now reads the playlist back in the same exchange and answers with the number of tracks HQPlayer kept, naming the ones it dropped. A play goes on with what was kept. A play that keeps nothing is refused at once, with the track names, where it used to wait 45 seconds and then blame the sound card. An enqueue that loses a track says so. A dropped file no longer wipes the title and cover of the rest of the album either, because the list Audiogravity follows is now the one HQPlayer really holds. A local track the library does not know is refused before anything is sent (400, formerly 503). For API clients: /library/queue answers routed to HQPlayer carry refused and refused_titles, media-server albums included.
  • [manual] The manual says what HQPlayer kept, and what Services shows when memory is not counted. Outputs & engines now explains that Audiogravity reads HQPlayer's playlist back and names the tracks it dropped, and Troubleshooting lists the new message — "HQPlayer could not open this track" — beside the others, since a message nobody can look up leaves the reader where they started. The troubleshooting entry for a box that does not count memory described 0 MB and a flat graph; the cards have shown a dash and no graph since 0.9.42, which the Administration chapter already said. The entry now describes what the screen shows, under a title that matches it.

Fixed

  • [core] The Systemd tab shows the real-time settings each audio service runs with, and saving keeps them. systemd reports a service's scheduling policy as a number — 2 for round-robin — and Audiogravity only understood names, so every service read "default", the five audio engines of the lab included, all of them running in real time. The editor starts from what the tab shows, so saving any other setting rewrote the service's settings file without its policy: a service put in real time through Audiogravity, with Audio Optimized for instance, would have lost it at its next edit (read from the code, not tried on a live service). The nice level was never read either — it was asked for under a name systemd does not have — so HQPlayer NAA, running at −10, read "default" too. Both are read now. A service that sets neither still reads "default", and saving writes nothing for them, so systemd's own defaults are not frozen into Audiogravity's settings above the package's.
  • [core] For API clients, a service's details agree with systemd. In GET /services/{name}, an unlimited memory limit read 17179869183G — systemd writes "unlimited" as the largest possible number when asked over D-Bus, and that number was converted as a size — and now reads infinity, as /services/{name}/properties already did. The network totals (io.ip_*) were always null because they were never asked for; they now carry the counts whenever IP accounting is on for the service. Nothing on screen showed either.
  • [ui] The Library button in the top bar now looks like the two buttons beside it. Between the menu and the settings wheel, it was the only thing in the bar drawn inside a framed box — taller than its neighbours, filled with a background, its icon small in the middle. It borrowed the generic style meant for buttons elsewhere in the interface instead of the one the top bar uses, and had done so from the start without anything saying why. It is now the same size, frameless, with its icon filling the button like the other two. The button at the foot of the page, which uses that generic style on purpose, keeps its frame.
  • [ui] Offline, the tabs stay within reach in a narrow window. With the tabs laid out as a bar along the top, in a window 768 px wide or less — a computer's browser window made narrow, a large tablet sharing its screen — the offline banner was given exactly the place of the bar and drawn over it: a 10 px strip of the bar showed below the orange, and a click on any tab landed on the banner, so changing tab was impossible until the box came back. Measured in a browser window 700 px wide. The bar now steps down below the banner and the page follows it; online, nothing moves. Phones were not affected: their tabs always sit in the side panel.
  • [ui] The Services charts follow the measurements. Each box began with thirty invented zeros — a flat line at zero, then a cliff on the right, for the first five to fifteen minutes (the box reports a service every 10 seconds, every 30 at rest; measured: two real points out of thirty after one minute). The charts were not redrawn at each new measurement either: they only moved when something else redrew the page. A missing reading was drawn as a zero.
  • [ui] The System charts no longer invent readings. An update carrying only the uptime added a point to every chart, copied from the previous one or at zero, and a machine without a temperature sensor drew a row of zeros headed "max 0.0 °C". A reading of exactly 0 was, on the other hand, taken as missing.
  • [ui] The expanded view of a Services chart opens on its first measurement. A single point was drawn as nothing (and computed as a division by zero), and a missing reading as a zero; the figure above the chart now shows a dash when the latest reading is missing, rather than an older value presented as current.
  • [ui] The Services chart history survives a reload. It was meant to be saved every 30 seconds, but the delay restarted with every measurement, so nothing was written while the tab was in use.
  • [core + ops] Your own library plays through HQPlayer on every box, not only on ours. To play a local file, HQPlayer is given an address on your network to fetch it from — and that address was written into the code as the one of the development machine in our lab. Every box handed it out. On a box anywhere else, HQPlayer was sent to fetch your files from a machine that does not exist on your network, and the local library never played through HQPlayer; in the lab it worked because the development machine happened to be running with the same library, which is precisely what hid it. Measured on the lab's ARM box: with the development machine stopped, adding a track answered "OK" and nothing reached HQPlayer's playlist. The address is now the box's own, taken on the route toward HQPlayer, which is also the right one on a box with two network connections or behind a VPN. The files are fetched from the same signed address network speakers use, with the file's extension at the very end: HQPlayer picks its decoder from the end of the address, and a DSD or WavPack file whose address ended with its signature was dropped without a word. HQPLAYER_STREAM_HOST stays for a box HQPlayer reaches through an address it does not own (NAT, a proxy): empty means detected, it is now present in the configuration of new and upgraded boxes alike, and a value that is not an http(s):// address is ignored with a warning instead of breaking every source sent to HQPlayer.
  • [core] MP3 files from your library play through HQPlayer. HQPlayer drops an MP3 when it is given its size — measured one response header at a time, the size alone decides — and one sent without a size, the way a radio is, never started when the file opened with its tag and embedded cover. MP3 files now reach HQPlayer without a size and from their first audio frame. Network speakers still receive the file as it is, so they can seek in it.
  • [core] An MP3 whose length HQPlayer cannot measure shows the library's length, and can be scrubbed. HQPlayer works out the length of nearly everything, but not of an MP3 without an Xing/Info header: the progress bar had no scale and could not be dragged, although the library knew the length. The library's figure is now shown for such a track — HQPlayer's own measurement still wins whenever it has one — and it is tied to the track it belongs to, so a radio queued behind never inherits it, even when the queue changes at the moment the track does. For these files the library's figure can run a little long: 2.4 s on each of the files measured, which matches the size of the cover embedded in them, so the bar stops just short of the end.
  • [core] Starting an album on HQPlayer while a radio plays no longer fails in silence. The first thing a push asks HQPlayer is to stop what is playing, and while it plays it takes about four seconds to answer — longer than the three Audiogravity waited. Its late answer was then read as the answer to the next command, so every answer that followed was shifted by one; and when two answers arrived together, the push stopped before Play, leaving HQPlayer stopped with an emptied playlist while the request answered 200. Each answer is now matched to its command by name, as HQPlayer names it. Verified on the lab's HQPlayer while music was playing: the album starts, and a dropped track is still named.
  • [core] A track from your library played through HQPlayer shows the library's own cover. It was shown through an address with no fallback: a file whose picture the music server does not find — a folder picture it does not look for — showed no cover at all, where an internet search used to find one; and on a network without a gateway the address pointed at the box itself and was refused. The cover now comes the way the library shows it: the file's own picture, then MusicBrainz. Verified identical, byte for byte, to the picture embedded in the file.
  • [core] Sending albums to HQPlayer no longer erases what the local queue knows. Every track sent to HQPlayer was also written into a list of 512 entries kept on the SD card, which nothing ever read for HQPlayer. It pushed out, oldest first, the durations and covers of the streams queued on the local output, which then showed --:--, and rewrote the file each time. Nothing is written there for HQPlayer any more.
  • [core] A play HQPlayer refused no longer leaves an old error behind. When HQPlayer kept nothing of a play, the check started by the previous play went on running, and 45 seconds later published its own "never started" about a playback that had already been replaced.
  • [core] A library on a network share that stops answering no longer freezes the box when HQPlayer or a speaker fetches a file. The file was looked up on the core's main loop, and a share that stops answering blocks without a time limit: volume, transport and live updates stopped for the whole box. The lookup now runs aside.
  • [core] A network speaker is told the right format for files in folders whose name reads like a web address. A path such as AC:DC/Back #1/… was parsed as an address, which lost the file's extension, and an M4A, OGG or Opus file inside it was announced as plain bytes. The formats Audiogravity names itself — FLAC, WAV, AIFF, MP3, DSF, DFF and WavPack — were never affected.
  • [core] Looking for HQPlayer on the network follows a box that has changed networks. The search scanned the network the box was on when the core started, and after a move found nothing until a restart.

Security

  • [core] Your library files are no longer served to anyone on the network without a signature. HQPlayer fetched local files through a route of its own, open to every device on your network and signed by nothing: anyone who could guess a path could download any file of the library. That route is gone. HQPlayer now uses the signed route network speakers already use.

[0.9.56] - 2026-09-17

Fixed

  • [ui] Offline, the interface now shows the data it says it is showing. A banner reading OFFLINE MODE — VIEWING CACHED DATA stretched across the screen while the panel underneath it said "Unable to connect to server. Please check your connection." Both were telling the truth, which is what made it worse: only the strip of figures along the top was ever restored, because it was the only surface listening for the state that was being kept. The panels each fetch their own data, and nothing handed them back what they had last seen — they simply asked the box again, got nothing, and reported it. Six now keep their last reading and serve it when the box cannot be reached: profiles, services, the system dashboard, audio software, accounts and the processor description. The reading is only served when nothing answered at all — a box that replies and refuses, say after a key is changed, still says so rather than showing hours-old state as though it were current. Three surfaces deliberately keep the error: the configuration and systemd editors, because a stale copy on a screen with a Save button offers to overwrite the live file with it, and the log viewer, because frozen logs presented as live mislead on exactly the subject they are opened for.
  • [ui] The offline banner no longer covers the top of the page on a phone. It is fixed below the top bar, so whatever scrolls underneath has to leave room for it — and the rule that did so was shut inside a desktop-only block. On a phone, which is where the banner is seen most, the first section title was cut in half and the chip beside it disappeared behind the orange.
  • [ui] The installed app starts offline after an update instead of starting locked. The app keeps a copy of everything it needs, and that copy is rebuilt at every release. Two files that the app cannot start without were missing from it: the one carrying the address and key of your box, and the one that holds the connection for live updates. So the first launch after an update, made without a network, opened an interface with no credentials — unable to ask the box for anything, and staying that way until it was reloaded with the network back — and no live updates, silently, since that failure is not one the app can see. Both are kept now.
  • [ui] Parts of the interface were fetched again on every single page load. The rule deciding which files never change spelled out the shape of the fingerprint in their names, and the tool that generates them may put a dash in it — so whichever files happened to draw one that day fell through to being treated as changeable: re-requested, and rewritten into the app's own copy, on each load. Which files were affected was redrawn at every build, so the cost moved around instead of being noticed. The images, icons and launch screens had a related problem of their own: they were re-checked against the box on every request, cache hit included, which is one round trip and one write per picture per page load — for files that cannot change until the next release replaces all of them at once.
  • [ui] Tapping a notification raises the window that is already open. It compared a full address against a bare path, then looked for the product's name inside the address — false on a box reached at a number, which is what the installer prints. Neither ever matched, so a second window was opened beside the one already showing the interface, every time.
  • [ui] The offline page is in English again, and names the product. The page the box actually serves had been translated to French at some point and called the product Gravity, while the fallback copy of the same page inside the app stayed in English — two copies of one screen, drifted apart. There is one text now, and the fallback has been reduced to the bare minimum so there is nothing left to keep in step.
  • [ui] Keeping a copy of the box's state no longer interrupts the main thread every few seconds. Saving it writes to browser storage, which blocks while it works, and it was done on every incoming measurement — several times a minute, for as long as the interface stayed open, on a machine whose spare capacity belongs to the audio. Only the last value is ever read back, so all the writes before it bought nothing. They are now grouped, and written out at once when the app is put away so nothing is lost.
  • [ui] Cold start is lighter. On every page load the interface quietly loaded nine of its heaviest screens in the background, so they would be available offline. They already were — the app's own copy covers them — so this did nothing but run nine modules nobody had asked for.

[0.9.55] - 2026-09-16

Changed

  • [manual] The manual shows the interface you are actually running. Its pictures were taken between eight and ten weeks before this release and the interface had moved underneath them: they offered MPD 0.23 where the box installs 0.24, an engine that is no longer listed and none of the two that now are, three outputs where a plain box lists six, a user card without the passkeys button beside it, a settings drawer without the Face ID switch, and an update banner announcing v0.9.11. Every picture in the manual has been retaken against a running box, at the size of a phone screen and at three times its resolution, so they stay sharp on the device most people read them on. Four captions were saying something the screen no longer says — the button is Restart Core, not "Restart Backend" — and were corrected with the pictures they sit under. The licence panel is shown twice, on trial and once activated, because it holds different things in each; the device identifier and the order number are blurred, an order number being the licence key itself.
  • [manual] Seven things the manual described and never showed. The stream-origin badge shipped in 0.9.54 was explained in words and had no picture at all — it is now shown, MPD beside the name of the station it is carrying, next to a paragraph saying why the engine alone tells you nothing. The bar at the bottom of every screen, the list of sources that the whole Library chapter is organised around, the radio collection, the signal chain drawn on a phone, and the tiles of the configuration editor were all in the same position: described, never pictured. Six new figures, placed where the text describes them.

Added

  • [manual] After an update, an app that shows an old version and says the core is offline. A device that was asleep or closed while the box was updated can keep serving the copy of the interface it already had. The old copy asks the box for things the new version moved, gets nothing back, and reports the only thing it can see — no answer — as CORE · OFFLINE. So the screen blames the box, and the box is fine: the address opens normally from any other browser. The troubleshooting chapter now says so, and gives the way out, including in the installed app, which has no reload button to press.

Fixed

  • [ui] The Library no longer offers to switch you to what you are already listening to. Playing a station and standing on the Radio screen, a banner announced "Radio Choco HD is now playing" and offered to switch you to the radio — the screen you were looking at. The two halves of that screen were not answering the same question: a station, a Qobuz album and a stream from a media server all travel over the same engine, so the field naming the engine says the same thing for all three, and the Library was reading the engine on one side and the actual source on the other. It now reads the source on both. The radio has a further quirk of its own — its screen is reached from the tab bar without changing the source you are browsing — so the banner now compares against what the screen is showing as well, and starting a station makes the radio the source you are browsing, as picking it from the sources screen would. That last step waits for the station to actually start: a station that fails to play no longer rearranges the tab bar underneath the error message. The banner also stops naming things that are not sources at all: with HQPlayer driven from its own remote, it offered "HQPlayer is now playing" and its Switch button sent the Library to browse a routing handle.
  • [ui] Dismiss now dismisses. The banner's Dismiss button cleared the banner and remembered nothing, while the box republishes what is playing about every three seconds — so the banner came back within three seconds, every time, and the button did nothing at all. The refusal is now remembered for as long as that source is the one playing, and lifts on its own once something else plays. It survives Roon naming itself in either of the two ways it does, and glancing at the Radio screen no longer counts as changing your mind.
  • [ui] Connecting a streaming account no longer moves you off the screen you were on. Signing in to Qobuz, Tidal or HIGHRESAUDIO refreshes the list of sources, and that refresh had come to adopt whatever was playing: it could drop you onto the radio screen, or replace the media server you were browsing with the local library. A refresh now refreshes the list of sources and nothing else. Arriving on the Library still opens on what is playing, which is the one moment that is a useful answer.
  • [ui] Opening the media server a stream is coming from. The Library asks the box for the address of a known UPnP server by a field name the box does not answer with — it publishes location, and the interface was reading the name that field carries inside the box rather than on the way out. An address that comes back empty is indistinguishable from a server that is no longer known, so this failed silently and always had. It is read under the right name now, which is what lets arriving on the Library open the server a stream is coming from rather than stopping at the list of sources.
  • [core] Sending music to a network speaker now stops what was playing in the room. Casting an album to a UPnP speaker left the box playing whatever it already had on its own DAC, so two different pieces of music came out of two outputs at once — and the player kept following the local one. The screen named the station, with its cover and its format, while the speaker played the album you had just chosen, over a signal path reading Radio → the speaker, which describes nothing. Nothing had gone wrong in what the box knew: it had the speaker's track, its artist and its album all along. The local output simply never stopped, and the player picks the source that is actually playing — so it went on picking that one, correctly, from a situation that should not have existed. HQPlayer hides this by accident, its network adaptor holding the sound card exclusively so the local player is stopped by being refused; a speaker on the network takes nothing away from your DAC, and there was nothing to hide behind. Handing over is now part of sending: the local player is stopped just before the speaker is given its music — late enough that a speaker gone from the network, or a streaming link that expires as it is fetched, leaves your music playing rather than stopping it for nothing, and once per album rather than once per track. A box whose local player is deliberately stopped, as the Roon and HQPlayer profiles do, is recognised as having nothing to hand over instead of being reported as a failure; a refusal, which the player answers rather than raises, is no longer dropped — that silence is what let the whole thing go unnoticed.
  • [ops] Installing the interface no longer runs a command by accident, nor ships a truncated comment. Every single install of the interface printed install.sh: line 347: -: command not found in the middle of its success report — alarming, and meaningless to anyone reading it. The unit file the installer writes is assembled by a shell block that fills in the paths of your box as it goes, which also means it acts on anything written between backticks; a comment explaining the leading - in front of the command systemd runs had its - written that way, so the shell ran - as a command and wrote the comment to your box with the one character it is about missing. The service itself was never affected — the - in front of the command is outside the quotes and always arrived intact, which is why this survived so long. Both the stray command and the mangled comment are gone, and a test now refuses any backtick left unescaped in a block of either installer, including the blocks nested one level down, which are plain text while the package is built and become live code when the install script runs.

[0.9.54] - 2026-09-12

Added

  • [ui] The players say where the music comes from, not only what carries it. Qobuz, Tidal, HIGHRESAUDIO, a station, a media server down the hall and the files on the box all travel over the same engine, so the Audio Pipeline tab's Now Playing card could name nothing but that engine: a Qobuz album and a webradio both read "MPD". Where it comes from was in the box's answer all along — the origin, and the name of the server or the station when there is one — and no screen read it. The card now carries it beside the engine, and leaves it out when it would only say the same word twice. Three things the box published and nothing showed go with it: a stream from a media server reads MinimServer rather than "UPnP", a recognised station reads its own name rather than "Radio", and a playback driven from HQPlayer's own remote reads HQPlayer rather than "External", which named nobody. All of it comes from the live player feed the interface already holds open — the box is asked nothing it was not already answering. The pipeline's own data never carried the origin, and putting it there would have meant asking the music engine a second time for what the player had already worked out, then a third time for HQPlayer and a fourth for a network renderer. The queue is deliberately left as it was: what it publishes about a track names the kind of source, never the particular server or station, so it has nothing specific to show.
  • [ui] The support report gives the address to type. Every half was in it already and no line held them together: the port the interface is served on under WEB INTERFACE, the name the box announces under NETWORK, the box's own address on the network quoted only inside a certificate warning. Joining them by hand is what sends a reader to port 80, where nothing listens, and makes a box answering perfectly on 8080 read as unreachable — reported from the field, off a report exact on every count. WEB INTERFACE now carries an Address line: http://10.0.4.254:8080 · also http://musics.local:8080. The LAN address leads because it does not need mDNS to reach the reader, and a box whose announcement is broken is precisely the box a support report is opened for; the .local name follows as the one that survives the lease changing. Written only where the interface answered — a TLS handshake alone is enough for the core to record a scheme, so a laxer test would have printed an address two lines above the report's own "NOT REACHABLE on port 8443" — and the announced name is left out when the daemon that would answer it is known to be stopped, the two probes behind that line being independent and able to disagree. The port is dropped when it is the scheme's default, a :443 reading as a second, different address. Neither address is a promise: both rest on a port measured from the box itself, and the name needs mDNS to cross the reader's network.

Fixed

  • [ui] The Admin page could have lost the marks saying who is online. Its two halves disagreed on the name of the field carrying that list — one wrote it under one name, the other read another — on the path taken when the viewer is not an administrator. While the tab counter existed the mismatch threw an error loudly enough to be seen; removing the counter is what would have made it silent, leaving nothing where the list belongs and the browser failing on the first user drawn. Found in review of that removal, and fixed with it.
  • [ops] A box that serves only the interface now answers to its own name. The .local name is the one address that survives the router handing out a new lease, and the certificate has always promised it — but the small service that answers it, avahi-daemon, was installed by the core package. On the standard box that is invisible, because core and interface share one machine. On a split install it put the promise on one machine and the answer on the other: the machine you actually open in a browser is the one serving the interface, and nothing there installed the daemon, so the name worked only if that machine happened to have it for some unrelated reason — the same accident, moved one package along, that having it depend on AirPlay used to be. It is now installed, enabled and started by the interface package, which is also the one that issues the certificate making the promise, so the two can no longer be separated. Placed before the HTTPS branch rather than beside the certificate it serves: a name is typed the same way behind a reverse proxy as in front of one, and gating it on the mode would have dropped it silently for every plain-HTTP install. The core no longer installs it, since nothing addresses an audio-only machine by name and AirPlay carries the daemon itself. Failure stays non-fatal both ways — a box that cannot announce its name still works perfectly on its address — but it is no longer silent: the box is probed again after the install rather than apt's exit code being believed, because apt can report success without the command becoming available, and that was the one case that produced no message at all. The two package-list refreshes such an install could run seconds apart are now one.

Changed

  • [manual] The manual describes what you do, not how it was built. A user manual had been carrying the specification alongside it: how many genres and sub-genres each streaming catalogue publishes, how many playlists a Tidal shelf held the day it was counted, why six of Qobuz's fifteen shelves are left out, which arrangement HIGHRESAUDIO asked for and approved, and what we had or had not verified on an iPhone. None of it tells a reader what to press, all of it dates the moment a provider changes their catalogue, and a count taken once and published as a fact is the kind of sentence nothing signals when it stops being true. It also editorialised on the services it browses — naming nine Qobuz shelves "the ones worth browsing" is not the manual's call. The browsing sections now say what each shelf holds and what a tap does. Internals went the same way: the systemd mount unit behind a network share, the RPC daemons an NFS mount would need, MPD's control socket, why the certificate authority is fetched over plain HTTP. One correction is a matter of fact, not of tone: the manual said all three streaming services are reached "through their official APIs". That is true of HIGHRESAUDIO alone — Qobuz's credentials are read out of its web player and Tidal's are the reverse-engineered ones of its Android client — so the claim is gone rather than softened. Four passages were rewritten rather than cut: the External badge now says what actually produces it (another app casting to your box, or HQPlayer's own remote) instead of alluding to it; HTTPS is stated as the recommended mode and why; a note recommends reserving a fixed address on your router, next to the one about the .local name; and a scan for UPnP media servers now says it only reaches the box's own network. Landing FAQ regenerated from the manual, which had drifted from it.
  • [manual] Two things the manual claimed that the code does not do. It listed an AirPlay receiver among the destinations you can send music to. Your box receives AirPlay; it never sends to one, and no output in the selector does. And it promised that when Tidal produces no sound, Audiogravity "checks what Tidal actually serves and names the cause instead of leaving you with silence". That check exists only when HQPlayer is your output — on your own DAC there is no message, only the silence, which is the hardest symptom to report and the one the manual was quietly telling you would never happen. The entry now says which case gets a message and which does not, so a reader whose Tidal is mute knows to look there rather than at their sound card. Both were found by reading the code rather than the comments around it: one of those comments asserts the protection is general, and it is not. Elsewhere the same pass dropped what a reader cannot act on — how the support report folds repeated journal errors, why MPD's control port is closed, the handling of a certificate you supplied yourself — and stopped claiming a bit-perfect DAC "commonly" has no volume control, advice that cannot be checked and ended by suggesting you pick a different output.
  • [core] [ui] Switching the order of the local library no longer makes the box read the whole library again. All, Recent and A–Z are answered by the box, over the complete collection, so touching one asks for the first page — and asking for the first page was the only way the box had of knowing that somebody wanted fresh data. It could not tell "the same shelf, in another order" from "go and look again", so every change of order re-enumerated the collection: one question to list the albums, then one round trip per album to find the file its cover is read from. Measured on a box holding 475 albums: 0.19 s of that work against 0.002 s to answer from the list already in hand, and the ordering never needed the fresh copy — the box holds the whole collection and can turn it around on its own. The intention is now said rather than guessed: only the ↻ Refresh button asks for a new reading. Nothing else changes on screen, and nothing is traded away: the box now asks its own music index whether the collection has changed — one question, measured at 0.17 ms against the 190 ms of the reading it can spare — instead of throwing its list away every minute on principle. So a collection that grew underneath appears at once, wherever the change came from, where it used to need the shelf to be opened again at the right moment; and a collection that has not changed in a fortnight is no longer re-read once a minute for nothing. The reading itself is also no longer done twice at the same time: two open tabs, or a second tap on ↻ before the first finished, each started their own walk through the collection, and they now share one — the same guard the streaming catalogues were given in 0.9.53, on the one enumeration that reads the box's own daemon.

Removed

  • [core] [ui] The Admin tab no longer counts who is connected. A figure beside Admin in the tab list read "people connected / accounts on the box" — shown whenever the tabs run down the side of the screen, which on a phone is always. It answers a question nobody asks of a box that plays music at home, and every load of the interface asked the box for it. Who is online is still shown where it means something: on the Admin page itself, each user carries a green mark for as long as they have the interface open. The interface now also ignores that figure if a box still sends it, so an interface updated ahead of its core stops showing it straight away rather than waiting for both halves to be upgraded — worth saying because admin is the name of a real tab, so a rule as broad as "keep the counters that name a tab" would have kept this one.

[0.9.53] - 2026-09-07

Added

  • [core] [ui] The radio is a source, like Qobuz or the local library. It streams over the same engine as the three streaming services, so it held no entry of its own and had no card on the sources screen — it existed only as a tab. It is now listed, it can carry the playing badge, and its queue is the shared MPD queue instead of an empty one. Its catalogue keeps its own screen with its own filters (country, genre, codec), so it is deliberately left out of the common search picker, and the tab bar drops the two tabs it cannot serve rather than offering an empty grid and an error.
  • [core] [ui] Every source now says what it is — sources[].kind: library, radio, streaming, roon or input. The sources screen filters on that instead of a hardcoded list of six ids, which is why the radio had no card in the first place: every source added to the backend had to be remembered in the interface too, and one was not.
  • [ui] Inputs are listed as sources. AirPlay and the UPnP bridge do put out sound, so they belong on the screen — but an input hides the source behind it (what a phone pushes over AirPlay has an identity the box cannot see), so it holds no catalogue: it is listed, it lights up while it plays, and touching it raises the player rather than a grid.

Fixed

  • [core] The public audio proxy no longer hands out what went wrong upstream, and no longer says nothing when it matters. The two endpoints renderers fetch audio from take no API key — they cannot, since a renderer sends no headers — so anything they return is readable by anything on the network. When a track could not be resolved they returned the streaming service's own error verbatim: its status, and a slice of its response body carrying identifiers of ours. Meanwhile the box's journal recorded nothing at all, so the one place the reason belonged was the one place it was missing. The caller is now told only that the track is unavailable, and the reason is written down where it is useful. Two neighbouring gaps went with it: a CDN that refuses a signed URL — a stale token, a mangled signature — left no trace either, which is exactly the failure this machinery exists to catch; and a track id chosen by the caller went onto a log line unescaped, so a crafted address could append a line of its own and have it read as if the box had written it. Ids and third-party error text are now stripped of anything that could end a line before being logged. A bare /highresaudio/stream/ with no track at all used to become an outbound request to HIGHRESAUDIO for whoever asked; it is answered locally.
  • [ui] The sources screen named the engine, not the source. A webradio playing showed "Local Library" as the active source, because Qobuz, Tidal, HIGHRESAUDIO, the radio and local files all travel over the same engine and the badge was keyed on that. It is now resolved from the content, so a station lights the Radio card and a Qobuz album the Qobuz card. A UPnP server lights its own card too, instead of the local library claiming a track served by a machine down the hall.
  • [ui] Several sources playing at once are all shown as playing. Casting AirPlay to one output while Qobuz plays on another lit only one card — whichever had started first — because the screen read the single source the player follows. It reads each entry's own state now.
  • [core] A second stream's card no longer waits for a page reload. The event that tells the interface something moved was raised only for the source the player follows, so a stream pausing, switching from a station to an album, or simply advancing a track published nothing at all. Verified not to add traffic: with two streams playing, twenty-two seconds of the live stream carry four events.
  • [ui] The "… is now playing" banner's Switch button lands where the banner says. It named the station correctly and then opened the local album grid.
  • [core] /library/albums and /library/search answer the same way for the same source. One returned an empty list for a mistyped source id — which reads as "this source is empty" and hides the typo — while the other returned an error for a perfectly real source. An unknown id is now a 400 on both; a real source that simply holds no local catalogue is a 200 with an empty result on both.
  • [core] Tidal now really says when a subscription has ended — 0.9.52 shipped that unable to work. The card read the plan with the stored access token as it stood, and that token lives a week with nothing renewing it unless something plays. On a box whose owner listens to anything else, Tidal answered 401, the answer came back "unknown", and unknown reads as subscribed — so the card announced Hi-Res on an account that had none, which is the very thing the feature was added to stop. Measured on a box the morning after the release: the token had expired 100 hours earlier, and one renewal through another path was enough to make the same lookup answer correctly. The lookup now renews like every other caller.

Changed

  • [core] The two addresses a streaming service serves audio from now behave the same way. One of them accepted shapes the other refused — an address with nothing after it, or one spanning several segments — and each of those became a request to HIGHRESAUDIO, on an endpoint that takes no key because the players on the network cannot send one. Nothing recorded why it had been built that way, so rather than guess it was measured: 940 track identifiers from the shop, the editorial playlists and the purchases, in two shapes, all of them made of the same kind of identifier joined by underscores. Not one contains the character the permissive form existed to allow, and that kind of identifier cannot contain it. Both addresses now answer the same, and anything that is not a track is refused before the service is asked.
  • [core] [ui] The box names the music on it "Local Library", everywhere, and once. It used to answer "MPD" — the name of the engine that plays the files, correct for the engine and wrong for the shelf of music it carries. The interface corrected it on the way to the screen, with a table of its own, so the two disagreed and only one of them was ever seen. The name now comes from the box, and the interface shows what it is given: the same words on the source card, on the now-playing badge and in the queue header, where the badge used to read the shorter "Library". Two more translation tables went with it — one existed only to recover the word "Local", the other rebuilt, in the interface, an answer the box already had.
  • [core] [ui] A UPnP media server is now identified, not merely named. When a track played from one of them, the box said which server by name only, so the sources screen had to match names to know which card to light — and two servers sharing a name lit both, with nothing to say which one was actually playing. The server's own identifier now travels with the track it serves, from the moment it is queued. On a network with one media server nothing changes; with two named alike, the right card lights. The same identity travels on the two paths where it was missing: a track cast to a network renderer, and one pushed to HQPlayer — where a local file now lights the Local Library card instead of naming the processor playing it.
  • [core] Opening a streaming shelf no longer asks the same question several times at once. Each catalogue list — genres, categories, labels, search filters, playlist groupings — is fetched once and remembered, but between the moment a screen finds nothing remembered and the moment the answer is stored there is a wait, and every other reader arriving in that window used to start the same fetch again. Measured on HIGHRESAUDIO: five readers arriving together sent five identical requests; they now send one. Qobuz and Tidal already had the guard, written by hand in each, and its easily-forgotten half — checking a second time after waiting your turn — is now written once for all of them, together with the ceiling that stops a catalogue walk from crowding the connection pool the audio itself uses.
  • [core] Qobuz and HIGHRESAUDIO now share the one route their audio actually travels through. The two services hand the box a signed address and nothing else to do with it, so their proxy endpoints had been written twice — the second created by copying the first, down to the wording of its documentation. Nothing was wrong with either, but the only change they had seen since had to be made in both files at once, and their tests had drifted apart: the byte relay was covered on one side, the purchased-track address on the other, and neither had the other's case. One envelope now serves both, and one test suite runs every case against both, so a behaviour can no longer be proven for one service and merely assumed for the other. Tidal keeps its own route on purpose: it rebuilds a stream rather than passing one through.
  • [core] Reading a configuration file is written once. Every service carried its own copy of the same few lines — does the file exist, decode it, log and carry on when it does not read — and the copies had already drifted: one decoded with a different library, another said nothing when it succeeded. The shared reader keeps the distinction that matters (a file that is not there yet is normal, a file that is there and unreadable is not) and restores the traceback that pointed at the failing service, which the copies had between them started to lose.
  • [core] The signature Qobuz requires on every playback request is computed in one place. It was written twice — once to play a track, once to find which credential works — and a wrong signature is indistinguishable from a wrong credential in Qobuz's answer, so a drift between the two would have been read as an expired account.

[0.9.52] - 2026-09-06

Fixed

  • [ui] A box that answers, and says what went wrong, is no longer reported as unreachable. The interface held that the core never returns 502 or 504 — only a proxy standing in for a stopped box does — so it replaced both with "Unable to connect to server. Please check your connection." The core returns both, and always has: 502 when the licence server is unreachable or answers nonsense, when a Qobuz sign-in cannot start, when a streaming favourite could not be written; 504 when the HIGHRESAUDIO catalogue is answering but slowly. On those screens the reader was sent to check their network while the box was fine, replying, and naming the real cause — with the badge beside the message reading CONNECTED. One rule now covers all three statuses: a 502, 503 or 504 carrying a message is the core speaking and the message is shown; one carrying none came from a proxy, since a proxy answers with HTML and leaves no message behind.

  • [core] [ui] A slow HIGHRESAUDIO no longer reads as a broken box. When their catalogue took its time, every shelf but one answered a flat "HIGHRESAUDIO unavailable" — the box owning a failure that was not its own. The honest answer existed already, and said the useful thing (their cold filtered search takes tens of seconds and then serves the same one instantly), but it was written on one route out of sixteen: the advanced search. It was never a decision, only a clause copied by hand forty times and completed once. The wording is now shared by every shelf, and says what it can honestly say everywhere — "HIGHRESAUDIO took too long to answer. It is not the box: the service is slow to reply." — without carrying over a promise about retrying that was measured on the search alone. The interface no longer classifies that answer as "something replied instead of your box": it never did so on the shelves, which show the core's own sentence, but the rule that said so was false and would have caught a licence or passkey screen the day one of them answered the same way.

  • [core] A HIGHRESAUDIO playlist grouping numbered zero disappeared from the strip. The row was kept on truthiness, and 0 is falsy, so a grouping HRA published simply was not there — with nothing anywhere to say one had gone missing. The same defect sat on the path that opens a grouping, one function away, and survived only because the ids are stored as text where "0" reads as true. Both are fixed, and a grouping with an unusable id (an empty one) is now dropped on purpose rather than listed as a shelf nobody can open. Two groupings sharing a title are no longer swallowed in silence: HRA's own filter addresses a grouping by its title, so two of them cannot be told apart by HRA either and nothing on our side recovers the second — what was worth fixing is that it vanished quietly, and it is now said once, with both ids.

Changed

  • [core] 281 lines of code nothing called are gone. Twenty-seven definitions with a single reference in the whole repository — their own: eight request/response models never sent or returned, nine client and infrastructure methods, three catalogue methods never wired to a route, and five helpers. An inventory had listed them a fortnight earlier; it was re-run before deleting rather than trusted, and that mattered — one entry had since gained a caller and was kept, another had already been deleted. Each definition was cut at the span its own syntax tree gives, not where a search suggested a block might end, and the published API of the library's fifty routes is byte-identical afterwards.

  • [core] The answer a streaming shelf gives when it cannot serve is written once. Forty copies of the same three clauses, differing only by the service named in the last one; thirty-two routes now share it. Three routes deliberately keep their own — an unknown Qobuz shelf and a malformed Tidal page or section answer 400, where the shared rule would make the same class of failure a 500 — and both the code and API.md name them, so a client is not told to expect one mapping for the whole catalogue. An unexpected failure is finally logged with its traceback: centralising the handler is what made that a one-line change instead of twenty-seven.

Added

  • [core] [ui] A streaming account whose subscription has ended now says so, instead of announcing a quality nothing plays. The sources screen showed Hi-Res · FR for Tidal and Studio · Hi-Res 24/192 for Qobuz — but both of those are what the box asks for, not what the service grants, and the two part company the day a plan ends. Measured on a real lapsed Tidal account: four tracks of 1'54, 7'10, 10'07 and 23'33 all come back as a 29.9-second manifest, at any quality setting, while the connection stays green and the catalogue keeps browsing. Qobuz does the same, serving a 30-second MP3 for every track it is asked for. Nothing in the playback path can tell that from a short track, so the music simply stopped after half a minute with no explanation anywhere. Each card now says what will actually be heard — No subscription · 30-second previews — and the state is re-read rather than stored once at sign-in, which is what makes it still true the day the plan lapses. The three services publish the same two fields for it, and a client reads them the same way whichever answered. What is not the same, and is documented as such, is what a lapsed account may still play: Qobuz and Tidal keep the whole catalogue browsable in excerpt form, while HIGHRESAUDIO refuses it outright and leaves only the purchases.

Fixed

  • [core] A Qobuz account whose subscription has ended could not connect at all — and was told the wrong reason. The sign-in refused any account Qobuz answers without a streaming-rights block, which is exactly what it removes when a plan ends. The browser was shown "Failed to exchange the authorization code. Please try again." — naming a code that was perfectly good, inviting a retry that could never succeed, while the real cause never left the journal. So the account was locked out of Qobuz entirely, including anything it had bought. Measured on such an account: Qobuz signs it in, answers its purchases, favourites, playlists, search and new releases with 200, and hands out a playable URL for every track. It now connects like any other, plays what Qobuz gives it, and the screen says why the music is short. HIGHRESAUDIO made and undid this same mistake a fortnight earlier; the reasoning is now written down in one place rather than learned twice.

Changed

  • [core] Asking Tidal what format it serves no longer costs a request when the box already holds the track. The format probe that runs before a push to HQPlayer called Tidal even for a track sitting complete in the seek cache — where the relay, one line further on, serves that file without calling Tidal at all. The cached file is the answer: the remux runs -c:a copy -f flac and is only kept on a clean exit, which a non-FLAC stream never gets. Replaying a track through HQPlayer now costs nothing where it used to cost one round trip.

Changed

  • [ui] The application's icon stands beside its name on every screen that shows the name. Two surfaces still showed the wordmark on its own. One is the passkey panel: it replaces the sign-in card's contents entirely, so the lockup somebody had been looking at a second earlier vanished at the exact moment they were asked to authenticate. The other is the start-up screen the app paints while it loads. Both now carry the same row as the sign-in header, from one shared rule rather than three copies of it. The start-up screen keeps a hairline of its own instead of the theme's, and that is not a detail: it paints a fixed ground — #12141c, the colour the installed app declares for itself — whatever appearance the interface is set to, because it is drawn before any theme exists. The icon is a near-black square, so on that ground it needs its edges given back in both appearances. Measured against that ground, the theme's border was worth 1.21:1 under Gravity, 1.28:1 under Minimal and 1.78:1 under Slate, against 15.28:1 for the screen's own — in a dark appearance the outline was there in the stylesheet and invisible on the screen.

[0.9.51] - 2026-09-01

Added

  • [ops] [core] A box now answers to its own name, and its certificate carries that name. Every certificate a box issues already promised <hostname>.local — the one address that keeps working when the router hands the box a different IP, which is the day every bookmark and every home-screen icon breaks at once, an installed app reopening for ever the address it was added from with no address bar and no error page to say why. Until now that name only answered by accident, on boxes whose owner happened to install AirPlay: its Debian package pulls in the service that announces names, and nothing else did. A box used as a local output or an HQPlayer endpoint had no reason to have it, so its certificate named an address nothing on the network answered to. That service is now installed, enabled and started with the rest — and if it cannot start, the install says so and carries on, because a box that cannot announce its name is still a box that works on its address. Two measured corrections came with it: the name is built from the first label of the hostname, which is what the announcing service actually publishes (a box called musics.1 answers to musics.local and never to musics.1.local), and a certificate that does not carry that name is now reissued. Only the address was ever checked before, so a certificate naming the wrong one was served for its full term — up to 825 days — and nothing on a running box would have replaced it. The authority every device was told to trust is not reissued in the process, which is what keeps the phones and computers of the house from all having to be set up again.

  • [core] [ui] The support report answers what a certificate incident actually asks. Five facts were missing, and a real case turned on every one of them: when the served certificate was issued — the day it changes is the day devices trusting it stop, and it was computable only by subtracting the issuing policy from the expiry, by hand; when the local authority was created and its fingerprint — an upgrade never reissues it, so those two tell "this authority changed, and every device stopped at once" from "this one device was never set up"; whether an interface's address is leased or fixed, which is whether it can still move; the name the box announces, asked of the announcing service itself rather than guessed from the hostname; and the date of the last self-update, since "done — 0.9.49 → 0.9.50" says nothing about whether that was an hour ago or last month. The report also flags an announced name its certificate does not carry — both facts were already in it, two sections apart, and nobody diffs them by eye.

  • [core] [ui] Qobuz browses like HIGHRESAUDIO does: five shelves, each opening a strip of its own. Qobuz had four buttons — Favorites, New Releases, Selection, Playlists — two of which were a single hard-coded shelf apiece out of the many its catalogue holds, which is the same defect HIGHRESAUDIO's bar had before it was restructured. The bar is now Favorites, Purchases, Shelves, Playlists, Genres, using the strip mechanism the HIGHRESAUDIO menu already introduced; nothing new was built for it. Shelves lists nine: New Releases, All New Releases, Selection, Qobuzissims, Press Awards, Best Sellers, Most Streamed, Ideal Discography and Harmonia Mundi. Qobuz accepts fifteen names there and states them itself in the body of its own error; six are left out, each for a measured reason — recent-releases answers nothing at all, most-featured returns the same fifteen albums as most-streamed (two names, one shelf), and universal-classic, universal-jazz, universal-jeunesse and universal-chanson are one undifferentiated shelf whose content matches none of the four names, so it could not be labelled truthfully. Purchases is new and is Qobuz's answer to the HIGHRESAUDIO vault, but a simpler one: a bought album is read and played through the ordinary path, ids and all, so nothing about the queue had to change. Playlists gains the account's own beside the ones Qobuz publishes — they were simply never listed, though the route that reads their tracks already worked on them. Qobuz has no third tree: its two published names, editor-picks and last-created, answer the same playlists id for id. Asking for a shelf outside the nine now answers 400 rather than 503 — a request that can never succeed is not a service that is down — which also makes the six excluded ones genuinely unreachable rather than merely undocumented. Genres brings thirteen genres and seventy-eight sub-genres, none of which were reachable before. A genre is not a shelf on Qobuz — it only ever narrows one, which is why the genre grid browses the Selection: measured across eight sub-genres and four genres, a sub-genre returns nothing at all on the new releases and between one and thirty albums on the Selection. Two genres publish one sub-genre or none, which is the catalogue and not a failure.

  • [core] Qobuz search asks once instead of three times. The same words were sent to the same endpoint three times over, once per kind of result, when leaving the kind out fills every one of them in a single answer — measured, 1.08 / 0.49 / 0.29 s for the one call against 2.87 / 0.64 / 0.70 s for the three. Album results also carry their year now, since every listing reads albums through one shared piece of code. There is no advanced search for Qobuz and there will not be one on their current API: the genre filter it accepts on a search is disregarded — four queries crossed with four genres each returned the same ten albums, filtered or not — and so are hires, release_type, order and sort. Filtering on our side would narrow the page of fifty rather than the catalogue, which is the very defect the local library's old Recent button had.

  • [core] [ui] Tidal browses the way the other two services do, and two of its shelves start working again. Tidal had five buttons above the album grid, and two of them were empty: Charts returned nothing at all, while Editorial showed the chart playlists mixed in with its own. The cause was the same for both — the box found their content by matching the word "chart" inside the headings Tidal writes on its own home page, and Tidal has since renamed that row "The Hits". Nothing reads a heading any more: the charts come from Tidal's own Top page, and the editorial shelf now excludes them instead of swallowing them. The bar is now five shelves — Favorites, Shelves, Playlists, Genres, Moods — each opening a strip, as HIGHRESAUDIO and Qobuz already do. Shelves lists the catalogue's own, Playlists gathers its three surfaces on one strip (the editors' selections, the account's own, and the charts), Genres brings twenty that were unreachable before, and Moods is new here — the shelf HIGHRESAUDIO has and Qobuz publishes nothing for. A shelf that holds nothing is left out, and that is decided by asking rather than by believing Tidal: one shelf declares it has albums and answers an error, while another declares the same and holds two hundred and eighty-one playlists, so trusting the declaration would have shown one dead button and hidden a real shelf. Two things Tidal does not have, and that are therefore absent: purchases, since it sells nothing, and sub-genres — its genres are one level deep, so the strip does not drill. One Tidal shelf could not be played at all: Exclusif is two hundred and eighty-one playlists and no album, and every card on it was taken for an album — pressing play asked for the tracks of an album that does not exist, and the favourite star would have filed a playlist among the albums. What a shelf holds is now stated by the box rather than guessed from the shape of a row, on every source. A sixth shelf, Explore, opens Tidal's own navigation: its 42 entries, which include HiRes, seven decades from the 1950s to the 2010s, 52 record labels and its rich genre pages.
