A page there holds either further pages — Record Labels leads to 52 — or sections of content, so the strip drills the first in place, the way the genres do, and shows the second on a strip of its own. Each section pages properly, through the handle Tidal gives it: asked for without the two parameters it insists on, that handle answers an error that reads exactly like "this list cannot be paged", which is how it came within an inch of being written off as a dozen frozen thumbnails.

Fixed

  • [core] An accented genre no longer lands at the end of a strip that claims to be alphabetical. Seen on screen, on Qobuz's classical sub-genres: Mélodies & Lieder sat between Musique vocale sacrée and Opéra, and Électronique ou concrète was exiled to the far end. The sort only folded case, which leaves an accent at its own code point — é is 233, past every plain letter — so every accented name was pushed behind every unaccented one. It now folds accents too, which is what puts the strip in the order a reader looks for. Not a French curiosity: Qobuz answers its genre names in the language of the account's country, and that country is not something the listener can change from their own account page, so an accented alphabet is the ordinary case there rather than the exception — a German or a Spanish account met the same jumble. A handful of letters carry no accent to fold — Ø, Æ, Œ, Ł, Đ, Þ — and Unicode offers nothing to do with them, so they were stranding Ørjan Nilsen, Æther, Łódź and Þórir behind Zappa: the same symptom, and not a hypothetical on a catalogue carrying labels like Norway's 2L. They are now mapped to the letters a reader files them under. Ligatures and typographic forms come along for the ride, so ﬂute sorts with flute and № 5 with no 5. The sort is shared with HIGHRESAUDIO, whose own lists are unchanged by the fix, and it is deliberately not a full alphabetical collation: languages disagree on where a folded letter belongs — Danish files Ø after Z on purpose — and settling that needs a locale the box has no reason to trust.

Changed

  • [ui] The sign-in card and the footer carry the application's own icon, drawn the same way. Beside the wordmark, the way the licence server's own sign-in sets it — the two now open on the same lockup. It is apple-touch-180.png, not the 512px icon: the same artwork, 61 kB instead of 465, and already in the service worker's precache, so on a box where the app is installed it costs no download at all. Sized at 36px against the mark's 42, which puts it a little taller than the capitals — the proportion that makes an icon and a name read as one block rather than a stamp set beside a word. On a dark card the icon needed help: its artwork is a near-black square, so it lost its edges against a card measured at #0a0a0a under Minimal and #12141c under Gravity, leaving the glyph floating with no bounds. A hairline in the theme's own border colour gives the square back its shape, rather than inventing a second, lighter icon — what the owner sees here stays what they see on their home screen. The footer already drew the same artwork, and drew it differently: 50px filling the bar's whole height, corners at 4 % of the side against the card's 22 %, so one square read as two marks depending on the screen. Both now name one shared rule, and the preview that opens when the footer icon is clicked joins them — its corners were flatter still. The radius is written as a proportion rather than a length, which is what lets the same rule serve a 36px icon and a 192px preview. And that footer icon is finally reachable: it opens a preview, but it was a bare image with a click handler, so no amount of tabbing ever landed on it and nothing announced it as something to press.

Fixed

  • [site] Links to a section of the manual land on the section. The manual's top bar stays on screen as you scroll, and nothing offset the headings for it: following a link to a section scrolled the page so the heading arrived exactly underneath that bar, out of sight. The reader landed mid-paragraph with no title to say where, which reads as a broken link. It affected every anchor in the manual, including the ones chapters use to point at each other and the FAQ on the landing page.
  • [core] Two lines of the support report said something they did not mean. no_license reads as a verdict from the licence server; it is what a box reports when it holds no licence file at all and therefore sent nothing — the ordinary state of a trial, and a hunt for a problem that does not exist. And MPD's last error was printed bare, as if it had just happened: MPD keeps it until something clears it and never dates it, so a stale line was there to be blamed for tonight's symptom. Both now say what they are.

[0.9.50] - 2026-08-31

Added

  • [core] [ui] HIGHRESAUDIO's menu, laid out the way its publisher asked for it. Their own audit put it plainly: eighteen pills ran end to end in a single bar, in the order their API happens to publish them, with the fourteen shop categories spilling off the edge — and the remark that started this was that the list needed organising, not lengthening. The bar is now seven shelves — Favorites, Vault, Categories, Charts, Playlists, Labels, Genres — each opening a strip of its own below it, which is the mechanism the genres already used; nothing new was built for it. Three shelves are new. Charts comes from an endpoint absent from their documentation and found in their own JavaScript client, as does Labels, which lists the seven they named, 2L and audite included — five of those seven serve the same albums as the … Highlights categories, which is their catalogue and not a fault, so nothing is de-duplicated. And Playlists gains Genre and Theme beside Editorial and Mine: not a third tree but two of HRA's own groupings of the selections they publish, so a grouped browse asks for the editorial tree and names the grouping beside it. Their specification presents those two as filters of the playlist endpoint; measured, they are not — that endpoint answers no data found for a genre, and only the dedicated routes reply, which is why the code takes the longer path their document says is unnecessary. Nothing is hidden and nothing is re-arranged beyond the order they asked for: the six categories they named come first, in their sequence, and the other eight follow in HRA's. Three of the fourteen appear nowhere in their plan — Bestsellers, UNAMAS, Featured Album — and all three serve real albums (50, 50 and 26, and the first two share only nine titles, so they are not one list served twice), so removing them would have put catalogue out of reach. The one shelf they asked for that does not exist is Explore: two of its four entries are already categories, and the other two match nothing their API serves — not a category, a genre, a theme, one of the thirty-seven moods, or a label — so it is not built, and the question is with them.
  • [core] The genres and themes of HIGHRESAUDIO's playlists are listed alphabetically. They arrive in an order that follows no rule anyone can read: the identifiers form a complete series yet come back as 4, 7, 5, 8, 1, 6, 3, 2, 9 — neither the identifier, nor the alphabet, nor the number of playlists, and no field in the answer explains it. Stable across repeated calls, so it may well be deliberate on their side; unreadable on ours either way, and a strip whose order cannot be predicted is worse for a listener than one that can. The same treatment the catalogue's own genres already had, at HIGHRESAUDIO's explicit request, and with the same key, so R&B still sits beside Rock. They are being told, so they can say if that order was a ranking after all — restoring it is one line.
  • [core] [ui] HIGHRESAUDIO's advanced search, copied from their own application, defects included. Searching Queen returned a list with little to do with Queen, and filling in a filter with the search box left empty did nothing at all — no result, no message. Both causes were ours. Their advanced endpoint requires no search term (label=ECM, composer=Bach, artist=Queen, genre=Jazz and format=fl192 each fill a page on their own, measured through the box), yet the box refused to ask below three characters — a rule that belongs to their quick search and was carried onto this one by mistake; and the interface only ran a search when the box held something, so the explanation it could have shown was unreachable. The form is now theirs, criterion for criterion: free text — the search box already on screen — artist, composer, label, year, format, mood and the order. Two fields are deliberately absent, and for different reasons. album belongs to their shop's nine-field form — a different back-end — and not to their application's, so copying the application leaves it out; the measurement only confirms nothing was lost, since the streaming API accepts it and disregards it (artist=Queen&release=2026 returned the same thirteen albums id for id with no album, with Innuendo, and with a nonsense value). Genre is absent because it has a shelf of its own on the browse. Year is HRA's own name for the year an album went online, not the year it was recorded — Innuendo is production year 1991 and reads 2026 — and it is named as they name it. The order offers their nine entries; + sorts ascending and - descending, measured on all four criteria, and their own web client labels them the other way round, so the measurement is what the list follows. An order they do not know does not get ignored by them, it returns nothing at all, so the list is closed and anything outside it is refused rather than forwarded. One defect of theirs is passed through untouched, on purpose: setting a format discards the words typed — queen and london in FLAC 192 both return the same fifty albums, the whole 192 catalogue — which is how their application behaves, and a form that quietly behaved differently from the one people already use would be the worse surprise. A second one is passed through the same way: mood is accepted and disregarded — measured on a result small enough not to be capped, the same thirteen albums came back id for id with no mood, with Amused and with Melancholic, two moods of opposite families, and a mood on its own answers nothing at all. It stays because the field is theirs and because that class of defect is not a property of this one control: album and the direction of importDate are the other two. All three are reported to them. What the form does not do is ask a question that cannot be answered: an order with nothing to arrange returns an empty list, so the interface says an order arranges results — add something to search for rather than spending their slowest call to come back empty. The form is applied by its Search button rather than on every keystroke: this is the one slow endpoint they expose (measured on the box, three different cold searches each ran past thirty seconds and came back in about a second on the retry, their side having cached them), and the box runs two of them at a time, so seven controls each firing their own would queue behind one another.

Fixed

  • [ui] HIGHRESAUDIO's playlist covers are shown whole. Their audit said the artwork was a rectangle displayed as a square; measured on thirty-two of them, taken from the oldest to the newest of their catalogue, every single one is 410 × 205 — a banner, not a sleeve. Shown in a square cell, only the middle half survived, and these are compositions laid out across their width: the title and the label's mark are exactly what the crop threw away. The cell now takes the shape of the artwork. Each place keeps whichever dimension its layout owns — a card fixes its width, so the banner is half as tall; a list row fixes its height, so the thumbnail is twice as wide and the rhythm of a list mixing both shapes never changes from one row to the next. One defect came out of that change and was caught on a real screen rather than in a test: the row's cover column named a fixed width, so the wider thumbnail overflowed it and printed itself over the first third of every title. Nothing could have caught it in the test suite — the test environment lays nothing out — so the column now takes its width from the cell, and a guard reads the stylesheet rather than the DOM to refuse a hard-coded one.
  • [ui] The favourite star and the add button no longer sit on two lines in a search result. A result row is a three-column layout — sleeve, text, one place at the end — and an album row on a streaming source carries two controls there, the star and the +. That is a fourth item for three places: the + wrapped onto a second line and came back at the far left, under the sleeve, with the star stranded alone on the right. It has been that way since the star was put on search rows on 2026-07-13, on Qobuz, Tidal and HIGHRESAUDIO alike, and it is the one screen in the application where a row carries both. The two now share a single place. The test environment lays nothing out, which is exactly why nothing caught it, so the guard counts the row's children instead — and refuses the other possible repair, a fourth column, which would leave a gap at the end of every row that carries only one control.
  • [core] A malformed row from HIGHRESAUDIO now costs one album, not a whole shelf. Their answers are not typed, and two of the shelves come from endpoints missing from their documentation altogether. A row that was not an object raised where the album is built and turned the shelf into a server error, rather than into a grid one entry short — the three other places that read their lists already guarded against it.

Changed

  • [ui] A streaming service is now shown by its own mark, and the library stopped pretending to report a state. HIGHRESAUDIO already appeared as its logo above the album grid, because that logo is its name written out; Qobuz, Tidal and Roon were still spelled in capitals beside it. All four now carry their own artwork, in a light and a dark variant apiece, each set at its own size, because these four images do not hold the same thing: their proportions run from 1.4:1 to 7.5:1, the share of the picture the name itself occupies runs from 11 % to 99 %, and the ink each one paints on screen differed by a factor of two. So no single rule — one height, one width, one size of name — makes them comparable; the sizes are a judgement, and the stylesheet carries the measurements it was made from. HIGHRESAUDIO keeps the size its own composition needs, and Tidal stays the odd one: its logotype is spaced so widely that matching the others' weight would make it half again as long as any of them. Each stays an <img> with its name as alt text, never a CSS background: a background paints nothing at all when the file is missing or the browser is in forced-colours mode, and the line would then be empty with no way to tell which source is open. The green pulsing dot that opened that line is gone with them. It had exactly one value — there was no state it could report — so it announced "live" at the one moment that is true by construction: you have just chosen the source and its covers are already on screen. A status light that never changes is a word spent in advance, unavailable the day a source really is in difficulty; and it animated for as long as the page stayed open, on a machine whose CPU budget is a first-order constraint.

[0.9.49] - 2026-08-29

Added

  • [ui] Light or dark, decided before signing in. The appearance switch lived in the configuration panel, which is behind the sign-in form: someone starting the box at night got the light palette full in the face until they were through it, and a reader who prefers dark had no way to say so on the one screen they always meet. A button now sits in the corner of the sign-in card — a moon on a light page, a sun on a dark one, always naming where pressing it takes you. It switches the appearance, not the theme: which of Minimal, Slate or Gravity applies is still chosen in the panel, and the button only flips that theme's palette. The choice is remembered where the pre-paint script reads it, so the next cold load opens dark with no white flash, and the browser's own chrome — the status bar of an app launched from a phone's home screen — follows in the same instant. The sequence behind both controls is now written once and shared, which is what the panel's switch had that the new button was missing: the event that repaints that chrome inside the application. Two faults found on the way, in code that had only ever run before the first paint and was now being called again on every flip: two thirds of the Safari remedy were absent, so a home-screen launch would have kept the old status-bar tint; and an unknown theme name — themes are extensible — threw after the palette and the stored preference had changed, leaving the switch half applied.

Changed

  • [core] [ui] [ops] The interactive API reference is off unless a box asks for it. /docs, /redoc and /openapi.json answered without an API key — they have to, since Swagger fetches the schema from the browser with no header of its own — so every box served the full map of its API to anything that could reach it: measured on a deployed core, 282 kB describing 188 routes, authentication, passkeys, licensing, system and self-update among them. None of it is an access, every route keeps its guard, but it is the floor plan, handed to an unauthenticated caller on a network that may hold a guest's phone or a compromised appliance. A new API_DOCS_ENABLED setting now governs the three routes together — together, because hiding the page while leaving the schema mounted would have removed the convenience and kept the exposure — and it ships off. They are unmounted rather than protected, so a request gets a plain 404: a route that does not exist should not answer 401. Boxes already installed are switched off at their next upgrade, and a box whose owner turned the reference on keeps it. The interface follows without being told: the core's entry point lists the endpoints it actually serves, docs is simply absent when they are not mounted, and the two buttons that opened Swagger disappear — a button leading to a 404 is worse than no button. Development keeps it on, which is also what the tooling that compares API.md to the live schema needs. One caveat for anyone switching it on: the Swagger page loads its own JavaScript from a CDN, so on a box with no route to the internet it opens blank.
  • [ui] The Audiogravity wordmark is set in type, the way the landing page sets it. The interface drew its name from an SVG file, and that file was never a logo: it holds two text nodes asking for Helvetica, never converted to outlines. Every Linux and every Android the interface runs on lacks that face, so the mark was rendered in whatever the system substituted — different from device to device, while looking deliberate on the machine it was authored on — and dark mode had to repaint the image with a filter, because a picture cannot take a colour. The name is now text, in the typeface the interface already ships: same treatment as the landing page, down to the superscript ty, on the splash screen, the title bar, the sidebar, the sign-in card and the credit line of both footers. The splash screen was the last to keep its own version — capitals, wide tracking — and it had a reason the others did not: it paints before any theme exists, so it can read no token. Only its colour needed that exemption, which is why the shared class declares none. Sizes were matched to the capital height the drawn mark occupied, so nothing moved. The footers were a case of their own — they already wrote the brand correctly but without its styling, so it came out at the weight of the link around it with the browser's default superscript, more than twice the size the mark uses. Reaching the weight the landing uses required declaring the Inter faces over the range they actually carry: the files hold every weight from 100 to 900, the stylesheet claimed 400 to 700, and a declared range silently clamps the rest — the heaviest weight the interface asked for was being rendered one step lighter. The declaration costs no bytes: it is the same file.
  • [ui] The pipeline tab is named for what it shows. Its heading read AUDIO DSP PIPELINE, on the desktop layout and on the mobile bar. There is no DSP stage: the placeholder node it once had left the diagram when the three services the box never shipped were removed, and signal processing is not on the roadmap. A title announcing a stage the page cannot show is a promise the page has to break.
  • [ui] The sign-in badge names the core, not "API". API · CONNECTED above the form named a protocol nobody signing in has to think about; what the check actually reaches is the box's own core — the word the installer, the service unit and the rest of the interface already use for it. The footer of the application says CORE: before the address for the same reason; the button beside it keeps "API", since what it opens really is the API reference.
  • [ui] The configuration panel drops the word before its version number. The footer of the panel read Version: v0.9.49-dev; the number and the icon beside it say what they are.

Fixed

  • [ui] Five files the interface claimed to cache, and one it offered as an app icon, did not exist. None of it failed in a way anyone could see. The service worker's app-shell list named three icons under names the repository has never carried — apple-touch-icon.png for apple-touch-180.png, favicon-32x32.png for favicon-32.png, favicon-16x16.png for favicon-16.png — beside two logo_audiogravity_*.png that exist nowhere at all; cache.add() catches its own failure, so this was five 404s on every install and update of the worker, and icons that were never actually precached. The push handler asked for two more absent files, so every notification was drawn with the browser's default glyph instead of the product's icon. And the web manifest offered the retired wordmark SVG as an app icon at sizes: "any" — the entry a browser reaches for first on a scalable surface — which should never have been there in the first place: that file is a logotype five and a half times wider than tall, and an app icon is square. All of them are now asserted against the repository: the precache list, the images the worker names elsewhere, and every manifest icon, shortcuts and screenshots included.
  • [core] An album the cover catalogues have never heard of showed no cover at all — even holding the picture. Reported on HIGHRESAUDIO's Vault, where HRA Mixed Up Sampler 2015.1 played with a blank sleeve in the player while the very same artwork appeared, correctly, on the Up next row two centimetres below. The queue row asks for the cover with the bare image URL; the player asks with the same URL plus the artist and album as a fallback. That fallback was the whole difference. Covers are cached twice — once per track, once per album so one fetch serves a whole record — and the album entry was being consulted before the URL the source had handed over. Cached as a miss it answered "no cover" outright, so the picture was never fetched: measured on the box at under 2 ms per refusal, while the URL served 83 kB of JPEG the moment it was asked directly. A miss on that entry only ever meant MusicBrainz and iTunes know nothing about this album — true of a promotional sampler, and of any obscure release on any source, and no reason to ignore artwork the service itself provided. The album entry may now answer only when it holds a cover; a miss still spares the two slow catalogue searches, which is what it was cached for. And the first track that resolves its own artwork writes it back, repairing the album for every other track instead of leaving them to wait out the cache.

[0.9.48] - 2026-08-29

Added

  • [core] [ui] HIGHRESAUDIO's Vault — the albums you bought, next to the ones you stream. A Vault pill on the HIGHRESAUDIO source lists the account's purchases and plays them like any album: on MPD, on a UPnP renderer, through HQPlayer. HRA keeps purchases in a second tree with its own routes and its own ids — a purchased album is <album>_<transaction>, which the catalogue route refuses, and the catalogue search does not find it — so every Vault id carries a vault: prefix that travels back to the queue, the way a playlist id carries mine: or editorial:, and the proxy reads the prefix to resolve each track on the purchases route. It never reads the id's shape: three segments where the catalogue has two is what one purchased album showed, not something HRA documents, so an unprefixed three-segment id is logged and left alone. The two routes are not interchangeable, measured on the same session and the same purchased track — the purchases route answers, the catalogue route answers NO SUBSCRIPTION without a URL once the account has no subscription — and HRA's own §1.2 names the purchases route for exactly this. One trap on that route is guarded: handed an id of the wrong shape it answers OK with the metadata and no URL, which read as a success would enqueue silence. The purchases' own playlist tree stays unexposed: empty on both accounts available, so untested.
  • [core] [ui] An HIGHRESAUDIO account without a subscription can now sign in, and play what it owns. The backlog said HRA refused such an account at the door; measured on one, HRA answers the login NOK / NO_SUBSCRIPTION and ships a valid session in the same body — one the purchases routes accept, down to a CDN that serves the FLAC (HTTP 206, fLaC magic). It was Audiogravity's own login that refused, on the label alone, and it would then have recorded the account as subscribed. A session is now accepted whenever HRA issues one, the state is recorded in HRA's own words (SUBSCRIPTION / NO SUBSCRIPTION, the second absent from their documentation), and /highresaudio/connection carries has_subscription. The sources card says next to the account that it can play its purchases only, and the browse offers it the Vault alone — the favourites, the playlists and every shelf answer NO SUBSCRIPTION to that session, so a bar that offered them would offer buttons that fail. Search declines the same way — it covers the catalogue, so instead of running a query that can only answer NO SUBSCRIPTION it says so and points at the Vault. And signing a different account in on the sources card resets all of it at once: what the library offers, the cached shelves and the ★ state all follow the account that is actually connected, not the one that was. A core that predates the field is read as subscribed: that is the only state its login could ever have saved.
  • [site] The landing carousel says what each screen is. Fourteen captures went past with nothing but a row of dots to tell them apart: a visitor saw a phone, not a product. Each now carries a sentence beside the device and three or four labels along its left edge, each one at the height of what it names and joined to the screen by a hairline — the source badge, the chain from service to DAC, the format actually being played, the output that can be switched mid-track. The labels are anchored to the capture rather than to the column, so the phone keeps its size and position whatever is written beside it, and their widths follow the free space instead of being fixed, which is what used to cut a sentence mid-word below 1856 px. They do not cross-fade with one another: consecutive screens deliberately share heights, so the outgoing label is gone before the incoming one starts, and only the visible screen's text is reachable — a transparent label is still found by Ctrl+F. Below 1550 px the flanks are too narrow to read and the labels are simply not shown.
  • [site] A DSD-over-HQPlayer capture joins the carousel. UPnP feeding HQPlayer Embedded, DSD128 at 5.6 MHz, and the DAC receiving it — the one path the fourteen other screens never showed. The capture supplied was a full-resolution PNG at 1.5 MB; it ships as a 63 KB WebP at the 760×1497 of every other screen, its phone outline matching theirs to the pixel.
  • [site] The landing says what a finished streamer is made of. A new Board and software section, between Where it sits and How it compares: network streamers sell for anywhere between a few hundred and several tens of thousands of euros, and for the part that makes one a streamer — receiving, decoding, handing the result to a DAC — it is a board running Linux with a software layer on top, most often the very layer Audiogravity drives. The claim is checkable rather than asserted: those components are GPL-licensed, so the manufacturer is obliged to list them, and the reader can settle it on their own machine. It names no brand and concedes the analog side outright — the DAC, the supply, the clocks are the reader's ground, and nothing has to be built: a Raspberry Pi, a fanless mini-PC or an Intel Mac mini with Linux on it is enough. Reuses .rt-list rather than adding a class that would style the same thing.
  • [site] A roadmap that promises nothing. A What's next section between the FAQ and the call to action, where a reader who has just been told the product is in beta asks where it is going: a ready-to-flash image, Diretta, AirPlay sending, multiroom. The wording is deliberately open — the list is not closed, none of it is promised, none of it belongs to the V1 scope, and the order is decided by what owners ask for — so the section ends on a way to answer rather than on a commitment.
  • [manual] The FAQ answers what "Public beta" means. First question of chapter 11, and therefore first on the landing: the V1 scope is complete and every feature described runs today; what remains is correction and optimisation, not missing features. It went in the FAQ rather than the hero because a caveat in the pitch deflates it, while the FAQ is where a cautious reader looks before the price.
  • [core] [ui] HIGHRESAUDIO's editorial playlists arrive on shelves instead of in a heap. There are 1762 of them, and they came as one undifferentiated list in the order HIGHRESAUDIO happens to publish it: finding anything meant scrolling. They file them on four shelves of their own — New Releases, Recommended, Popular, Moods — and a strip under the Editorial tree now picks one, HIGHRESAUDIO doing the narrowing on its side rather than the box pulling 1762 entries to sort them (Popular is 92). The strip opens on All, and that is not a formality: walking the whole tree found fourteen selections filed under no shelf at all — Mr. Slowhand — Eric Clapton, In the Spirit of Jazz, The Sky is Crying — old entries from before the field existed, and among the best of the set. A bar of four shelves alone would have made them unreachable, so the shelves narrow a list rather than dividing one. The four are not enforced either: HIGHRESAUDIO publishes no list of them, so an unknown shelf answers "nothing here" instead of an error, and one they add later works without an update. Leaving the Editorial tree drops the shelf with it — coming back to a narrowed list with no strip on screen to widen it is a dead end. Fixed on the way: an answer HIGHRESAUDIO gives as plain text rather than a list, which every shelf that matched nothing would have turned into a server error, and which five other calls read the same unsafe way.

Changed

  • [core] [ui] HIGHRESAUDIO's shelves, genres and artists, the way HIGHRESAUDIO asked. Their first feedback on the integration, 2026-08-28, named three things. The four shop categories they publish in German were relabelled here with words of our own — Tips, Top Album — and two of the four were wrong; they now carry HIGHRESAUDIO's own English: Listening Tips, Top Albums, New Release, Recently Added. The 26 genres and their sub-genres came in the order their API publishes them, which is no order a reader can use (R&B, Soundtrack, Guitar, Electronic…); both levels are now alphabetical, case-folded so a lower-case title does not sink to the end. A genre is addressed by its path, never by its rank, so nothing else moves. And the artists a search returns were in no order either — measured, twenty of them scattered — and are now alphabetical too, after the cap: the best matches are kept, and shown in the alphabet. Albums keep HIGHRESAUDIO's relevance order; the request was about artists.
  • [ui] A playlist now says it is one. Every streaming service maps its playlists onto the album model so one grid renders both, and nothing on a card told them apart — identical cover, title and byline; the only clue was the shelf heading, far above. HIGHRESAUDIO underlined it: "It is very important that you differentiate between albums and playlists." A playlist card now carries the word in the slot an album gives its year (a playlist has none, so the slot is never contested), a list row says it ahead of the byline, and the glyph behind a missing cover is the list, not the disc. On Qobuz and Tidal as well as HIGHRESAUDIO: the grid already knew which pill it was on to queue the right item type, and that same answer now drives what it shows.
  • [site] The landing said Audiogravity conducts your streamer. It is one. "Your streamer is your source. Audiogravity is the conductor" — the hero, the footer, the kicker's "streamer interface" and the three description / og: / twitter: metadata all described a layer sitting on top of a streamer the reader already owned, which is the opposite of what installing it does. It now opens on "Audiogravity is a streamer. A conductor, not a soloist." — the fact first, then the image that does the explaining, since a conductor plays no instrument. The paragraph under it stops saying it coordinates each component of your stack, which presumed a stack: it installs, configures and conducts them.
  • [site] How it compares claimed not to compete, above a table of competitors. Its lede read "sits at a different layer than the rest — does not compete with Roon, MPD or HQPlayer", directly above a table that lists Roon, Volumio, Moode, HQPlayer and JPLAY iOS and ends on a row of prices. It also put Roon and MPD in one bag, when Roon is a column here and MPD an engine Audiogravity installs. The distinction the sentence has to carry is Roon versus Roon Bridge, and HQPlayer versus its NAA — one of each pair is a rival, the other is what gets installed to be compatible with it. The column header drops Embedded.
  • [site] The pipeline drawing credited the NAA with HQPlayer's work. One box read HQPlayer NAA · DSD256 · upsample, when the NAA receives audio already processed. There are now three nodes where there was one — Audiogravity resolving the stream URL, HQPlayer applying filters and conversion, the NAA handing the result to the DAC. The lit path starts from Qobuz, Tidal and HIGHRESAUDIO, spelled in full, instead of from Roon Core, which is one source among five. Internet radio, the NAS and both UPnP roles join the drawing — the control app driving the box on one side, the network renderer being driven on the other — and dashed frames mark what actually runs on the box, so the radio, the streaming clients and the two UPnP roles stop reading as outside boxes. There are two such frames, carrying the same label rather than one each: the NAA endpoint runs on the box as much as the rest, but HQPlayer sits between them and may run on another machine, so a single frame could not hold both without claiming HQPlayer too. The amplifier makes way: eight nodes did not fit across 1000 px, and the bit-perfect link moves to NAA → DAC, where the property is decided.
  • [site] Three more places still said the reader owned a streamer. The hero was corrected; the same presumption survived in Features — "every layer of your streamer", "every engine your streamer runs" — and in the title of Installation, "On your streamer in one command". They now read every layer of the stack, every engine the box runs, and "One command, and it is a streamer.", which makes the installation title demonstrate the hero instead of contradicting its premise. One occurrence is deliberately untouched: "expose your streamer as a UPnP renderer" does mean the Audiogravity machine.
  • [site] Five layers did not add up. The title claimed Audiogravity "covers three, manages two", over a table labelled 2 × Audiogravity, 2 × manages, 1 × tunes — the count only worked by lumping the two layers it is with the one it tunes, and a reader who counted the labels found 2/2/1 and stalled. It now reads "is two, drives three": two layers it is, three it drives, five in all. True quoted away from its table, which the old wording was not.
  • [site] The link preview showed an opening line the site no longer uses. og:image was a still of the page taken by hand months ago: it still advertised "Your streamer is your source. Audiogravity is the conductor" while the og:description beside it already carried the new one, so a pasted link contradicted itself. Regenerated — and, more to the point, regenerable: audiogravity.ops/scripts/gen-og-image.js drives the headless Chromium that audiogravity.ui already carries. Four settings decide whether the result is usable, and each was established by looking at the output: captured at 1800×945 and downscaled, since a direct 1200×630 shot crops the phone and the price row; the carousel frozen on slide 0, which otherwise advances every 3.5 s mid-capture; dark theme forced, a fresh profile having no stored preference; and the announce bar hidden, because it carries a version number and the card is cached for weeks.
  • [site] Where it is developed, and by whom. Who I am now ends on "I develop and maintain it myself, from France" — the badge in the footer stays Made in the EU, France being in it.
  • [site] The carousel opens on a different record. The opening slide is the only one every visitor is guaranteed to see, so which sleeve is on screen is an editorial choice, not whatever happened to be playing when the capture was taken. A reader pointed out that the one there read as the opposite of what a listener is after, which is a fair thing to say about a shop window. It now opens on the HIGHRESAUDIO screen, and the Qobuz screen that opened before moves one slide down. Captions and annotations travel with their capture and never with the slot — --y is measured against the capture itself — so the move changes nothing but the data-slide number and which caption carries active; checked in the markup, fourteen slides, contiguous indices, a single active caption with its three annotations, and the opening capture still loaded eagerly as the page's largest paint. Both it and the slide after it are light-theme captures, so the carousel now opens on two light screens in a row.

Removed

  • [site] The carousel no longer shows Qobuz twice. Two of its captures were the same Qobuz screen — same badge, same Qobuz → MPD → USB → DAC chain, same DAC — differing only by the theme, so a visitor letting it run saw the same thing twice, five screens apart. The dark copy is set aside rather than deleted; the light one stays, one slide in.

Fixed

  • [ui] A box that was switched off told its owner the password was wrong. On an iPhone or an iPad only, and for a reason nothing on screen could reveal: the sign-in screen recognised a failed request by reading the browser's error sentence, and that sentence differs per engine — Failed to fetch in Chromium, NetworkError when attempting to fetch resource. in Gecko, Load failed in WebKit. The list knew two of the three, and includes('failed') swept WebKit's into Invalid username or password. So a phone whose box had stopped invited its owner to doubt what they had typed, while the API · OFFLINE badge two centimetres above the message said the truth. It now reads the shape of the failure rather than its wording: the only line that can know a request never left — the one that called fetch — marks it as it throws, and every screen downstream asks instead of guessing. A TypeError raised three frames later, in code handling a perfectly successful response, stays what it is.
  • [ui] The sign-in screen no longer answers with a status line. Whatever goes wrong, it now says something a person can act on, and each sentence was checked against the response the server really sends. A box that cannot be reached — no answer at all, or the 502 its own front returns when the core is stopped — names the address that was tried, so the reader goes to look at the machine instead of writing to support. A box that is running and has just crashed says so, and points at the support report: an unhandled error reaches Starlette, which answers in plain text with no message of its own, and that is a different event from a machine that is off. Six wrong passwords in a minute, which is exactly what someone does when they think they are mistyping, said HTTP 429; it now names the wait. A refused field said [object Object], because the core describes those in an array and the array was handed straight to the screen.
  • [ui] The retry button on the passkey panel did nothing. The panel a passkey-registered phone meets before the form re-enabled its button after a failure, while the listener had been registered to fire once and was already gone — so the second attempt it invited was one it had made impossible, until the page was reloaded. Nothing looked wrong: the button came back exactly as it had been. Double submissions are prevented by disabling it for the length of the attempt, which is the guard that belongs there.
  • [ui] One fetch boundary, and every screen reads it. The sign-in fix tagged a transport failure where the request is made; it did so in three places, each written by hand, next to four different ways of turning a refused response into an Error — and the differences were visible. The licence upload sat on its own fetch call, so an iPad in front of a switched-off box read WebKit's raw Load failed there, the very sentence the sign-in fix had just retired; the passkey panel fabricated a detail from statusText, so it answered the same outage as the password form with a different sentence, over HTTP/1.1 only; the main client always set a detail, so the rule written on "no detail" could never fire for it. There is now one boundary in net-errors.js — fetchOrThrow, with throwForStatus giving every caller the same shape: the status, a string detail or null, a 422's field list joined into one line and kept raw underneath, slowapi's rate limit read from where slowapi writes it. The API client, sign-in, passkeys, uploads, downloads and the licence panel all go through it, and the message helper every toast uses reads the same predicates — a 502 no longer says Bad gateway on one screen and cannot reach the box on the next. The three screens that printed err.message on a passkey or upload failure now say what the box did — or that it could not be reached.
  • [ui] The installed app on a phone can say the box is off. The service worker answers a request that could not leave the device with a synthetic 503 {"error":"offline"} rather than letting the browser reject — an iOS quirk, and an old one. Read as an HTTP status it is a lie: nothing answered, and every screen of the installed app therefore said Service temporarily unavailable in front of a machine that was simply switched off, with no hint that the network was the matter. The boundary recognises that marker and turns it back into the transport failure it stands for, so the home-screen app reports an unreachable box exactly as a browser tab does.
  • [ui] A request is retried only when nothing answered — and not when the phone already knows. The retry layer used "no status" as its test for a transport failure, which also matched a 200 whose body was not JSON — replayed three times, seven seconds, for a response that would never parse. It now asks the boundary. Two refinements followed review: the service worker's offline answer is final, so it is not retried at all (the installed app on a phone was making three attempts per call, from every poller on the page, at a box it had already given up on); and a connection that drops while a body is being read — a core restarting under a long request — is a transport failure and gets its one retry back.
  • [ui] Six toasts in the Settings passkey section had their arguments reversed. showToast(type, title, message) was called as (message, type): the toast received "Passkey removed" as its type and "success" as its heading, and rendered unstyled under the word success. Nothing failed — a wrong string is still a string — which is why it lasted. Fixed, and a source guard now refuses any toast whose first argument is not one of the four types.
  • [ops] The landing's FAQ generator swept the end of the chapter into the last answer. sync-landing-faq.py splits 11-faq.md on ## and takes everything after the final heading as that question's body — so the horizontal rule closing the chapter and the trademark notice under it landed inside "How do I update Audiogravity?", published as a literal --- and a paragraph still wearing its Markdown asterisks. The fault was latent, not visible: it is the sync that publishes it, not the edit, so it surfaced the first time the FAQ was regenerated after that notice was added. A top-level rule now closes the answers.
  • [site] The manual's footer was a filled band, but only for readers whose system is dark. The manual is a reading page and its footer is meant to be a single rule with the way back below it; under dark it came out as a #1a1a1a block spanning the column, as if borrowed from the commercial page. The stylesheet said background: none all along — the landing paints every <footer> through two rules of unequal weight, and the manual's own rule beat one of them and lost to the other. Which one applied depended on how the reader reached dark: picking dark in the interface gave the correct footer, leaving the browser to follow the system gave the band. The same theme rendered two ways, which is why nobody caught it. The manual's rule is now restated at each of the two weights; the landing keeps its own filled footer, unchanged.

[0.9.47] - 2026-08-25

Added

  • [lic] A trial length, once served to a box, is never taken back. The trial every box starts with is set on the licence server and served to the fleet, signed, at each box's daily check-in — and the box keeps no memory of it: it recomputes "days remaining" from whatever length it is served that day. So raising the setting reached every box, which is what one wants, but lowering it did too: a box at day forty of a sixty-day trial was back to "expired" the morning after the setting went to thirty, with nothing said to anyone. The server now serves each box the longest length it has ever told it — the current setting the first time a box asks, the larger of the two from then on. Raising the setting still reaches every box, trials already running included; lowering it reaches only boxes the server has never seen. Recorded from a box's very first fetch, because a box asks for its configuration before it first reports itself, and a length served in that window is a length served. The administration console shows the length granted to each installation, finds the ones served a given figure, and can forget one — the way to undo a length typed by mistake, since the setting alone can no longer take it back; the setting itself is capped at the ten years the box would honour anyway. Nothing changes on the boxes: the rule lives on the server, and every box already deployed follows it at its next check-in.
  • [lic] The licence server backs up its database every night, and checks the copy. Everything but the signing key lives in one file — orders, the licence files that can be re-sent, revocations, the trial record of every box — and the only copy ever taken was the one made around an upgrade. A timer now runs at 03:30: it copies the live database with SQLite's own online backup, which is consistent even while the server is writing where a plain copy of the file is not, verifies the copy's integrity before keeping it — a backup that cannot be opened is not one, and the time to learn that is not the day it is needed — compresses it, and keeps the newest thirty. A night missed while the machine was off is caught up at the next boot. Fresh installs get it; existing servers get it at their next update, which also takes a snapshot before restarting into the new code, so a migration that goes wrong has a copy from before it. Losing this file stops no box from playing — a licence is verified on the box — but it loses the record of who bought what, and every revocation: a revoked licence's own file is still validly signed, so without the record it reads as valid again.

Removed

  • [core] [ui] Three services Audiogravity claimed to manage, and never shipped. audiogravity-pulse, audiogravity-camilladsp and audiogravity-visualizer were written into the code as units the box always manages — since the very first commit — while nothing in any installer ever created them: no unit file, no binary, no package, on either platform. They cost a widened systemd query every ten seconds, and they were why the support report printed a full realtime-tuning line for three pieces of software that are not on the machine. CamillaDSP also held a reserved node in the audio pipeline diagram, the last trace of a processing stage that was never built. DSP is not on the roadmap and would be designed from scratch if it ever were, so the placeholder is gone rather than kept as a promise the diagram could not keep.

Fixed

  • [ui] Three downloads that could hand you nothing, without a word. Exporting a configuration, saving a service's config file, and fetching your licence file after activation each built their own download by hand, and each got the same two details wrong. A browser cannot write to your disk: a page has to put the content in memory, make a temporary address for it, click an invisible link, then release the memory — and click() does not download, it asks the browser to, which the browser does a moment later. Releasing the memory on the next line races it to the data and wins often enough; the click reports success and no file is ever written. One of the three did not even put its link in the page, which several browsers ignore outright — and swallowed any error on top. Nothing failed loudly, which is why it survived: it works on a fast machine with a small file, and misses on a phone, or on a bigger one. All three now go through the one helper that gets it right, alongside the support report, which was the only caller already using it. Two of the tests that covered this were asserting the faulty behaviour — they required the memory to be released immediately — so a correct fix would have looked like a regression to whoever tried it; they now check what that screen actually owes: the right content, under the right name.
  • [ui] HIGHRESAUDIO's mark is theirs again, at their contrast. The artwork shipped since 0.9.44 was cut out by hand from a flat image, and the cut had washed out the RES at the centre of the mark — its strokes measured 141 against the original's 59, so the one word the whole logo is built around sat at half contrast, close to vanishing into its own white plate. HIGHRESAUDIO supplied their official cut-out file on 2026-08-25; the three images the interface uses are regenerated from it, at the source's own proportions, and the wordmark reads as it was drawn. The light-theme variant still has to be derived — they publish only the negative version, white on transparency, which is invisible on a light background — but it now derives from a clean original: white ink turned dark, the centre plate left white with its dark RES, and a dark outline of the thickness the logo already uses on its bottom square, so the cross stays legible on white without inverting a single colour. The small badge beside Qobuz and Tidal is redrawn from the same source at proper proportions, and joins the mask treatment those two already use: at 24 pixels the mark measures 24×18 and none of it is legible either way, so what a reader gets is a silhouette — and a silhouette that follows the text colour stays visible on a selected card, whose background inverts to black under the light theme, where a fixed dark image would not. Measured in all four combinations of theme and selection.
  • [core] [ui] The support report was answering some questions wrongly, rather than not at all. A report that says nothing sends you looking; a report that says something false sends you the wrong way, and five of its sections did the second. Its streaming section read not signed in for Qobuz, Tidal and HighResAudio on every box, whatever the truth — the probe called a property as though it were a method, the resulting error fell into a catch that wrote "no", and nothing anywhere said so; on the machine where this was found, all three accounts were connected. Its scheduling section described the realtime tuning of software the box does not have, because systemctl show answers for any unit name at all with the defaults it would apply, and nothing there could tell a stopped service from one that was never installed. Its list of detected outputs printed bare card names, so a machine with an onboard codec showed PCH twice and HDMI three times — five lines nobody can act on, and the analog/digital pair is precisely where an AirPlay receiver ends up on the wrong socket. It showed which output Audiogravity believes it selected and never which one each service is really playing on, so the one fault most worth reporting — a box playing out of a socket nobody chose — was the one it could not show. And it repeated an identical failure once per line, twenty-one lines for seven occurrences of a single denial, in the order journalctl stores them, which is not the order they happened. Now: a probe that cannot run says unknown and names its error instead of answering "no"; a unit that is not loaded says so, and only a genuinely absent one is called absent — a masked unit is present, and deliberately so; every output carries its full label and its hw: address; every service shows the device its own configuration names, flagged when that disagrees with the selection on record, and for MPD that is the output MPD has enabled rather than the first one written in the file; and repeated failures fold into a single line carrying their count and the last time they occurred, in chronological order. The report lost a tenth of its size on the way.
  • [core] The now-playing view survived a removed pipeline node type by luck, not design. Removing the unused processing node left one reference to it on the now-playing path, which would have raised on every refresh. Caught by the test suite before release; the whole enumeration is now checked by a scan that finds any reference to a member that no longer exists.
  • [lic] The licence console spoke French to pick a file. Restoring a database offered Choisir le fichier — aucun fichier sélectionné in the middle of an English console. That text was never ours: a file field is the one control a page cannot dress or translate, and the browser draws it in its own language, so the console read differently depending on who opened it. The field is now put out of sight and a real button stands in its place, with the chosen file's name written beside it — in the console's own words and its own style, where the field used to be a grey slab that matched nothing around it. Out of sight, not out of reach: it is clipped rather than removed, so it keeps its place in the tab order and the button still answers a keyboard, which is the trap in every recipe for hiding one.
  • [lic] Cancelling a database restore let go of the file — the field did not. Choosing a backup and then cancelling left its name showing next to a Restore database button that had gone grey, which reads as a file still selected and a button refusing to work. The console had in fact forgotten it; only the field had not, because the line meant to empty it did nothing at all: it looked for the field in a place this screen does not use, then called something a file field does not have, and the whole thing failed silently. It is emptied for real now, on a cancel and after a restore that went through — a file that has been applied must stop being on offer.

[0.9.46] - 2026-08-24

Added

  • [core] [ui] The support report now answers the questions a box could not be asked. It said what the box was — version, licence, services, configuration files — and nothing about the world it lives in, so half of every support exchange was still spent asking. It now measures, on demand: how the interface is really served (plain HTTP or TLS, on which port, which version of the interface is deployed, whether the certificate has expired, whether it still names the address the box answers on, and whether the authority phones were asked to trust is the one signing it — all asked of the running server, never read from a configuration file that may not match); the network the music crosses (gateway, DNS, which interface actually carries the traffic, the negotiated speed of a wired link — a gigabit port running at a hundred is a thing that happens — the WiFi rate and signal, whether the clock is synchronised at all, and whether the internet and the licence server actually answer, with the time they took); the audio path as it is at that instant rather than as it was configured — which DAC the system can see and at what USB speed, and the format and sample rate genuinely crossing ALSA, which is the bit-perfect claim, measured; how the audio services are scheduled, with what systemd was told to apply next to what the running process actually carries, since a tuning file that failed to apply is exactly the difference between those two; what else is on the network — UPnP renderers and servers, an HQPlayer or a Roon Core, whether or not this box was configured to use them, because "not configured" and "nowhere to be found" are different answers; free space, and any filesystem the kernel has quietly remounted read-only; whether the deployed settings file carries what the code expects, by name, never by value; whether a self-update could actually download, asked of the release repository itself; and the last seven days of failures — the services' own, and the kernel's, where a DAC dropping off the bus, a dying memory card, an undervolted supply or thermal throttling speak, and where nothing else in the report would ever show them. Also the boot history, because five boots in two days is a hardware conversation, not a software one. Secrets are removed as before, and every measurement that fails says so: a probe that could not run is never printed as a negative finding.
  • [ui] The support report window says what it is doing, and when it is done. Collecting now takes a few seconds — it probes the network — and the window said only "Collecting…", motionless, which reads as frozen. There is now a turning indicator and the sentence names what is being probed while it runs, then gives way to a plain "Report ready." in the affirmative colour. The Copy and Download buttons stay unavailable until there is something to copy.
  • [core] [site] OpenHome is a switch now. The landing has said it from the start: OpenHome apps such as Kazoo work too, once OpenHome is switched on — and that "once" meant finding the right key in the raw configuration file, which is Expert-mode territory for what is a yes/no question. The UPnP bridge's form now carries an OpenHome Renderer checkbox: tick it, save, and a second renderer appears for Linn Kazoo and its kind, named after your box with an -OH tail so the two renderers a box then publishes never wear the same name in an app that lists both — and switching it on or off never renames the UPnP renderer your other apps already know. Verified live: renderer names measured on the network in both positions of the switch.

Fixed

  • [ops] Audiogravity can create its own backup folder again. Every configuration change makes a timestamped copy first, and the folder those copies live in is created at startup — except the permission granted covered the sub-folders and not the folder itself, so the very first attempt was refused, silently, on every box. Nothing broke while the folder happened to exist from an earlier install; on a fresh box the backups depended on luck. Found by the new report's journal section on its first run, which is the job it was written for.
  • [core] [ui] The box announces itself under the name you chose — not a hostname with a protocol suffix. Every box provisioned by Audiogravity appeared in JPLAY, BubbleUPnP or mconnect as hostname-UPnP/AV — a name nobody picked, wearing a protocol badge nobody asked for, and for someone who drives their streamer from their usual control app, that name is the product. The configuration form made it worse: its one name field promised "Name displayed to UPnP clients" and did not deliver — upmpdcli keeps two name keys, the form only wrote the one the suffix gets glued to, so typing Salon produced Salon-UPnP/AV, and anyone who came looking for the suffix found nothing to fix. The form's Friendly Name now writes what control points actually display, exactly: type Salon, see Salon. A fresh box defaults to audiogravity-XXXX with a short token of its own, unique per box, because two boxes in one home must not publish the same name and UPnP — unlike AirPlay — has no mechanism to tell them apart; the hostname is not used, since on a fresh image it is the distribution's name, which nobody chose either. An existing box migrates the moment its form is saved — any field, any reason — and its suffix disappears; a name someone had deliberately written into the file, odd ending and all, is kept as it is. The Reset to default action says up front that the name returns to its default: a reset is a reset, and it announces its one visible consequence. And measured on the real network before and after, on both platforms — not deduced from documentation.
  • [core] Saving from the configuration form no longer flips a tile to NOT CONFIGURED. The form's save rewrote the file from its parsed values, which dropped every comment — including the one line saying Managed by Audiogravity. That line is how the box tells a config it wrote from a distro default someone may have edited: lose it and the tile's badge flipped to NOT CONFIGURED, the support report called the file hand-written, and the natural repair — regenerate — was exactly the gesture that would overwrite the edit just saved. The marker now survives every form save. It is preserved, never invented: a config Audiogravity did not provision does not start claiming otherwise, because the support report's word has to stay trustworthy — and if the box ever cannot re-read the file to decide, it says so in its log instead of deciding silently.
  • [core] Checkboxes in the configuration form tell the truth in both directions. A 0 in the file showed as a checked box — the display asked "is there a value?" where it meant "is it on?" — and saving a checkbox wrote True into a file whose program only reads 0 and 1. Nobody had hit it yet because no toggle was exposed; the OpenHome switch below would have shipped both bugs on day one. Boolean settings now arrive at the form as real booleans and go back in the file's own spelling, for every service's form at once.

[0.9.45] - 2026-08-23

Fixed

  • [ui] The Configuration tab shows the result of a first-time setup, without a reload. The setup reported success, the three services came back configured — and the tab behind it was exactly the one from before: the invitation banner still there, the tiles still NOT CONFIGURED, the UPnP bridge still given as stopped. It took reloading the page by hand to see what had actually happened, at the very moment an owner is looking for confirmation that something did. The refresh had been written, and read line by line, and it was correct; it simply never ran. The setup panel lives inside a dialog, and a listener written by one component but rendered by another is called on the one that renders it — so both handlers looked for methods on the dialog, found nothing, and threw where nobody could see it. The box was reporting itself configured the whole time. The same trap was closing the installation-log dialog by luck rather than by design: its own close button wrote to the wrong object, and the dialog only shut because a parent happened to be listening — on its own it stayed open. Both are bound to their owner now, and the tab asks the box for its state once per setup instead of twice.

  • [core] [ui] A box with no music of its own can now be set up. The first-time setup refused to finish until a music folder was named, and the box refused too — yet a box with no local music is an ordinary thing: HIGHRESAUDIO, Qobuz and Tidal need no file on disk, and neither does an AirPlay receiver or a UPnP bridge. Their owners were pushed into inventing a path to get past the button, and an invented path went through: the only check was that it held no double quote, which protects MPD's configuration file rather than the person typing. What followed looked like a complete success — configuration written, MPD running, tile marked CONFIGURED, indexing reporting "finished" because there was nothing to walk — for a library that was empty, with nothing anywhere saying why. The setup now offers No music library — online listening only as a choice of its own, and MPD's music folder line is left out entirely rather than written blank, which would stop MPD from starting. The reverse is possible too: a box that started without a library can be pointed at one later, and one that has a library can be released from it — with a confirmation first, since that takes something away. A path that is given is now checked: absolute, existing, a directory, and readable by the account MPD runs as — a folder you can read is not necessarily one MPD can, and that difference is invisible once MPD is running. Each refusal says which of those failed, and says it before anything on the box has been touched.

  • [core] A library that was never configured no longer reads as a library that is empty. MPD refuses every database question on a box with no music folder, and that refusal carries none of the fields Audiogravity was looking for — so it came back as "no albums" and "no results". On a box whose collection had simply never been set up, the Library tab said the collection was empty, and search said your music did not contain what you were looking for. Both now answer with the reason: No music library is configured on this box. Add one from the Config tab, or keep listening to your streaming services. A genuinely empty collection is still reported as empty, and an album whose title happens to contain those words is not mistaken for the refusal.

  • [core] [ui] A configuration tile now says when the software behind it is not installed. The Services and Profiles tabs have always been able to name absent software; the Configuration tab could not. It showed a tile for a package that is not on the box, with the path of a file that does not exist, a button offering to edit it, and a download button with nothing to fetch — no date, no state, no badge, and nothing saying why. An owner could as easily conclude that something was broken as that the software was there. Such a tile is now dashed and greyed, carries an UNAVAILABLE badge, says the package is not installed and links to the Audio Software tab where it is installed. What can still be done, still can: a package removed without purging leaves its configuration file behind, and that file remains downloadable, because a file that is on the box can always be taken off it. What cannot, is refused: editing is closed, since saving a configuration also restarts the service, and no restart can succeed for software that is absent. Backups are not lost either — they come back with the package. The reverse case is left alone: an installed service whose file is missing keeps its editor, which is what creates the file.

  • [core] [ui] A stopped service shows as stopped in the Configuration tab, instead of showing nothing. The tab read each service's state from systemd's list of loaded units, which leaves out units that are installed but idle — upmpdcli on an ordinary box among them. Those tiles carried no state badge at all, and nothing distinguished "stopped" from "not installed there". The state is now read per unit, so RUNNING, STOPPED and FAILED appear wherever they apply. Two side effects worth having: the tab asks the box one question instead of two, and the answer is read over the system bus rather than by running a command — measured at no process creation where the previous approach created six per refresh, on a path the audio pipeline also uses. Coming back to the tab after installing a package now re-reads the list, so the tile you were sent to fix stops being stale.

  • [ops] [core] A box that comes back from an install or an update can play. Installing the MPD package is not the same as having its daemon: Debian arms the service when it actually installs the package, and does nothing when the package is already there. So a box carrying MPD with its service stopped — or simply not set to start with the machine — went through the whole installation with nothing noticing, and came out with an interface in front of something that could make no sound. Every installation and every update now starts MPD and sets it to start with the box. It sits with the switch that turns off MPD's on-demand activation, because that is what the two decisions are: taking away the mechanism that used to start the daemon by itself is what makes arming it necessary. A box with no network, which reaches that point with no MPD at all, is told what is missing rather than having its installation aborted. If you run yours as a Roon endpoint or an HQPlayer NAA only, stopping MPD is not enough — turn its ENABLED switch off too, or it returns at the next boot; an update turns both back on.

  • [core] A stopped MPD says so, everywhere, instead of failing anonymously. Everything Audiogravity plays goes through MPD, and the box stops it itself when you choose the Roon or the HQPlayer profile — so a stopped daemon is a state an owner reaches on purpose, and the only failure they can fix in one click. It was the one failure the box could not name: pressing play answered Playback failed / Queue operation failed, which says nothing, and the library answered with no albums at all — a statement that the collection was gone, on a box that may hold fifty thousand tracks. Playing, queueing, removing a track, searching, casting to a network renderer and starting a radio station now all answer with the same sentence: MPD is not running. Start it from the Services tab. A refusal is confirmed a second later before being named, because Audiogravity restarts MPD itself when you change output and the port refuses during that second exactly like a stopped daemon — an instruction to go and start what is already coming back would be wrong. A daemon that is listening and not answering is a different failure and is not reported as this one.

Added

  • [core] [ui] A support report you can read, and send if you want to. Helping someone with their box meant asking what it looked like, one question per day: the version deduced from a timestamp, a service assumed to be off from a screenshot, a hand-edited configuration guessed at from a description — and some of those deductions were wrong, which sent the search after a fault that was not there. System › Actions › Support Report now gathers it all in one gesture: version and architecture, the licence verdict and the last check against the licence server, uptime and load, which audio packages are installed and at which versions, the state of every service and whether it starts with the box, the outputs detected and the one each service is pinned to, whether each configuration file is Audiogravity-managed or was written by hand, the declared music library and whether MPD has actually indexed it — and the configuration files themselves, which is what nobody could see. Any unit that has failed elsewhere on the box is listed too: a NAS mount that never came up explains an empty library, and never appears in the audio stack. Passwords, API keys, streaming tokens and share credentials are removed before the report is built — the name of a setting stays so you can still see it is configured, its value does not — and so is the content of your library. Comments are dropped, which takes upmpdcli's configuration from three hundred and eighty-five lines to thirteen and makes it readable; what was cut is always counted, never silently. Nothing is sent anywhere: the report is shown to you, with Copy and Download, and travels only if you attach it to a message yourself. A part the box cannot collect says so in its own place, rather than taking the whole report down with it.

  • [site] [ui] What the box tells the licence server is now written down. Once a day, and when a key is activated, a box contacts the licence server — that is how a Pro licence stays bound to one machine, and how revocations, announcements and update offers arrive. What travels was written nowhere: not in the licence agreement, which had seven clauses and none about data, not on the landing, not in the interface. The manual now says it, in the Licence section of the Administration chapter, and the licence agreement carries a clause of its own: the Device ID, the primary MAC address, the operating system and architecture, the Audiogravity version, the current licence status and, during the trial, its start date — plus the licence file itself when it is verified, and the hostname you type when activating. The server records the address the request arrives from. Your music, your library paths, your service credentials and what you listen to stay on the box. Also said, because it is the part that is easy to get wrong: whether Pro is unlocked is decided on the box, from the signature of its licence file — no round-trip decides it.

  • [core] [ui] HIGHRESAUDIO's catalogue can be browsed by genre. The service arranges the same music a second way — twenty-six genres, a hundred and eighty-six sub-genres — and none of it was reachable. A Genres button now opens a second row under the shelves: the genres, then, once one is chosen, that genre and its own subdivisions, with a way back to the list. Choosing the whole genre reads as All, because HIGHRESAUDIO gives two of its genres a sub-genre of the same name and two identical buttons side by side read as a mistake; the heading above the albums names where you are — Soundtrack · Original Score. A genre they publish with nothing in it says so rather than looking broken.

  • [core] [ui] HIGHRESAUDIO's playlists can be browsed and played — theirs and yours. The service keeps two separate collections: the selections its editors publish, two hundred of them, and the playlists belonging to your own account. Neither was reachable, where Qobuz and Tidal have had their Playlists shelf from the start. A Playlists button now opens a second row with the two, and a playlist behaves exactly like an album — open it to see its tracks, or send the whole thing to the queue in one gesture, on the box's own DAC as on a network renderer or an HQPlayer. The two collections number their playlists independently, so the same number exists in both; each one now carries the name of its own collection, and nothing anywhere has to guess which of the two a playlist came from. An account that has no playlist of its own says so, rather than looking broken. Creating and renaming playlists, and adding or removing tracks, are not part of this: what HIGHRESAUDIO has made is readable, what you make stays where you made it, for now.

  • [core] [ui] A HIGHRESAUDIO search can be narrowed by composer or by record label. Two fields under the search box, for the two questions the words alone cannot answer: the composer of a classical recording, which appears on no other field, and the label — ECM, Pentatone — that a collector browses by. They narrow the search rather than replacing it, and the ordinary search is untouched while both are empty. Two other filters the service offers are deliberately absent: choosing an audio format makes HIGHRESAUDIO discard the words typed altogether — three different searches, one of them nonsense, return the same fifty albums — and a mood takes about a minute to answer the first time. Both have been reported to them, and both come back the day they answer differently.

  • [core] [ui] Every HIGHRESAUDIO shelf is reachable, under the name HIGHRESAUDIO gives it. Their catalogue is arranged in fourteen shop categories; the browse offered three of them, and had renamed two of those three — High-Res Essentials was shown as Discover, Editors Choice as Editor's Picks. The three were written into the interface, so the eleven others could not be reached at all: the label highlights, ECM, Pentatone, Sony, Universal and Warner; the bestsellers; the editorial tips; what has just been added. The core now lists the categories and the browse builds one button per category from that answer, in the order HIGHRESAUDIO publishes them — so a shelf they add tomorrow appears without an update, and every name on screen is the one their own listeners recognise. Four of their titles come back in German whatever language is asked for, which is a quirk of their API their own application works around the same way: those four are relabelled for display (Hörtipps → Tips), and the original title stays what the box uses to ask for the shelf.

Fixed

  • [core] Searching HIGHRESAUDIO can no longer get in the way of the music. Their filtered search is slow the first time it is asked something — measured between nine seconds and a minute, then instant on a repeat once their own cache is warm — so it now waits thirty seconds rather than fifteen, and says "try again, it usually returns at once" instead of reporting a failure. But a request that waits is a request that holds a connection, and every exchange with HIGHRESAUDIO shares the same handful of them, including the one that resolves a track when you press play. At most two searches now run at a time, so pressing play while someone is searching can never be the thing that fails. A box that cannot reach HIGHRESAUDIO at all is told exactly that, rather than being invited to try again for as long as it stays offline.
  • [ui] A search that fails now says what happened. The interface had no way of showing an error at all: HIGHRESAUDIO answering "this took too long, try again" appeared as No results for … — a different statement entirely, and the wrong advice. Failures are shown as themselves now. A search of one or two characters with a filter set is refused with its reason too, instead of returning nothing: their catalogue cannot be searched on fewer than three, which made U2 look like a catalogue with no U2 in it.
  • [ui] A row of buttons that continues off-screen now says so. The browse filters sit on a strip that scrolls sideways, with its scrollbar hidden — deliberately, since a scrollbar drawn across labels that short is worse than none. That was survivable while the longest row held five buttons; with fifteen it left the last one sliced through the middle of a word and no sign that anything followed. The edge that still hides buttons now fades into the page instead of being cut, and where a mouse or trackpad is used — a screen you drag needs no button — a chevron sits in each margin to move the strip one shelf at a time.
