Neither ever claims more than there is: on a row that fits, there is no fade and no chevron at all, and each disappears the moment its end of the strip is reached. Under Firefox the same strips were also drawing a real scrollbar, which the rule hiding it never covered.
  • [core] A HIGHRESAUDIO account signed in elsewhere no longer empties your own shelves. The service allows one signed-in device per account, so any other phone or computer signing in ends the box's session — an ordinary event, and one Audiogravity is built to recover from by signing back in on its own. It recovered from two of the three ways HIGHRESAUDIO announces a finished session, and not from the third, which is the one their account-scoped answers use: your playlists, your favourites, your purchases and your downloads. The box did not notice, did not sign back in, and answered with an empty list — telling you the account owns no favourites, on an account that owns some. All three forms are now recognised. Verified against a genuinely ended session rather than a simulated one: signing in from elsewhere, checking the service refuses the box's old session, and watching the shelves come back untouched.
  • [core] A momentary refusal from HIGHRESAUDIO no longer costs a restart. The list of shelves is fetched once and kept, since it does not change while a box is on. But HIGHRESAUDIO answers some failures — a session it has just invalidated among them — with a perfectly successful reply containing nothing, and that empty answer was kept exactly like a real one: from then on every shelf was empty, on a box that had no way of knowing why and no way back short of restarting. Only a real answer is kept now, so the next visit asks again, and the browse's own Refresh is enough to repair a bar that came up bare.

[0.9.44] - 2026-08-21

Added

  • [core] [ui] [ops] MPD is part of Audiogravity, not one of its options. The installer set up Audiogravity and none of the engines it conducts, so a fresh box reached the interface with nothing able to play: no output to choose, no music, and nothing on screen saying that the missing piece was one button away under Audio Software. But MPD is not an engine among others — the local library, the queue, internet radio and all three streaming services reach the DAC through it, and even the UPnP bridge is a front-end driving it. It is now installed with the box, and the interface no longer offers to remove it; the request is refused by the core as well, for anyone calling the route directly. A box used purely as a Roon endpoint or an HQPlayer NAA can stop the service instead — a stopped daemon costs nothing, and the setup stays possible without a box that cannot play at all.

Fixed

  • [core] [ui] HIGHRESAUDIO is shown as its owner defines it. Above the album grid the source was announced by the plain word HRA, their logo appeared nowhere on that screen, and elsewhere the name was set as Highresaudio. Their mark now heads their section — their own positive and negative artwork, chosen by theme, with its flat background made transparent so it sits on any of the interface's backgrounds instead of painting a rectangle over them; the drawing itself is untouched. Where the name is written it is now written in full, in capitals — now-playing badge, queue header, Sources card — measured as fitting uncut in the narrowest of them. A brand's owner defines its spelling, and the corporate identity supplied with their API is part of what allows the integration to exist.
  • [site] [ops] A box left with a dead access token now repairs itself. Boxes installed during early access hold the shared token in their configuration and present it on every update. Since the releases repository became public that credential can only hurt: sending nothing succeeds, while sending something GitHub refuses is answered 401 — so those boxes were about to fail every one-click update, and could not fetch the fix that would free them, the fix travelling in the very download the token was blocking. The bootstrap installers, which a box re-downloads at each attempt, now test the token they are handed and continue without it when GitHub refuses it — only on a refusal; a network outage stays an error rather than being quietly passed over. The token is dropped rather than forwarded, and the installer clears the stored value when no token is given, so the dead credential leaves the configuration instead of being ignored for ever. One update is enough, with nobody opening a terminal on the box.
  • [ops] The unattended update refuses to execute anything that is not a script. It fetched each installer straight into a shell, so a truncated transfer or a proxy error page would have been run — during an update, where nobody is watching the screen. Both downloads are now checked for a shebang first, and the failure is written to the update log.
  • [site] An address that does not exist on the website now says so. Any unknown path answered 200 with the whole home page instead of a 404 — the hosting serves the site's index.html whenever a request matches no file, and the site carried no 404.html to serve in its place. Harmless on a normal page; not here, where the entire install path is scripts fetched by their address and piped into a shell: a stale or mistyped one delivered ninety kilobytes of HTML to bash rather than failing, and curl --fail could not help, the server having announced success. The site now carries a real 404 page, which also points at the current installer names for anyone arriving on the ones used before the August rename.
  • [site] The all-in-one installer refuses to execute anything that is not a script. It fetched each half of the install straight into a shell, so anything the address returned was run. Both halves are now downloaded, checked for a shebang, and only then executed; a download that fails stops the install instead of letting it finish on a cheerful "installed", which a pipeline used to allow by reporting only the last command's status.
  • [site] The all-in-one installer no longer ends on an address that does not work. It closed by telling you to open https://<this-host>/, written into the script rather than derived from anything. The ui installer, two lines above, had already printed the address it actually deployed — protocol, host and port — and put that same address in the QR code. The two agreed only where a box serves HTTPS on the standard port; a box left in HTTP mode behind a reverse proxy was sent somewhere nothing listens. The closing line now points at the address that was just printed.

Changed

  • [site] [ops] Installing Audiogravity no longer needs an access token. Early access shipped behind a private releases repository, so every installation had to carry a personal access token: a 90-character credential that had to reach the new owner by e-mail, be pasted into a command line, and then live on in the box's configuration for one-click self-update. It was the same token for everyone, it expired on a fixed date, and a single mishandled copy revoked it for every owner at once — which is exactly what happened. The releases repository is now public and the token is gone from the whole path: --token is accepted but optional in all three installers, and the install command is a single short line anyone can type by hand. Boxes installed during early access still hold the old token in their configuration and must have it cleared once — an invalid credential is refused even when the repository is public, where sending none succeeds.

Fixed

  • [site] The install command on the landing page survives a copy-paste. The indentation of its continuation lines was written with non-breaking spaces, which browsers copy literally and shells do not read as whitespace: the pasted command handed curl a second, unresolvable address and answered curl: (6) Could not resolve host: before doing anything useful. The lines carry real spaces now, kept intact by the stylesheet rather than by an entity.

[0.9.43] - 2026-08-19

Fixed

  • [ui] The note explaining a dash on a service card now actually appears. 0.9.42 replaced an untrue 0 with a dash wherever a counter is switched off, and added a line under the Services title saying what the dash means and how to get the figure back. The dashes arrived; the line never did. The interface splits the box's measurement report into one message per service before delivering it, and the fact that says this machine counts no memory at all travels on the report as a whole — so it was being dropped on the way. Two tabs had been waiting on that whole report since v0.9.4 without anything ever sending it: the Services tab could never explain its dashes, and the Profiles tab never noticed a service starting or stopping until it was reopened. Both are fed now. Most visible on a Raspberry Pi, whose kernel starts with the memory counter off, where every card shows a dash for memory and the reason is exactly what the missing line was there to give.
  • [ui] A lost connection to the box now says so in the browser console. When the live stream dropped, the diagnostic message logged undefined instead of naming the failure: the worker sent it under one label and the page read another. Two of its three error paths were already right; this was the third, and the one an owner actually meets.

[0.9.42] - 2026-08-19

Fixed

  • [core] [ui] A figure nothing measured is no longer shown as zero. Three of the numbers on a service card depend on counters that can be switched off, and Audiogravity published all three absences as 0 under a flat graph — which is exactly what an idle service looks like. So a box measuring nothing looked like a box doing nothing. The cards now show a dash, with no graph, and the Services tab says once what a dash means and how to get the figure back. Disk and network are counted per service, and only once IO Accounting and IP Accounting are enabled for it in the Systemd tab — two switches, effective immediately. Memory depends on the machine: a Raspberry Pi kernel starts with that counter off, which no setting in the app can change, so the note links to the manual and its one-line boot change. CPU and tasks are always measured and are untouched. The manual gains both explanations, in Administration and in Troubleshooting.

[0.9.41] - 2026-08-18

Fixed

  • [core] The Roon retry no longer works your box for nothing, nor hides the one thing worth saying. The retry that 0.9.40 introduced was too eager and too quiet at once. Too eager: a box whose Roon Core is simply switched off probed for it every single minute, for as long as the box stayed on — a socket test, a network broadcast and four system queries a minute, on a machine tuned to leave the music alone. It now eases off to a quarter-hourly check while nothing answers, and returns to the quick cadence the moment you open the Roon card, so the click you make inside Roon is still noticed within the minute. Too quiet: the journal reported an unchanged situation once, which was the intent, but keyed that on a value another part of the box overwrites — so a Roon Core that died in the middle of a session, the one event an owner needs recorded, was the one guaranteed to leave no trace. It now always says so, and a Core that moved to another address is named again rather than silenced by an older report. A connection attempt that hangs — a Core that answers the door and then never finishes the introduction — is also confined to a single dedicated thread; before, one such attempt per minute quietly consumed the pool of workers the rest of the box shares, until nothing else could run and the Pipeline tab stopped answering altogether.
  • [ui] The Pipeline tab can be configured from a phone. Its CONFIG button — the way into the description of your hi-fi chain — existed only in the desktop layout, so on a phone there was no way in at all. That is the device most people have in hand when they notice something is off, and it is where an empty signal path sends you. The Pipeline tab now carries the same title bar on a phone, with INFO and CONFIG, and a guest still cannot edit anything. Writing a whole chain remains far easier at a keyboard — the description is a text file — but a phone is no longer a dead end.
  • [ui] [core] A signal path with nothing in it now says why. On a phone, the Pipeline tab showed the track playing and then a blank space where the chain of devices belongs — no box, no converter, no speakers, and nothing to explain the gap. The view draws a device only when music actually flows through one of the connections you have described, and a new box carries an example chain declaring a converter over USB and optical. A box playing through a HAT board — a converter mounted on a Raspberry Pi's connector — matches neither, so every device reads as idle and the whole path disappears. Measured on two boxes, both blank for exactly that reason. The panel now names the situation instead: it puts the two side by side — playing through: HiFiBerry DAC+ Pro against your chain declares: USB Audio Output, Optical Output — so you can see which of them has to change, and points at CONFIG just above. When everything does match and no music is on its way, it says that too. The manual gains the same explanation, in the Outputs chapter and in Troubleshooting.

[0.9.40] - 2026-08-17

Added

  • [ui] [core] The Roon card says where you are, and what is left to do. The last step of connecting to Roon happens inside Roon — enabling the extension — and the box cannot see you do it, so an expanded Roon card said "No zones available" and stopped there. The person who got through it read the box's journal over SSH to find out what was expected of him. The card now names the situation: no Roon Bridge running on this box, no Core answering, waiting for your authorization — with the extension's name to look for, straight from the box so the two can never say different things — or connected, with the number of zones. When it is waiting, I have enabled it checks again on the spot instead of making you wait for the next look, and the zone list refreshes itself the moment a session comes up.

Changed

  • [core] Setting Roon up no longer means opening a terminal. It used to take an SSH session, a configuration file, your Roon Core's IP address typed in by hand, and a restart of the box's backend — for a product whose whole point is that you never do that. Now: install Roon Bridge from the Audio Software page, then enable the Audiogravity extension in Roon → Settings → Extensions. Nothing else. Your box finds your Core on the network by itself (measured: five seconds), and Roon's own Enable click is what grants access — it always was, so asking for the same permission a second time in a file only made the feature hard to reach. The manual's Roon section is rewritten around the two steps. Three settings are gone with it, and none of them did what its name promised: the port was never read (Roon fixes it, and the value shipped was a different port entirely, closed on the Core), the switch duplicated Roon's own authorization, and the extension's name only made it harder to find in Roon's list — one box announced itself as "Aty" while its owner looked for "Audiogravity". Boxes that ran Roon Server rather than Roon Bridge were checked too: they get the same treatment, where an earlier version of this change would have shut them out.

Fixed

  • [core] A Roon Core started after the box is now picked up on its own. The box tried to connect exactly once, at startup: turn your Core on afterwards and Roon stayed absent until someone restarted the backend. It retries now, quietly and at most once a minute, without ever making the interface wait for it. The log stopped crying wolf too — waiting for you to enable the extension is written as the ordinary step it is, and names the extension to look for, instead of appearing as an error next to the real ones.

[0.9.39] - 2026-08-17

Fixed

  • [ui] Closing a browser tab no longer writes a crash report in the box's journal. Every time a browser dropped a connection it had finished with, the server that hands out the interface logged a full Python error trace — 29 of them in three days on one box, all identical, all harmless. The plain case was already handled; over an encrypted connection the same thing arrives under a different name, which was not. Nothing was ever broken, but a journal full of tracebacks hides the day something genuinely is. Only that one cause is silenced: a real encryption fault still shows.
  • [ops] A service you stop now stays stopped. Stopping MPD — from its card, or by switching to a profile that leaves it out — lasted under two seconds on a recently installed box: it was running again before the page had finished refreshing. Measured on one: stopped at 07:40:32, running again at 07:40:34. Debian ships MPD as two units, the daemon and a second one that holds its port and starts the daemon the moment anything connects to it; your box connects to that port continuously to report what is playing, so it revived what it had just been asked to stop. Audiogravity writes MPD's own configuration with the address and port it must listen on, so that second unit does nothing the daemon does not already do for itself — it is locked out at installation, and MPD now runs when, and only when, Audiogravity says so. Boxes that never showed the fault escaped it only because something had already switched that unit off; they get the same treatment at their next update, and uninstalling Audiogravity puts the unit back — running again at once, or from the next restart if MPD is holding the port by then. One side effect worth knowing: that second unit also decided where MPD could be reached, and it accepted connections from the whole network. MPD now listens where Audiogravity's own configuration has always said it should — on the box itself only. Nothing you use through Audiogravity changes: the interface, the UPnP renderer and playback all reach MPD from the box (measured on a box where MPD has always been local-only: the renderer is on the network and works). What stops working is a third-party MPD application on a phone or another computer talking to the box directly — which used to be possible by accident of the packaging, on a port that asks for no password.
  • [ui] The configuration editor stays in the view you chose. Opening a service's configuration lands on Guided, as it should; choosing Structured or Expert worked for about a second, then the editor slid back to Guided on its own — and any edit begun in the meantime went with it, without the usual question about discarding changes. Nothing about the click was at fault: the box streams each service's running state continuously to keep the status dots on the tiles current, and the editor mistook one of those refreshes for "a different service has been opened", which is the one moment it is supposed to return to the default view. It now tells the two apart by the service itself. Opening another service still starts you on Guided.
  • [ops] The end of the installation stops sending iPhone owners to a dead end. It printed the address, a QR code and "accept the browser warning to look around" — then explained, further down than most people read, how to trust the box's certificate. On an iPhone that order is not merely inefficient: Safari offers no way past the warning, so the address does not open at all and the QR code leads nowhere. The certificate step now comes first, and says which case you are in — required on an iPhone, optional-but-useful on Android and on a computer, where the warning can still be waved through for a look. The QR code follows, once the address will actually open. The user manual said the same thing in two places and is corrected with it.

[0.9.38] - 2026-08-16

Fixed

  • [core] The box now checks for new versions every day, and refreshes what it knows before answering. The check ran exactly once, a couple of seconds after the box started: one left on for three weeks answered with what was true the day it booted, and the "update available" notification could only ever appear in that one moment. It now repeats daily. More importantly, it first asks the system to re-read its package sources — the versions it reports come from a local list that nothing else necessarily refreshes, and whether anything does depends on the distribution: DietPi switches those automatic refreshes off, Raspberry Pi OS leaves them on. Same box, same code, two different answers. Measured on a DietPi box: the main Debian index was five weeks old, so a security update to MPD would have stayed invisible that whole time. Pressing Check updates now refreshes too, which is what the button was always taken to mean. The cost is bounded: the refresh is skipped when the lists are already recent (measured at 4.6 seconds for twelve sources when it does run), and an update already announced is not announced again every day.
  • [core] An installation no longer fails because of a repository that has nothing to do with it. Installing anything began by refreshing the system's package lists, and that step reports failure if any configured source is unreachable — a developer tools repository, a Node.js one, whatever else the box carries. The install stopped there, on a package that was very often already available locally. It now carries on and lets the package manager itself say whether it can find what was asked for. The refresh also waits its turn instead of failing when another operation is already using the package system, which is what used to happen when a check and an install overlapped.
  • [core] A download that cannot fit is refused before it starts. Nothing measured the free space, so a disk filling up mid-download left a truncated file — and on an update, the old version had already been replaced by the time the tool complained. The check asks for the size of the download plus as much again for unpacking, and never refuses when it cannot measure.
  • [ui] A card says when a service is installed but not configured. Installing a package does not configure it — that is a separate, deliberate step — so a freshly installed service runs on the settings its package ships and can play to the wrong output while looking perfectly ready. Nothing said so. The card now does, for the services Audiogravity manages, and only when it genuinely knows: a page that could not read the audio configuration says nothing rather than accusing everything. "Configured" means the file carries Audiogravity's own marker, not merely that a file exists — every package ships one.
  • [core] An upgrade keeps the configuration already on the box, and now says so on every path. All four audio configurations are files the packaging system tracks, and it asks what to do when a new default arrives. Only the plain-repository path stated the answer; the other three left it to the default behaviour, which happens to keep the existing file when nothing can be asked — an accident of the environment rather than a decision, and one that can surface as a failed operation instead. All four now state it.
  • [ui] The confirmation says when an operation will interrupt playback. Updating a package restarts the service it drives — the package's own installation script does it — and uninstalling stops it outright. That was not said anywhere, so an update chosen mid-album simply cut the music. The dialogue now says which service is about to be restarted or stopped, for the packages Audiogravity actually drives.
  • [ui] A card whose last operation failed offers a way out of it. It offered nothing at all — no retry, no removal — on the one card that most needs an action. What it offers now depends on what failed: an installation that failed leaves nothing on the box, so the card offers to try again; an update that failed leaves the previous version in place, so it offers to update or to remove.
  • [ui] An update badge can no longer outlive the update. The browser kept its own copy of the versions it had last been told about, with no expiry, and re-applied it over a fresh "nothing new" answer from the box — so a version that had stopped being offered could keep a badge lit on that browser indefinitely. The box now remembers them itself and re-checks daily, so the copy served no purpose.
  • [core] HQPlayer NAA finally reports the version its publisher offers, instead of the one you already have. It is not part of any package repository, so the system's package manager answered with the version already installed — Audiogravity compared a version with itself and always concluded "up to date". Measured on a box: 5.1.5-67 installed while Signalyst published 6.1.4-71, two major versions later, with nothing on screen to say so. The code that reads the publisher's own page had been written all along; nothing ever called it. Its update also no longer uninstalls first: the newer package is installed over the old one, so the audio output no longer disappears for the length of a download, and a failed download no longer leaves the box with nothing.
  • [core] Roon can be updated from the interface at all. Roon publishes no version number anywhere — its download has a fixed name and carries no version file — so the interface, which insisted on a number to compare, refused the action and left no way to update it. Updating Roon means re-running its own installer, which always fetches the current build; that is what the button now offers, saying plainly that there is nothing to compare and which build you currently have. A package that should announce a version and does not is still refused: that is a symptom, and a blind reinstall would hide it.
  • [core] A rebuilt UPnP bridge for 64-bit ARM now reaches boxes without a new release. Upstream publishes nothing for that architecture, so Audiogravity builds and hosts the packages itself — but their file names carry the version, and those names were written into the release. Publishing a rebuild therefore changed nothing until the next release edited them: the quick operation held hostage by the slow one. The file list now comes from the checksum manifest already published beside the packages and already downloaded to verify them, which also gives the version to display. The manifest is checked against the packages the release expects, so an unexpected, missing or duplicated entry stops the install before anything is downloaded.
  • [core] [ops] Uninstalling Roon Bridge now actually removes it, and its version is shown. On a box where Roon was installed by DietPi rather than by Audiogravity, the software card showed no version at all, and uninstalling reported success while deleting nothing — the program stayed on disk, complete, and only its service entry went away. The cause was a single assumption written down as a fact: that Roon lives in /opt/RoonBridge with its data under /var/roon. That is true of Roon's own installer, the one Audiogravity uses; DietPi's installs the same software in /opt/roonbridge with its data under /mnt/dietpi_userdata. Neither is the "right" one, and the distinction is not the machine or its operating system but who installed it — so a box running DietPi where Audiogravity installs Roon itself lands back on the first layout. Both paths are now read from the running service, which knows, and both are re-read after every install and uninstall so they cannot describe a state that has moved on. Removal goes through a small privileged helper that works the paths out for itself rather than being handed them, and it never touches the shared folder that sits above them — on a box with both Roon products, that folder holds the other one's data. It also refuses to report a removal it did not make: deleting a folder that is not there succeeds silently, and announcing that as done is exactly how the original fault stayed invisible — the service entry, the only thing that could still have found the files, was deleted right afterwards. If a previous attempt already removed that entry, the helper looks for both layouts by name rather than giving up on the one it exists for, and says plainly that the data folder can no longer be worked out.
  • [core] What your box is told it can install is now checked against what the sources actually publish. The list of architectures a package supports was read from the names of the folders on the software publisher's server. Upstream upmpdcli has a folder for 32-bit ARM that contains only its add-ons, not the program itself — and the repository's own manifest declares two architectures, neither of them that one. So every box carried "32-bit ARM supported" for a package apt would have refused to install. The manifest is now read, which is what apt itself obeys, and the presence of the actual package is confirmed for your architecture before it is offered.
  • [core] A ten-second network outage no longer marks software as permanently incompatible. If a publisher's site could not be reached while the list of installable software was being worked out, the package was recorded as unsupported — indistinguishable from software that genuinely has no build for your machine, and with no way back short of pressing the refresh button by hand. A box installed without internet came up with HQPlayer NAA, the UPnP bridge and Roon all marked unsupported, and nothing said why. The two are now told apart and the reason is written down and shown on the card: unreachable is unreachable, and it is worth trying again.
  • [core] [ops] Installing the UPnP bridge on a fresh box works. Adding a publisher's repository means writing two files owned by the system — a signing key and a source entry — and the permission the box grants its own service covered neither. The install failed at its first privileged step, on any box that did not already happen to have both files. Both are now granted, in exactly the shape the rest of the system already uses.
  • [ops] The permissions the box grants its own service name the files they apply to. The two new permissions — writing a repository's signing key and its source entry — named their destination with a wildcard. The permission system matches those without treating / as a boundary, so a wildcarded destination also matched paths climbing out of the folder it appeared to describe. Both destinations are fixed values, so both are now written out in full. The permissions file is also re-read after being written, and removed if it does not parse: a single malformed line disables the whole file, taking with it the permissions the rest of Audiogravity already depends on.
  • [core] The same repository is no longer declared twice. Audiogravity checked whether its own source file existed before writing one. A box where the same repository was already configured under another name, or in the newer file format, got a second declaration of it — which apt reports as a duplicate on every update. Any existing declaration of that repository is now found, whatever it is called and whichever format it uses.

Changed

  • [core] [ui] Roon Server is no longer offered on a box that already runs Roon Bridge, and the reverse. Roon Server already contains everything Roon Bridge does, so installing both means two Roon endpoints on one machine competing for the same audio output — and Roon's own installer neither detects the other product nor warns about it. The card now says which of the two is in the way instead of leaving a greyed-out button unexplained. A box that already has both keeps showing both, and keeps its uninstall and update buttons: the rule governs what can be installed, it does not take away the means of undoing a situation it exists to prevent.
  • [ui] The page's own INFO panel describes what the page now does. It still said that a card is dimmed when your architecture is unsupported — one of four reasons now — and, on checking versions, only that you should press a button, with nothing about the box checking daily on its own. In-app help is read before the manual, so help that describes the previous behaviour sends people looking for work the software has already done. The user manual's Audio Software section is updated in the same breath.
  • [core] A refused installation now says why it was refused. Whatever the reason — no build for your machine, a source that could not be reached, a conflicting package — the answer was always "not supported on this architecture", which for a conflict is simply untrue.
  • [core] The generated software list records what it was worked out against. The file the box builds for itself — which publisher folder to download from, which repository suite to use — carried no trace of the system release, architecture or date it was resolved for. Nothing could tell a current file from one left over from before an operating-system upgrade, still pointing at the previous release's downloads. It now says.
  • [ui] The interface no longer highlights its own labels when you press and hold, or drag across it. Audiogravity is a control console rather than a document, but a browser treats every button label, tab and section title as text waiting to be selected. A long press on a phone raised a blue highlight and the Copy / Look Up bubble over a button you were only trying to press; a mouse drag across the screen left a trail of highlighted words behind it. Selection is now off across the interface — and deliberately left on everywhere copying is the point: anything you type into, the details of an event, the differences shown before a service change is applied, and the short technical values such as an order number or a media server's address. The logs and the Device ID already had their own copy buttons and keep them. The terminal is untouched: it has always drawn its own selection rather than the browser's, and keeping the browser out of its way is precisely what leaves its copying intact.

Removed

  • [core] The "Test Package (Dummy)" card is gone from the Audio Software page. It installed nothing and existed only to exercise the install workflow during development, but it was declared alongside the real software, so every box carried it and every owner saw it. Removing it from the list the release ships is enough for a new installation; an existing box also rebuilds that list from the copy already on it, so both are cleared on upgrade — otherwise the card would come back the first time the page was refreshed.

[0.9.37] - 2026-08-13

Fixed

  • [core] The volume comes back after a DSD album — before, it stayed at maximum. DSD is a 1-bit stream that any attenuation degrades, so Audiogravity raises every volume to 100 while one plays and puts them back afterwards. The putting-back had a hole: it only ran if something was still playing at that exact moment. When a DSD album simply reached its end, or was stopped before choosing something else, the restore was skipped — and the levels it was holding were discarded in the same breath, so no later moment could recover them. Everything that played next did so at 100, and the only way back was to set the volume by hand. Measured on a box: 58 before the album, 100 during, 58 lost. It now restores whether or not the music is still running, and never forgets a level it has not put back. One nuance decided the shape of the fix: a player that fails to answer looks exactly like a player that stopped, and writing a level on the first would attenuate a stream still in flight — so the end of DSD is now confirmed by asking, once, rather than assumed from silence.
  • [ui] The volume slider no longer snaps back to a level you already left. Drag it and, a moment after letting go, it could jump back to somewhere in the middle of your own gesture before settling on the value you chose. The previous release fixed one cause of this and it does hold — measured, nothing arrives out of order any more. What remained is that a drag sends one command per movement, so for about a second after you let go the box is still publishing the levels your finger passed through. The slider was covering that with a fixed one-and-a-half second pause, which measured out at a quarter-second of margin: enough on an idle box, not enough on a busy one, a slower one, or after a longer drag. It now holds the level you asked for until the box confirms that exact level, so the margin no longer decides. The same gap existed in the bottom player, where nothing had gone wrong yet — it is closed there too.

[0.9.36] - 2026-08-13

Added

  • [core] Qobuz, Tidal and HIGHRESAUDIO play through HQPlayer. Choosing HQPlayer as your output used to forbid all three: the answer was an immediate refusal saying the route did not exist, and turning HQPlayer off was the only way to hear them — a hard choice between your streaming subscription and your DSP engine. Nothing had to be invented to lift it. Your local library, internet radio and media servers already reach HQPlayer as a web address it fetches by itself, and the streaming services already publish exactly such an address on your own network, because network speakers have been pulling them that way for months. The same mechanism now points at them. Qobuz and HIGHRESAUDIO go one better: the address redirects straight to the service's own servers, so your box hands over an address and steps aside rather than carrying hours of music — measured at four percent of one processor core during playback, which is the cost of watching, not of relaying. Tidal is the exception and has to pass through the box, because it delivers its audio in a form that must be converted first; measured on a genuine first listen, HQPlayer plays that conversion while it is still being written, knows the full track length, and never runs its buffer dry. Nothing is re-encoded on any of the three paths: what reaches your DAC is bit-for-bit what the service sent.

Fixed

  • [core] With HQPlayer as your output, the title, the artist and the cover follow the track — they used to stay on the first one for the whole album. Play an album and the progress bar moved on correctly at every track change while the screen kept showing track one, from the first note to the last. This was never fixable by asking HQPlayer: it reads no tags at all from a stream it fetches over the network, and answers "HTTP stream" for a perfectly tagged Hi-Res file. So Audiogravity now keeps the list it sent and reads which entry HQPlayer is on — the answer comes from its own side. It follows on everything it pushes: your library, media servers and the three streaming services alike. Two situations still show no title, and both are honest: a playback you started from HQPlayer's own remote, which Audiogravity never sent and cannot name, and a playlist you rearrange from HQPlayer itself — noticed, and left unlabelled rather than captioned with the wrong album. Internet radio is a separate matter and still shows the station rather than the song, because there it is HQPlayer that receives the stream and it keeps only the station's name.
  • [core] Playing an album while another one plays now replaces it, instead of queueing behind it. With HQPlayer as your output, starting a second album left the first one playing and added the new one after it — while the screen showed the new album's title and cover. You were looking at one record and listening to another, with no way to tell which. The command that empties HQPlayer's queue turns out not to remove the track that is loaded and playing; it is now stopped first, which empties it completely. This affected every source that goes through HQPlayer, your own library included.
  • [core] A track found by searching a media server plays through HQPlayer. With HQPlayer as your output, playing a search result from a UPnP server failed outright: the request was handed to the local library index, which was asked to find a file named after a web address and, reasonably, found nothing. Playing the same track from the server's folders always worked — only the search results were affected, and the failure looked like a broken server rather than a wrong turn inside Audiogravity.
  • [core] Tidal in a quality that cannot play now says so, instead of going quiet. Tidal's lossy qualities deliver AAC, which cannot be converted losslessly — so nothing came out, on any output, with no explanation. Audiogravity now checks what Tidal actually serves before starting, not what your account asked for, and names the cause. That distinction matters: an album unavailable in lossless is served as AAC even when your quality setting is right, and that is precisely the case a setting cannot warn you about. The check costs one request per album, not one per track.
  • [core] A refusal names the track the way your screen shows it. When HQPlayer cannot decode a format, the message quoted the file name; it now quotes the title, and an album still refuses as a whole rather than stopping halfway through.
  • [ui] [ops] The certificate you are told to install can now actually be fetched — on an iPhone it could not be, at all. Boxes installed with HTTPS were pointed at https://<box>/ca.crt to collect the authority to trust. That address cannot work, and the reason is circular: fetching it over HTTPS means already trusting the very certificate you are coming to collect. Safari on iOS refuses outright — no warning to accept, nothing to tap past — so the procedure was impossible on the device it was written for, and with it the app install that depends on it. The box now hands the authority out over plain HTTP on port 8081, and that port serves exactly one public file: every other address on it is redirected straight to the HTTPS interface — no interface, no API, no proxy in the clear. Being unencrypted costs nothing here, and is the point: a certificate authority is public by design — it is the thing you are meant to hand out — and it carries no key. If something else already occupies the port, the box notes it and carries on playing.
  • [ops] [site] The steps printed by the installer and written in the manual were wrong, and had never been performed. They were written from memory rather than from a device, so they named menus that do not exist and stopped short of the one action that matters. The iPhone steps are now the ones verified end to end on a phone: open the address in Safari and allow the download, then Settings → Profile Downloaded → Install, then Settings → General → About → Certificate Trust Settings and switch on Audiogravity Local CA — installing the profile alone changes nothing, that last switch is what makes the box trusted. The Android instructions no longer give a menu path at all: it differs between Android versions and between manufacturers, so any fixed path would be wrong for most phones. They describe the goal instead — install the file as a trusted authority from the system settings — with the two things that actually stop people: the phone must have a screen lock or Android refuses to store an authority, and since Android 11 only the Settings app may start the install, so tapping the downloaded file does nothing. The manual states plainly that, unlike iPhone, the Android path has not been verified on a device.

[0.9.35] - 2026-08-12

Added

  • [core] You can now move around inside a track played through HQPlayer, and its progress bar works. The bar was there but inert: the elapsed time counted up, the bar never moved, and dragging it did nothing. Audiogravity had HQPlayer down as unable to seek, and never asked it for a track's length. Asking its remote-control connection directly showed both assumptions were wrong — it accepts a jump to any position, and it works out a track's length by itself, including for a stream nobody described to it. Both are now used: the bar has a scale, it advances, and you can drag it. A live stream still shows none, which is the honest answer for something with no end. Two things follow that are worth knowing: the length HQPlayer measures is preferred over the one the source supplied, because it is right more often; and a playback started from HQPlayer's own remote — which used to show nothing at all — now at least shows how long the track is and where you are in it.

Fixed

  • [core] A track from your media server played through HQPlayer shows its length, and the progress bar moves. The elapsed time counted up correctly while the bar sat frozen at the far left and the remaining time read −0:00 — a bar with no total has nothing to measure progress against. Your server had published the length all along: for the track this was found on, it announces seven minutes twenty. Audiogravity read that value, then lost it twice over on the way to the screen — once because the record it fills when you browse a server had no room for it, and once because the record it fills when you search never copied it across. Both paths now carry it through. A track whose server publishes no length, or a live stream that has none, still shows none — which is the honest answer.
  • [core] The volume slider no longer jumps back to where it started before landing on the value you chose. Dragging it produced a brief, unmistakable flick back to the old position. The player describes itself to your screens from three places at once — a regular check, an immediate refresh after each command, and network-speaker events — and nothing kept them in order. A check that began reading just before your change finished reading just after, so it published the old volume after the new one had already gone out, and every screen dutifully followed it back. Measured on a real drag: 47 published, then 48, then 46. Each description now carries the instant its reading began, and one that started earlier than something already published is dropped. This was never only about volume — any control could be walked back the same way — but volume is where a drag makes it happen almost every time.
  • [ui] [security] The interface no longer hands out its own private key to anyone on your network. The box's certificate and its private key were stored inside the folder the interface serves its pages from, and the server made no distinction: asking it for /ssl/key.pem returned the key itself, in full, to any device on the network — no password, no login. That key is what proves the box is the box; with it, someone on your network could impersonate it. The store now lives outside anything the interface serves, an upgrade moves it there and deletes the exposed copy, and the server refuses that whole path as a second lock — including the spellings that try to slip past it. Only the public certificate, the one your devices are meant to install, is published.
  • [ops] The certificate your box makes for itself can finally be trusted — which is what lets it become a real app. If you install with HTTPS, the box signs its own certificate, and browsers warn about it because no authority vouches for it. Accepting that warning was enough to look around, so the problem stayed invisible; it was not enough to install Audiogravity as an app, because a phone only grants a site an icon, an offline start and notifications once it genuinely trusts the certificate. And trusting this one was impossible: it identified itself only in a field that Chrome, Safari and Firefox stopped reading in 2017, so it was refused outright, and installing it by hand as trusted did not repair that. Android's Install app prompt, in particular, could never appear. The box now creates its own small certificate authority, and signs the interface's certificate with it. You install that authority once per phone or computer — the installer prints where to get it and the exact steps for iPhone, Android, macOS and Windows, and the manual has a chapter for it — and from then on the box is trusted, warnings stop, and the app installs like any other. Once per device, and it holds for years.
  • [ops] Upgrading a box that is already installed replaces its certificate, and only when that certificate is broken. Every box installed before this release carries the unusable kind, so upgrading swaps it for one signed by the box's new authority — nothing to undo first, and if you had installed that old certificate on a phone it simply becomes obsolete. A certificate you supplied yourself is a different matter: a valid one is detected and left alone, whoever issued it, with no authority created and nothing published; the box only steps in if it has actually expired or carries none of the identification browsers require.
  • [ops] A box that changed address no longer serves a certificate for the old one. The certificate was written at the first install and never looked at again, so moving the box to another address — or a router handing it a new one — left it presenting a name that no longer matched, forever. It is now re-examined before every start and once a day, and reissued when the address changed or the expiry approaches. The authority is never reissued, so your phones and computers keep trusting the box through all of it, with nothing to redo.
  • [ops] The installer's QR code takes a third less room. It was drawn with the widest margin the standard allows, which on a terminal meant a block of 58 columns by 29 lines for a symbol that is only 21 modules across. The margin is now the minimum: same symbol, same precision — the modules are drawn exactly as before, which is what makes it scannable — in 46 by 23.

[0.9.34] - 2026-08-11

Added

  • [ui] A whole class of blank-panel bugs can no longer reach you. Nothing changes on screen today; what changes is what can ship tomorrow. The interface's stylesheets have been checked automatically for years, and its JavaScript had never been checked at all — so a rename that landed on one side only, leaving code reading a name that no longer exists, was found by whoever happened to open the right panel. That is not hypothetical: it happened twice in one afternoon in the audio pipeline diagram, and each time the panel stopped drawing entirely until the page was reloaded, because once that kind of error is thrown every later attempt throws again. The check that catches it takes about a second, refuses the commit that carries the mistake, and runs over every file on every test run so it cannot be walked past. It looks for exactly one thing — a name used and never defined — and deliberately nothing else: an interface is a matter of taste, a name that does not exist is a crash.
  • [ops] The installer ends with a QR code, and tells you how to make it an app. The interface lives at a private address on your own network — it cannot be shortened, guessed or looked up, so until now it had to be typed by hand onto the phone most people actually use it from. The installer now prints a QR code of that exact address at the end, followed by the three steps that turn it into a fullscreen app with its own icon on Android, iPhone and desktop, taken word for word from the manual. The certificate warning is explained where you meet it — right after the code, phone in hand — instead of three screens earlier. The code is only drawn when a person is watching: an upgrade, or the one-click self-update which runs detached with no terminal, prints nothing and installs nothing. On a narrow terminal it switches to a compact rendering, and on a very narrow one it steps aside and leaves the address alone rather than print a symbol that cannot be scanned.

Fixed

  • [core] [ui] A control that does not work no longer pretends it did. Press pause, skip a track, set the volume, toggle repeat or shuffle: the interface flipped immediately, and the player's answer was never read — the command counted as done the moment it was sent. Almost always the two agree; the exceptions were an interface showing a pause that never happened, or a volume the output cannot set because the DAC has no volume control of its own. Every transport command now waits for the player's verdict, and on a refusal the button or slider simply returns to the true state — without a message, deliberately: a control that visibly does not act is the honest answer, and a notification would be noise for something this rare. One case had stayed misleading for good: the volume slider kept showing the level you dragged to until the panel was closed. It now glides back to the real level a moment after you let go — invisible when the change took, honest when it did not. And after every command, refused or not, the player broadcasts its actual state, so whatever any screen was guessing is corrected within the second.
  • [core] Switching outputs can no longer end in silence that calls itself success. The switch enabled the output you chose and disabled the others in whatever order the list happened to come, and never read the answer. If the new output refused to open — in use by another program, cable pulled — the others were sometimes already off: every output disabled, and the screen reporting the switch had worked. The new output is now enabled before anything is disabled, in one atomic exchange, so a refusal leaves the previous output playing; and the refusal is reported with the player's own reason instead of a success that isn't one. The output you picked is also addressed by its identity rather than by its name, which two outputs in one configuration are allowed to share — picking the second of two same-named devices used to switch to the first.
  • [core] [ui] Adding music to the queue says when it did not work. Play an album, queue a track, start a radio station, remove a row from the queue: the answer was always "done", because the player's reply was never read. The cases where that matters are ordinary ones — a station whose address the player rejects, an album that left the library index since the page was drawn, a queue row someone already removed. Each looked like a click that worked and left you facing a screen that disagreed with the music. They now report what actually happened, with the player's own reason, and the queue view says so instead of writing it to a log nobody reads. Two exceptions are deliberate, because failing the whole request would lie in the other direction: when several tracks are queued and the list stops partway, the ones that made it in are kept and counted; and the titles attached to queued streams are decoration, so failing to write them never cancels an enqueue that worked.
  • [core] A library that never got indexed no longer reports that it did. After you point Audiogravity at a new music folder, it asks the player to re-index — and that request is sent in the exact moment the player is restarting, so it can be dropped. The reply was not read: the log said "rescan triggered", the five follow-up attempts were skipped, and the new library simply never appeared. The reply is read now, and a dropped request is retried like any other failure.
  • [core] A player that hangs can no longer pin a button with it. Every exchange with the player now carries a strict deadline of two and a half seconds covering the whole conversation — including one step, the greeting the player sends on connection, that previously had no limit at all: a player wedged mid-restart could hold a tapped button forever. One nuance is handled honestly: a command that times out was already delivered and may still act, so the interface does not assume it failed — it re-syncs on the state the player broadcasts right after.
  • [core] A Tidal track can be seeked the first time you play it. Until now the first play of any Tidal track ignored the progress bar: the track reaches the player as a stream being written while it plays, and the player only learns where a stream's parts are once it has read it through — so it declined every jump until the next play. What it needed already existed: a complete, seekable copy of the track finishes downloading a few seconds in, and was simply never used for the listen in progress. It is now — when you drag the bar, the track is quietly reopened from that copy and the jump lands, which looks exactly like an ordinary seek since a seek always pauses for an instant anyway. Two consequences worth knowing. Dragging the bar in the very first seconds, before the copy is ready, is still declined — and now says so instead of quietly doing nothing, which also means the position you see stays the position you are at. And a seek on a live radio stream is likewise reported as declined rather than silently ignored: a live stream has no end to jump to. Dragging the bar again while a reopen is already under way is declined too, rather than queued: each queued jump would have reopened the track in turn, so a few impatient drags meant hearing the track restart several times over.
  • [core] Tracks queued from Qobuz, Tidal, HIGHRESAUDIO or a UPnP server show their length. They showed --:-- where a track from your own library shows its duration. The reason is that MPD, which plays them, only learns how long a stream is by decoding it — so a track sitting in the queue, waiting its turn, had no length to report. The length was known all along: the service that supplied the track had said so when it was queued, and the value was being dropped a line later. It is now kept, and kept on disk, so it survives restarting the box. Internet radio still shows --:--, which is the honest answer for a live stream. Tracks already in your queue from before this update keep showing --:-- until they are queued again — their length was never recorded, so there is nothing to recover. The player's own remaining-time display draws on the same value, so it can no longer disagree with the queue about the track they are both showing.
  • [ui] HQPlayer's volume reads as a volume again. The DSP panel printed the level as -13.800000190734863 dB. Nothing was wrong with the level itself: HQPlayer keeps it in a smaller number format than the one it is read into, and the trailing digits are the noise of that conversion — a −13.8 that cannot be written exactly on the way across. It now reads -13.8 dB, and the slider beneath it moves in tenths to match, where it used to snap to the nearest half and leave the handle pointing somewhere other than the printed figure.
  • [ui] The library's tabs are named on a phone too, and the bar no longer runs off the screen. The five tabs — Browse, Search, Queue, Library, Radio — carried their names on a desktop and were reduced to bare glyphs on a phone, which is the screen the library is browsed from most: five unlabelled symbols, and nothing to say which was which. The names now sit under the icons everywhere. Making them fit meant taking back the room they needed: the source you are browsing has left the bar for the head of the content it describes — where it reads better anyway, since it says what you are looking at rather than where you can go — and the bar has stopped spending a sixth of a phone's width on its own margins. If it still runs short, a long source name or a narrow screen, the bar can be dragged sideways, and the selected tab is brought back into view whenever it changes.
  • [ui] Tapping the highlighted tab gets you out of where you are. Four screens show a tab selected that is not quite them: the outputs list shows Library, and the artist, Roon and UPnP browsers all show Browse. Tapping that tab — the obvious way back — did nothing at all, because the interface treated it as a tab you were already on. Easy to miss while the names were hidden; a clearly named, clearly selected, completely dead control once they were shown.
  • [ui] The buttons beside the tabs say what they do. Refresh, Outputs, and the way back from the outputs list were bare symbols carrying a tooltip, which a phone never shows. They now give their names like the tabs, set apart by a hairline because they do something rather than take you somewhere, and they are real buttons — reachable with a keyboard, which they were not.
  • [ui] Internet radio has an icon of its own. It was concentric arcs around a dot, drawn from the same vocabulary as the Wi-Fi and cast icons that sit near it, and at the smallest size the three were hard to tell apart. It is a radio set now: an object rather than a wave. The same drawing had also been standing in for the selected state of a radio button in two settings screens — a pun on the word rather than a meaning — and those use a proper filled circle.
  • [ops] The address the installer prints, and the certificate it issues, are the one your phone can actually reach. Both were taken from the first entry of the list of every address the machine holds — a list with no useful order. On a box running Docker, a bridge, a VPN or simply a second network interface, that first entry can be an address nothing outside the box can route to: the installer named it, and the certificate was issued for it. It now asks the routing table which address a packet leaving the machine would carry, which is the question actually being asked, and falls back to the old method on a machine with no default route. Certificates already generated are untouched.
  • [ui] On a phone or a tablet, every field is the size of its own label again. iPhone Safari zooms the page in whenever you tap a field whose text is declared smaller than a certain size, and it never zooms back out — so Audiogravity raised every field on a touch screen to that size. It worked, and it was the wrong price: a form was drawn with its fields visibly larger than the labels above them, on every screen that has one. What that guard actually inspects, it turns out, is the size a field declares — not the size it ends up drawn at. Fields still declare the size Safari wants, and are now drawn at the size the form was designed with. Measured on an iPhone: the page stays put, and the pair reads as one again. On an iPhone older than iOS 16.4 the field simply keeps its former size — bigger than its label, but still never zooming, because the protection never depends on the new part.
  • [ui] Dropdown lists were being protected from something that cannot happen to them. They were held at that same enlarged size from the start, on the assumption that every form control zooms. Nobody had checked. A dropdown opens a picker wheel, not a keyboard, and measured on an iPhone it does not zoom the page at any size — so the rule was buying nothing and costing the lists their proportions. It showed worst on the HQPlayer DSP card, where the Filter, Shaper and Mode lists sit under deliberately small labels and were being drawn a third larger than them. Lists now keep the size their own screen gives them, on touch as on desktop.
  • [site] The editions page listed the Pro library without HIGHRESAUDIO. Two sentences on that page still named only Qobuz and Tidal — the feature list for the Pro library, and the note about needing your own subscription — although HRA has been a first-class source since 0.9.10 and is named throughout the landing page. Anyone comparing the editions before buying, HRA included, would have read that their service was not covered. Both sentences now name all three.
  • [site] The third-party notices claimed more of HIGHRESAUDIO than HRA has actually done. The notice stated that HRA "reviewed the interface built on it". What HRA saw were early screenshots; the application itself has not been in their hands, and their own condition — to test and confirm before a public release — has not yet been met. A notice that credits a partner with a review they have not given is a claim about someone else, published on their behalf, and it is the kind of sentence that costs a working relationship if the partner reads it first. It now says what happened: HRA supplied their API and saw early screenshots of it. The rest of the paragraph is unchanged, and still makes no claim of partnership or endorsement.
  • [ui] [core] In your local library, All, Recent and A–Z now show three different things. They showed the same list. Two reasons: the albums already arrive sorted alphabetically, so A–Z re-did work that was done — and Recent never looked at a date at all. It took the first fifty albums alphabetically and called them recent, which is why a library starting with & and 10,000 Hz Legend opened on those under every heading. Recent now means what it says: most recently added to your library first, whatever the record's release year — a 1959 pressing copied in this week sits at the top. Albums added on the same day keep their alphabetical order, so a library that was indexed in one go stays readable instead of appearing in the order the disc happened to be read. And Recent is no longer capped at fifty: the whole library scrolls in that order.

[0.9.33] - 2026-08-09

Fixed

  • [ui] The sign-in screen can be read, and the button says when it is working. Its grey texts — the line under the logo, the version at the foot, the placeholder in each field, the link back to the password form — were set in a grey that falls below the contrast the text of an interface owes its reader: legible on a studio monitor, gone on a laptop in daylight. They are darker now, and the version line is no longer set at the size reserved for short uppercase labels. The message shown when a password is refused was stated in red on a pale red ground, which is the least legible way to say the one thing on that screen a person has to read; the alert keeps its red border and tinted ground, and the sentence itself is now plain dark text, with the icon beside it carrying the meaning for anyone who cannot see the colour. A failed passkey attempt is shown in that same alert rather than as a line of small red text unlike anything else on the page. And the spinner on the sign-in button, in dark mode, was drawn in white on a white button — the button appeared to do nothing at all while it worked. It now takes the button's own text colour. Three of the colours and sizes involved were asking for values that were defined nowhere in the interface, which is why a stylesheet that looked correct did not behave correctly.

  • [ui] An error inside a list can be read. The panel shown when a list of albums, stations or servers fails to load stated itself in red on a pale red ground, exactly as the sign-in message did and with the same result. It keeps its red border and tinted ground and states itself in plain dark text. It also takes that red from the colour each theme defines for an error, rather than from one theme's value that the others were quietly inheriting.

  • [ui] The faint grey text throughout the interface can be read. Secondary and tertiary text — labels, captions, metadata, placeholders, version lines: a hundred and seventeen places — was set in greys that look considered on a good monitor in a dim room and disappear on a laptop outdoors. They were between 2.08 and 3.5 times lighter than the background where legibility asks for four and a half. Every one of them, in all three themes and in both light and dark, now clears that bar, with the three levels of the hierarchy still distinct from one another. Nothing changes hue; what changes is how far each grey sits from the page behind it.

  • [ui] Coloured text — a status, a licence, an error, a warning — is readable at last. Those colours were doing three jobs with one value: outlining a badge, tinting its fill, and writing the words on top. A red dark enough to read is too dark for the outline it was chosen for, so the words lost. They now have their own reading shade, same hue, while the outlines and fills keep the colour you know: a green status was 2.15 times lighter than its background and is now 5. The whole interface is covered — roughly a hundred and fifty places, badges, statuses, validation messages and licence panels among them.

  • [ui] Two more things that had never followed your theme. A placeholder square behind album art in the queue was painted a fixed light grey, so it stayed light in every dark theme; and the legend of the audio pipeline no longer disagrees with the lines it describes. Both were reading colour names that existed nowhere in the interface, which is a mistake nothing reports — the colour written beside the name simply renders, forever.

  • [ui] Audiogravity no longer flashes white before it appears. The appearance you chose — theme and dark mode — was applied by the interface's own code, which cannot run until half a megabyte of it has been fetched and parsed. Until then every screen was drawn in the light palette, so anyone using the dark theme met a bright white flash on every cold start, on a device people mostly listen to in the dark. The browser's own bar flashed with it, having been given one fixed colour that belonged to a single theme whatever yours was, and the sign-in screen appeared in the default theme before switching to yours a moment later. All three are read and applied before anything is drawn now, so the first thing on screen is already the appearance you picked.

  • [ui] The interface is set in its own typeface, and the box serves it. Two separate things kept that from being true. The typeface was fetched from Google's servers on every load, which is a request to a third party, a visitor's address handed to it, and a wait before the first screen can be drawn — on a device that may sit on a network with no route out at all, it is simply a typeface that never arrives. And the default theme replaced it with whatever font the operating system provides, so on most installations the file was downloaded and then discarded: the interface looked like a Mac on a Mac, a PC on a PC, and never like itself. Inter now ships with Audiogravity and is served by the installation, on every screen including sign-in, with nothing requested from the internet and no font host left in the security policy. The default theme uses it like the others. It is stored alongside the rest of the interface, so it is there offline from the first visit.

  • [ui] Values you read digit by digit are in a typeface made for it. Frequencies, temperatures, identifiers, addresses, log tables and the terminal were set in Courier New — a typewriter face from the 1950s, thin on a modern screen and the one thing on an otherwise current interface that looked its age. They now use JetBrains Mono, served by the installation like the rest. Its figures are the same width, so a value that refreshes in place no longer makes the layout shift under the eye. The reverse was corrected with it: a handful of things that are read rather than transcribed had drifted into that typeface and have come back to the interface one — three status badges, a confirmation sentence, a title and an error label.

  • [ui] A theme can now carry its own typeface. Themes are the part of the interface open to contribution, and a theme that declared a typeface was obeyed everywhere except the sign-in screen, which pinned its own. The rule is now a single one with no exception: a theme states the typeface it wants, and the whole interface follows.

  • [ui] Seven screens no longer need the internet to appear. Configuration, Profiles, Performance, Systemd, Services, Audio software and the dashboard were built on a small library the interface fetched from a public code distributor every time it loaded. A missing typeface would have been a matter of appearance; missing code is not — those pages rendered nothing at all on a machine with no route out, or behind a network that blocks that distributor. The library ships with Audiogravity now, inside a file the interface already downloads, so it costs no request at all. It was in fact already installed and unused, and one screen was loading it that way, so the interface had been carrying two copies of the same library at two different versions.

  • [core] The music keeps priority over the rest of the box. Nothing on the machine told the system that playback matters more than the control interface: when the core did something heavy — installing a package, scanning, or simply misbehaving — it competed on equal footing with the player for processor, memory and disk cache. On an idle box this changed nothing and still changes nothing; the core spends about a tenth of a percent of one processor at rest, measured over three days. But under contention the player now wins two thirds of the processor, and a burst of memory from the core is slowed down rather than allowed to push the player's own data out to disk. A runaway is capped and restarted in seconds instead of dragging the whole machine into swap while you are listening.

  • [ui] An interface that has lost its key no longer hammers the box it cannot reach. A browser whose stored key was gone — cleared storage, a broken installation — kept sending every request anyway: cover art fetches even carried the literal word "null" where the key belonged. Measured on one box: 598 rejected requests in a day, each costing the player's machine a request cycle and a journal line, none able to succeed. Such a client now sends a single request, reads the refusal, and goes quiet: it shows "Not connected" with the reason instead of an interface that looks alive and loads nothing. Installations configured to run without a key are recognised by that same first request and keep working as before. The box's budget is for music; a client with a screen and a processor of its own carries its own failure now.

  • [ui] Small text you actually read is a size larger. Timestamps, log times, IP addresses, error messages, tooltips, track durations and the descriptions under a source were set at the same size as the badges beside them — a size chosen for things you identify at a glance rather than read. Twenty-nine of them move up one step. Badges, format tags and short labels stay where they were: the interface is a dense working surface and that density is deliberate.

  • [ui] The manual's title is bigger than its section headings again. It asked for the size above them and was given the same one, because two steps of the interface's type scale had been created with the same value — so the two rendered identically on a desktop and only came apart on a narrow window. The scale had eight names for seven sizes; it has seven of each now, declared in one place instead of two a hundred lines apart, which is how the duplicate went unnoticed. Nothing else changes size: every screen that used the affected step keeps the size it had.

  • [lic] The licence pages breathe evenly. The space between things had grown to twenty-one different values, a pixel or two apart in places — differences nobody chose and nobody can see individually, but which add up to a page that reads as slightly restless. There are seven now, and where a value had to move it moved outward rather than inward, so nothing is tighter than it was.

  • [lic] The page where you fetch your licence is set at a size meant for reading. It shared its text sizes with the administration console, which is a dense working surface — rows compared against rows, consulted at length. The portal is the opposite: opened once, often from a phone, by someone who has just reinstalled a machine and wants a file. Every size on it moves up one step. Behind that, the twenty-two sizes those pages had grown — eight of them within two pixels of one another, differences no eye resolves — are now six, and every one of them is a deliberate step.

  • [lic] Tapping a field on the licence portal no longer zooms the page in. iPhone Safari zooms in on any field whose text is smaller than a certain size, and does not zoom back out — the page stays magnified and the button you were reaching for is off-screen. The stylesheet already guarded against this, but six fields carried their size written on the element itself, which overrides any stylesheet: the guard was there and had no effect. Among them the device-id field on the portal, which is the first thing a customer touches.

  • [lic] The campaign preview showed the unsubscribe footer at the wrong size. The preview is drawn in a frame that does not load the interface's stylesheet, so the footer's size was dropped there and shown at the browser's default, while the message actually sent to recipients used the intended smaller text. A preview is consulted precisely to decide whether to send; the two now match, and are checked against each other.

  • [lic] The page where you re-download your licence no longer depends on anyone else. It loaded the code it is built from — and its typeface — from a public code distributor on the internet. That worked until it did not: an outage there, a company network that blocks it, and the page rendered nothing at all. Blank, with no explanation, at the moment someone who has reinstalled their machine comes to fetch their licence file. Both now come from the licence server itself, which is the only machine that has to be reachable for that page to work. The same applies to the administration console, where scripts fetched from a third party ran with full access to a page holding customer records.

  • [lic] The licence pages can be read in daylight, and in the light theme. Their light theme had never been given colours of its own, so it wore the ones drawn for a black page: greens, ambers and reds that measured as little as 1.4 times lighter than white where legibility asks for four and a half. A status, an expiry warning, a refusal — the things on those pages that exist to be read — were the least readable text on them. Every colour now has a value for each theme and a separate shade for when it is text rather than a border or a fill. Several were asking for colours defined nowhere at all, which is a fault nothing reports: a badge marking a customised template was drawing black on black and simply could not be seen, and the links to release notes, to support and to an announcement's address had lost any sign of being links.

  • [lic] The licence pages are set in the same typefaces as the rest of Audiogravity. They used whatever the system provided, so the customer portal looked like a different product on every machine that opened it. They now use Inter, and JetBrains Mono for the values read character by character — licence keys, fingerprints, order numbers — both served by the licence server rather than fetched from a font host.

  • [ui] The service state and its button are the same size again. On the network stability test, the green ACTIVE marker sat beside the STOP SERVICE button and was visibly shorter and narrower — a fixed height where the button derives its own from its padding, a smaller type size, and a monospaced family where the rest of the interface's uppercase labels use the interface font. The two now share a box, so they read as one control rather than two that do not line up.

  • [ui] The red "Remove" backdrop no longer frames a row nobody is removing. On an iPhone, swiping a list left drew a red frame around the station under the finger, and it stayed there — including while a station was being chosen. The backdrop that a swipe uncovers was painted under every row at all times, and a row that had been touched once kept the displacement it was given, which on iOS is enough for a sliver of that backdrop to show around its edges for the rest of the session. The backdrop now exists only while a row is genuinely being dragged left, and a row returns to being an ordinary row as soon as the finger lifts. Four lists share this gesture — radio stations, UPnP servers, renderers and the playback queue — so all four are fixed, and three neighbouring faults with them: dragging a row and then merely tapping another one used to strand the first, a rightward drag or the drift of a vertical scroll uncovered the backdrop under a row that had not moved, and a removed row handed its red backdrop to whichever row took its place.
