  • [ui] The licence panel says the same thing once. A trial announced the days left three times over — the badge, a sentence and the progress bar — one of them assembled from a template, so it read "27 day(s) remaining". The price was stated twice on the way to paying. The badge and the bar keep the count, the offer keeps the price, and the panel is two lines shorter. A box whose trial has ended is still shown the price, which is where the previous release's fix had left a gap: it removed the repeated price without noticing that the wording shown after a trial ends carries no figure of its own, and neither does the "About Licensing" help.

  • [ui] The purchase sentence in the licence panel is a whole sentence again. The price comes from the licence server, so it is empty until the first fetch lands — and stays empty where none is configured. It was dropped into the middle of the sentence regardless, which read "one-time payment of , no subscription": a stray comma, on the panel where the customer is being asked to buy. The clause is now left out when there is no price. The tests covering that sentence were checking a copy of the logic rather than the panel itself, so they could not have seen this; they now render the panel.

  • [ui] The System tab shows the core's logs again. The log panel asked the system journal for a service named audiogravity-backend, which was renamed some time ago. A journal asked for a unit that does not exist does not complain — it answers with an empty log — so the panel showed nothing at all, with no sign that anything was wrong. It now asks for the service that is actually running.

  • [ui] The interface calls the core "the core", as the rest of Audiogravity does. The System tab's action was labelled "Restart Backend", and the confirmation, the notification that followed it, the log panel's title and the connection indicator all used the same word — one component for which the documentation, the site and the manual have said "core" throughout. The indicator's tooltip was also the only text in the interface still written in French, and the development-mode prompt for an API key pointed at a backend/.env that does not exist. Nothing changes in behaviour; the addresses the interface calls are untouched.

[0.9.32] - 2026-08-06

Added

  • [site] The user manual has somewhere to be read. Every link to it pointed at GitHub, so anyone following "Where is the documentation?" landed on a code host — and the search traffic for our own manual went there too. The twelve chapters are now published as ordinary pages on audiogravity.app, with a chapter sidebar, working cross-references and the same look and theme as the rest of the site. They are rendered from the same Markdown the interface reads, and regenerated automatically whenever a chapter changes, so the copy on the site and the copy in the app can no longer disagree. Reading it is also now reachable from the top of the page, not only from the sentences that happened to link to it. Every manual page carries the app mark beside the wordmark at the top, and that is the way back to the site — alongside the link already at the foot of each page. Its contents page states which release it describes, taken from the version the release process already writes, so it follows a release without anyone remembering to update it. That is said once, at the entrance: a version on every chapter would invite "where is the manual for my version?", which has no answer while only one is published.
  • [site] Who is selling this. A short note beside the prices says who wrote Audiogravity and on what system, in the first person. Asking for a lifetime licence while saying nothing about who is behind it is a fair thing for a visitor to hesitate over.

Fixed

  • [core + ui + lic] A licence bought for a fixed period no longer lies to its owner, twice. These licences had never been issued, and the whole path was wrong end to end. While one was running the interface announced it as "Lifetime license active", with the end date shown nowhere — so a customer who bought a year believed he had bought the software outright. Then, on the day it ended, the same interface told him his licence file was "invalid or bound to a different device" — read as corrupted or stolen, and worth a support ticket every time. His licence panel now states the end date while the licence runs, and on the day it ends says so plainly: the licence ended on that date, and Audiogravity continues in Starter Edition. His order number stays on screen, and the panel offers renewal rather than the sales pitch shown to someone who never bought anything.
  • [lic] A mistyped end date is refused when it is typed, not months later on a customer's box. The date could be entered as free text on an order, and nothing checked it. A slip such as 31/12/2026 was stored as written: the administration went on showing the order as valid, while the customer's box — which refuses anything it cannot read — reported his licence as tampered with. Nothing connected the two, and the customer was the one who found out. The field is now refused on entry, an order already holding an unreadable date is shown as such in the administration instead of appearing healthy, and the licence server refuses it rather than ignoring it.
  • [core + lic] A licence now ends at the same moment for everyone. Each side worked out "today" in its own timezone, so a box in New Zealand and the server in Europe disagreed for about thirteen hours a day on whether a licence had run out. Both now settle it in UTC.
  • [ui] A licence bought for a period is no longer labelled a trial. The licence server marks every dated licence trial internally, including one sold for a year, and three separate screens relayed that word to the customer — the licence panel, the key check and the activation certificate, the last of these right after payment.
  • [site] The menu is usable on a phone again — in portrait it was not there at all. Below a certain width the section links were simply hidden, so on any phone held upright there was no way to reach a section but to scroll the whole page: eight entries, none of them reachable. Held sideways they did appear, but squeezed into a bar too narrow for them, with two of the labels breaking onto a second line and the first one touching the logo. The links now take a row of their own beneath the name and scroll sideways when there are more than fit — the same answer the licence server's own header uses. The threshold was set from where the single row genuinely stops working, not from where a phone stops, which is why landscape is fixed too.
  • [site] The top bar's links no longer sit on top of the buttons beside them. Between roughly 680 and 1000 pixels — a tablet, a small laptop — the section links overflowed their own row and were drawn over the theme switch and the releases button. Nothing looked broken to a page-width check, because the page was never wider: the elements simply overlapped. The releases button now steps aside on those widths, as its link is in the footer anyway, and the links sit closer together.
  • [site] Wording of the comparison table and the purchase steps. Four rows carried parenthetical asides that were the tallest thing in a phone-width column; they say the same thing without them — the streaming row keeps a short "(official APIs)", which is the part worth stating and the only one that fits. The first purchase step no longer names the payment provider in its title, and the activation step and the platform note both say "device" where one said "machine".
  • [site] The page calls the third-party engines "engines" throughout. Roon, MPD, HQPlayer and AirPlay were described as "players" in six places — the opening paragraph, the shared description search engines and social previews use, the footer line, a feature heading and a comparison row — while the section that introduces them is titled "Every audiophile engine" and the body text beneath that heading already said "engine" twice. The word "player" now means one thing on this page: the listening screen.
  • [site] What Audiogravity's relationship with HIGHRESAUDIO is, and is not. The third-party notices grouped HRA with Qobuz and TIDAL under a blanket "not affiliated with, endorsed by, or sponsored by", which stopped being accurate once HRA supplied their API for this integration and reviewed the interface built on it. HRA is now stated separately and precisely: a working arrangement, not a commercial one, with no claim of partnership or endorsement made anywhere.
  • [ui] The user manual fits a phone screen. Opening an illustrated chapter shifted the reader sideways and left it there: the screenshots are authored at a fixed width of 360 pixels, and nothing in the reader capped them, so on any screen narrower than that plus the reader's own margins the page had to be dragged back. An iPhone 13 offers just enough and hid the problem; every narrower model does not. Two other things pushed the same way and are fixed with it — a table wider than the screen took the whole reader with it instead of scrolling on its own, and a long command written inline in the text (an installation flag with a URL) could not be broken, so it set a width the reader could not go under. Tables now scroll by themselves and keep their structure for a screen reader. Checked on the twelve chapters across four screen sizes, from the smallest iPhone up.
  • [site] The landing page fits a phone. It had to be dragged sideways on every iPhone: the page wanted 470 pixels and the narrowest current model offers 375. Two things caused it — the top bar kept a GitHub button beside the logo long after the menu links had been hidden, and the "Audio priority" table kept a fixed label column that left the sentence beside it under a hundred pixels to wrap in. The bar now drops the button with the links, and that table stacks its labels above their lines on a narrow screen. Measured on six screen sizes in both themes, portrait and landscape.
  • [site] The "Coming soon" ribbon no longer sits on the announcement. Pinned to the top-right corner and above everything else, it landed squarely on the announcement text once that text wrapped onto three lines, which is what it does on a phone.
  • [site] The page fits around the notch and the home indicator. Added to a home screen it runs full-screen, where the sticky bar would have sat beneath the clock and, held sideways, the first characters of every line would have been cut by the rounded corner. The full-height opening section also now follows the screen rather than counting the strip the address bar sits over.
  • [site] Scrollbars and controls follow the theme. The browser draws its own widgets in the light scheme unless told otherwise, whatever the page colours say.
  • [ui + lic + site] The four installable apps no longer identify themselves under a retired domain. The interface and the licence administration announced themselves to the browser as net.di-marco.*, a domain being wound down, and the landing and the customer portal announced no identity at all — which leaves the browser deriving one from the address, so moving a page would have split one app into two. All four now use app.audiogravity. Note for anyone who already pinned one to a home screen during the beta: that identity is what the browser recognises an installed app by, so an existing icon keeps the old one and stops following the manifest — remove it and add it again. Five installs are concerned; doing this later, with a live fleet, would not have been an option.
  • [site] The landing's icons are drawn, not typed. Fifty-eight of them — every tick in the comparison table and in the plan lists — were the ordinary text character ✓, so each was drawn by whatever font the visitor's own machine offered: a different shape, weight and width on macOS, iOS, Windows and Android, right down the column where the product is compared with its alternatives. They are now drawn shapes on the same grid and in the same weight as the rest of the page, and the thirty-odd hand-built shapes already on the page were replaced by their equivalents from the set the app itself uses, so the landing and the interface no longer draw the same idea two different ways. The GitHub mark has no equivalent in that set and keeps its own logo. Each repeated shape is now written once and referenced where it is used, rather than copied into every cell — the page is 5.8% smaller, and a correction to a shape is a correction in one place.
  • [site] The page fits the smallest iPhone. The title is a single word that cannot be broken, and at its set size it alone was wider than a 320-pixel screen leaves — one word holding the whole page open, which is why that screen still had to be dragged sideways after the rest was fixed. It is set smaller there, and only there.
  • [site] The comparison table shows when it continues past the screen. iOS draws no scrollbar, so a table cut at the right edge reads as broken rather than scrollable. A soft fade now marks that edge, and disappears once the last column is reached — at that point nothing continues, and a cue still showing would only be painting over the figures.
  • [site] The figures under the opening section no longer show dividers with nothing beside them. The four figures — trial length, price, machine count, places left — are separated by vertical rules, drawn one per figure. As soon as they wrapped onto several lines, whichever one ended a line kept its rule and pointed at empty space. That happened on every phone width, and again on a desktop window around 1100 pixels where the section splits in two. They are now laid out in a grid, so a rule is drawn only where there is genuinely something on both sides of it.

[0.9.31] - 2026-08-04

Added

  • [lic] The licence administration can be installed on a phone. Added to a home screen it opens as its own app: it starts on its own mark over a black field rather than the browser's blank white page, keeps its own copy of the pages so a launch does not wait on the network, and answers a lost connection with a readable page instead of the browser's error. Orders, licences and installations are never kept — those are read live every time, because a cached copy would show a state the server no longer holds. Signing in now lasts twelve hours rather than until the app is closed: iOS ends a backgrounded app freely, which meant re-entering the token at every launch. The token is erased automatically once the twelve hours are up.
  • [lic] The installations table sorts by any column. Click a heading to order by device, status, version, address or dates. Versions and addresses compare part by part rather than as text, so v0.9.9 comes before v0.9.30 and 192.168.1.9 before 192.168.1.254 — the order you actually want when looking for the box left behind on an old build. Devices missing a value stay at the bottom either way.
  • [lic] The trial start date the server holds for a device is now visible, and can be forgotten. That date decides how long a box's free trial runs, and until now nothing showed it: a support question about a trial could not be answered, and a date recorded from a device whose clock was wrong could never be corrected. It appears in the installations table, and an administrator can clear it — an audited action. Clearing it repairs a wrong date; it does not hand out a new trial, which is what a licence with an expiry date is for.
  • [lic + ops] The licence server reports its own version. It carried a hardcoded 1.0.0 that had never moved and contradicted the releases it was cut from, so establishing which build was live meant probing the API's behaviour. Its version now comes from the same single source as the app and the interface, its health endpoint returns it, and the admin header shows it next to the title — the answer is now a glance, or a single request. The version badges of the licence-server and interface READMEs are propagated along with it, instead of being edited by hand every release.
  • [lic] The installations table can be searched, filtered and paged. A single field matches any part of a device, address, operating system, architecture or version, so finding one box no longer means reading the whole list; a second control narrows to one licence status. Only fifty rows are drawn at a time, and the count above the table states how many match out of how many were loaded — and says so plainly when the server's ceiling is reached, because a table quietly showing a subset reads exactly like a complete one. Sorting and paging apply to everything that matches, not to the rows on screen. The request behind it is now bounded server-side instead of accepting any size asked of it.
  • [lic] The admin and the customer portal now carry the product's own lockup and typeface. Both show the app icon and wordmark at the same figures as the landing page, in the same typeface, so the three no longer look like three different products. The administration top bar also gained direct links to each section, which scroll to it without hunting down a long page.

Fixed

  • [core + lic] Licences with an expiry date are accepted again — they never were. The licence server writes the expiry under one name and the app read another, so a time-limited licence could not be validated: the app found no date, assumed the licence had lapsed, and — since the file was there but refused — reported it as tampered with. Anyone given an evaluation licence would have been told their own licence was forged. The app now reads the right field and lapses a licence on the same day the server does. No customer was affected: none had been issued. A test on each side now locks the field names, so a rename breaks a build instead of an install.
  • [lic] Rate limits now count each caller, not the whole fleet at once. Every limit on the licence server was written per caller and behaved as one shared allowance, because behind the reverse proxy every request arrives carrying the proxy's own address. "Thirty a minute" meant thirty for all installations together rather than thirty each — invisible today with a small fleet, and a throttle waiting to happen as it grows. The real caller is now read from the header the proxy sets, and only when the request genuinely came through it.
  • [lic] The key-validation endpoint is rate-limited. It was the only route on the licence server without a limit, which left the one place that answers "does this key exist?" open to being asked without end. The key space makes guessing hopeless, but that is an argument for the key space, not for leaving the door open.
  • [lic] A recorded trial start can no longer be pushed further back. The server remembers when a device began its trial, so deleting the local files no longer buys a fresh one — that part is unchanged. What changed is that only the first report counts: the earlier rule took the smaller of the two every time, which meant anyone able to reach that endpoint with a device's fingerprint could report a start in 1970 and end that device's trial permanently. A wrong first date is corrected from the admin, as before, and the action is recorded.
  • [lic] The licence administration no longer holds a connection open for nothing. The page carried a development convenience — a link kept open so it reloads itself whenever a file is edited — and nothing stopped it from running on the deployed server, where no file is ever edited. Every open tab kept a connection to the server for a minute, was cut, and reopened it two seconds later, since the day the interface was written. Nothing broke and nothing showed, which is why it went unnoticed until the server's own error log was read. The page now asks only where development actually happens, and the server no longer offers the route at all unless it is told to.
  • [lic] The icons are drawn, not typed. Ticks, crosses, arrows, the refresh and copy buttons, the theme switch and two emoji were ordinary text characters. Twelve of the fifteen do not exist in the typeface the pages load, so each was drawn by whatever font the visitor's own machine offered — a different shape, weight and width on macOS, iOS, Windows and Android — and the copy button used a character rare enough that a system may show the missing-character box in its place. Every icon now comes from the same drawn set the app itself uses, on one grid, in one weight; a check keeps a stray character or a hand-drawn shape from creeping back in.
  • [lic] The licence server's pages fit a phone screen. The administration bar was held on a single line that needed 552 pixels — wider than every iPhone in portrait — so the theme and sign-out buttons sat off the right edge and the page had to be dragged sideways. Worse, a page wider than the screen carries anything anchored to it out as well: the order detail panel, whose own layout was correct, opened partly off-screen. The bar now wraps, the section links take a row of their own instead of being squeezed to nothing, and the whole interface was re-measured on five screen sizes from the smallest iPhone to a tablet.
  • [lic] Dates, numbers, dropdowns and search boxes follow the theme. The stylesheet named the three kinds of field it covered, so the nine other kinds in use kept the browser's own styling — white rectangles in the middle of a black page, as anyone who set an expiry date could see. Fields are now selected by what they are not, so a kind added later is styled by default rather than forgotten, and the browser is told which scheme to draw its own date pickers and dropdown lists in.
  • [lic] Tapping a field on a phone no longer zooms the page in. Every field was set below the size at which iOS enlarges the page on focus — and it never zooms back out, so one tap left the interface stuck at the wrong scale. Fields are raised on touch screens only; the density the interface was designed at is unchanged on a desktop.
  • [lic] The interface fits around the notch and the home indicator. Both pages ask to run full-screen when pinned to a home screen, but neither reserved the room iOS needs at the edges, so the sticky bar would have run under the status bar. The page height also now follows the screen rather than counting the strip the address bar sits over, which is what left content unreachable at the bottom.
  • [ops] The customer licence portal is reachable again — from the app, it never was. The "Download .lic" and "Upgrade v1 → v2" buttons open a page hosted on the licence server, but the proxy in front of it only ever exposed the API calls that page makes, never the page itself: both buttons landed on a redirect to an unrelated site. Anyone reinstalling a box had no way to fetch their licence back. The page and the three files it needs are now served; everything else, including the whole administration interface, stays behind its client certificate.
  • [ops] Links that carry values no longer arrive empty. The proxy passed on the address but dropped everything after the ?, so every unsubscribe link sent in a campaign email and every v1 → v2 eligibility check reached the server stripped of the order number and token that identify the request — and were answered "missing". Both routes were measured with and without values before and after the fix.
  • [lic] Campaign emails can no longer go out with dead unsubscribe links. The address those links are built on had to be typed by hand, the example beside the field still named the licence server's former domain, and nothing checked what was entered — a wrong value only showed itself once the mail had left, in other people's mailboxes. A fresh install now starts with the right address, and an unusable one is refused wherever it is saved, not only when a campaign is sent. An address already configured is never overwritten.

[0.9.30] - 2026-08-03

Fixed

  • [core] The free trial is kept in a single place, ending the licence-state noise on upgraded boxes. The trial start used to be mirrored to a second, system-level file that no installer had ever created — so it never worked on a deployed box, and where a leftover copy did exist the two disagreed and the core logged a licence error every few seconds. The trial now lives in one file, and the licence server remains the authority that restores a box's true start date. The obsolete file is removed on upgrade.
  • [core] The test suite can no longer write outside its sandbox. Two paths escaped it: the obsolete trial file, and the data directory holding the key that encrypts stored service passwords — on a machine where that directory was writable, running the suite could have overwritten a live installation's key. Both are sandboxed, and any future escape now fails the build instead of silently touching the system.

[0.9.29] - 2026-08-03

Added

  • [core + lic] The free-trial period is anchored to a device's genuine first-use date. The licence server records, per device, the earliest trial start it has seen and returns it signed on each check-in; the box adopts it. The trial length therefore reflects real first use and stays consistent across a reinstall, rather than restarting from local state alone. Tamper-proof: the anchor is signed and bound to the device, so a box can neither forge it nor move it forward.

Fixed

  • [core + lic] Trial and expired boxes now receive announcements and update notifications. These fleet broadcasts previously reached only a box with a currently-valid licence — a box on its free trial (which never contacts the verify endpoint) and an expired one (whose verification stops short) were silently cut off and could sit on an obsolete version with no notice. They now ride the public config channel every box already polls, so every box is notified. The update offer is bounded to the box's own major (derived server-side from the reported version), so a box is never pushed across a major — that stays a deliberate paid upgrade.
  • [ui] The announcement banner shows proper icons instead of emoji. Version/promo/alert/info announcements, the dismiss button and the "Learn more" link now render Lucide SVG icons, coloured per type, consistent with the rest of the interface.
  • [core] A trial box whose two anti-tamper files disagree on the start date self-heals instead of locking up. A legitimate desync (OS reinstall, config restore, a permission change) used to wedge the box in a permanent "tampered" state and log an error on every check; it now reconciles to the earliest valid start — tamper-safe, since the earliest can never lengthen a trial — and repairs both files. A start that genuinely predates the OS install is still rejected.

Changed

  • [core] The public licence config (PayPal URL, prices, contact) is served from the 24 h cache instead of re-querying the licence server every time the licence panel opens — this config is near-static, so the per-open round-trip was wasteful.

[0.9.28] - 2026-08-03

Added

  • [core + lic + ui] The free-trial length can be changed for the whole fleet without shipping an update. The 30-day trial is now a floor the licence server can lengthen: set a number in the admin, the server signs it, and every box adopts it at its next check-in — a promo or a longer beta becomes a setting, not a release. It cannot be abused locally: the value must carry the server's signature and can only extend beyond the built-in floor, never shorten below it, so an unreachable server or an edited cache falls back to the built-in length. The licence panel now counts down against the real total (N / total days).

[0.9.27] - 2026-08-02

Security

  • [lic] The licence server's privileged routes fail closed when their secret is missing. The endpoints only Audiogravity's own backend should reach — recording a box's last-seen, releasing a device binding, checking a key, verifying a licence — are guarded by a shared secret. If that secret was left unconfigured the guard did nothing and the routes stood open to anyone who could reach the server, which is internet-facing. They now refuse every call when the secret is unset, and the secret is compared in constant time. The installer now requires the key rather than offering to skip it, and warns on upgrade if an existing server was left without one.
  • [lic] The box heartbeat endpoint is now rate-limited per client. The shared secret that guards it filters random traffic but is present on every box, so it cannot be treated as private forever. The heartbeat is now capped at 30 calls a minute per source — plenty for a real box, which checks in about once a day — so the endpoint cannot be used to flood the device table.

Changed

  • [core + ops] The licence server moved to its own domain, lic.audiogravity.app. New installs point there, and an upgrade rewrites an existing box's endpoint (host only — same port, same behaviour). The previous address stays live in parallel, so a box that has not upgraded keeps working until it does; nothing is required of the user.

Fixed

  • [core] A box no longer reports its licence as invalid when the licence server is merely unreachable. The online check only ever receives its verdict — valid, revoked, expired, not-found — as a normal reply. A rejected shared secret, a rate limit or a server error would arrive differently, and the box mistook that for a definitive "invalid" instead of "could not reach the server", so a transient hiccup could grey out a perfectly good licence. Those cases now read as unreachable and the last known state is kept.
  • [lic] A licence-email campaign no longer loses recipients on a passing provider hiccup, nor double-sends after a crash. A send that failed once — a rate limit, a timeout — was marked failed for good and never retried, while the progress showed nothing missing. Each recipient is now retried on a later day, up to three attempts, before being given up on. And because an attempt is now stamped before the send rather than after the whole batch, a restart midway no longer leaves rows looking un-sent and re-sending them the same day. An upgrade also no longer briefly doubles the day's send allowance.

[0.9.26] - 2026-08-01

Fixed

  • [ui] The window that asks for your password no longer pushes its Confirm button off the screen. On an iPhone, tapping the field to type zoomed the whole page in — Safari does that to any field whose text is smaller than 16px — and the dialog's right-hand side, Confirm included, ended up outside the visible area. The update simply could not be validated. Audiogravity has carried a rule since 0.9.20 that lifts every field to 16px on a phone precisely to prevent this, but that one field had its size written directly on the element, where no rule can reach it.

  • [ui] Text fields no longer zoom the page on a tablet, or on a phone held sideways. The protection above was tied to narrow screens rather than to touch ones, so it stopped applying past 768 pixels — which is every iPad, and every iPhone in landscape. It now follows the pointer: a finger gets it, a mouse does not.

  • [ui] The pencil that edits a radio station can now be aimed at. Two things made it nearly unclickable. It was the smallest of the three icons on the row and, in compact mode, its target was 24 pixels across; it is now 36 by 44, and all three glyphs are the same size. And the swipe-to-remove gesture armed after 8 pixels of sideways travel anywhere on the row, including on top of the buttons — so a tap that drifted slightly started removing the station instead of opening the editor.

  • [ui] Pressing a button on a list row no longer drags the row away. The fix above applies to every list that supports swipe-to-remove: radio stations, the playback queue, UPnP servers and renderers. A gesture that starts on a button now belongs to that button.

  • [core] Radio search no longer hammers the catalogue when it is unwell. Audiogravity believed it had five mirror servers to fall back on. It has one: three of those names no longer exist, and the other two are aliases of the same machine — which the catalogue itself confirms, advertising a single server. So a failed search meant four requests fired at the very machine that had just refused, inside a third of a second. It now makes one, and asks again only when the first got no answer at all — a timeout or a broken connection, never an explicit refusal.

  • [core] Audiogravity now goes quiet when the catalogue asks it to. If the catalogue answers that it is being asked too often, the box stops sending anything for as long as it requested, instead of carrying on.

  • [core] The catalogue's servers are now found the way its documentation prescribes. Audiogravity asked an address on the catalogue's own server which servers were available — so when that machine went down, the mechanism meant to route around it went down with it. It now resolves them by DNS, which is served by separate infrastructure and answers even when the catalogue does not.

  • [core] A radio search you have already run keeps working while the catalogue is down. Rather than an error, Audiogravity shows you the result it last obtained for that exact search, for up to a day — and those stations still play. They were only listed at first: starting one asked the catalogue again, which was still down, and the answer was "station not found" for a station right there on screen.

  • [core] A station that cannot be reached is no longer reported as missing. Playing a station while the catalogue was unreachable said it did not exist, which sent people looking for a station that was never gone. Audiogravity now says the catalogue is unavailable, and gives the reason. "Not found" now means what it says: the catalogue answered, and it does not know that station.

  • [core + ui] "Search failed" now says what actually failed. Every radio search failure showed the same three words, whether the fault was on your box or at the provider — so an outage two thousand kilometres away sent people looking through their own settings. The message now distinguishes a catalogue that is down from one asking to be queried less often, and says when to try again.

  • [ui] The key your box publishes is now the one that wins. The interface preferred a copy kept in the browser over the key the installer publishes, so a wrong value stored once outranked it for good: every upgrade republished the right key and lost, and the interface stayed locked out with nothing on screen to explain why. The published key is now authoritative, and a leftover copy is discarded.

  • [ui] Typing in radio search no longer sends a query for almost every letter. The pause before a search left the box was shorter than the gap between two letters typed with a thumb: one session sent sixteen queries for a handful of words.

  • [ui] Leaving radio search no longer drops its results into the tab you switched to. A search still on its way arrived after you had moved on and replaced what that tab was showing, so hits for what you had typed appeared under My Live Radio or Favorites. They could then be swiped away, which asked the box to remove a station that list never held. Leaving the Search tab now abandons the search with it, and the longer pause introduced above — which widened the window this could happen in — is safe to keep.

  • [ui] Saving a radio station while the catalogue is down now says so. Starring a station, or adding one to My Live Radio, showed "Could not update" whatever the reason — the same wording for a provider outage as for a fault on your box. Both now report what the catalogue said, and when to try again, exactly as search already did.

  • [ui] A station that will not start no longer blames a part of the box that is working. The message named the local player as the thing to go and check, when the cause is just as often the catalogue being unreachable, or the chosen output refusing the stream. It now names both possible causes — the catalogue, or the chosen output refusing the stream — instead of pointing at one that is working. (Telling the two apart, core-side, is a follow-up.)

  • [core] A network speaker you added by its address is now found even when multicast does not work. Audiogravity looks for speakers by shouting on the network; where that shout cannot travel — some routers, some subnets — you can give the address directly instead. That direct probe was skipped whenever the shout itself failed, which is exactly the situation it exists for. The two are now independent, and a search that cannot send anything at all stops immediately rather than listening to silence for its full duration.

  • [core] Audiogravity now stops sending play reports when the radio catalogue asks it to. A refusal on the report channel was noted and ignored, so every new station played sent another report to a server that had explicitly asked for quiet. It now holds off for as long as it was asked — and only the reports stop: searches, which you are waiting on, still go through.

  • [core] A box with no multicast route no longer writes a warning a minute. Looking up cover art from the local player retries every minute during playback, and the network search it uses had been made to log at warning level along with the ones you trigger yourself.

Security

  • [ops] A failed self-update can no longer be turned into a way to gain root on the box. The privileged helper that performs an update ran with administrator rights and took the address it downloaded-and-ran, and the file path it wrote, from whoever called it. Anything already running under Audiogravity's own limited account could therefore hand it a malicious address and have it executed as administrator — a local privilege escalation, not reachable from the network, but real. The helper now fixes those values itself and accepts only a checked list of the rest; a supplied address or path is ignored. Proven on real hardware: an injected address is refused and never runs.

Fixed

  • [ops] Undoing a failed self-update now puts the whole box back, not just the program. An update reinstalls far more than the core binary — the service definition, the update mechanism itself, the package registry, the privileged helpers — but undoing one only restored the binary, then reported success. A box could be left running parts of a version that had just failed, including an update mechanism that could no longer update it, while its log said everything was fine. The undo now restores everything the update overwrote, reloads the service definition, and says so honestly — if any part could not be put back, it reports that instead of a clean success. Verified on a real box: nine files an update overwrites are all restored, and the box can update again afterwards.

Added

  • [core] Audiogravity now tells the radio catalogue which stations you play, as its documentation asks. That count is what ranks stations by popularity — the ranking Audiogravity sorts your own search results by, and which it had until now been consuming without ever contributing to. Only stations from the catalogue are ever reported, never the ones you add by hand, and nothing is sent when playback fails to start. This sends the station's identifier to a third party: set RADIO_REPORT_PLAYS=false in the configuration and no play ever leaves the box.

  • [core] Audiogravity now says who it is when it talks to your network speakers. Requests to a UPnP renderer — reading its description, subscribing to its events, sending it transport commands — went out with no identification, so a speaker's log showed an anonymous Python client. Every outbound request now carries the same Audiogravity signature the rest of the box already used.

  • [ops] The new radio setting reaches boxes that are already installed. Upgrading adds it to the existing configuration rather than leaving the machine on a default it never agreed to.

  • [ops] Uninstalling no longer leaves a service running on the machine. Performance tuning installs a small service that re-applies the CPU governor at every boot; the uninstaller never removed it. A machine you had removed Audiogravity from therefore kept having its processor scheduling changed at each start, by a service with nothing left on the machine to explain where it came from. It and its helper script are now removed with everything else.

  • [ops] --purge now really removes everything. It left behind the backups of your audio services' configuration files in /var/backups/audiogravity — copies that can contain your MPD password or Shairport authentication keys — along with the CPU governor setting and the box's state directory. All three now go with the rest.

Removed

  • [ui] The API key is gone from the Settings panel. It was offered as a field, with a reveal button, to every logged-in user. It protected nothing — the key is already inside the page your browser loads, because the installer puts it there — and it was the only thing on a running box that could store a wrong key and cut that browser off from your music. It has no replacement: nothing needs to be typed there any more.

  • [core] The SSDP network search has one implementation instead of three. Finding a network speaker, finding a media server and finding the local renderer for cover art each carried their own copy of the same search — so a fix or a hardening applied to one reached neither of the others. They now share one, each keeping only what genuinely differs: what it looks for, and whether it also probes hosts directly.

Documentation

  • [site] The manual explains where radio stations come from, and what is sent back. A new section says that search depends on an outside catalogue Audiogravity does not run, that your own stations keep playing when it is down, what a play report contains — the station's identifier, nothing else — and how to switch it off.
  • [site] The Radio Browser notice describes what Audiogravity actually does. It claimed the box auto-discovers the nearest mirror through an HTTP endpoint; discovery is now by DNS, there is no nearest mirror to pick from, and the play reporting was undocumented.
  • [site] The manual no longer documents the API key field removed above. Its screenshot of the Settings panel is retaken as well, so the picture matches what the panel now shows.
  • [site] The third-party notices now list every outside service the box calls. Only Radio Browser was named, while cover art also queries MusicBrainz (release data, CC0), the Cover Art Archive (a cooperation between the Internet Archive and MusicBrainz, which asserts no licence over the images themselves) and the iTunes Search API as a last resort. Each entry states what it is used for, what its provider requires of a client, and what Audiogravity sends.
  • [site] The manual now says what uninstalling actually leaves behind. It gave two commands and nothing else, so following it to the letter left you believing the machine was clean while your whole configuration was still there. It now states what each form removes, documents --purge, and names what is deliberately kept: the audio software installed from the Audio Software page — MPD, Shairport-Sync, upmpdcli, HQPlayer NAA, Roon Bridge — which are ordinary system packages that keep working on their own.

[0.9.25] - 2026-07-28

Fixed

  • [core + ui] The window that shows an installation no longer stays stuck on "Installing…" after the work is done. Its log lines only ever reached the browser as they happened, and nothing was kept for a browser that missed them — a reconnection, a phone that put the page to sleep, a second of bad network. The window then froze on the last line it had received and stayed there, while the installation carried on and finished without it. Measured on a real box: the package was installed in six seconds, and the panel was still announcing work in progress six minutes later. Audiogravity now keeps the log of the operation in progress and the interface asks for what it missed — when it reconnects, and each time the operation moves on. Nothing is lost any more, and nothing is shown twice.
  • [core] An installation no longer stays silent for as long as it lasts. Everything the package manager printed was collected and only written to the log once the command had finished, so between "Installing…" and the end there was nothing at all — a healthy installation looked exactly like a dead one, which is what made the previous problem impossible to tell apart from a real failure. Lines now appear as they are produced.
  • [core] A successful removal no longer reports an error. Removing AirPlay ended on a red ERROR — rmdir: failed to remove '/var/lib/shairport-sync' — while the removal had worked perfectly. That line comes from the shairport-sync package's own removal script, which produces it deliberately and ignores it (the directory is obsolete and usually absent). Audiogravity was reading the severity of each line from its wording, so anything containing "failed" was painted red, whoever had written it. What the package manager prints is now shown as plain information; the verdict comes from the result of the operation itself.
  • [core] Simulation mode works again. Asking to simulate an installation instead of performing it failed every time, on both architectures, with an internal error message that said nothing about it.
  • [core] An installation that exceeds its time limit now says so. It reported an internal error instead of the timeout, so a package manager stuck waiting on something looked like a defect in Audiogravity. The limit itself is also enforced again: it had stopped applying to commands that spawn others — which is every installation. The abandoned command is also cleaned up properly instead of leaving a dead process entry behind.
  • [core] The log of a long installation no longer grows without limit in memory. The cap of 500 lines was silently dropped at the start of each operation, and output arriving without a line break was accumulated with no bound at all.
  • [core] Reading an installation log while it is being written no longer fails. Consulting the log of an operation in progress could fail outright, at the exact moment it is of any use.

[0.9.24] - 2026-07-28

Fixed

  • [site] The install command now really installs what the release notes describe. The one-line setup command fetches two smaller installers from audiogravity.app, and those two copies had not been refreshed since 30 June — five releases. So the 0.9.23 fix that lets a bare machine install itself was announced but never reached the only command users are given: a machine without Python still hit the old dead end. The access token was also still mandatory there, months after it had been made optional. Both copies are now in step with their source, and the release procedure checks it before every release.
  • [core] The configuration check no longer reports a file as missing while the editor has it open. Each audio service keeps its settings in a file, and where that file sits depends on how the service was installed: /etc when it came from the distribution's packages, /usr/local/etc when it was built from source. The location was written down in /etc/audiogravity/audio-config.json, which can only name one of them — so it was wrong on the other kind of machine. The Config tab coped by looking in both places; the configuration check did not, and flagged the file as absent on the very machine that was editing it. Every ARM box was in that situation for AirPlay. Both now ask the same question of the same source.
  • [core] A profile that starts one delicate service is no longer reported as inconsistent. Marking a profile as requiring care warned as soon as it also started an ordinary service — "Roon + HQPlayer" is delicate because of HQPlayer, and Roon Bridge beside it is ordinary. The only way to silence it was to mark those services delicate too, which changes how the box behaves when one of them refuses to stop. A warning you can only clear by degrading behaviour is one people learn to ignore. It now appears only when a profile asks for care while starting nothing that warrants it.

Changed

  • [core] Audiogravity now knows by itself where each service keeps its configuration. The path no longer has to be written in audio-config.json: the appconfigfile field is ignored, and new installations ship without it. Boxes set up earlier still carry the line — it does nothing, breaks nothing, and can be deleted. The way the file is read is decided the same way: a file is parsed according to the service it belongs to, rather than guessed from its name.

[0.9.23] - 2026-07-28

Fixed

  • [ops] Installing on a bare machine no longer stops at a missing prerequisite. The one-line install command checked that Python was present and gave up if it was not — while the installer inside the package it was about to download would have installed Python itself. That installer was never reached: the check runs first, so the repair was unreachable from the only install path anyone is ever told about. The command now installs what is missing before carrying on. It always could — it already runs with administrator rights.
  • [ops] The installer no longer reports installing things it did not install. Setting up a box pulls in a few system tools — the latency benchmark, the network test, the component that makes Tidal playable. If the machine could not refresh its package lists (no network, an unreachable mirror), the installation step was skipped entirely and the installer still printed "System dependencies installed". The box finished setup believing it had those tools and discovered otherwise much later, somewhere unrelated. The opposite case was no better: if the installation itself failed, the whole setup stopped dead on the package manager's raw error, before the service had even been created. Each step is now reported on its own, and what counts as success is what is actually on the machine afterwards, not what the package manager claimed. Anything still missing is named, together with the feature that will be unavailable — and the setup finishes either way.
  • [ops] Installing the interface on a machine without openssl no longer stops without saying why. The interface is served over HTTPS by default; that needs a certificate, and the installer creates one with openssl — a tool Debian marks as optional and does not guarantee is present. When it was missing the installer stopped dead immediately after announcing it was generating the certificate, and the error was discarded, so nothing at all appeared on screen to say what had gone wrong. Measured against the previous version: the install simply ended, silently. openssl is now installed when needed — only in HTTPS mode, since an installation sitting behind a reverse proxy never uses it — and if that cannot be done, the reason is printed. In the same step, a machine that does not have a network address yet no longer receives a certificate whose name field is empty.
  • [ops] Push notifications no longer depend on which packages a machine happened to already carry. Setting up a new box generates the keys that let it send notifications, and that step needs one cryptography library. It was fetched with pip, which is not installed on a stock ARM machine — so on ARM the step failed without saying so, and notifications worked only where that library happened to be present for an unrelated reason. It now comes from the machine's own package manager, which carries it on both architectures. Where pip did exist, this also stops Audiogravity from writing into the system's Python installation and hiding the version the operating system put there — something Debian deliberately protects against.

[0.9.22] - 2026-07-27

Changed

  • [ui] Opening the app no longer downloads one album cover per track waiting in the queue. The list of what is queued shows a small cover beside every track, and that list lives in the Library tab — which is built into the page from the very first moment, even when you are looking at another tab. A hidden image is still fetched, so a queue of sixty-seven tracks asked the box for sixty-seven covers before you had touched anything, all of them the same album seen sixty-seven times over. Measured on a phone with a cold start, that was half of everything the app requested while starting — 271 requests against 137 once fixed — and it grew with the queue: a few hundred tracks waiting meant a few hundred requests. Covers are now fetched only when they are about to come into view. Nothing on screen changes.
  • [ui] The Pipeline tab on a phone no longer keeps the box busy while you look at it. Left open, it asked the server for the entire pipeline every five seconds — and that answer is expensive to produce: measured at around 570 ms of work each time, which adds up to close to seven minutes of processor time every hour, on the very machine that is playing the music. It was also pointless: the core already recomputes the pipeline when it actually changes and announces it. The tab now reads the pipeline once when it opens and is told about every change afterwards, so an open tab costs nothing. The only thing still asked for on a timer is which output each source is steered to, which has no announcement of its own and is now checked every fifteen seconds instead of every five. Nothing on screen changes, and the pulsing dot next to each active source now fades in colour rather than shrinking — one less permanent drawing layer per stream on the devices least able to spare them.
  • [core] The message shown when the box refuses to start is now in English, like the rest of Audiogravity. When a security key is missing or too weak, the core refuses to start and prints what is wrong and how to repair it. That message — and only that one — was still in French.

Fixed

  • [core] Twelve more formats HQPlayer cannot read are refused up front instead of failing halfway. Audiogravity only refused the formats it had been told about one at a time; anything else was pushed to HQPlayer, accepted, and failed a moment later — pointing at the sound card rather than the file. Checked against Signalyst's own list of accepted formats, the refusal list now also covers AC3/E-AC3, DTS, Musepack, TAK, TTA, Shorten, Speex, AMR, MKA/WebM and AIFC (compressed AIFF). Nothing HQPlayer actually reads was added: the ten accepted source formats and the M3U/M3U8/PLS playlists are pinned by tests, because refusing a track that would have played is worse than a late failure.

[0.9.21] - 2026-07-26

Fixed

  • [core] A track a media server names .mp4 is now refused up front, like the same track named .m4a. HQPlayer decodes neither — they are two names for one container — but only the second spelling was on the refusal list. Serve your library through Plex, which publishes every M4A as file.mp4, and Audiogravity pushed the track anyway: HQPlayer accepted it, then failed, and you were told about a sound-card problem that did not exist. The same file served by MinimServer was correctly refused. On a library that is largely M4A, this was the common case rather than an edge one. The player also showed an empty format for those tracks, and now names the codec.
  • [ui] The handle that brings the player back no longer needs several attempts. The small tab at the bottom of the screen restores the Now Playing bar, and it used to ignore roughly one touch in two. Two reasons, both fixed: it only reacted to a movement under 10 px or an upward swipe past 30 px — doing nothing in between, which is exactly what a finger produces on a target 18 px tall — and that target was less than half the size Apple and Google recommend. The tab looks identical, but the area that answers the finger now extends above it, and any touch that is not a deliberate downward drag brings the player back.
  • [core] Asking for a real-time stream that does not exist now says so, instead of going silent. The live updates Audiogravity pushes travel on named streams — one for the machine, one for the player, and a few narrower ones. Asking for a name that does not exist used to open the connection and then send nothing at all, for ever: no event, no error, nothing to say the name was wrong. Audiogravity's own documentation even offered one of those wrong names as an example. An unknown name is now refused immediately, and the refusal lists the names that work.

[0.9.20] - 2026-07-25

Fixed

  • [core] A track sent to HQPlayer that never makes a sound now says so — and no longer holds up your click. Audiogravity checks that playback really started, but that check used to run while you waited: up to twelve seconds before the answer came back. Worse, it gave up too early on a heavy chain — measured on a DSD128 track upsampled to DSD, playback starts twenty-eight seconds after being sent, and HQPlayer stops answering at all while it warms up, so a perfectly good play was announced as failed fifteen seconds before the music started. The check now runs in the background: playback starts at once, and if no sound comes out the reason appears under the output, where a busy sound card is already explained. What counts as proof changed too — a position that advances, not HQPlayer's word: with the sound card held by another player it reports "playing" with its position frozen for as long as you leave it, which used to be taken as success. The message clears on its own as soon as the music really plays, however you restarted it.
  • [core + ui] Setting your machine up no longer needs a licence. Editing a service's configuration file, and the guided setup that generates the whole audio stack, were both reserved for Trial and Pro — so a Starter user could run the box but not configure it. Both are now open, and the Config tab is no longer greyed out. The guided setup stays administrator-only: it rewrites every service's configuration and mounts network shares, which is a question of role, not of edition.
  • [ui] The source list now updates the moment you connect or disconnect a service. Connecting HIGHRESAUDIO, Qobuz, Tidal or HQPlayer left the list unchanged — the screen you were looking at read it once, when it opened, and never again. You had to leave the page and come back. UPnP servers were worse: removing one never disappeared from the search picker at all. Every one of those actions now refreshes the list straight away.
  • [core] Importing a configuration is checked again in Starter Edition. The import itself was never licence-gated, but the validation that runs just before it was — so on Starter the check answered 403, the app logged it to the console and imported the file anyway. The gate removed a safety net from unlicensed users instead of protecting anything. Configuration validation is now open; the topology validation stays licence-gated, since it serves the Pipeline view.
  • [core] A source Audiogravity cannot identify is refused, instead of being taken for your local library. The classification ended on a catch-all, so anything unrecognised was declared "local" — and therefore eligible to be pushed to HQPlayer, which resolves tracks through your library and would have found nothing there. Anything Audiogravity cannot name positively is now refused with its reason, whatever the selected output. Nothing that played before can start being refused: the local path already required the very same recognition.
  • [core + ui] HQPlayer and network speakers no longer show up in the library source picker. While one of them was playing, it appeared among the sources you can browse, next to Qobuz and your local library — an entry with no catalogue behind it. They are outputs and processors, not sources: they stay visible in the player, which is where they belong, and are no longer offered as somewhere to browse.
  • [ui] The output line names your output even when nothing is playing, and tells you when it is stopped. It read the output's name from a field that is empty on a box whose audio layout declares no converter, which showed as "No output selected" while a perfectly good DAC sat there. The name, its state and its errors now all come from the same output list, so a speaker that is selected but idle is named — and marked Stopped rather than looking as if it were playing.
  • [core + ui] A selected speaker that cannot be reached now says so. The player state told you an output's name and what it was doing, but not whether it could be contacted at all — an asleep speaker was recognisable only by its playback state being blank, which also means "reached it, it said nothing". The two are now distinct, and the output line reads Unreachable instead of leaving you to guess.
  • [ui] "Up next" follows the output carrying the sound, not an internal handle. The fullscreen player decided where to read the upcoming track by testing an internal routing name. It now tests where the audio actually comes out, which is the same answer today and will keep working for any network output added later.

Changed

  • [core] A speaker you selected but cannot be reached now refuses the play, instead of quietly using the local output. Sending music while the chosen speaker was asleep, off the network or still reconnecting made it come out of the local DAC without a word — the wrong device, with no explanation. Audiogravity now says so and asks you to wake it up or pick another output. This is the rule it already applied when two outputs were selected at once.
  • [core] "HQPlayer" is no longer one of the origins a track can carry. An origin says what the music is — your library, Qobuz, a radio station. HQPlayer is a processor: what it plays keeps its own origin. Nothing produced that label any more; it was still advertised to clients.

Refactored

  • [core] One place now decides where music goes — all of it. Half the routing decision (HQPlayer or the local output) already lived in one module, but the other half — the local player or a network speaker — was copy-pasted at seven play paths, each asking the speaker manager itself. A path added without that paragraph silently stayed local, which is exactly the failure that decision point exists to prevent. Both halves are now answered in one call. No change for you: the same music goes to the same place, including the rule that "add to queue" never goes to a speaker (they have no persistent queue) while it does go to HQPlayer, which has one.

  • [core] The Now Playing split is finished. The modules extracted earlier were still reached through a compatibility shim left in the old file; every caller now points at the right module and the shim is gone, along with two places that reached into another module's private data instead of asking it.

  • [core] A track HQPlayer cannot decode is now refused straight away, whatever it came from. HQPlayer does not handle ALAC, AAC, OGG, Opus, WMA or APE — and until now only internet radio checked. Send it an ALAC album from your library, or an AAC track from your media server, and HQPlayer accepted the request, then failed to start: several seconds later Audiogravity reported it as a sound-card problem, which it never was. The check now sits on the one path every source goes through, so the answer is instant and names the real cause — "…is M4A, which HQPlayer cannot decode. Turn HQPlayer off as output to play it on the local output." An album is checked before anything is sent, so a single unplayable track is caught up front instead of stopping the music halfway through — with the trade-off that a mixed album (say a FLAC record with one ALAC bonus track) is refused whole rather than played without that track; the message names the offending track. Formats Audiogravity cannot identify are still attempted rather than blocked on a guess: only what HQPlayer is documented not to handle is refused.

Removed

  • [core] The two leftover routes that pushed music straight to HQPlayer are gone. They dated from before Audiogravity decided server-side where each track should play: back then the app had to pick HQPlayer itself. Since that decision moved to the box, nothing called them any more — the app even had a test forbidding their use, because going through them would skip the arbitration and let content reach HQPlayer that it cannot decode. Nothing changes for you: every way you start music already went through the normal path. Removing them means there is now exactly one door into HQPlayer, so a rule applied there cannot be bypassed.

[0.9.19] - 2026-07-20

Added

  • [core + ui] When an output can't play, Audiogravity now says why instead of staying silent. The sound card is exclusive by design (that is what makes bit-perfect playback possible), so only one player can hold it at a time. If you press play while another one still holds it, a notification tells you straight away — "Output in use by another player — stop it to play here" — and the fullscreen player repeats it under the output, with the engine's exact message as a tooltip. Previously you got silence and no explanation, and the reason was only readable if you thought to open the fullscreen player. The notification appears once when the problem starts, not on every refresh. Costs nothing: the reason was already in the data Audiogravity reads every second, it was simply discarded.
  • [ui] A refused playback now tells you why, instead of looking like a click that did nothing. When Audiogravity cannot start a track, the backend explains the reason precisely — but the three places you start playback from (browse, search, UPnP browser) dropped that explanation into the browser console, which nobody reads. All three now surface it as a notification, in the server's own words. This covers every refusal, not one case: an unreachable UPnP server, an expired stream, a source that cannot reach the current output. Enqueueing from the UPnP browser gets the same feedback as everywhere else too — it was the last place where adding a track could fail in complete silence. Successful playback stays silent by design: the music starting is the feedback.
  • [core] The reported output failure now always describes the current attempt. MPD keeps its last playback error indefinitely — the protocol says starting playback clears it, MPD 0.24 does not — so a problem solved minutes earlier stayed on screen for good, and would now have triggered a spurious notification too. Every playback command Audiogravity sends is prefixed with a reset, so the message you see is about the music you are trying to play right now.

Removed

  • [core + ui] "Spotify Connect" no longer appears as an available source. Audiogravity does not support Spotify, yet it still advertised it in the source list (a leftover service entry) — offering something that could never play. The service and its now-dead badge/label are removed. A Spotify stream reaching Audiogravity through AirPlay is unaffected: it is shown, correctly, as AirPlay.

  • [core + ui] The "Use HQPlayer as output" setting now follows your system, not your browser. It lived in each browser's local storage, so your phone and your laptop could disagree on where music should go — one sending playback to HQPlayer while the other sent it to the local output. The setting is now stored on the Audiogravity box, shared by every client and remembered across restarts. Better still, the routing decision itself moved to the backend: the app no longer chooses, so the two can no longer diverge at all. Audiogravity also now refuses to enable it when it could not actually play — no HQPlayer configured, or its network audio daemon (NAA) not running — telling you why instead of sending every play into silence. Playing a streaming source while HQPlayer is the output likewise says so plainly (only the local library can be routed through HQPlayer today) rather than doing nothing. The toggle also stays reachable while it is on even if HQPlayer stops answering (host asleep, app closed): the setting keeps routing playback from the box, so hiding its only off switch would have left every play failing with no way to stop it. You may need to re-enable the toggle once after updating.

Fixed

  • [core + ui] Your HQPlayer output setting can no longer be switched off behind your back. The app checked whether HQPlayer's audio daemon was running and, if not, turned the setting off for you. Harmless when the setting lived in your browser — but it now lives on the box, so any device merely showing the HQPlayer panel while that daemon restarted (which happens every time you change HQPlayer's audio output) silently disabled the setting on all your devices at once. Enforcement moved to the box: it refuses to enable the setting without a running daemon, and refuses to send a track to a dead one, telling you which daemon is missing instead of changing your choice. The switch also ignores a second tap while the first is still being saved — rapid flips could leave it showing the opposite of what was stored.
  • [core] Disconnecting HQPlayer now gives the sound card back. Disconnecting cleared the connection without stopping HQPlayer, so its audio daemon kept the card open — and with the address gone, Audiogravity had no way left to stop it: local playback stayed broken until a restart. It now stops HQPlayer before disconnecting. The app was already doing this on its own; anything else (a script, an older version) was left with a stuck card.
  • [core] Starting playback when HQPlayer's daemon is down now fails immediately, with the reason. The setting is remembered across restarts, so a box rebooting with that daemon stopped kept trying: every track waited a couple of seconds then failed, on every device, with no clue why. Audiogravity now checks first and answers straight away — "its network audio daemon (NAA) is not running on this box".
  • [core + ui] A cast you started is no longer labelled as coming from somewhere else. When you sent a radio station or a media-server track to a network speaker, the player could badge it External — the label meaning "another app is driving this speaker". Audiogravity was trying to recognise the music from its stream address, which is impossible for a radio URL and only works for a media-server track until an internal list fills up or the box restarts. The origin now travels with the track when Audiogravity casts it, so a station shows as Radio and a server track under its server's name. It still says External when someone really does take the speaker over — the address is checked, so the label stays honest.
  • [core] Music loaded on your speaker by another app is no longer mistaken for your library. A third-party controller pointing the speaker at a network share (an address that isn't a web link) was labelled Library, as if the track came from Audiogravity. It now shows as External.
  • [ui] The "External" badge shows its name and icon instead of raw text. The label was missing from the app's badge list, so it displayed the bare word external with a generic note icon.
  • [core] A slow-starting track through HQPlayer is no longer reported as a failure. Audiogravity checks that playback really began — but it gave up after 2.5 seconds, while HQPlayer reports "stopped" during its warm-up. On a heavy processing chain (upsampling to DSD, convolution) a perfectly good track was announced as failed just before the music started. It now waits long enough for that warm-up, and checks immediately instead of always pausing a quarter-second first, so a track that starts at once is confirmed faster. It also stops clearing its shared status reading, which used to make every other part of the app query HQPlayer separately exactly when it was busiest.
  • [ui] The progress bar no longer jumps backwards after you move it. Moving the cursor sent the bar to the right spot, then a status update already in transit — still carrying the old position — pulled it back for a moment before it jumped forward again. It looked like the move had failed, and invited a second attempt. The bar now holds your target until the player confirms it, and gives up holding on its own if the device really refused the move.
  • [core] The player no longer credits an idle speaker for sound coming out of your DAC. When a network speaker was selected but stopped, Audiogravity treated it as the destination — so a track playing locally (AirPlay, for instance) was labelled "→ ". Worse, the app only reports problems for the destination it believes is active, so a genuine local sound-card error stayed invisible in exactly that situation. The destination is now whichever output is actually carrying the audio; when nothing plays, the selected speaker keeps its place so an idle setup stays visible.
  • [core] A stopped network speaker can be started again from the player. After a restart of Audiogravity — or once another source had played — the player had no handle on the selected speaker, so pressing play did nothing and returned an error. The handle is now derived from the output list, which covers every case.
  • [ui] "Up next" follows the track you are looking at. With a cast running and another source playing at the same time, switching to that other source still showed the speaker's queue as its upcoming track. The upcoming track is now read from the item on screen.
  • [core] Radio now respects your output choice — it used to always play locally. The "send playback to HQPlayer" decision was wired into the library only, so starting a radio station while HQPlayer was your output pushed it to the local player instead — and since HQPlayer still held the sound card, you got silence and a "device busy" error. Every way of starting music (library, media server, radio) now goes through the same routing decision, so a new one cannot silently miss it again.
  • [core] Adding a track to the queue no longer stops what you are listening to. With HQPlayer as output, "add to queue" on a media-server track was treated as "play now": Audiogravity cleared the queue and jumped to that track, while telling you it had been added. Adding now appends, and leaves the current track playing. Adding also no longer overwrites the Now Playing card with the queued track's title and cover.
  • [core] Roon works again while HQPlayer is your output. Any Roon playback was refused with "this source cannot be played through HQPlayer — turn HQPlayer off to play it on the local output", which was wrong on both counts: a Roon zone is its own output and never uses the sound card HQPlayer replaces. Roon plays normally again, whatever your local output is.
  • [core] Selecting two outputs at once now tells you, instead of both pretending to work. Nothing prevented having a network speaker selected and HQPlayer as output: playback went to HQPlayer while the player showed the speaker as the destination. Audiogravity now says the two choices conflict and asks you to turn one off — until the output arbitration lands, it refuses rather than guessing which device you meant.
  • [core] Sending music to HQPlayer no longer reports success when nothing plays. Audiogravity used to report the outcome of its conversation with HQPlayer, not the outcome of the playback: it answered "done" when HQPlayer had accepted the track but could not actually start (typically because the local sound card was still held by another player), and conversely answered with an error on a play that was working fine. It now checks that HQPlayer really started and reports the reason when it did not — "its audio output is unavailable (most often the local sound card is still held by another player)".
  • [ui] Turning off "Use as output" for HQPlayer now gives the DAC back — local playback works again. The toggle only changed where Audiogravity sent new plays; HQPlayer stayed loaded and its network audio daemon (NAA) kept the sound card open, so playing locally afterwards failed silently (Failed to open ALSA device "hw:0,0": Device or resource busy). Switching the toggle off — or disconnecting HQPlayer — now stops HQPlayer so the card is released. Casting to a network renderer was unaffected (it never uses the local card).
  • [core] Pausing a cast no longer makes the mini-player vanish. A network-renderer cast (and any virtual source such as HQPlayer) is now kept visible in the mini-player while Paused — it was treated as "not playing" and filtered out, so pausing it collapsed the mini-player (the fullscreen was unaffected).
  • [core + ui] Seeking on a network renderer is more robust, and the progress bar follows. A seek now tries both ABS_TIME and REL_TIME (some renderers advertise both but honour only one, e.g. upmpdcli); the new position is refreshed immediately so the progress bar jumps to it instead of appearing stuck for up to the 5-second poll interval (with an optimistic bar update for instant feedback). If the device rejects every seek method (UPnP 501 for an HTTP stream it can't seek) Audiogravity disables the seek control for that track instead of erroring repeatedly — it re-enables automatically on the next track.

Changed

  • [core + ui] HQPlayer now appears as what it is — a processor in the audio chain, not a source. When Audiogravity sends music to HQPlayer, the player badges the item with its real origin (Library) and the signal path shows the full processing chain (e.g. Library → HQPlayer Embedded → Music Streamer → DAC) — HQPlayer is a DSP step, not where the music comes from. When HQPlayer is driven outside Audiogravity (its own remote), the item shows External with the live format — the HQPlayer API does not expose the track title for external playback, so that sub-case is deliberately title-less for now. Transport commands and the DSD amplifier protection keep routing to HQPlayer through its (unchanged) internal routing identity; the DSD volume-slider exception and DSD-lock badge in the player now key on the routing identity too, which also extends them correctly to network-renderer casts (both are self-managed devices).
  • [core + ui] A cast to a network renderer now shows the content for what it is — the renderer becomes an output. When you send music to a network renderer (a Marantz/Linn player, another Audiogravity box), the player now badges the item with its real origin — Qobuz, Library, the UPnP server's name, or External when another app drives the renderer — and shows the renderer as the output ("→ renderer name", output bar), instead of presenting the renderer itself as the source. Under the hood every now-playing item now carries two separate identities: what it is (display: origin, played_on) and what plays it (routing: control_id) — commands always go to the device that plays the content (MPD locally, the renderer for a cast), so play/pause/next keep working no matter how the item is badged. The player state gains a runtime outputs[] list (local DAC + selected renderer, with live transport state, built from in-memory caches only — zero added polling) with active_output_id and queue_next; a selected-but-stopped renderer stays visible through it (same on-screen result as before, no more synthetic "Stopped" item). The renderer's transport state now lives in one place (outputs[]): the player UI no longer consumes the renderer_status channel (kept for the Settings card), reactivity preserved via an event bridge (renderer GENA events → player state push, ~1s). The DSD amplifier protection is re-keyed on the routing identity, so a cast badged "Qobuz" can never have the renderer's physical volume forced to 100. Both control endpoints accept the new control_id handle (source_id still works).

Refactored

  • [core] The Now Playing service is split into focused modules (behaviour-preserving).
